# Textbooks

There is no single reference textbook for this subject. Students should attend lectures and study these lecture notes. A bibliography of all the works cited is provided at the end. For further reading, the following documents and books may be consulted.

P. GRÉGOT, *La Bible, Parole de Dieu. Introduction théologique à l'étude de l'Écriture Sainte*, Desclée & Co, Paris 1965. An excellent textbook, though now somewhat dated. In English:*The Bible, Word of God: A Theological Introduction to the Study of Scripture*, Desclée, New York 1968. In Spanish:*La Biblia, palabra de Dios. Introducción teológica al estudio de la Sagrada Escritura*, Herder, Barcelona 1968. Not available in Italian.

# Bibliographical references

These pages have been created to serve as a reference point for students undertaking the 'General Introduction to Sacred Scripture' course, a 12-hour semester-long module in the first cycle of the Faculty of Theology at the Pontifical University of the Holy Cross. They are therefore deliberately written in a pedagogical style, whilst the content is provisional in nature.

# Note on the second edition

This edition contains no substantial differences from the first (2018). In addition to the correction of typographical errors and the reworking of certain paragraphs, the bibliography has been updated, the presentation of the New Testament text has been expanded, and the section on the Gospel of Thomas has been moved from the Excursus to Part III.

Rome, 21 September 2021

- G. DᴇIᴀNᴀ, Introduction to Sacred Scripture in the Light of 'Dei Verbum', Urbaniana University Press, Vatican City 2009. In English: Introduction to the Sacred Scripture in the Light of 'Dei Verbum', 2014. Useful for a first approach.

- Mannucci - L. Mazzinghi, The Bible as the Word of God: A General Introduction to Sacred Scripture, Queriniana, Brescia 21 2016. Useful as a supplement on certain points.

- M. PRIᴏᴛᴛᴏ, The Book of the Word: An Introduction to Scripture, Elledici, Turin 2016.

- V. BADAGUER, Introduction to Sacred Scripture, Eunsa, Pamplona 2019. An excellent textbook, particularly regarding the relationship between the Bible and revelation, and interpretation.

# Some useful texts in English, Spanish and other languages

The following four articles, taken from R. E. BROWN - J. A. FITZMYER - R. E. MURPHY (eds.), *The New Jerome Biblical Commentary*, Geoffrey Chapman, London 1990, correspond to the core content of our course:

- R. F. COLLINS, 'Inspiration', 1021-1022.

- R. E. BROWN - D. W. JOHNSON - K. G. O'CONNELL, 'Texts and Versions', 1083-1112.

- !) R. E. BROWN - R. F. COLLINS, 'Canonicity', 1034-1054.

- R. E. BROWN - S. M. SCHNEIDERS, 'Hermeneutics', 1146-1165.

The NJBC has been translated into several languages. In Italian: Nuovo grande commento biblico, Queriniana, Brescia 1997. In Spanish: *Nuevo Comentario Bíblico San Jerónimo. Nuevo Testamento y artículos temáticos*, Verbo Divino, Estella 2004. In Portuguese:*Novo Comentário Bíblico São Jerônimo: Novo Testamento e Artigos Sistemáticos*, Academia Cristã 2011. In Polish:*Katolicki komentarz biblijny *, Vocatio, Warsaw, 2010.

In Spanish, in addition to the Spanish version of the NJBC articles cited above, the following three entries from C. IᴢqᴜIᴇRᴅᴏ (ed.), *Diccionario de teología*, Eunsa, Pamplona! 2014, are well worth reading:

- S. AᴜSÍN - C. JÓᴅᴀR - B. ESᴛRᴀᴅᴀ - J.M. DÍᴀᴢ-RᴏᴅᴇᴌᴀS, 'Biblia', pp. 8-98.

- G. ARANDA, 'Inspiration of Sacred Scripture', 511-517.

- !) G. ARANDA, 'Canon bíblico', 99-104.

# Documents of the Magisterium of the Church and of the Pontifical Biblical Commission

CONCILIUM VATICANUM II, Dogmatic Constitution *Dei Verbum* on Divine Revelation (18 November 1965). # INTRODUCTION

Aristotle (Metaphysics V,1) stated that the sciences are distinguished from one another by their object, that is, by the part or aspect of reality that they study. In the case of the General Introduction to Sacred Scripture (IGSS), it is clear that the object of study is the Bible.

However, this statement needs to be clarified, because the same subject matter can be studied by different sciences, each from its own perspective. Using scholastic terminology, we can say that the material object of this science is the collection of books known as the 'Bible', whilst its formal objectthat is, the perspective from which the material object is studied-is the Bible within the Church. It is not, therefore, a matter of studying the biblical books merely as ancient literature or as a fundamental part of cultural history, but of studying them theologically. Our subject can be defined as 'that branch of theology which examines all the issues necessary for a correct understanding of the sacred books[^1] '(1). A more informal, yet also more didactic, definition is proposed by Artola and Sánchez Caro. The aim of this subject is to answer the following question: what is the Bible for the Catholic Christian[^2]? 2

At first glance, these definitions certainly seem rather general and lacking in any particular significance. For this reason, we shall not dwell on the problems associated with defining the subject[^3].

Nevertheless, we should not forget that the question of what Sacred Scripture is within the Church constitutes the subject of our study. Indeed, each part of the pro- This programme can be understood as an attempt at an answer. The course is structured into four main parts:

- I The Bible and revelation.

II Text.

III Canon.

- IV Interpretation.

What is Sacred Scripture? In the first part, we shall see that it is a written testimony to divine revelation, but not only that: by virtue of its inspiration, it can also be called a revelation of God, that is, the Word of God. The second and third parts define the Bible in more concrete terms: which words (text) and which books (canon) make it up. As for the fourth part, one might get the impression that it is no longer a matter of defining Holy Scripture, but of understanding its message. However, since the essence of a book lies in its message, the study of biblical interpretation also constitutes an answer to the question of the identity of Scripture.

This lecture note is followed by an appendix which explores in greater depth certain issues relating to the nature of the charism of inspiration. This section complements the first part, which discusses inspiration in relation to revelation. Finally, there are some digressions on specific topics.

Before beginning to explain the first part, it is useful to provide - by way of introduction some basic information: the various names of the Bible, the analogy between the Bible and the mystery of the Incarnation, some historical background, the characteristics of books in antiquity, the list of books that make up the Bible, and the languages in in which they were written.

## 1. The Names of the Bible

When referring to the collection of sacred books common to Jews and Christians, we are now accustomed to using the word 'Bible'. Other equivalent terms are 'Holy Scripture' or simply 'Scripture'. The plural forms are also used: 'Scriptures', 'Holy Scriptures'. These names are not neutral, but imply a certain idea of what the Bible is. The use of the adjective 'sacred' or 'holy' implies a religious dimension. On the other hand, the use of a singular term to refer to many books reflects a unified conception of them.

Before analysing the meaning of these terms, it is worth asking whether the Bible refers to itself and, if so, how. Generally speaking, the books that make up the Bible have little 'self-awareness'; that is to say, they speak to us without introducing themselves. Genesis, for example, has no title nor does it begin by saying 'this book is sacred' or anything of the sort, but starts immediately with the account of the creation of the heavens and the earth.

modern editions have to include an introduction before each book to assist the reader.

In the Old Testament, there are very few references to the 'Bible' as a collection of different books. In 2 Maccabees 8:2, we read that Judas Maccabeus, before a battle, encouraged his men to be ready to die for the laws and for their country and 'then read the sacred book (τὴν ἱερὰν βίβλον) aloud in public' 1. In this instance, the 'sacred book' does not refer to all the books of the Old Testament, but most likely only to the Book of the Law, that is, the Torah or Pentateuch (cf. 1 Maccabees 1:56-57;3,48). In 1 Maccabees 12:9 we find a similar expression: 'the sacred books' (τὰ βιβλία τὰ ἅγια), but it does not specify which ones.

A reference to the collection of books in the possession of the Jews appears in the prologue to Sirach, which speaks of the teachings contained 'in the Law, the Prophets and the other writings'. It does not state that these books are holy or sacred, nor does it use a single term, but refers to three groups (we shall return to this text on pp. 170-171).

In a non-biblical Jewish text, dated to around the 2nd century BC, the so-called Letter of Aristeas, the expression 'the Scripture' appears for the first time in a generic sense (cf. nos. 155 and 168). However, this is a reference to the Torah, not to the entire Bible[^5].

The books of the New Testament resemble those of the Old Testament in that they do not present themselves as sacred books, with the exception of the Book of Revelation. However, amongst the New Testament authors we find numerous references to their 'Bible', that is, to the books we now call the Old Testament. To refer to the Scriptures of Israel, the New Testament authors use the following expressions:

- 'book' (βίβλος or βιβλίον, literally 'scroll') is always used in reference to a specific book: 'the Book of the Prophet Isaiah', 'the Book of Psalms';

- 'letter' (γράμμα) appears only once in the New Testament to denote Scripture, and this is also the only instance in which it is accompanied by the adjective 'sacred' (ἱερός): 'the sacred letters' (τὰ ἱερὰ γράμματα, 2 Tim 1:15)!.

- The most common expression is 'the Scriptures' (αἱ γραφαί); it also appears in the singular: 'the Scripture' (E γραφF).

The difference between the plural and the singular warrants a comment. In the Synoptic Gospels and in the Acts of the Apostles, the expression 'the Scripture' always refers to a specific passage, not to the Scriptures as a whole. For example, in Mark 12:10, after the parable of the vineyard tenants, Jesus quotes a psalm (Ps 118:22-23) as follows:

> Have you not read this Scripture (τ2ν γραφ2ν 'The stone which the builders rejected has

> become the cornerstone (…)'?

In contrast, in the Gospel of John, in Paul's letters and in 2 Peter, both the plural and the singular are used to refer to the collection of books we now call 'the Bible'. For example, in John 1:15, Jesus states that 'the Scripture cannot be set aside'. And in John 20:9, the evangelist says that the disciples 'had not yet understood the Scripture, namely that he was to rise from the dead'. In both cases, it does not appear that a specific text is being referred to, but rather the Scriptures of Israel in general. This terminology becomes more significant when compared with the references to the Bible made by a Jewish author of the time, Flavius Josephus (c. 15-100 AD). Josephus never uses a singular term. All the terms he uses are in the plural: 'Scriptures' (γραφαί), 'records' (ἀναγραφαί), 'books' (píp ʎ oi, βιβλία) or 'letters' (γράμματα), often describing them as 'sacred[^7]' 1. The shift from 'Scriptures' to 'Scripture'-that is, from the plural to the singular-which is found in some New Testament authors, is not insignificant. This change seems to reflect a new theological conception: the Scriptures of Israel are beginning to be regarded not merely as a collection of books,

but in some sense as a single book.

Our word 'Bible' embodies this shift from the plural to the singular. It derives from the Greek βιβλία - which is the plural of βιβλίον, 'scroll', 'book' a n d was subsequently adopted into Christian Latin to refer to the sacred Scriptures as biblia, -orum, which is a plural tantum, that is, a word used only in the plural. In the Middle Ages, the word gradually became a feminine singular noun, *biblia, -ae*, and has remained so in Italian ('la Bibbia') and in other modern languages, where it has also become the most common term for referring to the Holy Scriptures[^8].

On the one hand, it is undeniable that the books of the Bible differ from one another in terms of human author, era, language, literary style, social context, theological message, and a long list of other factors. On the other hand, the Bible can, in a certain sense, be regarded as a single book, not

ταúττν):

not only because it has the same divine author, the Holy Spirit, but also because it tells a unified story, from Genesis to Revelation, which culminates in Jesus Christ[^9].

The name 'Bible' embodies both the divine and human nature of the sacred books. We shall discuss this mystery in these lessons.

## 2. An analogy between Scripture and the Incarnation

As a starting point for discussing the nature of the Bible, we can draw on an analogy that helps to frame the subject from the outset: Scripture is both divine and human, just as Jesus is the eternal Word of God and is also truly human[^10].

The Second Vatican Council's constitution *Dei Verbum* presents this analogy in the following terms:

> For the words of God, expressed in human language, have become like human speech, just as the Word of the eternal Father, having taken on the frailties of human nature, became like man (DV 13[^11])!.

The comparison of Scripture with the Incarnation of the Word reminds us, first and foremost, that the divine origin of the biblical books is a mystery of faith. For just as no Christian can claim to understand the mystery of the union of the two natures, human and divine, in the person of Christ, so too no one will ever be able to fully comprehend the character of the Bible as the Word of God.

Furthermore, this analogy enables us to avoid two opposing extremes in the understanding of Sacred Scripture, both of which are equally mistaken[^12].

- On the one hand, the Bible did not 'fall from heaven' as Muslims say of the Qur'an; that is to say, it is not a timeless divine Word of absolute value, untainted by human limitations. Understanding Sacred Scripture in this way would imply falling into an error similar to Docetism, which denied that Jesus was truly human, or to Monophysitism, according to which Christ's divinity absorbs his humanity. It is not Christian to deny the true humanity of the biblical words, which, as such, are necessarily bound up with concrete historical circumstances, cultural influences, the expressive limitations of a language, and so on. The Bible is entirely human in its origin, in its words, in its modes of expression and also in its content.

- On the other hand, it would be equally wrong to neglect, diminish or deny the divinity of Scripture, that is, its value as God's revelation to the Church. To continue the analogy with Christological heresies, attempting to separate the divine message from the human elements that contain it would be an error similar to Nestorianism, according to which Jesus was a man in whom the Son of God was present. Instead, denying the Bible's status as the Word of God can be compared to more modern phenomena, such as atheism or agnosticism. The true humanity of the biblical words must not make us forget that, through them, God has spoken in the past and still speaks to us today. A purely human understanding of the Bible is always possible, but it does not grasp its full depth, nor can it perceive its unity. Similarly, attempting to understand Jesus whilst setting aside the supernatural dimension produces a partial - and distorted - view of his person and his life.

In a speech delivered in 1993, on the occasion of the fiftieth anniversary of the encyclical *Divino Agente Spiritu*, Saint John Paul II referred to the analogy between the Incarnation and Scripture. In precise terms, he explained some of the consequences of the Bible's divine and human character, particularly with regard to the interpretation of Sacred Scripture and the importance of understanding literary genres. Here are his words:

> Divino Agante Spiritu, as is well known, particularly recommended to exegetes the study of the literary genres used in the sacred books (…). This recommendation is based on the concern to understand the meaning of the texts with as much accuracy and precision as possible and, therefore, within their historical and cultural context. A false conception of God and the Incarnation leads a number of Christians to adopt the opposite approach. They tend to believe that, since God is the Absolute Being, each of his words has an absolute value, independent of all the constraints of human language. There is therefore, according to them, no scope for studying these constraints in order to make distinctions that relativise- would diminish the significance of the words. But this amounts to deluding oneself and, in reality, rejecting the mysteries of scriptural inspiration and the Incarnation, by resorting to a false notion of the Absolute. The God of the Bible is not an absolute Being who, by crushing everything He touches, would suppress all differences and all nuances. On the contrary, He is the Creator God, who created the astonishing variety of beings 'each according to its own kind', as the account in Genesis states and recounts (cf. Gen. 1). Far from erasing differences, God respects and values them (cf. 1 Cor. 12:18, 24, 28). When he expresses himself in human language, he does not assign a uniform value to every expression, but utilises its possible nuances with great flexibility, whilst also accepting its limitations. This is what makes the task of exegetes so complex, so necessary and so fascinating! None of the human aspects of language can be overlooked. Recent advances in linguistic, literary and hermeneutical research have led biblical exegesis to incorporate many other perspectives (rhetorical, narrative, structuralist) alongside the study of literary genres; other human sciences, such as psychology and sociology, have likewise been welcomed to make their contribution (…).

> However, this study is not sufficient. To uphold the coherence of the Church's faith and the inspiration of Scripture, Catholic exegesis must be careful not to confine itself to the human aspects of the biblical texts. It must, above all, help the Christian people to perceive the word of God more clearly in these texts, so that they may receive it more fully and live in full communion with God[^13].

In summary, the Christian understanding must respect the divine and human character of Scripture. We can express this principle in pedagogical terms by saying that the Bible is 100 per cent human and 100 per cent divine. Any explanation of its nature must avoid both a denial and a diminution of these 'percentages'. With regard to inspiration (cf. pp. 242-249), we shall see that the Church has rejected all those theories which, in one way or another, have sought to eliminate or alter any of these percentages.

## 3. Historical Background

In what follows, I shall briefly recall certain historical events, without critical analysis or bibliographical references, as they are recorded in Scripture and other sources. I shall omit the accounts of the Pentateuch - the origins of heaven and earth and of humanity, the Flood, the patriarchs, the Exodus from Egypt, the covenant on Mount Sinai and the pilgrimage in the desert - and those recounted in the Books of Joshua and Judges - the entry and settlement of the twelve tribes in the Promised Land - are assumed to be known, at least in their essential outlines. I shall therefore begin with the era of the monarchy. I shall dwell a little longer on the Hellenistic and Roman periods, which are generally less familiar to students.

The beginnings of the monarchy in Israel are recounted in 1 Samuel. The last of the judges is the prophet Samuel, who anoints Saul, of the tribe of Benjamin, as the first king. But Saul is rejected by the Lord and replaced by David, son of Jesse, of the tribe of Judah, with whom a dynasty begins - the 'House of David' - which will endure for a long time, with its seat in

Jerusalem. The generally accepted dating for the Davidic kingdom is around 1000 BC. Solomon, son and successor of David and the epitome of a wise man, built a house for the Ark of the Covenant, the Temple of Jerusalem.

After Solomon's death, the kingdom split into two: the Northern Kingdom, also known as Israel, with Samaria as its capital, and the Southern Kingdom, Judah, which retained Jerusalem as its capital.

The next event to note is the ca-

fall of Samaria at the hands of the Assyrian army in 22/21 BC, followed by the deportation of its A century later, in 612, the capital of the Assyrian Empire, Nineveh, was destroyed. Nebuchadnezzar (605-562) founded a new empire, with Babylon as its capital. Nebuchadnezzar himself destroyed the walls of Jerusalem and the Temple in 587 BC. Many biblical texts lament this:

> O God, the nations have invaded your inheritance: they have desecrated your holy temple, they have reduced Jerusalem to ruins (Ps 78:1).

> How desolate lies the city that was once so full of people! (Lamentations 1:1).

This defeat brought with it the end of the monarchy and political independence, and the beginning of the exile in Babylon. But the Babylonian empire was short-lived. In the mid-6th century BC, Cyrus, King of the Persians, emerged; he conquered Babylon in 599 BC and succeeded in establishing one of the most vast political organisations of antiquity, the Persian Empire, which stretched from Asia Minor to India.

Cyrus allowed the Jewish community in exile to return to Jerusalem and to rebuild the walls and the Temple, a process that took place in several stages from 550 BC onwards (the date of the so-called 'Edict of Cyrus'). Amidst countless difficulties, the Temple was rebuilt in Jerusalem; it is now known as the 'Second Temple', to distinguish it from the one built by Solomon. During this period, the prophets Haggai, Zechariah and Malachi, the priest and scribe Ezra, and Nehemiah were active.

Two centuries after Cyrus, it was the Greeks' turn. Thanks to the rapid and spectacular victories of Alexander the Great (356-321 BC), an empire emerged to take the place of the Persian one. This was the Hellenistic Empire, which would last only a very short time as a political entity, but which would leave a legacy of the spread of the Greek language and culture throughout the oikoumene, the inhabited world known at that time.

On Alexander's death, his vast empire was divided amongst his generals. The land of Israel became part of Egypt (capital: Alexandria), ruled by the Ptolemies, also known as the Lagids. However, in 200 BC, following the Battle of Panium (Caesarea Philippi, now Banias), won by Antiochus III, Judea came under the control of Syria (capital: Antioch), ruled by the Seleucids.

During the reign of Antiochus IV Epiphanes (15-164 BC), son of Antiochus III, a revolt led by the Maccabean brothers broke out in reaction to Antiochus IV's policy of Hellenisation. In 167, the 'abomination of desolation' took place (cf. 1 Maccabees 1:54; Daniel 9:27; 11:11), namely the erection of an altar to Olympian Zeus in the Temple of Jerusalem. In 164, following a series of military victories, the leader of the revolt, Judas Maccabeus, succeeded in purifying the Temple and securing a degree of political and religious independence. After him, his brother Simon Maccabeus founded the Hasmonean dynasty. However, as the years passed, the relative autonomy they had achieved was increasingly eroded to to the benefit of the new world power, Rome. A significant date to note is 6 BC, the year in which General Pompey entered Jerusalem and Judea came under Roman sovereignty[^14].

Jesus was born around 6 BC, when the emperor of Rome and of the world was Octavian (Caesar Augustus). Judea was ruled by Herod the Great, a king subject to Rome, famous for his cruelty, but also for having enlarged and embellished the Temple in Jerusalem (pictured: a model of the Temple in the first century AD, Israel Museum, Jerusalem).

Jesus died around AD 10 under the procurator Pontius Pilate, whilst the emperor was Tiberius.

The final date to note is AD 70, the year of the destruction of the Second Temple by Titus, a general in the Roman army and future emperor, which took place during the Jewish revolt against Rome in the years 66-70, also known as the 'First Jewish Revolt', as there would be a second and final one from AD 112 to 115, also known as the Bar Kokhba revolt.

Here is a table summarising the key dates and names already mentioned:

## 4. Books in Antiquity

To understand textual criticism, the formation of the biblical canon and also certain characteristics of the charism of inspiration, it is essential to recall when writing first emerged and what books were like in antiquity.

Writing is one of the greatest inventions of all time, so much so that it marks the transition from prehistory to history. Conceived as a mnemonic device for accounting, writing appeared in Mesopotamia around 1200 BC. All evidence suggests that the Sumerians were the inventors of this system and that, throughout the entire fourth millennium, writing was used exclusively for the Sumerian language[^15].

In the first half of the second millennium, in the land of Canaan, the alphabet was created another ingenious invention, as it reduced hundreds of logograms and syllabograms to around thirty signs, one for each phoneme. The alphabet was devised for the ' ' 'Proto-Canaanite' language, from which Phoenician, Hebrew and Aramaic would later emerge[^16].

### 4.1. Materials

In the ancient world, neither paper - which arrived in the West from China in the 7th-8th centuries AD - nor, of course, printing - a European invention of the 15th century - existed (we shall discuss printing later: pp. 112-118). All books were composed and copied by hand.

Various materials could be used as a surface on which to write. There are inscriptions on stone, metal and clay (such inscriptions are the subject of epigraphy). Stone and metal are highly durable, but they are difficult to transport and ill-suited to long texts. In this regard, we might recall the stone tablets on which it is said that God (Ex 31:18) or Moses (Ex 34:18) wrote the law on Mount Sinai; or the twelve stones on which Joshua later also wrote the law (Josh 8:32); or the beautiful words of Job, who insists that the testimony of his innocence before the divine court must not be erased:

> Oh, if only my words were written down, if only they were set down in a book, were engraved with an iron stylus and lead, and were

> carved forever into the rock! (Job 19:23-24).

Perhaps some biblical texts were written on clay tablets, which were the most common writing material in Mesopotamia. But the manuscripts preserved to this day are all made of papyrus or parchment (the subject of palaeography). We therefore focus on these two types of material[^17].

#### 4.1.1. Papyrus

In 2 John 12, the author of the letter says: 'I have many things to write to you, but I did not wish to do so with paper and ink [διὰ χάρτου καὶ μέλανος]; I hope, however, to come to you and to be able to speak to you in person'. The term 'paper' here translates the Greek word chartês (χάρτης), which refers to a sheet or scroll of papyrus.

Papyrus is a plant (Cyperus papyrus) that grows in marshes and near shallow lakes. Particularly abundant in the Nile Valley, papyrus plants were used for various purposes, including the production of writing material, also known as 'papyrus'. Fibres were extracted from the papyrus stalks; when these were layered and interwoven like a net and then pressed, they formed a sheet.

> A single sheet could be between 10 and 29 cm wide and between 20 and 10 cm high, but the standard dimensions were 25 cm in height and between 18 and 20 cm in width. (…). Each sheet had fibres running horizontally on one side and vertically on the other. Papirologists usually refer to these two sides as the recto and the verso respectively[^18].

Usually, writing was done on the surface with the fibres running horizontally (recto), but if necessary, the verso could also be used. A papyrus written on both sides is known as an 'opisthograph'.

The scroll containing Jeremiah's oracles, which was torn up by King Jehoiakim with a 'scribe's penknife' (a small knife) and burnt in the brazier (cf. Jer 36), must have been made of papyrus; otherwise, it would not have been easy either to tear it up or to burn it[^19].

#### 4.1.2. Leather

The use of animal skin (especially calf, goat or sheep) as a writing material dates back to ancient times. There is a manuscript on skin dating from 2000 BC.

If necessary, it was possible to erase the text by scraping it off and write over it. Such manuscripts are given the technical name 'palimpsests', from uá ʎ in ('again') and mám ('to scrape, to wash').

The Jews began to use leather for transcribing biblical texts during the Persian period (5th-4th centuries BC). Among the manuscripts found at Qumran, leather scrolls are far more numerous than those made of papyrus!. Later, the rabbis decreed that the Torah should be copied onto leather, the only material considered worthy[^20].

The technique for preparing the hide developed particularly during the 3rd century BC in the city of Pergamon (Asia Minor), from which 'parchments' take their name. Parchments allow writing on both sides of the sheet and therefore have a greater capacity to contain text than papyri.

Contrary to a widely held belief, it should be clarified that, strictly speaking, not all leather manuscripts are parchments[^21].

When St Paul asks Timothy to bring him back the cloak and the books he had left at Troas, he adds 'above all the parchments' (μάλιστα τὰς μεμβράνας, 2 Tim 4:13), probably because they were of greater value than papyrus books.

> Papyrus was by far the most widespread writing material during the Hellenistic period and the early Roman imperial era, but parchment was also used; in certain areas and for certain purposes, it was clearly preferred to papyrus and later, in the medieval period, replaced it entirely[^22].

All the New Testament manuscripts dating from before the 4th century that we know of are on papyrus.

The parchments containing New Testament texts that have survived date from the 4th century onwards[^23].

### 4.2. Format

As mentioned, over time, parchment eventually replaced papyrus as the material on which to write. A similar transition took place with regard to the format of the books: the scroll gave way to the codex.

#### 4.2.1. Scrolls

The basic form of ancient books was the scroll, known as měgillâ in Hebrew, biblos or biblion in Greek, and volumen in Latin.

In the case of papyrus scrolls, the scroll was made by gluing the sheets together[^24]. In contrast, in the case of leather scrolls, the joints between the sheets were made by sewing them together with fibres, rather than by gluing. Consequently, they were not completely smooth and could not be written on.

It is interesting to note that the author of a book did not write on a pre-prepared scroll, but on loose sheets, which allowed them to insert new material before joining the various sheets together, or to change their order. Long before the advent of the computer, writing a book was often a matter of 'cut and paste'. Plato has left us an account of how a book or scroll was composed. After defining the philosopher, Socrates, the protagonist of the dialogue, puts a question to Phaedrus:

> Would you not rightly call someone who possesses nothing of greater value than the things he has composed or written-turning them this way and that for a long time, joining one part to another or removing sections-a poet, a composer or a lawgiver? (Phaedrus 278 d-e[^25]) 2.

When compiling the final scroll, the length of the text to be copied was taken into account. When the author considered the text ready, he had it copied onto a scroll prepared specifically for the purpose, that is, one whose length was measured to contain exactly the amount of text prepared. In this process, it was not uncommon for a sheet to be placed in the wrong position at the last moment. If the author did not check the final result, it could remain that way[^26]. This method of compiling books also explains why, at a later stage, material could be added to the- at the beginning or end of the scroll. We may mention chapters 11 and 14 of Daniel, and, in the New Testament, John 21 and Romans 16[^27].

Usually, each scroll contained a single work. If the text was too long, two or more scrolls were used. Among the biblical books, the five books of the Torah were normally copied, each on a separate scroll, hence the name 'Pentateuch' (from πέντε, 'five', and τεῦχος, 'vessel, urn', and later 'case, volume').

H. Y. Gamble explains how a scroll was read and how this practical consideration also determined the length limits of the scrolls:

> The length of a papyrus scroll (…) ranged on average from y to 10 m. Shorter works required less space, but the upper limit was rarely exceeded. The maximum length depended not on the manufacturing process-since scrolls of any length could be produced-but on the reader's comfort. A scroll longer than 10 or 11 metres was too cumbersome for the reader to handle; they would hold it with both hands, using the left hand to gradually roll up the part already read, whilst the right hand unrolled the part to be read. This method is illustrated in many ancient depictions of readers with scrolls. The standard length eventually came to be strictly dictated by custom. During and after the Hellenistic period, the division of voluminous works into books (tomoi, libri) was determined as much by the conventional length of the scroll as by considerations of content. The oldest large-scale works (such as those of Herodotus, Thucydides and Homer) were subdivided on the basis of the customary length of scrolls, a factor which the authors of new substantial works took into account when dividing their own texts. In this way, the physical unit of the scroll also came to serve as a literary unit[^28].

It is likely that the current division of certain books is due not to content-related reasons, but to the capacity of the scroll. Specifically, in Hebrew, 1-2 Samuel filled one scroll and 1-2 Kings another. However, as Greek script takes up more space than Hebrew script, when translating these books into Greek it seems that it was necessary to use two scrolls for each, hence the current division into 1 and 2 Samuel and 1 and 2 Kings. The same can be said of the Book of Chronicles[^29]. However, for various reasons, some short books were systematically copied onto a single scroll[^30], such as the Twelve Prophets - at Nahal Hever: in Egypt, a papyrus scroll dating from the 1st century AD was found containing the text of the Twelve Prophets in Greek -. Even the five books which, from a certain point onwards, are referred to as Měgillôt were copied onto a single scroll 1.

In the Gospel of Luke, an episode from the life of Jesus is recounted which reflects the use of scrolls for synagogue readings:

> He came to Nazareth, where he had been brought up, and, as was his custom, on the Sabbath he went into the synagogue and stood up to read. He was handed the scroll of the prophet Isaiah; he unrolled the scroll and found the passage where it was written: [quotation from Isa 61:1-2 follows]. He rolled up the scroll, handed it back to the attendant and sat down (Luke 4:16-20).

The verb used to describe how Jesus 'unrolled' the scroll implies that, by separating the two cylinders, he immediately found the passage from Isaiah 61:1-2, without having to search for it by unrolling the book forwards or backwards, as in the image shown above this paragraph[^31].

#### 4.2.2. Codices

Very gradually, from the first century AD onwards, the scroll format began to be replaced by the 'codex' (Latin: codex), which more closely resembles modern books. A codex consists of several quires, that is, sheets folded into quarters, which, when bound together, form quires. 'The very earliest codices, whether Christian or not, tended to be of the single-quire type, with a maximum capacity of around two hundred pages[^32]'!.

Subsequently, advances in technique enabled the capacity of codices to increase enormously, leading to the large codices of the 4th century, the first capable of bringing together the Old and New Testaments.

I reproduce below three illustrations that help to better understand the descriptions just given[^33].

- (a) a sheet of papyrus used to write a letter;

- (b) a sheet of papyrus folded vertically to form a notebook and subsequently a codex;

- (c) a written papyrus scroll; the vertical lines indicate the joints where the individual sheets are glued together.

The greater convenience of the codex compared to the scroll explains why it became the predominant form in the 4th century. However, this transition was not the same for everyone. Curiously, Christians were much quicker to switch to the codex than their contemporaries, as shown by papyri from the 2nd century:

> The comparative data are instructive. Among the Greek books in our possession dating from before the 3rd century AD, more than 98 per cent are scrolls, whilst in the same period the surviving Christian books are almost all codices. Among Greek books, the codex does not appear in such proportions...

> before the 3rd century (when fewer than 20 per cent were codices), and it was only at the beginning of the 4th century that the codex began to be used almost as frequently as the scroll (48 per cent[^34]) 1.

Gamble puts forward an intriguing hypothesis to explain Christians' preference for the codex: the example set by St Paul, who is said to have chosen codices because they were easy to carry, is thought to have been decisive[^35]. However, our interest here lies solely in understanding the physical characteristics of ancient books, to avoid an anachronistic view of the Bible.

## 5. The list of biblical books

A fundamental step when embarking on the study of the Bible is to learn the names of the books that make it up. It is also useful to know the order in which they appear in modern Bibles, which is not random but has a specific meaning and practical utility. Indeed, the readings proposed by current liturgical lectionaries for specific occasions (for example, Masses for the dead) are presented in this order. It is also useful to know the abbreviations for the names of the books of the Bible. There are various ways of abbreviating them; here I shall follow those used by the Catechism of the Catholic Church, as set out on p. 12.

We shall look at the names and abbreviations in Italian, but it should be borne in mind that these vary from language to language. Some books may be referred to in very different ways. In English, the Apocalypse is called 'Revelation' and in German 'Offenbarung '. In the past, Latin abbreviations were often used. Thus, 'Io' or 'Ioh' stood for 'Iohannes', that is, the Gospel according to John. An old abbreviation that can cause confusion is 'Thren '. It stands for 'Threni ', derived from 'θρῆνοι', the Greek name for the Lamentations. The same applies to '1-2 Par' (from 'Paralipomena'), which is equivalent to '1-2 Chr'.

The total number of books in the Catholic canon is y!, comprising 46 from the Old Testament and 27 from the New Testament. These figures depend on how certain books are counted, such as 1-2 Sam, which may be regarded as a single book or as two. The number y! is the highest possible.

### 5.1. The Hebrew Bible

Before studying the books of the Old Testament, we shall look at those of the Hebrew Bible (BH, from the Latin name, Biblia Hebraica), that is, the Bible of modern Jews, which, according to various reasons - which we shall discuss later, in connection with the ca-

- is not identical to the Old Testament. The Hebrew Bible contains 24 books, which are divided into three groups: the Law, the Prophets and the Writings. In Hebrew, they are called Torah (רהוֹתָּ), Nebiim (ְנִביִאים) and Ketubim (ֻתִביםכְּ). One of the ways in which Jews refer to their Scriptures is 'Tanak', an acronym (from ἄκρος, 'end', and ὄνομα, 'name'), that is, a word formed from the first letter of each of these three groups.

Not all the books of the Tanakh have equal value. At the centre lies the Torah of Moses. The Prophets explain or comment on the Torah, whilst the Writings comment on both the Torah and the Prophets. This hierarchical way of understanding the Scriptures can be represented graphically by a diagram of three concentric circles, as shown in the image.

The Law, or Torah, consists of five books: Genesis (Gen), Exodus (Ex), Leviticus (Lev), Numbers (Num) and Deuteronomy (Deut).

The collection of the Prophets of the BH is divided into two groups, each comprising four books. The 'Earlier Prophets' are Joshua (Josh), Judges (Judg), Samuel (1-2 Sam) and Kings (1-2 Kings). The 'later prophets' are Isaiah (Is), Jeremiah (Jer), Ezekiel (Ez) and the Book of the Twelve Prophets (we shall look at the list later).

The third group, the Writings, contains 11 books. Here they are in order:

- Psalms (Ps)

- Job (Job)

- !) Proverbs (Prv)

- Ruth (Ruth)

- Song of Songs (Song)

- Ecclesiastes (Qo, Eccl)

- Lamentations (Lam)

- Esther (Est)

- Daniel (Dan)

- 10) Ezra (Ezr) and Nehemiah (Neh)

- Chronicles (1-2 Chr)

When the Temple in Jerusalem no longer existed, some medieval manuscripts, such as the Cairo Codex of the Prophets (10th century), drew a comparison between the tripartite structure of the Hebrew Bible and the three sacred spaces into which the sanctuary was internally divided

(

We may conclude this introduction to the Hebrew Bible by quoting a passage from the Babylonian Talmud (6th century CE), which is the oldest known text containing a list of the Prophets

and the Writings. There is no mention of the Torah, as there was no dispute amongst the rabbis regarding these five books and their order. The text mentions all the books that make up the Prophets and the Writings in the current BH, with some differences in the sequence (note the positions of Isaiah and Ruth). It also names those who were considered their authors:

> This is the correct order of the prophets: Joshua, Judges, Samuel, Kings, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Isaiah, the Twelve Prophets. (…) This is the correct order of the writings: Ruth, Psalms, Job, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, Song of Songs, Lamentations, Daniel, the Book of Esther, Ezra, Chronicles. (…) Who wrote them? Moses wrote his own book and parts of Balaam and Job; Joshua wrote the book bearing his name and the last eight verses of the Torah; Samuel wrote the book bearing his name, Judges and Ruth. David wrote the Book of Psalms, including the work of the Ten Elders: Adam, Melchizedek, Abraham, Moses, Heman, Jeduthun, Asaph and the three sons of Korah. Jeremiah wrote the book bearing his name, the Book of Kings and Lamentations; Hezekiah and his colleagues wrote Isaiah, Proverbs, the Song of Songs and Ecclesiastes; the men of the Great As-

(see image). The Writings would correspond to the vestibule (in Hebrew, ulam); the Prophets to the next space, the nave or 'the Holy Place' (heikal); whilst the Torah would be comparable to the holiest place, the Holy of Holies The assembly wrote Ezekiel, the twelve minor prophets, Daniel and the Book of Esther. Ezra wrote the book bearing his name and the genealogy of the Chronicles up to his own time[^37].

We shall return to this passage from the Talmud in connection with the history of the for mation of the canon (p. 187).

### 5.2. The Old Testament

For Catholics, the Old Testament contains forty-six books. These are usually divided into three groups: historical books, poetic and wisdom books, and prophetic books. As can be seen, this does not correspond to the tripartite division of the Hebrew Bible. The structure is no longer circular centred on the Torah, but linear, pointing towards Christ, who is announced especially by the prophets.

There are twenty-one historical books. The first five form the Pentateuch: Gen, Ex, Lev, Num and Deut. The following are arranged according to the chronology of the events recounted:

- Joshua

- Judges

- Ruth. It is placed here because it tells a story set in the time of the Judges and because Ruth is the great-grandmother of David, who is discussed at length in the Books of Samuel.

- 1 Sam

- 10) 2 Sam

- 1 Kings

- 2 Kings

- 13) 1 Chr

- 2 Chr

- Ezra

- Ne

- 17) Tobit (Tb)

- Judith (Gdt)

- Est

- 20) First Book of Maccabees (1 Macc)

- Second Book of Maccabees (2 Macc)

The number of poetic and wisdom books is easy to remember: seven. The sequence in this case is based on the chronology of the figures to whom the books are attributed:

- Job. Job probably occupies first place because the story is set in the time of the patriarchs (or perhaps because it is attributed to Moses).

- Ps, traditionally attributed to King David.

- !) Prv, attributed to King Solomon.

- Qo or Eccl, attributed to Solomon.

- Song of Songs (Ct), attributed to Solomon.

- Wisdom (Wis), attributed to Solomon.

- Sirach or Ecclesiasticus (Sir, Ecclo), written by Jesus Ben Sira around 180 BC.

To avoid confusion, it is preferable to refer to Qohelet and Sirach rather than Ecclesiastes and Ecclesiasticus. We shall do so in what follows.

There are 18 prophetic books, divided into the 'major' prophets and the 'minor' prophets. There are four major prophets (Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel and Daniel), plus Lamentations and Baruch (Bar), which follow Jeremiah[^38]. There are twelve minor prophets, a number symbolising the tribes of Israel. The order appears to be partly chronological, as the first place is occupied by Hosea, the oldest. However, this is not an absolute criterion, not least because the chronology of these prophets is uncertain.

- Hosea (Hos)

- Joel (Joel)

- !) Amos (Am)

- Obadiah (Abd). In English: Obadiah.

- Jonah (Jon)

- Micah (Mic)

- Nahum (Na)

- Habakkuk (Ab). In other languages with an initial 'h': Habacuc or Habakkuk.

- Zephaniah (Zeph). In English: Zephaniah.

- 10) Haggai (Ag)

- Zechariah (Zc)

- Malachi (Ml). Malachi announces the return of Elijah and is therefore appropriately placed just before the Gospels.

### 5.3. The twenty-seven books of the New Testament

In the New Testament we have four Gospels (Matthew, Mark, Luke and John), the Book of the Acts of the Apostles (Acts), twenty-one epistles and a book of revelation (Revelation). The epistles are divided into two groups: the fourteen of the Pauline corpus and the seven Catholic epistles.

The epistles of the Pauline corpus are arranged in an order that is not strictly chronological, but follows the criterion of length, amongst other factors. Generally, they range from the longest to the shortest. The Epistle to the Hebrews is placed at the end, as it is a special case, being an anonymous text.

- Epistle to the Romans (Rom)

- First Letter to the Corinthians (1 Cor)

- !) Second Epistle to the Corinthians (2 Cor)

- Epistle to the Galatians (Gal)

- Epistle to the Ephesians (Eph)

- Epistle to the Philippians (Phil)

- Letter to the Colossians (Col)

- First Epistle to the Thessalonians (1 Thess)

- Second Epistle to the Thessalonians (2

Thess) 10) First Epistle to Timothy (1 Tim)

- Second Letter to Timothy (2 Tim)

- Epistle to Titus (Tit)

- 13) Epistle to Philemon (Flm)

- Epistle to the Hebrews (Heb); in other languages with an initial 'h': Hebrews, Hebreos.

The order of the Catholic epistles is as follows:

- Epistle of James (Jas)

- First Epistle of Peter (1 Pet)

- !) Second Epistle of Peter (2 Pet)

- First Epistle of John (1 Jn)

- Second Epistle of John (2 Jn)

- Third Epistle of John (! Jn)

- The Epistle of Jude (Jude)

Why does the Epistle of James precede those of Peter, the prince of the apostles? We do not know the reason for this sequence, but in his Epistle to the Galatians, St Paul uses the same order:

> James, Cephas and John, regarded as the pillars, gave Barnabas and me the right hand of fellowship, so that we might go to the Gentiles and they to the circumcised (Gal 2:9).

The 27 books of the New Testament are as follows: Mt, Mk, Lk, Jn, Acts, Rom, 1 Cor, 2 Cor, Gal, Eph, Phil, Col, 1 Thess, 2 Thess, 1 Tim, 2 Tim, Titus, Philem, Heb, Jas, 1 Pet, 2 Pet, 1 Jn, 2 Jn, 3 Jn, Jude and Rev.

### 5.4. Chapters and verses

The division of the books of the Bible into chapters dates back to the early 13th century. It is attributed to the Englishman Stephen Langton (c. 1150-1228), Chancellor of the University of Paris (who later became a cardinal and Archbishop of Canterbury). The numbering system, which spread rapidly, was established in Paris based on the Vulgate (the Latin Bible[^39]) 1. To mark this date, it is worth noting that one of the first authors to quote Scripture taking account of the division into chapters was St Thomas Aquinas (1225-124).

Subsequently, there were several attempts to subdivide the chapters into smaller units. For the Old Testament, the verse divisions proposed by the Dominican friar Sante Pagnini in his 1527 edition of the Latin Bible are used today. However, his division of the New Testament into verses was not successful. A Protestant named Robert Estienne (1503-1559) - known in Latin as Stephanus -carried out the division of the chapters into verses in Geneva, which subsequently became the standard. He first applied this to the New Testament, which he published in 1551. Then, in 1553, he published a complete Bible in French, with all the verses numbered (adopting Pagnini's numbering for the Old Testament).

This division of the text has become universally accepted. We may quote Perrella's view on the matter, which is entirely reasonable:

> This division into chapters and verses (…) has its serious shortcomings, such as the fact that it sometimes divides the text rather mechanically without taking into account the development of ideas or the narrative; nevertheless, changing it would be a remedy worse than the disease, given the great upheaval it would cause[^40].

Unfortunately, in some books of the Old Testament (Psalms, Sirach, Jeremiah, Baruch, Ezekiel and various minor prophets), different chapter and verse numberings coexist, for various reasons. For example, in some Bibles the book of the prophet Malachi is divided into three chapters; in others, into four.

Furthermore, readers are sometimes surprised to discover that a verse is 'missing' from the Bible they are holding. For example, if we look for Luke 23:17 in the CEI Bible, we will not find it. The text jumps from verse 16 to 18:

> (…) 'After I have punished him, I will set him free.' [17] 18 But they all shouted together, 'Away with this man! Set Barabbas free!'

This is not an error or an oversight. The verse numbered 17 in the Vulgate has been omitted, as it is not considered part of the original text; as the note in the CEI Bible itself states, 'it is omitted from the most authoritative manuscripts.' This is a normal consequence of the work of textual criticism, which we shall explain in Part II.

Regarding the method of citation, in Italian a comma is used to separate the chapter from the verse (Dt 1,1; Jn 1,16), whilst in other languages, such as English, a colon is used (Deut 1:1; John 1:16).

The hyphen signifies 'from verse x to verse y' (Deut 1:1-5), whilst a full stop after ' ' a verse signifies 'verse x and then verse y' (if I say Deut 1:1.5, I exclude verses 2-4).

## 6. The languages of the biblical books

### 6.1. Introduction

The Bible was written in three languages: Hebrew, Aramaic and Greek. This fact highlights the great diversity that exists amongst the biblical books, which were written not only in different eras and places, but also in different languages. Such linguistic diversity is not without consequences. The trilingual nature of the Christian Scriptures prevents the temptation to canonise a language, in the strong sense in which many ancient cultures or religions attributed their own language to God. For Christians, there cannot be a 'sacred language' in this sense[^41].

The term 'sacred language' is sometimes used to refer to the language employed in the liturgy. Here too, there has never been a single language within the Church: whilst in the West the liturgy was in Latin, other rites used - and still use - other languages, such as Greek, Syriac or Coptic.

As means of communicating the word of God, all human languages can become 'sacred'. The Bible is, in fact, the most widely translated book in the world. At the same time, however, it is important to know and study the original languages in order to understand with pre- To neglect this endeavour would be a sign of disrespect towards Sacred Scripture. It is true that not all Christians are able to read the Bible in the original languages. But it would be a grave mistake to think that, therefore, no one needs to know them, because translations are sufficient. Pius XII expressed himself clearly and authoritatively on this point in the encyclical *Divino Agente Spiritu* (1943):

> There is now such an abundance of resources for learning those languages that a Bible interpreter who, by neglecting them, cuts off his own path to the original texts, cannot escape the charge of carelessness and sloth (EB 547).

This is not the time to learn Hebrew or Greek. In what follows, we shall confine ourselves to highlighting some of their characteristics and shall mention the language of each book.

### 6.2. Old Testament

#### 6.2.1. Hebrew and Aramaic

The 24 books of the BH were written in Hebrew, except for a few chapters of Daniel (Dan 2:47:28) and Ezra (Ezra 4:8-6:18 and 7:12-26), which are in Aramaic. There is also a phrase in Aramaic in Jer 10:11 and two Aramaic words in Gen 11:47. There are also some anomalies in the Greek versions of Daniel and Esther, which we shall discuss later (p. 48).

Hebrew belongs to the Semitic language family, alongside Ugaritic, Phoenician, Aramaic, Akkadian and Arabic. The Hebrew alphabet has 22 letters, all of which are consonants. Vowels, at least originally, were not written.

Notable features of Hebrew include:

- a preference for parataxis over hypotaxis, that is, the organisation of sentences more by coordination than by subordination;

- the use of parallelism, a fundamental feature of the Hebrew style in poetry. This consists of expressing an idea twice, either with equivalent expressions (synonymic parallelism) or with contrasting expressions (antonymic parallelism). Here is an example of a text that combines both types of parallelism:

> Therefore the wicked shall not stand in the judgement, nor sinners in the assembly of the righteous,

> for the Lord watches over the way of the righteous, whilst the path of the wicked leads to ruin (Ps 1:5-6).

- the marked scarcity of adjectives, which is resolved through the use of nouns. For example, to refer to a merciful king, one says 'king of mercy'. Some nouns become

a simple basis for an adjectival construction, as is the case with 'son' (ןבֵּ, ben). Thus, a 'son of Israel' is an Israelite, 'a son of twenty years' means 'a twenty-year-old', whilst 'son of death' (1 Sam 20:31; 26:16; 2 Sam 12:5) refers to a person who deserves to die. Even the expression 'son of man', so familiar to us through Jesus' use of it, in itself denotes nothing more than a human being, as can be seen from this synonymous parallel:

> 'What is man that you are mindful of him, the son of man, that you care for him?' (Ps 8:5).

- There are no comparative or superlative forms of adjectives. There are various ways of expressing the superlative, including the repetition of the noun as a complement to the noun: for example, the 'Song of Songs' is the song par excellence; the expression 'Holy of Holies' serves to indicate the holiest place; whilst the 'King of Kings' is the supreme king and the 'vanity of vanities' denotes the ultimate vanity.

- As regards the use of prepositions, it is interesting to note that one of them, בְּ (be), which is usually translated as 'in', has a broader meaning. For example, the CEI version translates the Hebrew be in Psalm 2:9 as 'with': 'You shall break them with a rod of iron', whilst the Latin Vulgate (Vg) renders it as ' ' in 'virga ferrea confringes eos '.

#### 6.2.2. Books written in Greek and those preserved only in Greek

The seven books of the Old Testament that do not form part of the Hebrew Bible (Tobit, Judith, 1-2 Maccabees, Wisdom, Sirach, Baruch) present a more varied picture as regards the languages in which they were written and preserved.

These seven books have been preserved in Greek, but in only two cases - Wisdom and 2 Maccabees - are we certain that they were written in that language.

The most interesting case is that of Sirach. The prologue states that the book was written in Hebrew. However, for a long time the text of Sirach was known only in its Greek version; the original Hebrew had been lost. However, in 1896, a number of manuscripts containing Sirach in Hebrew[^42] were identified, originating from the Genizah h (storehouse) of the ancient synagogue in Cairo (Egypt). Later, at Qumran and Masada, further manuscripts containing fragments of Sirach in Hebrew came to light, confirming the authenticity of the texts from Cairo. In total, we now have about two-thirds of Sirach in Hebrew 1.

Images of the Hebrew manuscripts of Sirach, with English translations, are available at www.bensira.org.

The Greek text we have of 1 Maccabees is a translation based on a Hebrew original, which has since been lost. St Jerome (4th-5th centuries) states that he saw 1 Maccabees in Hebrew: 'Machabaeorum primum librum hebraicum repperi' (Prologus Galeatus, full text on p. 192[^43]) 1.

Something similar is thought to be the case with Baruch (along with Jeremiah's letter) and Judith: the original language is thought to have been Hebrew or Aramaic, but the surviving text is in Greek[^44]. To date, no fragments of 1 Maccabees, Judith or Baruch have been found in Hebrew or Aramaic.

The Book of Tobit fell into the same category until the Qumran discoveries: the original was undoubtedly Semitic, but the text has survived only in Greek. Today we can distinguish it from the other cases, because among the manuscripts found at Qumran, four have ap appeared containing fragments of Tobit in Aramaic and one in Hebrew[^45].

#### 6.2.3. Two special cases: Daniel and Esther

Finally, we must say a few words about the Greek versions of Daniel and Esther, which present certain anomalies.

The Book of Daniel, as it is read today in the Church, follows the Greek version, which is longer than the Hebrew-Aramaic text of the BH. Only in the Greek text does the prayer of Azariah (Dan 3:24-45) appear, followed by the song of the three young men in the furnace (Dan 3:46-90). Furthermore, the Greek Bible includes several stories featuring Daniel as the protagonist, which were added to the book as appendices: the story of Susanna (Dan 13) and the stories of Bel (Dan 14:1-22) and the serpent (Dan 14:23-42). It is generally believed that these texts are Greek translations of a Semitic original, which has been lost. The reason for their absence from the Hebrew-Aramaic text is unknown.

The situation regarding the Book of Esther is more complex. The so-called Greek 'additions' to Esther are not in fact additions: the Greek version of Esther differs from the Hebrew text in many respects, not merely in the inclusion of certain passages. For example, in the Hebrew text of Esther, God is never mentioned, whereas one of the distinctive features of the Greek version is the prayers of the main characters, Mordecai and Esther. These differences have led to the CEI Bible (2008) presenting two Italian translations of Esther side by side: one translated from the Hebrew and the other from the Greek. In fact, the differences are so significant that one might speak of two distinct stories and, therefore, two different literary works. To help illustrate the difference, we can quote the opening of both books:

> In the days of Ahasuerus-that Ahasuerus who reigned from India to Ethiopia over one hundred and twenty-seven provinces-at that time, then, King Ahasuerus, who sat on the throne of his kingdom in the citadel of Susa, in the third year of his reign, held a banquet for all his princes and his officials (Hebrew Esther 1:1-3).

> In the second year of the reign of the great King Artaxerxes, on the first day of Nisan, Mordecai, son of Jair, son of Shimei, son of Kish, of the tribe of Benjamin, had a vision in a dream (Greek Esther 1:1a).

In the Vulgate, St Jerome complicated matters even further, because he translated the Hebrew text of Esther into Latin and then placed at the end of the book, after Est 10,! (Est 10,4-16,24), all the sections of the Greek Esther that are absent from the Hebrew text. Most modern Bibles insert these sections in the places they occupied in the Greek text, distributed throughout the book (as in the NVg, the BibJer, previous editions of the CEI Bible, etc.), distinguishing the verses with letters so as not to alter the usual numbering. Consequently, there is a mixture of the history of the Hebrew text with elements drawn from the history of the Greek text, resulting in some inconsistencies[^46]. For this reason, the decision taken by the latest edition of the CEI Bible appears to be more appropriate.

### 6.3. New Testament

In the case of the New Testament, the question of the original language can be answered quite simply: all 27 books have been preserved in Greek and were originally written in Greek.

The only possible exception is not really an exception at all. According to the testimony of Papias of Hierapolis (2nd century AD), quoted by Eusebius of Caesarea (HE!.39.16), the Gospel of Matthew was first written 'in the language of the Jews' and then translated into Greek:

> As for Matthew, the following is stated [by Papias]: 'Matthew, then, compiled a collection of the oracles in the Hebrew language, and each person interpreted them according to their own ability[^47]' 1

However, we have no fragments of Matthew's Gospel in either Hebrew or Aramaic. Furthermore, the Greek text of Matthew shows no signs of being a translation, although it does contain Semitic features. Consequently, it is now thought that a version of Matthew in a Semitic language never existed. Papia may have been referring to a collection of sayings of Jesus, which the evangelist used as a source in compiling his work.

It is surprising that all the books of the New Testament were written in Greek, given their origins, especially in the case of the Gospels. In fact, Jesus probably knew how to speak Greek, but his mother tongue was Aramaic, as can be seen from certain words preserved in the Gospels in this language: Talità kum (Mark 5:41), Egatà (Mark 10:14), Simon Bar-Iona (Matthew 16:17), Mammon, 'riches' (Mt 6:24; Lk 16:9, 11, 13), Abba (Mk 14:16; cf. Rom 8:15; Gal 4:6), 'Eloi, Eloi, lama sabachthani ' (Mk 15:14; cf. Mt 27:46). As for the apostles and the first disciples of Jesus, their mother tongue was Aramaic, with the exception of Paul of Tarsus, a Jew born in the Greek-speaking diaspora.

Yet today we have no documents containing the words of Jesus or the apostles in Aramaic or Hebrew. Any attempt to trace back to an original and 'pure' form of Christianity, free from Hellenistic influences and any 'foreign' influence, must face this truly insurmountable difficulty.

The choice by the authors of the New Testament to write in Greek reflects not only the widespread use of this language throughout the world at that time, but also the universal and missionary spirit of Jesus' first disciples, as well as their openness to Greek and Roman culture, whilst remaining faithful to their origins and identity.

> The New Testament, written in Greek, is characterised throughout by a dynamic process of inculturation, because it transposes Jesus' Palestinian message into the Jewish-Hellenistic culture, thereby demonstrating a clear desire to transcend the boundaries of a single cultural milieu[^48].

The language of the New Testament is the Greek known as Koine (from uoin2 &ιá ʎ suτο@, 'common language'), which differs somewhat from 'Classical' Greek-that of Sophocles and Plato, so to speak.

One of the general characteristics of New Testament Greek is that it contains many Semitisms, that is, words and expressions derived from Semitic languages: Hebrew and Aramaic. For example, Jesus concludes the parable of the wedding feast of the king's son by saying: 'Many are called, but few are chosen' (Mt 22:14). The phrase is better understood in the light of Semitic syntax, in which the contrast between 'many' and 'few' is a way of expressing a comparison between 'all' and 'not all'. Jesus means that not all who are called will be chosen, without specifying whether the difference in number is great or not. The message is that the initial call does not guarantee final salvation[^49].

Here are other expressions in the New Testament that are examples of Semitic features:

- linguistic turns of phrase such as 'he replied, saying' or 'and it came to pass that';

- the creation of new terms, such as á ντ íyiris@, 'Antichrist' (1 John 2:18, 22; 4:1; 2 John 1:y);

- the use of the preposition ἐν with an instrumental meaning, influenced by the Hebrew preposition

Hebrew preposition בְּ (be). For example, in Mt 3:11, John the Baptist declares: Ἐγὼ µἐν úµã@ pαuτí(m έν u&ατι, that is, 'I baptise you in water', in the sense of 'with water'. In Heb 1:1-2 it is stated that God spoke to the fathers 'through the prophets' and that now he has spoken 'in the Son', that is, 'through the prophets' and 'through the Son'.

- The use of words with a meaning different from the usual Greek one. For example, the term hodos (ó&ó@), which means 'way', is used to mean 'way of life' or 'doctrine' (cf. Acts 18:25; 19:23; 22:4; 24:14, 22; Jas 1:8; 2 Pet 2:2, 15, 21; Jude 11). The word doxa (δόξα), 'appearance' or 'opinion', is used to refer to 'glory'; whilst the word sarx (σάρξ), which means 'flesh', is used in the sense of the Hebrew term bashar (רשָׂבָּ), that is, 'man'. Thus, the expression 'all flesh' means 'the whole of humanity' (Mt 24:22; Mk 13:20; Lk 3:6; Jn 17:2; Acts 2:17; Rom 3:20; 1 Cor 1:29; 15:19; Gal 2:16; 1 Pet 1:24); a similar expression is 'flesh and blood' (Mt 16:17; 1 Cor 15:50; Gal 1:16; Eph 6:12).

Whilst all authors share the Koine Greek language, each has their own style. The most elegant Greek in the New Testament is undoubtedly found in Luke's prologue (Luke 1:1-4), whilst at the opposite extreme we might place the Greek of the Book of Revelation, which is somewhat peculiar and even contains frequent syntactic errors[^50].

# PART I THE BIBLE AND REGENERATION

(…) regenerated not from a perishable seed but an imperishable one, through the living and enduring word of God. For 'all flesh is like grass, and all its glory like a flower of the field. The grass withers, the flowers fall, but the word of the Lord endures for ever'. And this is the word of the Gospel that has been proclaimed to you. (1 Pet 1:23-25; the quotation is taken from Isa 40:6-8).

Today, no Catholic finds it difficult to affirm that the Bible is the Word of God, probably due to the influence of the liturgy of the Mass. Indeed, at the end of each reading, the words 'the Word of God' are proclaimed, or 'the Word of the Lord' after the Gospel. But this has not always been the case. In the Mass prior to the Second Vatican Council, this acclamation did not exist after the readings. Even more significant is the fact that, during the discussions to draft the constitution Dei Filius at the First Vatican Council (1870), a phrase stating that the sacred books 'truly and properly contain the written Word of God' (continent vere et propie Verbum Dei scriptum) was rejected. The issue centred on the adverbs, particularly 'proprie ', which seemed excessive and ambiguous. Indeed, Dei Filius states elsewhere that one must believe everything contained in the 'verbo Dei scripto vel tradito '. Thus, the First Vatican Council did not in itself reject the application of the expression 'Word of God' to the Bible, but at the same time did not wish to abuse it[^51].

In what sense does the Church believe that the Bible contains or expresses divine revelation or the Word of God? This question will guide our study in this first part.

As a first approximation, to reveal means 'to make known something secret or previously unknown; to unveil, to confide' (definition from the Garzan-

). To reveal therefore consists in communicating a message to someone who does not know it, by someone who does know it.

A prerequisite for the possibility of divine revelation to humankind is that God is capable of speaking. This idea may seem self-evident to us, but it was not so in the ancient world:

> God speaks to humankind. This conception, fundamental to Jewish-Christian theology, is by no means trivial, even if, unfortunately, linguistic convention risks obscuring its noetic significance. It constitutes, first and foremost, a direct contrast to the zoomorphic representations of paganism (of the Ancient Near East, in particular), which, to characterise the deity, adopted the figure of the animal-an unmistakable sign of strength and vitality, yet at the same time a terrifying and violent reality because it is devoid of speech. On the other hand, the idea of the speaking God, characteristic of the biblical perspective, also contrasts with the Greek conception of God as the Unmoved Mover or the Thinking Principle-a self-sufficient and self-referential reality-and is therefore entirely at odds with the biblical God, who defines himself essentially as a Being of relationship, as the God of the covenant[^52].

A second necessary prerequisite for God's revelation to humankind is that God addresses them in a language they are able to understand, that is, in human-and therefore not divine-language. To reach humankind, the word of God must take on human form. For this reason, it is legitimate to apply the general principles of human communication and language to divine revelation. We cannot explore either the philosophy of language or the theory of communication in depth here. We shall need to explain certain concepts as we proceed with the programme. For the time being, it suffices to recall the basic structure present in every

act of communication:

sender s message s receiver or addressee

In the case of divine revelation, the sender is God himself; the message, his words; whilst the recipient is the people of God and, through them, all humanity. However, whilst this description is true, it is too simplistic, because God does not speak to us directly, nor does he communicate solely through words. In what follows, we must refine this framework and, above all, enrich the concept of revelation by adding the historical and salvific dimensions.

## 7. The Word of God in History

The reference text for the following exposition will be the Dogmatic Constitution on Divine Revelation *Dei Verbum* of the Second Vatican Council, published on 18 No ember 1965 following a long and complex editorial process[^53].

### 7.1. Why study Dei Verbum?

In the first few decades following the Council, it seemed self-evident that the IGSS should take its cue from the guidelines of Vatican II. All textbooks published after 1965 begin with a summary of the statements of Dei Verbum. However, with more than half a century having passed and the postconciliar euphoria now a thing of the past, it is worth explaining why it remains appropriate to take Dei Verbum as a starting point for studying Sacred Scripture. In other words, we must ask ourselves why we should base our study on this text and not, for example, on what the Bible itself says or on the reflections of the Church Fathers on the subject.

We have not chosen Dei Verbum as our guiding text merely because it is a recent document there are later ones, such as the Post-Synodal Apostolic Exhortation *Verbum Domini* of 2010 - nor merely because it possesses magisterial authority - there are many others with similar authority but above all because a careful reading of *Dei Verbum* saves a great deal of time. Indeed, if we were to seek elements to explain the relationship between Scripture and revelation in the Bible itself, in the Fathers, in theology, and so on, it would prove a difficult and overly lengthy undertaking for these lectures. Dei Verbum, on the other hand, has the merit of having summarised some two thousand years of theological reflection in a presentation that is comprehensible, authoritative and - according to many authors - truly brilliant.

Indeed, unlike the situation at the time of the First Vatican Council, the Second Vatican Council did not need to concern itself unduly with apologetics, and was therefore able to present the contents of the faith 'in their own right', or, more precisely, according to the Church's self-understanding[^54]. In *Dei Verbum*, this means that the document seeks to explain divine revelation not as a response to rationalism, but by adopting, so to speak, the perspective of revelation itself. Furthermore, the Second Vatican Council drew on the great advances made by theology in the first half of the 20th century. For this reason, in*Dei Verbum *, revelation - and the Bible - is much more than which is a collection of truths, as we shall see. Revelation is presented as an event-: God comes to meet humankind to bring them into communion with himself.

### 7.2. Theme and structure of Dei Verbum

Dei Verbum speaks of the Bible, but this is not its main subject. In fact, this document deals with Scripture within a discussion of God's revelation to humankind and its transmission, as is clear both from the title - 'Dogmatic Constitution on Divine Revelation' - and from the beautiful words of the preamble (DV1):

> In reverent listening to the word of God and proclaiming it with firm confidence, the Holy Council makes its own these words of Saint John: 'We proclaim to you eternal life, which was with the Father and was revealed to us: we proclaim to you what we have seen and heard, so that you too may be in communion with us, and our communion may be with the Father and with his Son Jesus Christ' (1 Jn 1:2-3). Therefore, following in the footsteps of the Councils of Trent and Vatican I, it intends to set forth the authentic doctrine on divine Revelation and its transmission, so that, through the proclamation of salvation, the whole world may hear and believe, believe and hope, and hope and love (cf. St Augustine, *De catechizandis rudibus*, 4.8: PL 40, 116).

An initial insight into the content of Dei Verbum can be gained by considering its structure. Following the preamble just cited (no. 1), the constitution is divided into six chapters:

- On Revelation Itself (nos. 2-6);

- De divinae revelationis transmission (nos. 7-10);

- !) On the divine inspiration of Sacred Scripture and its interpretation (nos. 11-113);

- On the Old Testament (nos. 14-16);

- On the New Testament (nos. 17-20); and finally

- On Sacred Scripture in the Life of the Church (nos. 21-26).

The chapter headings show that more space is devoted to Sacred Scripture (chapters!, 4, 5 and 6) than to revelation (chapters 1 and 2). But it should also be noted that Scripture always remains within the realm of revelation.

Indeed, the expression 'Word of God', which gives the constitution its name, applies primarily to revelation, but can also be applied, as we shall see, to the Bible. Must we, therefore, equate Sacred Scripture with revelation? If we seek a definition of the Bible in *Dei Verbum*, we will not find one directly. However, we shall glean as a central idea that the Bible is to be understood within the framework of divine revelation and its transmission. Furthermore, one of the main merits of this document lies in the fact that it does not merely stating this principle, but in providing a conceptual framework for understanding it without oversimplification or reductionism.

### 7.3. Revelation and its economy (DV2)

Here is the text of DV2, divided into two paragraphs, a) and b), to facilitate commentary:

- It pleased God, in his goodness and wisdom, to reveal himself in person and to make known the mystery of his will (cf. Eph 1:9), through which, by means of Christ, the Word made flesh, human beings have access to the Father in the Holy Spirit and are made partakers of the divine nature (cf. Eph 2:18; 2 Pet 1:4). For through this Revelation, the invisible God (cf. Col 1:15; 1 Tim 1:17) in his great love speaks to humankind as to friends[^55] (cf. Ex 21:11; Jn 15:14-15) and converses with them (cf. Bar 1:18), to invite them and admit them into communion with himself.

> b) This economy of Revelation comprises events and words that are intimately connected, so that the works accomplished by God in the history of salvation manifest and reinforce the doctrine and the realities signified by the words, whilst the words proclaim the works and elucidate the mystery contained within them. The profound truth, moreover, that this Revelation reveals about God and the salvation of humankind, shines forth for us in Christ, who is both the mediator and the fullness of the whole of Revelation (cf. Mt 11:27; Jn 1:14, 1; 14:6; 1 Jn 1:1-1; 2 Cor 1:16; 4:6; Eph 1:1-14).

Firstly, a significant omission should be noted: DV2 explains what revelation is without making any reference to its recording in writing. There are biblical references, but Scripture as such will only appear in Chapter 2, which deals with the transmission of revelation.

In summary, DV2 presents:

- the essential elements of divine revelation (already described, in essence, through the beautiful quotation from 1 John in the preface). Its origin is God's goodness and wisdom; its content is none other than God himself and his plan of salvation in Christ; its recipients are humankind in general; its purpose consists in communion of life with God; and finally, its mode: God speaks to humankind as to friends 1.

- the divine plan of revelation. Revelation is not chaotic, fragmented or scattered, but follows a structured design or plan (oikonomia) which is realised:

- 'in the history of salvation', becoming one with it,

- through events and words that are 'intimately connected' with one another,

> ! and culminates in Christ, its 'mediator and fulfilment'.
