We shall discuss the role of Jesus Christ later, in connection with DV!-4. For now, we must comment on the structure of revelation in works and words and its ca historical character.

#### 7.3.1. Deeds and words

Revelation consists not only of words, but also of actions. To state that revelation is a plan comprising works and words may perhaps seem obvious. However, in some theological textbooks prior to Vatican II, a distinction was made between natural and supernatural revelation, stating that the former was realised through facts and the latter through words[^56].

When referring to works, the Latin text uses two terms: gesta and opera. These are not, therefore, mere facts (which would be referred to as facta), or 'things that happen', but actions, works carried out by someone - specifically by God, the subject of revelation. Here is an outline from DV2b:

- Works manifest the doctrine and the realities signified by words. elucident gesta verba

The revelatory value of works must be understood in the sense that 'a picture is worth a thousand words'. For example, the attributes of the God of the Bible cannot be fully grasped simply through verbal formulations. Psalm 116 invites us to praise the Lord 'for his mercy endures for ever', a phrase repeated no fewer than 26 times. But divine mercy is manifested more through an action, such as forgiveness, than through words. Indeed, the same psalm bases the call to praise on the works of salvation accomplished by the Lord. Words would remain empty without revelation through works. The death of Jesus speaks more eloquently of love for the neighbour than the parable of the Good Samaritan (though the parable helps us to understand the meaning of Christ's sacrifice).

· Works confirm the doctrine and the realities signified by the words.

In this context, the 'works' referred to relate above all - though not exclusively - to the miracles and extraordinary works of God, which are not mere manifestations of power, but always bear the character of a sign. Miracles are not limited to confirming a doctrine, but also share in the revelatory value of works in general and require a response from those to whom they are addressed. Jesus calls for faith in his person on the basis of the works he performs (cf. Jn 10:38; 12:16).

· Words proclaim the works and elucidate the mystery contained within them.

This sentence offers us an opportunity to discuss the communicative functions of language io. In a famous study, the German linguist and psychologist Karl Bühler (189-1963) sought to understand human language. Following Plato, he chose to present it first and foremost as a means of communication: language is an instrument (organum) for communicating something about reality to one another[^57].

According to Bühler, three functions of language as a means of communication can be distinguished: representational, expressive and appellative. All three are present in every act of linguistic communication, albeit to varying degrees. If I say 'smoking is prohibited' to someone who is lighting a cigarette, I am not only conveying information to them (representative function), but I am also asking them to change their behaviour (appellative function) and perhaps expressing my disapproval of tobacco (expressive function[^58]) 2.

The text of DV2 refers to two of the three functions just described. Indeed, the idea that words proclaim deeds seems to correspond to the appellative or interpellative function of the word of revelation. This function, evident in the commandments, in calls and in missions, is very much present in the biblical writings.

Furthermore, the idea that 'words illustrate the mystery' corresponds to the representational function of linguistic communication. No mention is made of the expressive function of language, but this is not a significant omission, since the Council Fathers did not intend to provide an exhaustive account, nor indeed to follow Bühler's framework.

To conclude, we may add an observation on the representational function of biblical language. In themselves, actions are ambiguous. Even the events of salvation history possess a certain ambiguity, although they are never entirely devoid of determination, provided one starts from the premise that it is God who brings them about. Even the most striking works, if not accompanied by words to explain them, can be interpreted in other ways. For example, some Israelites claimed that God had brought them out of the land of Egypt to let them die of hunger or thirst in the desert (cf. Ex 16:3; 17:1; Num 14:1-4).

We can take another example from the Gospel of the meaning inherent in these works. In Jesus' actions there is an implicit Christology, independent to a certain extent of his words. Nicodemus tells him:

> 'Rabbi, we know that you have come from God as a teacher; for no one can perform the signs that you perform unless God is with him' (John 3:2).

To say that Jesus comes from God is not enough to fully understand his identity, but it serves as a valid starting point. On the other hand, interpreting the casting out of demons as an act performed by virtue of the power of their prince is contradictory (cf. Mt 12:22ff.; Mk 3:20ff.; Lk 11:14ff.).

These works, therefore, have a certain significance, though one that is not entirely clear. Here the importance of language becomes apparent: normally, the meaning of events requires a verbal explanation. In a certain sense, Jesus' death speaks 'for itself'. Yet at the same time it requires interpretation, and so Jesus himself proclaims and explains it in various ways - for example, as 'giving his life as a ransom for many' (Mt 20:28; Mk 10:45) or through the parable of the murderous vineyard tenants (Mt 21:33-44; Mk 12:1-12) or that of the Good Samaritan (Lk 10:25-37)-.

#### 7.3.2. Revelation and history

As we have seen, DV2b speaks of a plan of revelation (oeconomia revelationis) and thus indicates that the words and events of revelation are neither isolated nor arbitrary. They do not follow one another by chance, but according to a plan or design.

It is then stated that God carries out his works 'in the history of salvation' (in historia salutis). We can highlight two points implicit in this expression.

1) In historia. Christian revelation is linked to specific places and times from which it cannot be separated. Polytheistic peoples regard nature as the place where the divine manifests itself, whilst certain religions of India, China and Persia seek it in wisdom. Christians, recognising that God is knowable through through creation, obey a revelation that is the fruit of divine intervention in history[^59].

Affirming the historical character of revelation implies, first and foremost, that it should not be understood as if it were merely a myth, even though it may contain mythical elements. That is to say, the word of God is not an abstract idea expressed or recounted as if it had actually happened, merely to make it more comprehensible. It is true that God's works and words reveal God's very nature and, in this sense, revelation possesses a certain universality, in that it makes known God, who is faithful to himself: revelation therefore contains a teaching, a doctrine. But the history of salvation cannot be reduced either to a system of theoretical propositions, nor to a repetitive cycle that would reflect God's unchanging way of acting. In some biblical books - Judges, and more explicitly Ecclesiastes - a cyclical view of time appears, but in Scripture taken as a whole a linear conception of history predominates, particularly evident in the way the New Testament writings refer to Jesus as the fulfilment of the divine promises of the Old Testament.

It should be added that the historical nature of revelation does not imply that all biblical accounts are equally historical. We shall discuss the relationship between the Bible and history further in the section devoted to interpretation (cf. pp. 211-219).

- In historia salutis. Secondly, revelation takes place within a history; that is to say, it consists of a series of events that have a beginning, a connection between them and, above all, an ending that gives them meaning. These are not merely facts that happen or follow one another without any structure. They are words and actions that possess a unity which depends on the continuity over time of God and the people of Israel - the speaker is 'the God of the fathers' - and, above all, on Christ, the culmination of the history of salvation.

By definition, every story can be told. Indeed, a story must be understood narratively; otherwise, it remains incomprehensible. And what is narration? In itself, every narrative implies a synthesis of disparate elements (it is impossible to recount everything), which reveals the transition from an initial state to a final one. The structure of the events recounted - the plot - is understood starting from the ending. In the case of divine revelation, which takes a historical form, Jesus is the 'end point' or in narrative terms - the 'pivotal point' of the story[^60].

As one might surmise, it is not enough to speak of narrative form in order to understand the historical nature of revelation. This is a narrative, but it also contains elements of true history; that is, it recounts events that actually took place and which are linked by real causal relationships[^61].

In short, what must remain absolutely clear is that history is not merely the framework for abstract and timeless truths. To put it another way: if we were to explain what Christianity is to someone who knows nothing about it, we could not presc from telling them, in some way, the story of salvation[^63][^62].

### 7.4. Revelation in DVs

The description of divine revelation in Chapter 1 of Dei Verbum unfolds more in successive waves than by following a linear path. In fact, the essence of divine revelation had already appeared in paragraph 1. Paragraph 2 develops the concept, explaining what it consists of and discussing its organisation or economy.

Paragraphs 2-4 set out the content of revelation once more, showing in greater detail the stages of the plan mentioned in paragraph 2. It should be remembered that we are speaking of de ipsa revelatione: no mention is yet made of its recording in writing. Here is DV!:

> God, who creates and sustains all things through the Word (cf. Jn 1:1), offers humankind, through created things, a lasting witness to himself (cf. Rom 1:19-20); moreover, wishing to open the way to a higher salvation, he revealed himself to our first parents from the very beginning. After their fall, through the promise of redemption, he restored them to the hope of salvation (cf. Gen 1:15), and he took constant care of the human race, so as to grant eternal life to all those who seek salvation through perseverance in doing good (cf. Rom 2:6-7). In due course, he called Abraham, to make of him a great nation (cf. Gen 12:2); after the patriarchs, he instructed this people through Moses and the prophets, so that they might recognise him as the one living and true God, a provident Father and a just judge, and might await the promised Saviour, thus preparing the way for the Gospel throughout the centuries.

The opening words refer to 'natural' or 'cosmic' revelation. A verb in the present tense is used God 'offers' - to indicate that this manifestation is not part of the history of salvation 1.

The other verbs in the paragraph, all in the past tense, refer to individual, historical events, the subject of which, in every case, is God. There are individual recipients of divine revelation (the first parents, Abraham, Moses, the prophets) and collective ones (the people). Among these individuals, some act as mediators: through them (per Moysen et Prophetas) God instructed the people.

Other elements present in the text are as follows:

- There are different stages in the history of salvation.

- Each stage may have its own substance, if it has an end in itself. Specifically, the formation of the people is presented with a specific purpose: 'so that they might recognise him as the one true and living God, a provident Father and a just judge'.

- (!) But at the same time, the purpose of this stage - and, it is understood, of the others too - is open to the future: 'and might await the promised Saviour'.

As is easy to see, this description contains, in essence, a theology of divine revelation prior to Christ, which rests on a balance between two points: its intrinsic value and its orientation towards Christ. Dei Verbum will return to this subject in n the value that the Church attributes to the Old Testament (Chapter IV, nos. 14-16).

### 7.5. Jesus Christ, Mediator and Fulness of all revelation (DV 2 and 4)

To better understand the importance of what follows, it is helpful to recall the debates on the 'essence' of Christianity that arose between the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The aim was to identify the distinctive idea of the Christian religion, that is, the element that sets it apart from all others. Some believed that universal brotherhood or love for one's neighbour constituted the essence of Christianity; for others, however, it was its ethical purity, or finally its sublime concept of God. The historian Adolf von Harnack (1851-1930) considered that the essence of Christianity could be summarised in three great values: the fatherhood of God, brotherhood amongst men, and the infinite value of the human person. Not infrequently, these attempts were intended to reinvent the Christian religion, in order to adapt it to the parameters of rationalism.

Among these intellectuals, Romano Guardini (1885-1968) provided the correct answer to the question of what constitutes the essence of Christianity:

> Ultimately, Christianity is not a theory of Truth, nor an interpretation of life. It is all these things, but its essential core does not consist in them. This core is constituted by Jesus of Nazareth, by his concrete existence, by his work, by his destiny - that is, by a historical personality[^64].

This statement is dear to Benedict XVI. In *Verbum Domini* he says:

> The eternal Word, which is expressed in creation and communicated in the history of salvation, became man in Christ, 'born of a woman' (Gal 4:4). Here, the Word is not expressed primarily through speech, concepts or rules. Here we are confronted with the very person of Jesus. His unique and singular story is the definitive Word that God speaks to humanity. From this we can understand why 'the beginning of being a Christian is not an ethical decision or a great idea, but an encounter with an event, with a Person, who gives life a new horizon and thereby its decisive direction' (Deus caritas est, 1[^65]) 2.

In a different context, the Catechism of the Catholic Church puts forward a similar argument:

> Christian faith, however, is not a 'religion of the Book'. Christianity is the religion of the 'Word' of God, that is, of a Word which is not 'a written and silent word, but the incarnate and living Word' (St Bernard of Clairvaux, Homilia super 'Missus est', 4, 11: Opera, ed. J Leclercq-H. Rochais, vol. 4 [Rome 1966], p. 57[^66])!.

The expression 'religion of the Book' derives from the Qur'an, which speaks of the 'people of the Scripture' as a description of Jews, Christians and Muslims. However, as the Catechism states, it is not correct to describe the Christian faith in this way[^67].

At the end of DV2, the relationship between Jesus Christ and revelation was expressed through two titles: 'mediator' and 'fullness'. It is not easy to draw precise boundaries between the concepts of 'mediation' and 'fullness' in the case of Christ, because the latter encompasses the former. DV4 - one of the most beautiful paragraphs of Dei Verbum - may be regarded as an explanation of this:

> Having spoken to us on many occasions and in many ways through the prophets, God 'has now, in these last days, spoken to us through his Son' (Heb 1:1-2). For he sent his Son, that is, the eternal Word, who enlightens all people, so that he might dwell amongst men and reveal to them the mysteries of God (cf. Jn 1:1-18). Jesus Christ, therefore, the Word made flesh, sent as 'a man to men' (Epistle to Diognetus, 5:4: FᴜNᴋ, Patres Apostolici, I, p. 403), 'speaks the words of God' (John 3:34) and brings to fulfilment the work of salvation entrusted to him by the Father (cf. John 5:36; 11:4). Therefore, he-in whom the Father is also seen (cf. Jn 14:9)-by the very fact of his presence and through the revelation he makes of himself through his words and deeds, through signs and miracles, and especially through his death and resurrection from the dead, and finally through the sending of the Spirit of Truth, fulfils and completes Revelation and confirms it with divine testimony, namely that God is with us to free us from the darkness of sin and death and to raise us up to eternal life.

The essential novelty of the revelation that takes place in Jesus Christ does not depend on his words, but on his person, in whom the divine and human natures are united. One cannot, therefore, separate his role as the fullness of revelation from that of mediator. Before Christ, there were mediators of revelation, but they were so only imperfectly or, to use the terminology of DV2, they were not mediators 'of the whole of revelation'. In other words, Christ's mediation surpasses previous mediations precisely because he is the fullness of revelation.

Clearly, this is not merely a quantitative difference, as if Jesus had revealed a greater number of truths to be believed than his predecessors. Christ's mediation is qualitatively different from all other mediations of revelation, because he is both God and man. In the dual nature of Jesus Christ lies the foundation by which he constitutes both the full revelation of God and its perfect mediation. Jesus is not a way, but the Way, for no one comes to the Father except through him (cf. Jn 14:6). No one has seen the God who speaks in revelation - 'No one has ever seen God' (Jn 1:18) - except when Jesus revealed him to us - 'the only Son, who is God and is in the bosom of the Father, he has revealed him' (ibid.) -. On the other hand, he is like men in every respect, except for sin (cf. Heb 2:17; 4:15), and can therefore speak of God in language we can understand: he is a man sent to mankind. One of the most eloquent biblical passages on this subject is the opening of the Letter to the Hebrews, to which DV4 alludes:

> God, who in times past spoke to our ancestors many times and in various ways through the prophets, has in these last days spoken to us through his Son, whom he appointed heir of all things and through whom he also made the world (Heb 1:1-2).

In explaining revelation, the Catechism of the Catholic Church follows Dei Verbum very closely. Under the heading 'Jesus Christ, Mediator and Fulness of Revelation', in no. 65 it quotes a passage from St John of the Cross, who comments on Heb 1:1-2 as follows:

> From the moment he gave us his Son, who is his one and definitive Word, he has told us everything at once in this one Word and has nothing more to say… For what he once spoke in part through the prophets, he has spoken in full through his Son, giving us this 'all' which is his Son. Therefore, anyone who still wishes to question the Lord and ask him for visions or revelations would not only be acting foolishly, but would also be offending God, because they are not fixing their gaze solely on Christ and are seeking other things and novelties [St John of the Cross, The Ascent of Mount Carmel, 2, 22, cf. Liturgy of the Hours, I, Office of Readings for Monday of the second week of Advent[^68]] 1.

In summary, drawing on DV4 and paragraph 65 of the Catechism, four significant points may be highlighted:

- Revelation through the prophets can be described as incomplete or partial ('en partes', as St John of the Cross puts it). Dei Verbum does not state this explicitly, but it is implied in the quotation from Hebrews 1:1-2 and in the assertion that Christ fulfils, completes and confirms revelation.

- Revelation points towards Christ. Therefore, as well as noting that it takes a historical form, as we have seen, we can also speak of its progressive nature.

- !) Revelation in Christ is complete in itself: God has nothing else to tell us, for his Son is his one and only Word. Revelation in Christ is also complete with regard to its recipients: humankind needs nothing else, nor can it aspire to anything greater than Christ.

- Revelation is not made up solely of words, but is found above all in a person: the Son, the one and only Word of God.

Finally, we must comment on the concluding paragraph of DV4, concerning the definitive nature of Christian revelation, which reads as follows:

> The Christian economy, therefore, inasmuch as it is the new and definitive Covenant, will never pass away, and no further public revelation is to be expected before the glorious manifestation of our Lord Jesus Christ (cf. 1 Tim 6:14 and Titus 2:13).

A common way of referring to the conclusion of the process of establishing the deposit of revelation is to say that revelation came to an end 'with the death of the last apostle' (usually identified as John, son of Zebedee, who died around the year 100). This formula is habitually used to mark the difference between the apostolic generation[^69] and the post-apostolic generation 1.

However, the Council did not wish to use these terms. One of the Council Fathers, Cardinal Ernesto Ruffini of Palermo, had suggested including the phrase. But the commission did not accept 'that it should be stated explicitly that revelation came to an end with the death of the apostles. Indeed, the proposal is already present where it is stated that Christ completes revelation; moreover, the formulation is not without its problems, for various reasons[^70]' 2.

It is not stated what these problems are, but we might suppose that one of them is that the expression is too precise, as if an exact date could be established on which revelation came to an end. Furthermore, we do not know who the last apostle was, nor when he died: for this reason, it is not particularly relevant whether or not one speaks in these terms. (We shall have to return to this point in connection with the formation of the canon. The closure of the biblical canon is, in fact, a consequence of the conviction that God has nothing further to say).

Finally, it may be noted that DV4 leaves room for the possibility of private revelations.

*

What consequences does the unique role of Jesus Christ within revelation have for understanding revelation as a whole? Here we can go a little beyond what Dei Verbum states. As we have seen with regard to DV2, revelation is not only historical, but is a story. We have said that in every story the decisive element is the turning point of the narrative, because it is from this that the other elements of the plot are explained. Jesus constitutes the pivotal point of revelation, the culmination of the story. The earlier stages, whilst truly being a revelation of God, must be understood in relation to him, as a preparation for the Gospel, as the final sentence of DV states[^71]! 1.

At this point, a problem inevitably arises, one as old as the Church itself: that of the Christian value of the Old Testament. If in Christ we have the complete message, why not dispense with the Old Testament? If the fullness of revelation is found in Jesus Christ, what is the point of retaining a partial revelation now?

The answer is not straightforward, but what we have just said about the narrative nature of Christian revelation helps us to understand that Jesus Christ would be incomprehensible without knowledge of the preceding stages. The very title 'Christ' cannot be understood unless one reads the Old Testament. In fact, not even 'Son of God' is comprehensible without taking into account God's manifestation to Israel.

On the other hand, if revelation were a body of timeless truths and history merely their wrapping, it would be logical to prefer the perfect formulation of those truths, eliminating the imperfect ones. The result of such a purification, however, would be Gnosticism, no longer Christianity.

One final observation: DV2 describes the economy of revelation as an interaction between works and words. Evidently, this interaction is realised in its fullness precisely when the fullness of revelation arrives: Jesus Christ is the Word of God, who becomes man; the Incarnation is a work.

*

We shall omit a commentary on nos. 5-6 of Dei Verbum. They essentially reiterate the teaching of the First Vatican Council in Dei Filius, with minor modifications. The most notable difference is structural: the Second Vatican Council discusses revealed truths at the end, rather th ly at the beginning, of the chapter devoted to revelation itself.

## 8. The Transmission of the Word of God

In Chapter I of Dei Verbum, divine revelation is described in full, without any mention of its recording in writing. However, it is clear that revelation, being historical, involves a process and therefore a transmission, which is the subject of Chapter 2. It is here that Sacred Scripture comes into view.

From the very title of Chapter 2, 'The Transmission of Revelation', we can deduce two important ideas:

- revelation as transmitted is distinct from 'revelation in itself' or 'revelation in history'. The difference is real, but at the same time Dei Verbum will endeavour to prevent this from being understood as a qualitative difference: the transmission of revelation, in fact, neither obscures nor diminishes the Word of God, because it is foreseen, accompanied and guaranteed by God himself;

- the title indicates that the whole of Chapter II speaks of 'tradition' in a broad sense, that is, transmission (from traditio, handing down). Consequently, the discussion does not start from the framework of two sources of revelation (Scripture and tradition), because everything is tradition, although distinctions are subsequently made within it, including the assignment of a special place to Scripture.

The presentation of Scripture and Tradition as 'the two sources of revelation' became customary in theology and catechesis after the Council of Trent and remained in use until Vatican II. In reality, it is not found in the decrees of Trent, but stems from their subsequent interpretation. One need only read the first Tridentine decree on Scripture carefully to realise that the 'two sources' model is a simplification[^72]. In Dei Verbum, this model was abandoned:

> Biblical studies and theological reflection have made everyone, including Protestants, aware that Scripture and tradition are not two realities that can be separated from one another: tradition precedes Scripture; Scripture itself is the fruit of the development of tradition[^73], indeed its privileged moment; tradition continues even after Scripture; Scripture - as the privileged moment of tradition - constitutes the primary criterion of validity for judging the subsequent development of tradition 1.

The starting point for discussing revelation becomes unified rather than dual, because Dei Verbum begins with Jesus Christ. Indeed, just as Christ was placed at the centre when describing revelation itself (Chapter I), so too will this be the case in Chapter II; but now the focus will not be on the life of Jesus, but on Jesus as preached, on the ' ' 'gospel[^74]' 2.

### 8.1. Orality and writing

Before reading Chapter II of Dei Verbum, however, it is worth reflecting on the differences between oral and written communication, even though this is not a topic frequently covered in IGSS textbooks. Why address this issue? Revelation is God's communication to humankind through deeds and words that are intimately interconnected, within a history that culminates in Jesus Christ. The Bible, on the other hand, is a text (a collection of texts), and therefore cannot, on its own, either act or speak. Nevertheless, Sacred Scripture is revelation. For this reason, we must ask ourselves whether, and to what extent, a spoken word and a written word can be identical.

Orality and writing are two different systems with a common purpose: communication, the 'transmission of a message' in the broadest sense, including here the three functions of language representational, expressive and appellative - which we discussed above (p. 59). How do they differ?

#### 8.1.1. Priority

Writing is a secondary form of communication compared to spoken language. Humans have been speaking since time immemorial. Writing, on the other hand, was invented at a specific point in time (around 1200 BC, as mentioned above on p. 10).

Furthermore, every human being first learns to speak and then to write. Writing is, in fact, secondary not only chronologically but also by its very nature, in that it is constructed using symbols (graphemes) that correspond to sounds (phonemes), at least in our alphabet. Other writing systems, such as the Chinese one, also seek to represent sounds graphically, albeit in a different way. It is unthinkable to reverse the order, that is, to imagine a spoken language arising from a written one.

More profoundly still, we can say - using Aristotelian terminology - that orality has ontological priority over writing: that is to say, oral language can exist without written language, but written language could not exist without the existence of oral language.

In divine revelation, this priority is respected: the original communication is oral. The God of Israel does not reveal himself to the people by writing, but above all by speaking through the prophets. Jesus preached and performed miracles, but he wrote nothing; nor did he send the apostles to write, but to preach. The books come at a se condary stage.

#### 8.1.2. Situation

Orality and writing entail different communicative situations[^75]. Orality presupposes the copresence of sender and recipient and therefore the simultaneity of the acts of transmission and reception. Writing, on the other hand, presupposes the absence of the sender and the duality of acts: one of writing and another of reading. Communication takes place only with the second act: a book that is never read communicates nothing.

For oral communication, the diagram presented on p. 54 applies. However, if one wishes to represent written communication, the diagram differs slightly:

Written communication via the internet has created intermediate cases, in which written communication is possible whilst the sender and recipient are both present. However, such cases do not invalidate the description given above.

#### 8.1.3. Communicative effectiveness (advantages and disadvantages of each system)

If one wishes to avoid any ambiguity, oral communication is undoubtedly superior to written communication. When two people are talking to one another, if one has not heard or has not fully understood what the other has said, they can always ask for a repetition or an explanation. Feedback is possible: misunderstandings can be resolved as they arise. Written communication, on the other hand, does not allow for the immediate resolution of misunderstandings, due to the sender's absence at the time of communication.

On the other hand, writing has a significant advantage over oral communication: it lends permanence to the message. Once put in writing, a message endures beyond the moment in which it was spoken and can be read several times and by several people. As the ancient Latin proverb says, 'verba volant, scripta manent '.

In this regard, we may quote a passage from the Phaedrus (2y4c-2y5b), in which Plato offers a critique of writing. Socrates, the main character of the dialogue, recounts how the god Theuth presented his various inventions to Thamus, king of Egypt, amongst which were the letters (of the alphabet), devised as a method to prevent men from forgetting:

> 'This knowledge, O King, will make the Egyptians wiser and better able to remember, for with it the remedy for memory and wisdom has been rediscovered' (2y4e).

But King Thamus is not convinced of the usefulness of this new art and replies with great wisdom:

> O most ingenious Theuth, there are those who are capable of creating the arts and those who are instead capable of judging what harm or what benefit will accrue to those who use them. Now, as the father of writing, out of affection you have said precisely the opposite of what it is worth. The discovery of writing, in fact, will have the effect of causing forgetfulness in the minds of those who learn it, because, relying on writing, they will become accustomed to remembering from the outside by means of external signs, and not from within and by their own efforts: therefore, you have found not the remedy for memory, but for recalling (Phaedrus 2y4e-2y5a, translation by G. Reale).

Thamus's words are undoubtedly reasonable and profound. We can agree with him (and, through him, with Plato). In today's culture, where a vast quantity of information is readily available, Plato's concern for true human knowledge - writing within the soul rather than outside it - remains highly relevant. At the same time, we must note that, in order to criticise writing, Plato had to make use of it to write the Phaedrus. Without writing, we would never have known what he thought of it!

#### 8.1.4. Context and Autonomy

Oral communication, which necessarily takes place face-to-face, is usually accompanied by other, non-verbal codes that serve to contextualise verbal language. For example: the musical code (singing), the paralinguistic code (vocalisation, tone of voice, laughter), the kinesic code (gestures and body movements) and others[^76].

Unlike oral communication, a written text has practically only one code for communication: the verbal one. One need only consider the difference between an oral and a written examination. In the former, the student can gesticulate, pay attention to the teacher's facial expressions, repeat a sentence with different intonations, and so on. All these resources are absent in a written exam, in which, at most, the student may underline a word or write it in capital letters.

As a result of this lack of resources, a written text must include elements that help to avoid ambiguity. To put it more precisely: in written communication, the context must be embedded within the text. In spoken language, it is enough for someone to say, for example, 'please close the door' for the listener to understand what is meant: who is being addressed and which door is being referred to. In a written text, however, one must explicitly state who is speaking, when, where, to whom, and so on. Essentially, a written text must have a coherent discursive structure in order to communicate effectively. This is one of the main reasons why a written text cannot and should not be conceived as a mere transcription of spoken discourse. Journalists who have to transcribe an interview recording are well aware of this need: it is not enough simply to reproduce the words spoken by the interviewee; an introduction is required that explains, at the very least, who the interviewee is.

Largely thanks to this need to make the context of the message explicit, the written text is, in a certain sense, autonomous. This autonomy has an important consequence: the written text can endure over time and be applied to different contexts, which may slightly alter the meaning of the message, usually enriching it.

#### 8.1.5. Social function

So far, we have described the main communicative aspects of orality and writing. We can now add a few brief observations on their social function within cultures.

Writing, just like oral traditions, transmits knowledge and experiences. Both are vehicles of memory, which in turn manifests and defines a community's identity. Generally, in the development of peoples, there comes a point when the group's traditions are put into writing. In this way, they move from the family sphere to the social and political spheres. Thus, they lose their vitality but gain stability and authority. The text acquires a canonical and normative value: one must adhere to what is written. The written text also allows for 'controlled knowledge'. Among ancient peoples, where few people could read and write, the recording of traditions in writing also meant that these came under the control of scribes, usually officials in the king's service and responsible for education at court[^77].

To summarise, we can list six differences between oral and written communication:

- writing is secondary to orality;

- orality presupposes the simultaneous presence of sender and recipient, whereas written communication requires two distinct acts, and communication takes place only in the second, the act of reception (reading);

!) oral communication allows for the immediate clarification of a misunderstanding;

- writing fixes the message and gives it a certain permanence;

- oral communication always takes place within a specific time and space and may be accompanied by many non-verbal cues, which provide the context for the message, whereas writing must incorporate the context into the text to avoid ambiguity.

- Writing fixes oral traditions, giving them a form that becomes normative if it is socially accepted.

We shall return to this description when we discuss the recording in writing of the r apostolic preaching, in connection with DV8-9.

#### 8.1.6. Why did Jesus not leave any written texts? (STh III q.42, a.4)

In STh III, q.42, a.4, St Thomas wonders whether Christ ought not to have written down his message, given that his teachings were meant to endure for ever and the Scripture was created precisely to preserve memory over time[^78]. Furthermore, according to Exodus 24, God had written the tablets of the law. If Christ had left us a written text, he would have ensured that no one could misunderstand or misrepresent his doctrine. Yet these arguments conflict with the fact that in the Bible we have no book written by Jesus: sed contra est quod nulli libri ab eo scripti ha-

in the canon of Scripture.

In his reply, Thomas puts forward three reasons why it was appropriate for Christ not to have written anything. We are interested in the first two:

- Christ is the most excellent teacher and therefore deserves the best method of teaching, which consists in imprinting the doctrine upon people's hearts[^79].

- 'Because of the excellence of Christ's doctrine, which cannot be contained by letters (…). If Christ had transmitted his doctrine in writing, people would have considered that this doctrine contains nothing more profound than what is written[^80]'!.

As regards the first of the two reasons just mentioned, it is clear that Thomas agrees with Plato (cf. above, pp. 72-72): teaching must remain within the soul, not outside it, and for this purpose the spoken word is more effective than the written word. Thomas's reasoning seems valid, but insufficient: Jesus could have taught orally first and then written it down.

The final reason is the most interesting. St Thomas seems to be saying that it is fitting for us humans to have the Gospels as testimonies to Christ's preaching-which are not identical to itbecause in this way Christ's life and doctrine transcend any textual representation. In this way, the temptation to reduce the teaching of Jesus Christ to a fixed and unique formula. The words of Jesus in each individual Gospel necessarily appear as a version of what he said, simply because of the distinction between orality and writing.

Thomas's argument highlights some of the differences between oral tradition and scripture. Scripture is necessary for the doctrine to endure over time, but first it had to be implanted in people's hearts. Furthermore, St Thomas leaves room to distinguish between the Bible and revelation: the Bible is divine revelation, but this cannot be reduced to the Bible; it is not exhausted in its written expression.

### 8.2. The Transmission of the Word of God according to Dei Verbum (DVy-zo)

Following this digression, we may resume our commentary on Dei Verbum. Chapter I set out what God's revelation consists of. Chapter II, on the other hand, is entitled 'The Transmission of Divine Revelation'.

Within Chapter II, it is possible to distinguish two parts, although there is no clear-cut division. On the one hand, DVy discusses the Gospel received by the Apostles[^81] and which they in turn passed on, whilst DV8-10 deals rather with the subsequent stage, namely the transmission of what the Apostles preached.

Some authors refer to the first stage as 'constitutive' or 'foundational' tradition because, although revelation is complete in Christ, during the apostles' ministry it is still, so to speak, taking shape. This form will become normative for the future: in the subsequent phase, which may be termed 'ecclesiastical' or 'dependent' tradition, revelation is transmitted always with reference to the apostolic faith, that is, in the manner in which it was preached by the apostles 1.

The constitutive tradition could also be described as 'apostolic', but it is better to avoid this, as this adjective is usually applied to the tradition of the Church in general, insofar as it derives from the apostles.

#### 8.2.1. The Constitutive Tradition (DVy)

DVy is a long and dense passage that requires careful reading. Here is the first sentence:

> God, in His infinite benevolence, decreed that what He had revealed for the salvation of all peoples should remain intact for ever and be handed down to all generations.

This statement does not refer to a historical event, but sets out the theological premise underpinning the historical account that follows. Indeed, the starting point for understanding the transmission of revelation lies in God's plan, which has as its horizon the universality of salvation (the salvific purpose of revelation described in DV2 reappears here). The text emphasises totality: God wills that what he has revealed to save all peoples - not just Israel - should reach all generations throughout time in its entirety - without suffering any diminution or corruption.

Although it may seem obvious, this theological principle is of paramount importance for understanding the long and difficult process through which revelation has come down to us. Without acknowledging the active involvement of divine providence, it would certainly be impossible to explain the fidelity with which the deposit of revelation has been handed down, without distortion or reduction. In other words, the transmission of revelation is not a purely human process, as if God's intervention in history had ended with the apostolic generation.

Let us continue reading DVy:

> Therefore, Christ the Lord, in whom the entire Revelation of the Most High God finds its fulfilment (cf. 2 Cor 1:20; 16-4:6), commanded the apostles that the Gospel-first promised through the prophets and fulfilled and proclaimed by him in person-should be preached by them to all as the source of every salutary truth and every moral rule (cf. Mt 28:19-20 and Mk 16:15; Council of Trent, Decree on Canonical Scriptures), thereby imparting to them the divine gifts.

From this sentence onwards, DVy 'descends', so to speak, from the speculative to the historical plane. First of all, it speaks of Jesus Christ. Before analysing the sentence, however, one must note a d and surprising omission. The paragraph says nothing about the transmission of revelation prior to Christ because it focuses on the essential. However, in discussing the sending of the apostles to preach, it states that the Gospel had been 'promised through the prophets'. In this way, Old Testament revelation is included in its Christological and salvific dimension, as a promise of Christ.

Perhaps one of the reasons for this silence is that very little is known about the process of transmitting revelation before Jesus. But above all, one must take into account the the theological difference that exists between the two stages of transmission. The tradition prior to Christ cannot truly be distinguished from the act of revelation itself (and therefore such a tradition is implicit in DV!). The disciples of the prophet Isaiah, for example, were able to develop and enrich their master's teaching. And in this case, we cannot draw a clear distinction between the transmission of an original revelation and the addition of new revelations, which likewise come from God. Indeed, the Book of Isaiah reflects precisely this development, and something similar occurs with many other writings of the Old Testament. It is important to bear in mind that, once written down, words of divine origin become fixed, but not immutable, because the texts undergo a continuous process of updating, enrichment or expansion. The legislation contained in the Torah is brought up to date with the addition of new precepts and is set within a context that recounts the history of the people's origins, as the foundation of the covenant. Nor does the word of the prophetic oracle remain unchanged after it is put into writing, but it may be supplemented with commentaries and is sometimes placed within the framework of the people's history through narrative accounts[^82].

With Christ, however, revelation is completed, and therefore everything that comes after him is merely transmission-a transmission that does not seek to add anything to the fullness achieved once and for all.

If revelation culminates in Christ, the content of the apostolic preaching can be nothing other than Christ, who died and rose again, as Saint Paul says:

> Whilst the Jews demand signs and the Greeks seek wisdom, we, on the other hand, proclaim Christ crucified: a stumbling block to the Jews and folly to the Gentiles; but to those who are called, both Jews and Greeks, Christ is the power of God and the wisdom of God (1 Cor 1:2224).

> For we do not preach ourselves, but Christ Jesus the Lord (2 Cor 4:5).

We may therefore ask ourselves why Dei Verbum - which on this point follows Trent - uses the word 'Gospel' to refer to what Christ commands the apostles to preach. Evidently, the 'Gospel', the good news, is none other than Christ himself (cf. Mk 8:15; 10:29). The content of the apostolic preaching is the Gospel of Christ (cf. Rom 1:9; Gal 1:6-11).

The Gospel is Christ. But the Jesus who is announced, proclaimed or preached cannot be completely identical to Jesus 'in the flesh', to Jesus as he was present until his ascension into heaven. The Gospel is an announcement, a proclamation, and therefore a di-

. Historical revelation can be transmitted to all people of all times, but not everyone can be its direct witness, because deeds and persons are unique and unrepeatable. Those who hear the preaching do not enjoy the direct experience - as the apostles did - of revelation in history, consisting of deeds and words, but receive it through human discourse - above all verbal - which gives it meaning[^83].

The text of DVy states, using a phrase taken from the Council of Trent, that the Gospel must be preached 'as the source of all salutary truth and of every moral rule'. This reflects the dual dimension of apostolic preaching: first, the truth of Christ, and second, its consequences for human behaviour.

Let us continue reading from DVy:

> This was faithfully carried out, both by the apostles-who, through their oral preaching, examples and teachings, passed on both what they had received from the mouth of Christ whilst living with him and observing his actions, and what they had learnt from the promptings of the Holy Spirit-and by those apostles and men in their circle who, inspired by the Holy Spirit, set down the message of salvation in writing (cf. Council Tridentine Council, op. cit.; First Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution *Dei Filius*, ch. 2).

Following DVy's description, the fulfilment of God's plan of salvation culminates in Christ and is proclaimed by the apostles (on the meaning of 'apostle', cf. pp. 268-280). More precisely, three distinct activities appear in the text, each with its own subject:

- the apostles transmit the Gospel, chiefly through oral preaching, but also through example and institution;

- the apostles and certain men from their circle (viri apostolici) write down the message of salvation; and finally

- !) the apostles appoint successors to the ministry.

As regards point 1), DVy states that the apostles transmit not only what they learnt directly from Jesus, but also what is taught to them by the Holy Spirit. The Spirit's revelatory function does not consist in adding truths to Christ's revelation (as if he had forgotten them!), but in ensuring that the apostles remembered and understood Jesus' words, as he had promised (cf. Jn 16:12-14[^84]) 1.

Furthermore, apostolic preaching includes 'examples and institutions'. This is intended to indicate that:

> The apostles' witness extends (…) beyond preaching in the strict sense: it encompasses the entire sphere of worship and the sacraments (especially Baptism and the Eucharist), and the entire sphere of moral conduct and the moral governance of Christian communities[^85].

This point is confirmed by!), which mentions the apostles' role in appointing bishops as successors to preserve the integrity and vitality of the Gospel.

As regards point 2), reference is made, alongside the apostles, to 'apostolic men', to include inspired authors such as Mark or Luke, who are not strictly speaking apostles. One Council Father expressed his fear that the mention of such 'apostolic men' might extend the period of public revelation too far. The commission replied as follows: 'This fear seems unfounded: indeed, everyone knows that these are Mark, Luke, etc[^86].'!.

We can now read the following statement by DVy:

> The apostles, moreover, so that the Gospel might always be preserved intact and alive in the Church, left the bishops as their successors, 'entrusting to them their own place as teachers' (St Irenaeus, Adv. Haer. III, 1, 1).

To summarise DVy's description, it is worth drawing up a brief diagram, whilst bearing in mind that, like all diagrams, it inevitably involves a certain degree of simplification.

Revelation is vertical (from God to humankind), whilst its transmission is horizontal (from person to person). Within this horizontal movement, a distinction must be made between the first stage (constitutive tradition) and the second (ecclesiastical tradition). The arrow should be translated as 'transmits to'; the content of the transmission is always the Gospel.

Paradoxically, the apostles in a certain sense give form to revelation, yet at the same time they simply seek to transmit it.

> If it is legitimate to say that in every message the form is part of the content, in the process of Christian revelation it can be said that the human recipient is also part of the message. It has sometimes been noted that faith is a revelation that has received a response; if this logic is applied to the form of revelation, we must conclude that the recipients of revelation are in some way part of it. This is particularly true in the case of the apostles: they are the ones who - as witnesses to the risen Christ - receive the full message of God's revelation and express it in human language[^87].

The apostles are not to be found 'before' the Church, but within it. Through them, continuity between Christ and the Church of all ages is guaranteed[^88].

Before reading DV8, we must also quote the final sentence of DVy, which broadens our perspective to the eschatological fulfilment:

> This sacred Tradition and the Holy Scriptures of both Testaments are therefore like a mirror in which the pilgrim Church on earth contemplates God, from whom she receives everything, until she comes to see him face to face, as he is (cf. 1 John 3:2).

#### 8.2.2. Ecclesiastical Tradition and the Place of Scripture (DV 8-9)

DV8 takes the historical account begun in DVy a step further. The starting point is no longer the Gospel proclaimed by Christ, but the apostolic preaching and its preservation within the Church. Although Dei Verbum does not make this distinction - which is not clear-cut - it can be said that in paragraph 8 the focus shifts to ecclesiastical tradition, that is, to the reception of the apostolic preaching by the post-apostolic Church, which preserves it as a deposit for all time.

The paragraph is lengthy and includes some topics that do not pertain to our course, such as the progress of tradition and the value of the Fathers' witness. I have highlighted in bold the elements that will be commented on:

> Therefore, the apostolic preaching, which is expressed in a special way in the inspired books, was to be preserved through an unbroken succession until the end of time. The apostles, therefore, in passing on what they themselves had received, exhort the faithful to adhere to the traditions they had learnt both orally and in writing (cf. 2 Thess 2:15), and to fight for that faith which had been handed down to them once and for all (cf. Jude 1:3). (Cf. Second Council of Nicaea; Fourth Council of Constantinople, Session X, Canon I). What was handed down by the apostles, moreover, encompasses everything that contributes to the holy conduct of the people of God and to the growth of the faith; thus the Church, in her doctrine, her life and her worship, perpetuates and transmits to all generations all that she is and all that she believes.

'In a special way': this is the first reference to a distinction between the Bible and other forms of transmission of revelation. What this special character consists of will be set out in DV9.

'What was handed down by the Apostles encompasses everything (…)'. Apostolic preaching is complete for the life of the Church. Here we are speaking of tradition in the passive sense or, in other words, of the deposit of revelation.

How might we define tradition in the broad sense? It is difficult to say anything more precise than 'the living transmission of revelation'. Tradition is identified with the very life of the Church, as DV8 suggests: 'the Church (…) perpetuates and transmits to all generations all that she is, all that she believes', and this in three fundamental areas: faith, morals and liturgy: 'in her doctrine, in her life and in her worship'. It is the task of fundamental theology to describe and distinguish the various contents of tradition, the ways in which it is transmitted, its documents and monuments, the criteria for discerning between apostolic tradition and ecclesial traditions, and so on[^89].

It is important for us to add to DV 8 that the category of the 'Word of God' can be applied to apostolic preaching. Although Dei Verbum never states this explicitly, St Paul affirms it when he reminds the Thessalonians of their reception of the Gospel preached by him:

> We give thanks to God without ceasing because, having received the word of God which we proclaimed to you, you accepted it not as the word of men but, as it truly is, as the word of God, which is at work in you who believe (1 Thess 2:13).

After speaking of the development of Tradition within the Church, DV 8 states:

> The teachings of the holy Fathers bear witness to the life-giving presence of this Tradition, whose riches are infused into the practice and life of the Church which believes and prays. It is this Tradition that makes known to the Church the entire canon of sacred books and, within the Church, fosters a deeper understanding of the sacred Scriptures and ensures that they remain constantly active. Thus God, who spoke in the past, never ceases to speak to the bride of his beloved Son, and the Holy Spirit, through whom the living voice of the Gospel resounds in the Church and, through the Church, in the world, introduces believers to the whole truth and causes the word of Christ to dwell within them in all its richness (cf. Col 3:16).

The priority of tradition is not a characteristic exclusive to constitutive tradition-in the obvious sense that preaching precedes the writing down of the text-but is a characteristic that also applies to ecclesiastical tradition, within which the inspired books are recognised. Thanks to tradition, not only is the canon known, but Scripture itself lives, acts, is at work, and does not lose its relevance. As the text puts it in a beautiful phrase: 'God, who spoke in the past, does not cease to speak to the bride of his beloved Son'. This is precisely the meaning of the expression 'Word of God', which will appear in no. 9.

Dei Verbum takes great care not to separate Scripture and Tradition. This concern lies at the heart of the next paragraph.

*

# DV9, though shorter than the previous issue, is in fact more relevant to us:

> Sacred Tradition and Sacred Scripture are therefore closely linked and interrelated. Since both spring from the same divine source, they form, in a certain sense, a single whole and are directed towards the same end. Indeed, Sacred Scripture is the Word of God insofar as it has been handed down in writing through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit; as for Sacred Tradition, it transmits in its entirety the Word of God - entrusted by Christ the Lord and the Holy Spirit to the Apostles - to their successors, so that, enlightened by the Spirit of truth, they may faithfully preserve, expound and spread it through their preaching; it follows, therefore, that the Church attains certainty regarding all revealed matters not from Scripture alone and that, consequently, both must be accepted and venerated with an equal sense of piety and reverence (cf. Council of Trent, Decree on Canonical Scriptures[^90]) 1.

It is stated - for the first time in the document - that Sacred Scripture is the Word of God. Of Sacred Tradition, on the other hand, it is stated that it transmits the Word of God, but not that it is the Word of God itself[^91].

To understand this distinction, it is worth noting that not everything written in the early days of the Church forms part of Sacred Scripture, although in some cases it contains the apostolic preaching without distortion. For example, the Didache - a work written around 90-100 AD - aims to present the teachings of the apostles. It is not a heretical text; that is to say, in the light of the Church's doctrine, it contains no deviations or errors. The same could be said of other writings that chronologically belong to the apostolic era (the so-called Apostolic Fathers), such as the the first letter of Pope Saint Clement of Rome or the seven letters of Saint Ignatius of Antioch. They are a precious testimony to the preaching of the apostles. We can say that they contain the apostolic preaching, which is the word of God, but not that they themselves are the word of God. Why?

As we have seen above (p. y!), the transition from the oral to the written form necessarily implies that the message takes on a new form. Therefore, absolute identity between the spoken and the written word is impossible. The New Testament is a written expression of the apostolic preaching, and is therefore different from the oral delivery. For example, if it had been possible to record St Peter's speech on the day of Pentecost and then someone had simply transcribed it, the result would be a text with a different message, not least because it would be ambiguous: who is speaking and to whom? When and where? Instead, the text of Acts 2:14-16, which perhaps does not reproduce word for word what Peter said, has been received by the Church as a text of apostolic authority which, by virtue of inspiration, is the word of God.

Indeed, DV9 suggests that, by virtue of the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, it can be said that Scripture not only contains the apostolic preaching, such as the Didache or other ancient writings, but that it is the word of God. Inspiration provides that extra element which makes a text the word of God for the Church, and not merely a written testimony to the word of the past. We shall discuss the nature of the charism of inspiration on pp. 242-261.

Sacred Scripture transmits the word of God by virtue of being the word of God itself, through inspiration. This is a strong statement from *Dei Verbum* defining the Bible. At the same time, however, it is set within a context that precludes a simplistic identification between the Bible and the Word of God, since it remains clear that there are two realities prior to Scripture that are the 'Word of God': the historical revelation fulfilled in Jesus Christ and the apostolic preaching, which are the source of tradition, from which Scripture springs. Indeed, Dei Verbum always understands Scripture within the transmission of revelation, that is, within Tradition in the broadest sense.

In summary, the expression 'Word of God' can be applied analogically to three different realities.

- Firstly, the Word of God is Jesus Christ and, with him, the entire historical manifestation of God to Israel, which reaches its culmination in the person and life of Christ.

- The apostolic preaching is also the Word of God, that is, the Gospel proclaimed by the apostles, the first proclamation of Christ made by the witnesses of the Resurrection.

- Thirdly, inspiration-by which God is the author of Scripture-allows us to affirm that the Bible not only transmits the Word of God, but is the Word of God itself.

These three expressions of the one Word of God, who is Christ, cannot be separated[^92].

At the beginning of this section (p. 53), we asked ourselves why the First Vatican Council did not wish to state that the sacred books contain the true and proper Verbum Dei scriptum. The answer depends in part on a terminological issue: the verb 'contain' could have implied that the books are the Word of God only in terms of content and not in terms of form[^93]. But behind these nuances lay a deeper problem. Indeed, outside the framework of revelation and its transmission, the phrase established too hastily a strong identity between revelation and the Bible.

Instead, the phrase omitted in the 1870 is taken up again in almost the same terms in *Dei Verbum*:

> Sacrae autem Scripturae verbum Dei continent et, quia inspiratae, vere verbum Dei sunt. (DV24) [The Sacred Scriptures contain the Word of God and, because they are inspired, are truly the Word of God].

DV1o, the final paragraph of Chapter II of Dei Verbum, describes the relationship between Scripture, Tradition and the Magisterium. We shall examine this later, in connection with the role of the ' ' Magisterium in the interpretation of the Bible (cf. pp. 221-226).

#### 8.2.3. The Lutheran principle of sola Scriptura

Having read Chapter 2 of Dei Verbum, it is practically essential to consider the Protestant principle of sola Scriptura, which requires a brief explanation of its origins.

Without claiming to provide a comprehensive account of the doctrine of Martin Luther (14811546), we may say that the starting point of his thought - which was far from systematic - can be formulated as an anthropological thesis inseparable from his life experience: nothing that man can do is worthy of merit before God. To attain salvation, therefore, man can do nothing other than recognise his own nothingness, his inability to act well, and open himself through faith to the grace of Christ, which is entirely free, that is, granted by God without any connection to the conduct of the recipient. According to the Lutheran interpretation of the Epistle to the Romans, justification by faith in Christ entails the denial of any salvific value of good works (sola gratia, sola fide).

On the basis of these principles, Luther rejects all human mediation as worthless in the sight of God. No man can claim the right to administer divine gifts or to interpret revelation, as if he were above God's grace or word, for Christ alone is the mediator. Luther vigorously rejects anything that stands between the believer and Jesus Christ, the only one who can save him (solus Christus or solo Christo).

However, we do not see Christ. His presence today is found in the Word of God, contained in the Bible. Every believer can therefore draw near to Jesus Christ through the individual reading of Scripture. Thus, the principle of 'sola gratia' is followed by that of 'sola Scriptura ' - shared by Luther, Calvin and Zwingli - which consists in the rejection of any authority other than that of the text itself. For the Protestant Reformers, Scripture is not the supreme authority, but the sole authority; the exclusive criterion of faith. There is no room for either tradition or the magisterium of the Church. In a famous statement, Luther declares that Scripture needs no interpreters, but must be 'its own interpreter':

> It is indeed necessary that Scripture, as judge, should pass judgement here, which cannot happen unless we give Scripture the primary place in all matters attributed to the Fathers; that is to say, that it should be, in itself, most certain, most straightforward, most open, an interpreter of itself, demonstrating everything from everything, judging and enlightening[^94].

One of the most important Lutheran theologians of the twentieth century and a leading figure in ecumenical dialogue, Oscar Cullmann (1902-1999), sought to justify the principle of sola Scriptura, without completely denying the role and authority of tradition, but limiting them to the apostolic era. His thesis is that tradition, insofar as it is oral transmission, did exist, but its normative function ceased once Scripture was formed and the canon defined. Cullmann sees a schism between the apostolic Church- which possessed magisterial authority and thus established the canon, and the post-apostolic Church, which is bound to submit to the received canon[^95].

Cullmann's argument raises an interesting question. If Scripture is the written record of the Word of God and, by virtue of inspiration, is also the Word of God, we might ask why the Bible alone is not sufficient today as the sole and supreme authority. In response, one might say:

- historically, the recording of the apostolic preaching was not intended to replace the other means of transmitting it. Luke writes his Gospel to reaffirm the teachings he received from Theophilus (cf. Lk 1:1-4), not to replace them. Paul exhorts the Thessalonians to follow what he has passed on to them either orally or in writing: 'Therefore, brothers and sisters, stand firm and hold fast to the traditions that you have learnt, whether by our spoken word or by our letter' (2 Thess 2:15[^96]) 2. And finally, in 2 Peter 1:20, it is stated that Scripture cannot be interpreted privately. The authors of the New Testament did not have the idea of sola Scriptura in mind.

- Furthermore, the principle of sola Scriptura presents a linguistic impossibility: there is no such thing as a text without context, that is, without extra-textual elements that guide its reading. The 'natural' context of Scripture is the living tradition of the Church: it was born within this tradition, is transmitted through it, and is its intended recipient. Certainly, it is possible to read the Bible outside of tradition, but in that case this tradition is replaced by another context: the Bible as a literary classic, the Bible as a document of the history of religions, and so on.

As we have seen, the Bible is inseparable from the living tradition that presents it to us as an authoritative text. And this is so not because Dei Verbum says so, but because, in fact, it is not possible to have a text as a norm outside a community which, by tradition,, regards it as normative.

## 9. Jesus and the Scriptures of Israel

At the beginning of this section, we noted that it was possible to understand Sacred Scripture within the framework of revelation on the basis of the guidance offered by the Bible itself, but that this path was long and complex. We therefore preferred to follow the more concise exposition contained in Dei Verbum.

Now, given the special place occupied by Scripture within the economy of the transmission of revelation - that is, that it transmits the word of God, being itself the word of God - one may explore the pages of the Bible in search of indications that might confirm, clarify or enrich the description given by Dei Verbum.

In the Old Testament, certain texts show that the words of divine revelation to Israel - whether the word of the Torah or the prophetic oracles - possess an authoritative character which, in principle, is not diminished when these words are set down in writing. Among the Jewish people before Christ, the value of the written Torah in particular was recognised as the word of God[^98].

For a more detailed analysis of how certain texts were regarded as the 'word of God' in Israel, see the Excursus! (pp. 281-!oo). For now, it suffices to note that the notion of inspired Scripture does not appear explicitly in the Old Testament. The inspiration of Moses and the prophets[^99]-that is, the presence of the Spirit within them, which is beyond doubt-is one thing; the recognition of the inspiration of the books is quite another.

> No text in the Old Testament explicitly reflects on the divine origin of a text or a book. (…) It is only by analogy that one can speak of evidence of inspiration for certain Old Testament texts where there is a conviction regarding the efficacy of the word insofar as it comes from God. This efficacy is reflected in the document written by God - such as the tablets of the covenant - or by God's command. But in this process, which extends from the prophetic word to the composition of the written text, neither the Spirit of God nor any particular action of God is mentioned that would enable the writer to write that, and only that, which God intends to communicate to humankind for their salvation[^97].

The Old Testament does not explicitly speak of the inspiration of the writers in their capacity as writers, nor of their texts. Nevertheless, Christians regard all Scripture as the word of God, not only because it contains it, but because it is inspired and has God as its author. How did the Church arrive at such a bold assertion? To answer this, we must examine the teaching of Jesus and the apostles concerning the Scriptures of Israel.

As we have seen in connection with nos. 2 and 4 of Dei Verbum, Jesus Christ is at the heart of the Christian faith. He, the Word of God made man, reveals the Father, not through a written text, but in his person and through his life, his works and his words, above all his death and resurrection (cf. Heb 1; Gal 4:4; Jn 1; etc.). Precisely because of this central position of Christ, it is natural to ask what Jesus said about the Scriptures of Israel and what role they played in apostolic pre tion, in order to understand the Church's faith regarding the Bible.

### 9.1. The teaching of Jesus

The Law and the Prophets up to John: from then on, the kingdom of God is proclaimed, and everyone strives to enter it (Lk 16:16)

Jesus and the authors of the New Testament often spoke of the Scriptures, referring to the sacred books of Israel. In the introduction, we noted that the use of the term in the singular - 'the Scripture' - to refer to the collection of the ancient writings of Israel is a distinctive feature of certain New Testament texts (cf. p. 22). We shall now see that this terminological innovation stems from a deeper innovation: a new conception of the books, arising from the fullness of revelation accomplished in Christ. This new conception flows from the teachings of Jesus himself, which will lead, amongst other things, to the recognition of the entire corpus of Israel's Scriptures as the Word of God 1.

Jesus, as a devout Jew, would go every Sabbath to the synagogue in Nazareth before beginning his public ministry, where he would recite the Psalms and listen to the Torah and the Prophets 2. Yet, when he began to preach, he demonstrated a profoundly original approach to interpreting the Scriptures (cf. Lk 4:16-21). What particularly surprises his audience is the unusual authority with which Jesus presents himself in relation to the Law, placing himself above not only the rabbis of the time, but even Moses himself (among many passages, cf. Mk 1:22; 10:2-8; Mt 5:21-48).

At the beginning of the Sermon on the Mount (Mt 5-7), Jesus makes a programmatic proclamation in which he sets out his attitude towards the Torah and the Prophets:

> Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them, but to fulfil them [οúu i ʎ θον uατα ʎ ũσαι á ʎʎ à πληρῶσαι]. Truly I tell you: until heaven and earth pass away, not a single iota or a single stroke of a pen of the Law will pass away, until all has been fulfilled (Mt 5:17-18).

Jesus acknowledges and defends the enduring nature of the revelation received by Israel. At the same time, he implies that it is imperfect, since he has come to fulfil it, in the sense of bringing it to its fullness (πληρῶσαι, literally 'to fill'). The rest of the discourse clarifies what kind of fulfilment Jesus is speaking of. He, in fact, offers a 'full' interpretation of the Torah and the Prophets, not only in terms of content, but above all because, with unprecedented boldness, he places himself above it:

> 'You have heard that it was said to the people of old: 'You shall not murder; whoever murders must be brought before the court.' But I say to you: whoever is angry with his brother must be brought before the court. And whoever says to his brother, 'You fool', shall be brought before the Sanhedrin; and whoever says, 'You madman', shall be condemned to the fire of Gehenna (Mt 5:21-22).

The fullness of the Torah that Jesus came to bring does not consist in the demand for a rigid and detailed observance of all the Mosaic laws and precepts, but rather in a deeper understanding of the teaching of the Torah and the Prophets. Particularly in the Gospels of Matthew and Mark, we find several episodes in which Jesus interprets the texts authoritatively and often in a new way, independent of tradition, especially in his disputes with the scribes and Pharisees.

> From the very beginning of his public ministry, Jesus had adopted an original personal stance, distinct from the interpretation prevalent in his day, which was that 'of the scribes and Pharisees' (Mt 5:20). There are numerous examples of this: the contrasts in the Sermon on the Mount (Mt 5:21-48), Jesus' sovereign freedom in observing the Sabbath (Mk 2:27-28 and parallels), his way of relativising the precepts of ritual purity (Mk 5:1-2 and parallels), his radical demands, by contrast, in other areas (Mt 10:2-12; 10:1-2 and parallels) and, above all, his welcoming attitude towards 'tax collectors and sinners' (Mark 2:15-17 and parallels). This was not, on his part, a whim born of a rebellious spirit, but, on the contrary, a deeper fidelity to God's will as expressed in the Scriptures (cf. Matthew 5:17; 9:13; Mark 7:8-13 and parallels; 10:5-9 and parallels[^100]) 1.

Jesus thus presents himself as a rabbi who brings the Torah and the Prophets to their fulfilment, displaying an authority that arouses surprise and offering a profound and original interpretation.

But he goes much further still. On several occasions, from the very beginning of his public ministry (cf. Lk 4:21), Jesus proclaims that the prophecies of the Scriptures will be fulfilled in his life - especially in his passion, death and resurrection. He maintains that the prophetic oracles preserved in writing, which were yet to be fulfilled, will find their fulfilment in him. For example, in the third announcement of his death and resurrection, Jesus makes an explicit reference to the fulfilment of the Scriptures:

> 'Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and everything that was written by the prophets concerning the Son of Man will be fulfilled: for he will be handed over to the Gentiles, he will be mocked and insulted, they will spit on him, and after scourging him, they will kill him, and on the third day he will rise again' (Luke 18:11-12; cf. Matthew 20:18-19; Mark 10:22-24).

As the hour of his death draws near, Jesus becomes more insistent. According to Luke, Jesus' final words before leaving the Upper Room refer precisely to the fulfilment of the Scriptures in his Passion, identifying himself with the mysterious figure of the Servant of the Lord described by Isaiah:

> 'For I tell you, this passage of Scripture must be fulfilled in me: 'He was counted among the wicked.' Indeed, everything concerning me is coming to fulfilment' (Lk 22:15; cf. Is 51:12[^101]) 1.

On arriving at the garden, in the presence of those who had come to arrest him, Jesus explains that everything that is about to happen is part of God's plan as revealed in the Scriptures:

> At that very moment Jesus said to the crowd: 'As though I were a thief, you have come to arrest me with swords and clubs. Every day I sat in the temple teaching, and you did not arrest me. But all this has happened so that the Scriptures of the prophets might be fulfilled.' Then all the disciples deserted him and fled (Mt 26:55-56; cf. Mk 14:48-49).

The quotations could be multiplied, but those given here suffice. It is worth asking which Scriptures Jesus is referring to. From what has been handed down to us, there is no indication that he ever listed the books he regarded as sacred. In the Upper Room he quotes Isaiah; along the On the way to Gethsemane, he quotes Zechariah, and in the garden he speaks generally of the 'Scriptures of the prophets'. Is he perhaps thinking only of the prophetic books when he speaks of his passion and death?

No. When Jesus speaks of the fulfilment of the Scriptures in his life, he does not limit himself to the prophetic books, not even in the broad sense of the term in the BH (the former and latter prophets[^102]) 1. The Psalms, too, form part of 'the Scriptures' that are to be fulfilled: indeed, in John 13:18, Jesus speaks of the fulfilment of Scripture and quotes Psalm 40:10[^103].

In John 5:39, Jesus urges the Jews to search the Scriptures, for 'it is these very Scriptures that bear witness to me'. For Jesus, therefore, the Scriptures in general - without further specification - offer a prophecy of his mission.

After his resurrection, Jesus emphasises the fulfilment of the Scriptures in him as a prerequisite for understanding all that has taken place. The disciples on the road to Emmaus are not only rebuked for their lack of faith, but also receive a lesson in exegesis covering the entire Bible:

> He said to them, 'O foolish ones, and slow of heart to believe all that the prophets have spoken! Was it not necessary that the Christ should suffer these things and enter into his glory?' And, beginning with Moses and all the Prophets, he explained to them what was said in all the Scriptures concerning himself (Luke 24:25-27).

Moses is placed on a par with the prophets, and thus the Torah is regarded as prophecy: we shall return to this point later (pp. 97-103).
