On Easter Sunday itself, this time in the presence of the apostles, the risen Jesus repeats the teaching he had given them before his passion, but now he gives them the ability to understand it:

> Then he said: 'These are the words I spoke to you whilst I was still with you: everything written about me in the Law of Moses, the Prophets and the Psalms must be fulfilled.' Then he opened their minds to understand the Scriptures and said to them: 'Thus it is written: the Christ will suffer and rise from the dead on the third day, and in his name

> 'to preach repentance and the forgiveness of sins to all nations, beginning from Jerusalem' (Luke 24:44-47).

This passage shows how 'all the Scriptures' (Lk 24:27) are equivalent to 'the Law of Moses, the Prophets and the Psalms' (Lk 24:44). Jesus sets no limits; he does not say that only part of the Scriptures of Israel is fulfilled in him, but all of them, without distinction. Subsequently, the Church had to determine the precise limits of 'all the Scriptures' by defining the canon, as we shall see in Part III[^104].

In summary, based on the words of Jesus as recorded in the four Gospels, it can be said that:

- Jesus regards the Scriptures as a unified whole, above all as prophecy, and he probably does so with the entire books in mind, not just the individual oracles.

- These Scriptures prophesied his deeds, his words and, in particular, his passion, death and resurrection, which in turn are the fulfilment of the Scriptures. Jesus fulfils the Law, for he brings it to its fullness. Above all, he fulfils the prophecies, which referred to the salvation he brought. And finally, we may also say that he fulfils the great biblical narrative, for with him the history of salvation reaches its turning point (cf. above, pp. 60-62).

- It is the task of humankind to understand the Scriptures, to 'search them' (cf. Jn 5:39) in order to discover the meaning of God's message as a testimony to Christ. The Scriptures are understood by looking to Jesus; Jesus is to be understood by reading the Scriptures.

One of the consequences of Jesus' teaching is that we begin to regard the Scriptures of Israel as a self-contained collection of books, for the figure of Jesus marks a break with earlier history and the beginning of a new era. Indeed, he himself states that the Torah and the Prophets extend only as far as John the Baptist, because the kingdom of God is revealed through the works of Jesus (cf. Mt 11:13; Lk 16:16). Or, as St Paul says, with him comes the fullness of time (cf. Gal 4:4). From the time of Jesus Christ onwards, it is possible to view the ancient Scriptures as a unified collection, for he presents himself as the one foretold in those writings and as their fulfilment. The era of promises thus comes to an end.

Jesus did not speak of God's role in the composition of Scripture (inspiration), nor did he explicitly maintain that Scripture is the word of God. But he laid the foundations upon which the Church would come to affirm this, as we shall see.

### 9.2. From Jesus to the Apostolic Preaching and the New Testament

Philip found Nathanael and said to him: 'We have found the one

about whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the Prophets: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth' (John 1:45).

As described in the Gospels, Jesus' identity presupposes knowledge of and acceptance of the Scriptures of Israel. He is the Son of God, not of just any deity, but of the God who revealed himself to Israel; he is the Christ, that is, the Messiah, the King of Israel. In him the promises made to the patriarchs and the salvation foretold by the prophets find their fulfilment.

Consequently, in order to proclaim the good news of the risen Jesus - to preach the Gospel - it becomes essential to refer to the Scriptures. For example, when Saint Paul writes to the Christians in Rome, he presents himself as an apostle 'chosen to proclaim the Gospel of God, which he had promised through his prophets in the Holy Scriptures' (Rom 1:1-2); and the reference to the Scriptures in defining the Gospel is also present at the end of the same letter: cf. Rom 16:25-26. When Paul himself wishes to summarise in a few sentences the Gospel he has received and passed on, he twice uses the phrase κατὰ τὰς γραφὰς, secundum scripturas, which later found its way into the Creed:

> For I passed on to you, first of all, what I myself received: that Christ died for our sins in accordance with the Scriptures, that he was buried, and that he was raised on the third day in accordance with the Scriptures (1 Cor 15:1-4).

Including the fulfilment of the ancient promises in Christ is not merely one possibility amongst many, but is an integral part of the apostolic preaching. The speeches of Peter, Stephen and Paul recorded in the Book of Acts also contain, in various ways, at least one allusion to the fulfilment of the Scriptures in Jesus. Particularly illuminating in this regard is Paul's speech in the synagogue at Antioch in Pisidia (cf. Acts 11:16-41).

We have seen above how, on several occasions, Jesus quotes certain passages, pointing out that they are being fulfilled in specific circumstances of his life (cf. pp. 90-94). In John 5:19, he invites us to search the Scriptures, for they bear witness to him. It is therefore no surprise that the apostles, the evangelists and the early Christians heeded this invitation and set about sifting through the ancient Scriptures, seeking - each in their own way - to understand how God had prepared for and foretold the coming of his Son. Indeed, all the books of the New Testament contain this characteristic of the Christian message, albeit expressed in different ways. This is not the place to examine all the New Testament writings from this perspective, but we can briefly review each of the four Gospels.

- Matthew is the one who most frequently uses the 'fulfilment formula', which, in its full version, reads as follows: 'All this took place to fulfil what the Lord had spoken through the prophet' (Mt 1:22). This is followed by an explicit quotation from a passage, which is applied to a specific episode in the life of Jesus. The formula reappears, often in abbreviated form, in Mt 2:15, 17, 23; 4:14; 8:17; 12:17; 13,35; 21:4; 26:56; 27:9.

- Mark speaks explicitly of the fulfilment of the Scriptures in Jesus only once, but at a very significant moment: the beginning of the Passion (cf. Mk 14:49). And the Gospel begins with a quotation from the Scriptures (cf. Mk 1:2-3).

- !) As regards Luke, we have already mentioned the most significant passages, which are Jesus' preaching in the synagogue at Nazareth (Lk 4:16-21), the third announcement of the Passion (Lk 18:31-33) and the teaching of the risen Jesus (Lk 24).

- In the Gospel of John we find the idea of the fulfilment of the Scriptures expressed in a similar way to Matthew. But John adds that the disciples, whilst Jesus was among them, often failed to understand the Master's actions and words. However, after the Resurrection, they recognise who Jesus was and understand him in the light of the Scriptures. For example, when Jesus speaks of destroying the temple, referring to his own body, the evangelist comments:

> 'When he had been raised from the dead, his disciples remembered that he had said this, and they believed both the Scripture and the word that Jesus had spoken' (Jn 2:22).

We find a similar observation later on, in the account of Jesus' messianic entry into Jerusalem: cf. Jn 12:14-16 and, for the quotation, Zech 9:9.

*

At times, the effort made by the authors of the New Testament to find prophecies everywhere that were fulfilled in Christ can produce unconvincing results, which strain the texts, at least according to modern criteria. For example, in Matthew 21:9-10 it is said that Judas's betrayal fulfils Scripture, but the quotation is a combination of two different texts that seems arbitrary:

> Then was fulfilled what had been spoken through the prophet Jeremiah: 'And they took the thirty pieces of silver, the price of the one on whom the sons of Israel had set a price, and they gave them for the potter's field, as the Lord had commanded me.'

The first quotation does not come from Jeremiah, but from Zechariah 11:12-13 (for the second, cf. Jeremiah 12:6-15). When faced with cases such as this - which are also frequent in patristic exegesis - various explanations may be sought. For example, one might argue that it is a matter of a common exegetical practice of the time, far removed from our modern way of thinking, which is true. But the important thing is to understand that the evangelists do not usually cite these texts as evidence or proof of Jesus' messianic status, but follow the reverse approach: the starting point is faith in him as the Messiah and Son of God, who fulfils the Scriptures, and only afterwards do they seek out the texts that confirm or explain this faith, which will be more or less adapted as the case may be.

Conversely, if one adopts an apologetic perspective, which seeks to defend the credibility of the Christian faith, one must first demonstrate the original meaning of a prophecy and then point to its fulfilment in Christ. This approach is legitimate, useful and also very difficult[^105]. But this is not how the early Christians reasoned. The apostolic preaching was intended to proclaim the good news of the coming of the Messiah, more than to demonstrate rationally that Jesus must be the Messiah.

### 9.3. The mutatio scripturarum: the Scriptures as the word of God

> The books of the Old Testament, fully incorporated into the preaching of the Gospel, acquire and reveal their full meaning in the New Testament (cf. Mt 5:17; Lk 24:27), which they in turn illuminate and explain.

Dei Verbum, n. 16.

As we have seen, early Christian literature speaks of the 'Scriptures' or the Torah and the Prophets, referring to the books handed down by the tradition of Israel without any special distinctions or specifications. However, in an almost imperceptible process, the Scriptures acquired a new status amongst Christians-one that was more unified and more sublime than the veneration that the various Jewish groups held for their own sacred writings[^106].

To describe the nature of this change, some have used an apt expression: with Christ, a transformation or mutation of the Scriptures (mutatio scripturarum) took place[^107]. For example, Origen expresses it thus in his commentary on the Gospel of John:

> The Word of God, who was with God in the beginning, is not, in its fullness, a multiplicity of words; it is not many words, but a single Word that encompasses a a great number of ideas, each of which is a part of the Word in its entirety (…). And if Christ refers us to the 'Scriptures' as those that bear witness to him, he regards the books of Scripture as a single scroll, because everything that has been written about him is summarised in a single whole[^108].

In stating that all the Scriptures speak of Jesus and are fulfilled in him, the authors of the New Testament confer upon them, in addition to the unity emphasised by Origen, a deeper value than that which they enjoyed before Christ. 'The writings of the New Testament recognise that the Scriptures of the Jewish people have a permanent value as divine revelation', says the PCB[^109]. But this is a value we might add - which was not previously attributed to the Scriptures in general, but only to the Torah and, in part, to the prophetic books (cf. Excursus!, pp. 281-!oo).

Indeed, if in the New Testament the entire Old Testament is regarded as God's revelation, this is because Jesus had previously declared that all the Scriptures speak of him. In addition to the passages already cited, we may recall Jesus' words in John 5:

> 'Do not think that I am the one who will accuse you before the Father; there is already one who accuses you: Moses, in whom you place your hope. For if you believed Moses, you would believe me too; for he wrote about me. But if you do not believe his writings, how can you believe my words?' (John 5:45-47).

To believe in Jesus, one must first believe in Moses, that is, believe in what he wrote: the Torah[^110]. But to truly believe in Moses means recognising that he wrote about Jesus. Jesus invokes the authority of Moses, but Moses' authority ultimately rests upon that of Jesus, thanks to whom we know that Moses wrote prophetically, and thus it takes on a new significance: Moses not only transmitted God's Torah, but also foretold the days of the Messiah. Thus, Moses appears not only as the lawgiver of Israel, but above all as a prophet of the Messiah. Certainly, Moses was already regarded as a prophet (cf. Deut 18:15-18; 14:10), but Jesus emphasises this aspect above that of lawgiver, which made him a unique figure. Consequently, as a prophet of Christ, Moses is no longer at the centre of revelation, but becomes part of a line that continues through the other prophets right up to John the Baptist.

Indeed, the mutatio scripturarum is particularly clear in the case of the Pentateuch, which Christians are beginning to regard more as prophecy than as law. Jesus says: 'For all the Prophets and the Law prophesied until John' (Mt 11:13; cf. Lk 16:16). The circular structure of the Tanakh no longer applies; instead, a linear structure now prevails (cf. p. 41). St Paul, too, reaches a similar conclusion regarding the prophetic value of the Torah:

> In his letters to the Galatians and to the Romans, the apostle argues on the basis of the Law to demonstrate that faith in Christ has brought the regime of the Law to an end. He shows that the Law, as revelation, foretold its own end as an institution necessary for salvation (Gal 3:6-14, 2425; 4:4-7; Rom 3:9-26; 6:14; 10:5-6). The most significant statement in this regard is found in Romans 3:21, where the apostle affirms that the manifestation of God's righteousness in the justification offered by faith in Christ took place 'apart from the Law', yet is nevertheless 'in accordance with the testimony of the Law and the Prophets[^111]' 1.

The most daring way in which the new value of the Jewish Scriptures has been expressed is the application to them of the concept of the 'word of God', already developed in the Old Testament, but not in reference to the texts themselves. For the ancient Jews, the books as such were not regarded as the word of the Lord, although some contained it, especially the Torah (cf. pp. 281-100). What gave rise to their being regarded as a word inspired by God and, ultimately, as the word of God himself, is the conviction that the Scriptures as a whole are prophecy, because they proclaim the mystery of Christ.

In this process of the sacralisation of the text, a fundamental role is played by its assimilation to the authority of the words of Jesus. The words spoken by Jesus are divine; his preaching is the word of God (cf. Lk 5:1), because he is the Word made flesh.

Secondly, the Gospel-the apostolic preaching-is also regarded as the 'word of God' (cf. Acts 4:13; 6:2, y; 1 Thess 2:13; 1 Pet 1:23-25) or the 'word of the Lord' (cf. Acts 8:25; 1:44; 15:15-16; 2 Thess 3:1), and this for two reasons: firstly, because its content is none other than Christ, but also because the authority of Christ is truly present in his messengers[^112].

To illustrate this process, we may read a beautiful passage from 1 Peter, quoted in part at the beginning of this first part:

> You know that it was not with perishable things such as silver or gold that you were ransomed from your futile ways inherited from your forefathers, but with the precious blood of Christ, a lamb without blemish or defect. He was chosen before the foundation of the world, but was revealed in these last times for your sake; and through him you believe in God, who raised him from the dead and gave him glory, so that your faith and hope may be in God. Having purified your souls by obedience to the truth so that you may love one another sincerely as brothers and sisters, love one another deeply, from the heart, having been reborn-not from perishable seed but from imperishable seed-through the living and enduring word of God. For 'all flesh is like grass, and all its glory like a flower of the field. The grass withers, the flowers fall, but the word of the Lord endures for ever' (Isa 40:6-8). And this is the word of the Gospel that has been proclaimed to you (1 Pet 1:18-25).

The word of God spoken of in Isaiah is not human speech, but the revelation of his glory (cf. Isa 40:5), that is, the return of the Lord to the midst of Jerusalem after the punishment of exile, to bring relief to his people. In this context, the passage from Isaiah 40:6-8 emphasises the stability of God's word, in contrast to the fickleness and fragility of human words: God will be faithful to his promises.

In 1 Peter, the Isaianic text on the word of God is applied to Christ present in the apostolic preaching, who frees people from their sins and gives them new life-that is, makes them Christians. 'The word of the Gospel', received by the addressees of 1 Peter through human beings, is not merely a human word 1.

He acts as a confirmation of the personal bond with Christ that enables him to act 'in Christ's stead' (2 Cor 5:20), so that the community welcomes him 'as though he were Christ himself' (Gal 4:14). Thus, Christ himself speaks through the apostle and confers upon his testimony the character of the word of God', L. Scheffczyk, 'Holy Scripture, the Word of God and of the Church', Revue biblique 23 (2001) 154-166, 160. This article has also been published in German and English.

- In the Letter to the Colossians, too, the identification of apostolic preaching with the concept of the Word of God is made explicit, through the convergence of both in the mystery of Christ. The letter is addressed to Christians who have come to the Church from paganism: 'Once you too were strangers and enemies, your minds set on evil deeds; but now he [Christ] has reconciled you in the body of his flesh through death, to present you holy, blameless and beyond reproach before him; provided that you remain grounded and steadfast in the faith, unshaken in the hope of the Gospel which you have heard, which has been proclaimed throughout all creation under heaven, and of which I, Paul, have become a minister. Now I rejoice in the sufferings I endure for your sake, and I complete in my flesh what is lacking in the sufferings of Christ, for the sake of his body, which is the Church. Of this I have become a minister, in accordance with the commission entrusted to me by God for your sake, to fulfil the word of God-the mystery hidden for ages and generations, but now revealed to his saints. God chose to make known to them the glorious riches of this mystery amongst the nations:

Thirdly, following the revelation in Christ and the proclamation of the Gospel, the consideration of the word of God extends to the Scriptures of Israel:

> The realisation that Jesus' sayings and the Gospel message were the word of God led Christians to a new understanding of the entire Old Testament. It was not only what Jesus had proclaimed and the content of his apostles' preaching that constituted the word of God; the Old Testament, too, was the word of God, having reached its fulfilment in Christ 1.

Artola demonstrates this assertion by examining the way in which the New Testament authors quote the Scriptures. Sometimes, a text is quoted as the 'Word of God' (cf. Jn 10:35) or is introduced as follows: 'the Holy Spirit says' (cf. Heb 3:7). And it is not only oracles or legal precepts that are quoted in this way, but also psalms or editorial passages, especially in Hebrews (cf. 1:5-11; 1:y-11, etc.; but see also Acts 4:25). Artola concludes:

> For the New Testament, the Old Testament Scriptures are, as a whole, the word of God in a comprehensive yet concrete sense. The empirical act of transcription, its consolidation through the concept of Scripture, and its liturgical and kerygmatic use thus led early Christianity to regard the entire Old Testament as the word of God 2.

In the light of Christ, the fullness and fulfilment of revelation, the whole of Scripture is understood as prophecy and therefore as the word of God.

> It is on the basis of faith in Jesus Christ as the definitive revelation of God that the apostolic generation perceives the function of 'Scripture' and, consequently, its character as divinely inspired in its entirety as that collection of books (…). A new function of the whole of 'Scripture' is discovered: to reveal God's plan, which was fulfilled in the death and resurrection of Christ. Thus, it is not merely the legal texts or prophetic oracles that serve as the medium of divine revelation, but the books as such, with their entire content!.

'Christ in you, the hope of glory. It is he, in fact, whom we proclaim, admonishing every person and teaching everyone with all wisdom, so that we may present everyone perfect in Christ. For this I labour and struggle, drawing strength from him who works in me with mighty power' (Col 1:21-29). Unlike the passage from 1 Peter, no biblical text is quoted here. However, the mention of the 'word of God', which was to be brought to fulfilment, must be understood as an allusion to the biblical concept of the dabar Adonai. In Colossians, the word of the Lord in the Old Testament is presented as the veiled expression of the future reconciliation of humankind with God, revealed and realised in Christ.

- A. M. Artola - J. M. Sánchez Caro, The Bible and the Word of God, Paideia, 1994, 42. Cf. also Basta - Bovati, 'He spoke to us through the prophets', 75-77.

- Artola - Sánchez Caro, The Bible and the Word of God, 43.

- !. My own translation. 'It is from faith in Jesus Christ as the definitive revelation of God that the apostolic generation perceives the function of 'Scripture' and, consequently, its character as divinely inspired as this collection of books (…). A new function is discovered for the whole

The final step in this process will be the simplest: the writings recounting the life of Jesus and the letters and other writings containing the apostles' preaching will begin to be assimilated into the Scriptures of the Old Testament. We shall discuss the emergence of the New Testament as sacred Scripture later, in the explanation of the formation of the canon (cf. pp. 152-168).

Before concluding this section, there remains one point to clarify. If the biblical texts were inspired by the Holy Spirit at the time of their composition, how can we say that the Old Testament as a whole was not regarded as the word of God before Christ? Can texts acquire revelatory value after their composition?

To answer this, it is worth revisiting the fundamental model of communication:

sender → message → recipient or addressee

Until the message is received by the recipient, communication does not take place. In the case of the Scriptures of Israel, these included various elements: oracles, precepts, narratives, prayers, etc. Before the coming of Christ, a large proportion of these texts were not regarded as the word of God to his people. Inspiration was certainly present, but not fully operative, so to speak. The fullness of revelation that came about in Christ unlocked the potential prophetic meaning of all the Scriptures, and God was then able to establish communication through all those texts.

If one has an overly 'ontological' understanding of the word of God, as if it were something entirely objective, independent of how it is received, it may seem disconcerting to say that certain texts have become the word of God.

We can find support for this assertion in a somewhat enigmatic passage from 2 Corinthians. St Paul maintains that Jews who do not know or accept Christ read the Torah as if they were veiled:

> Strengthened by this hope, we speak with great boldness and do not act like Moses, who used to put a veil over his face so that the Israelites might not see the end of what was merely transient. But their minds were hardened; indeed, to this day that same veil remains, unremoved, when the Old Testament is read, for it is in Christ that it is taken away. To this day, when one reads Moses, a A veil lies over their hearts; but when they turn to the Lord, the veil will be removed. The Lord is the Spirit, and where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is freedom. And we all, with unveiled faces, reflecting as in a mirror the glory of the Lord, are being transformed into that very image, from glory to glory, in accordance with the working of the Spirit of the Lord (2 Cor 1:12-18).

The interpretation of a text depends not only on what the author has written, but also on the capacity and disposition of the readers, as we shall see in Part IV.

*

The Christian understanding of Scripture, as the Word of God, written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, authored by God and addressed to the Church, will not be complete until the canon has been explained. We shall discuss the relationship between the inspiration of the books and revelation in the following pages, whilst we shall discuss the canon in Part III.

## 10. Revelation and Inspiration

Having commented on the first two chapters of Dei Verbum, we have seen what Jesus says about Scripture. We have also seen how, following the teaching received from Jesus, in apostolic preaching and in the New Testament the concept of the Word of God is extended to the whole of Scripture, above all as prophecy concerning Christ (thus overcoming the qualitative distinction between the Torah and the rest of the books).

### 10.1. Biblical testimonies on inspiration

The final point, which we have summarised with the patristic expression mutatio scripturarum, finds its expression, so to speak, in two New Testament texts that affirm the inspiration of the Scriptures: 2 Peter 1:20-21 and 2 Timothy 1:16. All IGSS textbooks comment on these two passages, which are regarded as the classic references for the Christian doctrine of biblical inspiration[^113]. Here we shall add a third text, taken from 1 Peter.

#### 10.1.1. The Spirit and the Scriptures (1 Peter 1; 2 Peter 1)

From 1 Peter, we have cited above (p. 100) a passage in which the preaching of the Gospel is identified with the word of God described by Isaiah (1 Pet 1:21-25). A few verses earlier, Saint Peter exhorts the faithful to endure with joy and patience the trials afflicting them, keeping in mind the salvation of their souls. The passage that concerns us seeks to provide a foundation for the comfort and hope that the letter aims to offer its recipients, who are reminded of the great gifts they have received, including a revelation-the Gospel-longed for by the ancient prophets and the angels:

> The prophets searched and probed into this salvation, foretelling the grace intended for you; they sought to know what time or circumstances the Spirit of Christ within them was indicating, when he foretold the sufferings destined for Christ and the glories that would follow them. It was revealed to them that they were servants-not for their own sake, but for yours-of the things now proclaimed to you through those who brought you the Gospel by the Holy Spirit, sent from heaven: things at which even the angels long to look (1 Pet 1:10-12).

The text speaks neither of the Scriptures nor of inspiration. It does, however, contain a statement of paramount importance for the Christian understanding of inspiration: the very same Spirit who acted in Christ and in the proclamation of the Gospel was already at work beforehand, in the prophets of Israel, so that they might proclaim the passion, death and resurrection of Jesus. The connection between these prophets-about whom no further details are given-and the Scriptures will be made explicit in 2 Peter.

*

2 Peter is a farewell letter or, more precisely, a letter belonging to the literary genre of testaments. Saint Peter - whether he wrote it himself or another wrote it in his name - senses that his departure from this world is near and wishes to remind the baptised of the teachings of the faith. Among these is that of the 'mighty coming' of Jesus, the Parousia:

> Indeed, we have made known to you the power and coming of our Lord Jesus Christ [τ2ν τοũ uυρíου Eµĩn íτσοũ xριστοũ &úναµιν uαì uαρουσíαν], not because we followed cleverly devised myths, but because we were eyewitnesses of his majesty (2 Pet 1:16).

Peter then alludes to the manifestation of Christ's glory at the Transfiguration (cf. vv. 1-18), as an argument in favour of hope in his glorious coming. Immediately afterwards, he presents the word of the prophets as a second reason that strengthens hope in the Day of the Lord:

> And we also have the word of the prophets, which is very reliable [τòν urofitiuòn ʎ óγον], to which you would do well to pay attention, as to a lamp shining in a dark place, until the day dawns and the morning star rises in your hearts (2 Peter 1:19).

Until the full light of the sun-that is, the glorious Lord-arrives, we must find our way by the light of this word. Which word and which prophets are we talking about? The oral preaching of the prophets or their writings? This is made clear straight away:

> Know this first of all: no prophetic scripture [προφητεία γραφῆς] is to be interpreted privately, for no prophecy ever came by the will of man, but men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit [úuò uνsúµατο@ áγíου φsρóµsνοι έ ʎ á ʎ цσαν áuò θsοũ ắνθρmuοι]. There have also been false prophets among the people, just as there will be false teachers among you (2 Peter 1:20-2:1).

We can highlight the following points from the text:

- it affirms the divine origin of all prophecy, attributing it to the Holy Spirit;

- it moves without distinction from the oral tradition, 'the prophetic word', to the written word, 'the prophecy of Scripture', before returning once more to the oral tradition: 'certain men spoke';

- !) the Holy Spirit's intervention is linked directly to people, not to texts; but since it speaks of interpretation or explanation, it is clear that the author is always referring to written prophecies;

- The divine origin has implications for the interpretation of the Scriptures.

If we combine this passage with the idea that all Scripture has a prophetic character, as Jesus suggested, it is not difficult to conclude that all Scripture is inspired. In, in fact, we find this statement in 2 Timothy.

#### 10.1.2. Inspired Scripture (2 Timothy!)

In 2 Timothy, which, like 2 Peter, can be regarded as a farewell letter, Saint Paul admonishes Timothy not to be led astray by new doctrines that oppose the truth:

> But you must remain steadfast in what you have learnt and firmly believe. You know those from whom you learnt it, and you have known the Holy Scriptures from childhood: these can instruct you for salvation [τà &υνáµsνá σs σοφíσαι sí@ σmτцρíαν], which is obtained through faith in Christ Jesus. All Scripture, inspired by God, is also useful for teaching, convincing, correcting and training in righteousness [πᾶσα γραφὴ

> θεόπνευστος u α ì í φέ ʎ ι µ ο @ uρò@ &ι&ασuα ʎ íαν, uρò@ έ ʎ sγµóν, uρò@ έuανóρθmσιν, uρò@ uαι&síαν τ2ν έν &ιuαιοσúνy], so that the man of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work (2 Tim 3:14-17).

The application of the word 'inspiration' to the Bible derives from this text. Scripture is θεόπνευστος, that is, 'breathed' by God (from θεός, 'God', and πνέω, 'to breathe').

On its own, the adjective - which is a hapax legomenon in the Bible, that is, a term that appears only once - can be interpreted either in the active sense, that is, Scripture inspires God, or in the passive sense[^114], that is, Scripture has been inspired by God. Exegetes agree that in this case the word is to be understood in the passive sense 1.

The text does not mention the Holy Spirit, nor does it explain what is meant by 'inspired'. But it links this characteristic of the Scriptures to their usefulness. They do not serve to bring about salvation, which comes through faith in Jesus, but they have the capacity to impart the wisdom that leads to this salvation. And they also serve to teach, convince, and so on. Without the Scriptures, the man of God is not complete. This value of the sacred writings derives from their inspired nature, from the 'divine breath' - that is, the Holy Spirit - present within them.

*

It is worth clarifying that, although these passages speak of the inspiration of the prophets or the Scriptures as if it were a traditional doctrine, in reality it is, in some respects, a rather new concept within the Jewish world.

As far as we know, neither the Old Testament nor Palestinian Judaism in the time of Jesus had reflected on the status of the sacred books. It was only in the Hellenistic context that a theoretical explanation began to take shape with Philo of Alexandria, particularly with regard to the composition, translation and interpretation of the Torah. However, Philo's descriptions, whilst containing elements that the Church Fathers would later draw upon, differ from the Christian concept of inspiration, which arose primarily to justify the reference to Christ in texts predating him and which was subsequently extended by analogy to the books of the New Testament[^115].

Something similar must be said regarding the notion of God as the 'author' of Holy Scripture a notion that would emerge in the second century, in St Irenaeus's polemic against Gnosticism and later against various forms of Manichaean dualism. The fundamental aim of this idea was to defend the unity between the Old Testament and the New Testament, which Gnosticism sought to break by attributing the Old Testament to a god other than the Father of Jesus. Later, it would become a complement to the idea of inspiration: God is the principal author of the Bible, whilst the writers are its instrumental authors (we shall explain these concepts on pp. 250- r 255).

Section

### 10.2. Inspiration within the framework of revelation and its transmission

Following the first two chapters of Dei Verbum, we have seen the place occupied by Scripture within the economy of revelation and its transmission. We then completed our overview of Dei Verbum by examining what Jesus and the apostles said about the Scriptures. Now, to conclude Part I, we should return to Dei Verbum to see where inspiration is mentioned.

The first chapter of Dei Verbum describes divine revelation in itself, as the manifestation of God through works and words interwoven within a history-the history of salvation. This history reaches its culmination in Jesus Christ. In this chapter (DV 2-6), there is no reference either to the sacred books or, consequently, to their inspiration.

It is worth asking why Dei Verbum does not speak of inspiration in its description of historical revelation. Would it not have been appropriate to do so? Indeed, in the Creed we say that the Holy Spirit 'spoke through the prophets' and so we might say that he inspired them. Certainly, it is legitimate to speak of the Spirit's action upon all the mediators of revelation in terms of 'inspiration'. However, one must not equate this supernatural intervention with the inspiration of the books, even though the two realities are connected. For the sake of clarity, it is best to reserve the term 'inspiration' for the charism associated with the biblical books. This is what theology has done, and this is also the approach taken by *Dei Verbum*, even though one of the merits of this document lies precisely in not isolating the inspiration of the books, but in presenting it in relation to revelation and its transmission: inspiration is subordinate to historical revelation, which culminates in Christ[^116].

bibliche 12 (2000) 15-18. For a comparison between Philo and 2 Timothy, cf. J. Herzer, ''Moved by the Spirit of God': The Inspiration of Scripture according to 2 Timothy 1:16 and in Philo of Alexandria' in R. Deines and K.-W. Niebuhr (eds.), Philo and the New Testament: Mutual Perceptions; I. International Symposium on the Corpus Judaeo-Hellenisticum, 4 May 200, Eisenach/Jena, Mohr Siebeck, 2004, 22-240.

The second chapter of Dei Verbum (DV 10) sets out the transmission of revelation. In DV 10, the writing of books is mentioned as one of the means used by the apostles to spread the Gospel. And inspiration is immediately discussed: it is stated, in fact, that certain apostles and men close to them 'under the inspiration [sub inspiratione] of the Holy Spirit, set down in writing the message of salvation'. The existence of inspiration at the time of the composition of the books (in this context, those of the New Testament) is therefore recognised, and it is attributed to the Spirit. Both are traditional assertions, but a certain novelty arises from the structure of Dei Verbum: inspiration, like Scripture, appears within the transmission of revelation, and not within revelation itself[^117].

At the beginning of paragraph 8, there is a second mention of inspiration. Speaking of apostolic preaching, Dei Verbum states that this is 'expressed in a special way in the inspired books [in inspiratis libris]'. Inspiration is therefore not limited to the moment of the books' composition, but constitutes an inherent characteristic of the books themselves, which remains within them. This is still a traditional assertion.

In paragraph 9, there is a third reference to inspiration, more significant than the previous two. It is stated - for the first time in Dei Verbum that the Holy Scriptures are the word of God, and here the function or effect of inspiration becomes apparent:

> Sacred Scripture is the Word of God insofar as it is handed down in writing through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. [Sacra Scriptura est locutio Dei quatenus divino agante Spiritu scripto consignatur].

We must bear in mind that, as is already evident from the title of the document - 'Dogmatic Constitution on Divine Revelation Dei Verbum' - an equivalence can be established between 'the Word of God' and 'divine revelation'. We can therefore substitute the terms in the sentence just quoted:

> Sacred Scripture is divine revelation insofar as it is handed down in writing through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit.

Thanks to inspiration, Scripture is not merely a written testimony to the apostolic preaching or to the revelation to Israel; that is to say, it is not simply It is not merely the texts that transmit revelation, but a text of which we can say that it is the divine word, to the extent that, as DV8 states, 'God, who spoke in the past, does not cease to speak to the bride of his beloved Son'.

Revelation, insofar as it is history, is no longer present. In order to be transmitted, it has taken various forms: oral preaching (the 'Gospel' as good news), liturgy, examples of life, and even written discourse, which, by virtue of a special action of the Holy Spirit, may itself be regarded as revelation, whilst differing from the historical revelation in deeds and words that culminates in Christ.

In the third chapter, Dei Verbum devotes an entire paragraph to explaining what inspiration consists of (DV 11), which we shall discuss later (pp. 250-255). However, the most important elements for defining inspiration emerge in chapter 2, particularly in DV 9, as we have just emphasised. Further on, in no. 24, Dei Verbum emphasises the relationship between the inspiration of the Bible and its character as the word of God, using a very clear formulation:

> The Sacred Scriptures contain the Word of God and, because they are inspired, are truly the Word of God. [Sacrae autem Scripturae verbum Dei continent et, quia inspiratae, vere verbum Dei sunt].

Dei Verbum thus presents inspiration in relation to both revelation and its transmission: inspiration ensures that the sacred books transmit revelation whilst being revelation themselves, the Word of God. As Cardinal Scheffczyk explains:

> The doctrine of inspiration seeks to establish that the Bible, even with the collaboration of the human author, is and remains the Word of God, despite being expressed in human words and contained within the teaching of the Church[^118].

At least in the case of the New Testament, where this distinction can be made, inspiration pertains to the transmission of revelation rather than to its historical development. But this charism of the Spirit ensures that the transmission retains, so to speak, the same power and normative authority as the original revelation:

> The novelty of the conciliar statement lies in suggesting that inspiration should not be understood as an act of God aimed at making Scripture a new event of revelation - just as the intervention of the inspired prophet or the apostle is new when they set forth the word of God -, but that Scripture must nevertheless be understood as revelation[^119].

In summary, in *Dei Verbum* Scripture appears first and foremost as a testimony to and transmission of revelation rather than as an event of revelation. However, by virtue of its inspiration, Scripture is a revelation of God. Thus, Dei Verbum succeeds in reconciling two fundamental assertions: on the one hand, Christian revelation, insofar as it is historical and personal, cannot be equated with the Bible; on the other hand, the Bible is truly the word of God, who speaks today to his people, the Church, through the sacred texts.

> The Bible has, on the one hand, a direct connection with the prophetic and apostolic word of Israel and the early Church, and, on the other, with Christ and the Father, through the Spirit who inspires it. And this is why the word of the Bible represents a privileged moment of revelation. If it were merely a historical record of prophetic oracles, it would have value as a book of origins, as a document of the faith of past generations, without necessarily and always containing a normative word for future generations. Instead, its relationship with the Word through the Spirit makes this word a living and enduring force through which God is revealed for all time[^120].

We may attempt to offer a definition of inspiration that takes account of what has been explained so far. It is the action of the Holy Spirit by which certain books are not only a testimony to divine revelation, but also the word of God to the Church.

At the beginning of the first part, we asked ourselves in what sense the Church believes that Sacred Scripture is the word of God. I hope to have shown that the answer - which is not straightforward - requires us to highlight the relationship of the books to Christ (historical revelation, apostolic preaching) and to the Holy Spirit (the charism of inspiration, the Church). It is therefore essential to emphasise that it is not sufficient to define inspiration as 'the action of the Holy Spirit by which the Bible is the Word of God'. This notion, which is very widespread among the general public, should be avoided, as it does not reflect the connection between inspiration- a connection with historical revelation that culminates in Christ and therefore risks becoming a simplistic notion of inspiration, as if every word of the Bible came directly from God, without any link to historical revelation. We shall return to the nature of inspiration at the end (pp. 242-261).

# PART II TEXT

In the introduction, we examined some physical characteristics of ancient books: the material from which they were mostly made - papyrus or parchment - and their format - primarily scrolls or codices - (pp. 10-18). We also discussed the languages in which the books comprising Sacred Scripture were originally written: Hebrew, Aramaic and Greek (pp. 45-52).

We must now complete our study of the biblical books in their material form by explaining how the texts were transmitted (copied) and how one might attempt to reconstruct the 'original' text. We shall also need to say a few words about the main translations of the Bible, both ancient and modern.

# II. Introduction: the books before the printing press

Around 1457, Johannes Gensfleisch, known as Gutenberg, completed the production of the first printed book (which was the Psalter in Latin) in Mainz, Germany. This technical advance marked a turning point in book production, as movable type allowed books to be mass-produced without the need for manual copying.

## 11. INTRODUCTION: THE FIRST BOOKS OF THE DECADE

Following the invention and spread of the printing press, books became a more widely available, cheaper and therefore more accessible commodity for everyone, which had an enormous impact on the development and spread of Western culture. Libraries were no longer the exclusive preserve of monasteries, the high clergy or certain members of the nobility; now the middle classes too could access books. The printing press became a major means of social dissemination, not only of culture in the highest sense of the term - but also of political and religious propaganda. The printing press is certainly an instrument of power[^121].

Today we are so accustomed to printed books that it is difficult for us to imagine a world without them. With the printing press, the text of a book (the letters, words and sentences) becomes fixed, in the sense that its content is completely identical across the various copies of the same edition. Before the advent of the printing press, however, all books were copied by hand (hence the term 'manuscripts'), both for preservation and for distribution.

The work of manual copying could be carried out in various ways. The most common method was for the scribe or copyist to have the text to be copied in front of them, as shown in the image, which depicts a 15th-century scribe (the Frenchman Jean Miélot) working in his scriptorium.

Another method, used to produce several copies at the same time, involved gathering several copyists around a reader who read aloud

The process of manual copying inevitably produced variations in the text as it was transmitted, which only come to light when different manuscripts are compared. These differences within the text of the same book are technically referred to as ' ' or 'textual variants '.

### 11.1. The study of textual variants

A critical edition is a presentation of an ancient text in which the main variants found in the manuscripts are indicated. It usually contains two sections: the proposed text and the notes with the variants, technically referred to as the 'critical apparatus'. We shall see numerous examples of this in what follows. The production of critical editions is one of the fruits of textual criticism.

Textual criticism applies to all texts transmitted prior to the advent of printing and which have therefore undergone a process of textual diversification, not just biblical texts: it applies to the Homeric epics, Mesopotamian texts, Cicero's letters, the works of St Augustine, Dante Alighieri's *Divine Comedy*, and a very long list of others. Similarly, it can also be applied to printed books that have undergone a complex publication process, as is the case, for example, with the works of William Shakespeare.

To determine which variant is closest to the original, the critic employs certain 'rules' or principles. Firstly, one must take into account the varying value of manuscripts, which depends primarily on their age (the older the manuscript, the closer it is to the original). One must also consider the number of manuscripts that contain a particular variant. Age and number are external criteria. Internal arguments are then also employed: taking into account possible errors or changes introduced by the copyist, preference should be given to the variant that explains the origin of the others (lectio difficultior, lectio brevior). We shall provide some examples later on.

Textual criticism is concerned with the study of textual variants, with a twofold aim:

- to reconstruct the original text as far as possible, based on the existing manuscripts;

- to understand the process of transmission, which often enables us to ascertain how the texts were understood in each historical period.

However, it should be noted that the primary aim of textual criticism - that is, the attempt to arrive at the originals - presents two major difficulties in the biblical field, one factual and the other conceptual:

- we have no original biblical manuscripts in the strict sense, no 'autograph[^122]' 1, but only copies. All the surviving manuscripts date from well after the composition of the corresponding book;

- the very notion of an 'original text' is problematic, particularly with regard to the books of the Old Testament. This point warrants further explanation.

In the case of the Old Testament, the notion of an original text is problematic first and foremost because we generally do not know who the authors of the books are, nor when they were written. Furthermore, almost all the books of the Old Testament are the result of a long process of 'development', during which they underwent changes: additions, glosses, revisions and re-editions[^123].

In some cases, the differences between the text in the original language and the ancient versions allow us to reconstruct the stages of this process of the book's 'development'. For example, the Greek version of Esther is longer than the Hebrew original, as we have seen (p. 48). Conversely, the Greek translation of Jeremiah is considerably shorter than the Hebrew text we possess, which can be explained if the translator relied on an older Hebrew text, to which additions were made after the translation into Greek. The situation regarding the Book of Sirach is even more complicated. We have the complete text in Greek, but the Greek manuscripts show significant differences amongst themselves, suggesting the existence of two Greek versions of the book: a long one and a short one. If we consider the Hebrew fragments of the book, discovered in recent times (cf. pp. 47-48), these too present two different forms. What, then, is the original text of Sirach? The text written by the original author, or the final version? The question also has theological implications: which is the inspired text? We shall discuss this further in * * (cf. pp. 131-133).

### 11.2. Some examples of textual variants

Depending on the cause that gave rise to them, we can divide textual variants - that is, as already explained above, the differences in the text between copies of the same book - into two broad groups: unintentional errors and deliberate changes.

#### 11.2.1. Unintentional errors

The most common unintentional errors are skipping a line or a word whilst copying, repeating a word, or, in the case of copying from dictation, confusing a sound and thus mistaking one word for another.

For example, instead of 'the former things have passed away' (Rev 21:4), an important manuscript, the Codex Sinaiticus, reads 'the sheep have passed away'. The copyist wrote

πρόβατα (sheep) instead of πρῶτα (things of the past), thereby spoiling the beautiful description of the new heavens and the new earth. As is

the case with the New Testament, the notion of an 'original' text has been called into question. Some prefer to speak of an 'initial' text. Cf. M. W. Holmes, 'From 'Original Text' to 'Initial Text': The Traditional Goal of New Testament Textual Criticism in Contemporary Discussion' in B. D. Ehrman and M. W. Holmes (eds.), The Text of the New Testament in Contemporary Research, Brill, 2012, 67-688.

As can be seen in the image, the error is so obvious that it was corrected in the manuscript itself by a second hand.

A famous error is that found in 1 Sam 13:1. The first sentence of the verse, handed down without variation in all the major Hebrew manuscripts, reads:

> Saul was one year old when he began to reign [ָ֖נהשׁן־ָבֶּ לוּ֣אשָׁ וָֹמְל֑כבְּ]

The expression נהשׁבן־ occurs 53 times in the Hebrew Bible and means 'to be one year old'. However, the statement in 1 Sam 13,1 is inconsistent with the account of Saul, who was not a child when he was anointed by Samuel and proclaimed king (cf. 1 Sam 9-10). It is likely that the copyist omitted a word (a number), but we cannot know which one, as there are no variants in the Hebrew manuscripts: they all contain the same phrase, without any differences. The ancient versions do not help us to resolve the problem. In fact, the Greek translation omits the entire verse, perhaps because the translator recognised the problem and, not knowing how to resolve it, chose to ignore it. In the Latin Vulgate we find an exact translation of the Hebrew text (which, incidentally, is a fine example of the sometimes extreme fidelity with which St Jerome sought to translate):

> Filius unius anni Saul, cum regnare coepisset. [Saul was one year old when he began to reign]

It is so obvious that this is an error that many modern versions have attempted to correct the text. Here are some examples of emendations, in chronological order:

(1901): Saul was forty years old when he began to reign. CEI (1994):

> Luther Bible (1545): Saul war ein Jahr König gewesen. KJV (1611): Saul reigned one year. American Standard Version Saul was thirty years old when he began to reign. Nova Vulgata (1979): Filius annorum Saul, cum regnare coepisset. CEI (2008): Saul was in the prime of his life when he began to reign.

Other translators have preferred to point out that a word is missing:

> RSV (1952) and NRSV (1989): Saul was … years old when he began to reign. [Very similar in The New American Bible (2002)].

> Bible de Jérusalem (1973): Saül était âgé de … ans lorsqu'il devint roi. Einheitsübersetzung (1980): Saul war … Jahre alt, als er König wurde. TOB (1988): Saül avait … ans lorsqu'il devint roi.

> El libro del Pueblo de Dios (1990): Saul was … years old when he began to reign.

In 1 Sam 13:1 we find an example of what is technically termed a 'corrupt' text, that is, a text which has undergone such alterations during the process of copying that it is impossible to reconstruct the text as it was before the errors occurred.

#### 11.2.2. Intentional changes

Examples of deliberate changes include harmonisation with parallel passages, corrections for theological reasons, glosses to explain a difficult phrase, etc.

Let us take as our first example Mark 9:29, the phrase with which Jesus responds to his disciples, who had asked him why they had been unable to cast out a demon. Some manuscripts (the Codex Sinaiticus, the Codex Vaticanus and others) record this text:

> This sort of demon cannot be driven out in any way, except by prayer.

Many other manuscripts contain a slightly longer phrase:

> This sort of demon cannot be driven out by any means except through prayer and fasting.

Generally speaking, it is simpler to assume that a copyist added something rather than that they omitted it. Therefore, the shorter phrase would be preferable as the lectio brevior. However, the opposite is also possible: that the longer version is the older one and that a copyist subsequently omitted a few words by mistake. The critic's judgement is not easy, and it is often the case that the solution is not obvious. It is also important to bear in mind the number and, above all, the age of the manuscripts containing one or the other phrase. In this case, the age and quality of the manuscripts are the criteria that tip the balance. In fact, scholars consider that the original text of Mark did not say 'and fasting', which would be a later gloss or expansion, perhaps due to the importance of the practice of fasting in the early Church[^124].

Let us look at Mark 3:14. In the manuscripts we find this variant:

> He appointed Twelve, so that they might be with him and that he might send them out to preach (Alexandrian Codex).

> He appointed Twelve - whom he called apostles - so that they might be with him and that he might send them out to preach (Sinaiticus, Vaticanus and Ephrem's Rescript).

It is possible that a copyist 'completed' the sentence, drawing on information from Luke 6:1, where the words 'whom he called apostles' appear. This would be a case of harmonisation, which is quite common amongst the three Synoptic Gospels. However, the shorter reading in the Alexandrian Codex could simply be the result of a copyist's oversight. Once again, we see how difficult it is to determine which reading is the oldest.

Finally, a clear example of a theological correction is found at the end of the account of the seven-day creation (Gen 1:1-2:4a). In Gen 2:2a, the Hebrew text reads:

> On the seventh day, God completed the work he had done. [ָעָ֑שׂה ֲאֶ֣שׁר ַהְשִּׁביִ֔עימַלאְכ֖וֹתְּ הי֙םַבּ֣וֹיּם ť ֱאִ ַוְיַ֤כל] In contrast, the Greek translation reads: On the sixth day, God finished the work he had done. [καὶ συνετέλεσεν ó θsò@ έν τq Eµέρą τq έuτy τà έργα αúτοũ, ầ

> ἐποίησεν]

The most likely explanation for the difference is that the translator noticed that the Hebrew phrase was ambiguous and wanted to avoid the impression that God had also worked on the Sabbath. For this reason, he deliberately altered the text.

## 12. The Text of the Old Testament

It is not possible to discuss the text of every single book in detail, so we shall have to content ourselves with an overview. For educational purposes, the presentation of the history of the transmission of the biblical texts will follow a 'descending' order, so to speak. In other words, we shall begin with the texts we have today, and then describe their earlier stages.

Anyone wishing to read the Old Testament in the original languages must consult at least two sources:

- for the 24 books of the BH, one must consult a critical edition of the BH, such as the Biblia Hebraica Stuttgartensia (BHS);

- for the books of the Old Testament not included in the BH (Tobit, Judith, 1-2 Maccabees, Wisdom, Sirach and Baruch), for the Greek version of Esther, and for certain parts of Daniel (as well as for the Greek versions of all the other books), one must consult a critical edition of the Septuagint (LXX).

### 12.1. The text in Hebrew

#### 12.1.1. From the BHS (20th century) to the Masoretic Text (6th-9th centuries)

The BHS is the most recent complete critical edition of the BH. Publication was completed in 1977 (the latest reprint dates from 1997). A new critical edition of the BH, known as the Biblia Hebraica Quinta, is currently in progress. However, many volumes are still missing before it can be completed[^125]. For this reason, the BHS remains the standard of reference.

Below is a page from the BHS (image taken from toolsforstudyingthe-hebrewbible/criticaleditions). In the centre is the biblical text in Hebrew (in this case, Gen 21:1-22:13). Apart from the verse numbers, the rest is a reproduction of the Hebrew text from a medieval codex, Codex L. In fact, the BHS does not present a text reconstructed by the editors, but reproduces the text of the Codex Leningradensis (L or B 19a), also known as the St Petersburg Codex, to which is appended a critical apparatus listing the main variants attested in other Hebrew manuscripts and in ancient versions.

At the bottom of the page, there are two types of annotations. The annotations further down constitute the critical apparatus (in English, 'critical apparatus '), compiled by the editors of the BHS. Those above, in very small type, are references to the so-called 'masora magna', that is, to comments made by the ancient editors of the text, the Masoretes. On the left, you can see some small annotations. These words also come from Codex L. They are called the 'masora parva' and are observations made by the Masoretes.

Codex L dates from the medieval period; more specifically, it was completed in the year 1008/1009. It contains the 24 books of the BH, with the vowels and marks of the Masoretes, that is, the so-called Masoretic Text (MT). The name derives from the fact that this text is the result of the work of the 'Masoretes', Jewish scribes who were active in the 6th-9th centuries. In addition to copying the texts of the BH, the Masoretes added some marginal notes (known as 'masorah', hence their name) and, above all, added marks to indicate the vowels.

The image below shows two vowels marked in a manuscript containing the MT. The vowel points were added to aid in the correct reading

the texts. However, it is important to bear in mind that in cases where the same consonants can be vocalised in different ways, the Masoretes had to make choices. Therefore, the MT itself contains interpretations, which are generally well-founded but sometimes debatable[^126]. 1

In addition to L, the MT is also preserved in other medieval manuscripts, notably the Aleppo Codex (c. 9th century AD) and the Cairo Codex of the Prophets. The BHS is based primarily on L because this manuscript is complete, whilst the others are missing certain sections. The Aleppo Codex begins with Deut.

28.17; the preceding pages - and others too - were lost in 1947. Photographs can be viewed at on

aleppocodex.org[^127].

#### 12.1.2. The transmission of the text prior to the MT

To refer to the text of the BH prior to the work of the Masoretes, which subsequently became the MT, the term 'pre-Masoretic' or 'proto-Masoretic' is used.

Before the Masoretic period, the text was transmitted by copyists known simply as soferim, that is, scribes (2nd-6th centuries). They confined themselves to copying the text without adding almost anything: there are very few annotations dating from this period. Even the surviving manuscripts from this period are few in number and, as mentioned, present the text without vowels. (Generally speaking, for all ancient texts, the further back in time one goes, the fewer manuscripts have survived to the present day).

The oldest surviving BH manuscripts are those found at Qumran. They date from the 2nd century BC to the 1st century AD. Before Qumran and the other discoveries of the 20th century, the oldest known BH manuscripts were all medieval.

Thanks, then, to the Dead Sea Scrolls, it has been possible to go back a millennium!

The implications for textual criticism arising from the analysis of these manuscripts can be summarised in two points:

- on the one hand, some of the biblical texts from Qumran are virtually identical to the consonantal text of the Masoretic Text (MT), which has confirmed the fidelity with which this text was transmitted by ancient and medieval Jewish scribes;

- on the other hand, other Hebrew biblical texts found at Qumran have revealed that several textual 'families' existed prior to the yo. This discovery has led, amongst other things, to a reassessment of the Greek version of the LXX.

According to some scholars, the text copied by the soferim derives from a text established between the two Jewish wars against Rome, that is, between the yo and 1 AD. In this context, 'establishing' the text does not mean that the Jews prepared a new edition, but rather that they made a selection of manuscripts of the Hebrew text from among the various ones that existed, upon which all subsequent copies are based[^128].

This hypothesis is based on the fact that the BH manuscripts dating from before the year yo AD exhibit significant textual variations amongst themselves. Indeed, amongst the Hebrew texts found at Qumran, whilst many have a text similar to the MT, others contain texts closer to the Septuagint or to other ancient texts, such as the Samaritan Pentateuch. By contrast, the manuscripts found at Wadi Muraba'at and Nahal Hever, dating from after the year yo, present a text that is almost identical to that of the future MT[^129].

Other authors speak of a standardisation of the text that took place in the first century, but not necessarily after the year yo[^130].

We know practically nothing about the transmission of the BH text before Christ. As a rather anecdotal piece of information, it is worth noting that two silver scrolls, dating from the 7th or 6th century BC and bearing inscriptions, were found in a tomb at Ketef Hinnom (Jerusalem). Both contain the so-called 'blessing'

'priestly' or Aaronic, almost identical to the Masoretic Text (MT) of Num 6:24-26 (cf. COS 2.8:221). We may say that this is the oldest biblical text known to us, although 'bi ' is, in this case, a completely anachronistic term.

### 12.2. The Septuagint (LXX)

#### 12.2.1. The origin of the translation

A text from the 2nd or 1st century BC, known as the Letter of Aristeas to Philocrates, recounts the origin of the translation of the Torah into Greek (which is the earliest known translation of biblical books).

By order of King Ptolemy (probably Ptolemy II Philadelphus, founder of the Library of Alexandria, who reigned in Egypt between 285 and 247 BC), Demetrius, the librarian, was tasked with obtaining a Greek copy of the Law of the Jews for the library. To obtain it, Demetrius made a request to the high priest of the Temple in Jerusalem, and from there twenty elders (six from each tribe) were sent with the Torah scrolls in Hebrew. After being received by the king with great honours, they completed the translation together in twenty days.

This account gives rise to the name 'Septuagint' (Septuaginta, LXX), which came to be used in Christian times to refer to the Greek translation of the Old Testament (for the sake of simplicity, the number 'two' was reduced to 'one'). Although the name 'Septuagint' should strictly refer only to the Greek version of the Pentateuch (the Letter of Aristeas mentions only the translation of Moses' Torah), this term came to be applied to the Greek translation of the entire Old Testament.

Generally speaking, scholars agree that the account in the Letter of Aristeas concerning the origins of the translation has some historical basis. Indeed, we have some evidence confirming that a Greek translation of the Pentateuch was produced in the 3rd century BC. We also know that the translation work did not stop at the five books of the Torah, but continued into the 2nd century with the other books, as the prologue to Sirach informs us (cf. pp. 170-171).

There are various hypotheses regarding the reasons behind this version. Some believe that the main cause is not to be found in King Ptolemy's initiative to complete the library, as recounted in the Letter of Aristeas, but in the Jewish community's need for a text of the Torah for synagogue readings that the Alexandrian Jews could understand, given that they no longer understood Hebrew. Other authors suggest that the version served an apologetic purpose or was intended to promote their own culture, whilst others maintain that it was indeed King Ptolemy who compelled the Jews to translate the Torah, so that it might serve as their own law within the co- community, or simply because the Library of Alexandria was an instrument of political propaganda[^131].

Without claiming to resolve these issues, we may add that the account given in the Letter of Aristeas concerning the origin of the Greek translation of the Pentateuch was undoubtedly intended to show that the translation had been undertaken with the approval of the authorities of the Temple in Jerusalem. The attempt to defend the legitimacy of the translation is evident, most likely because certain groups considered it unlawful or sacrilegious to translate the Torah.

Later, the account would be embellished with miraculous elements to show that not only the high priest, but God himself, approved the translation. Philo, speaking of the origin of the Septuagint, says that the elders were taken to an island and there:

> as if inspired by the divine, they prophesied not one thing and another, but all the same names and the same words, as though an invisible prompters were whispering them into each one's ear[^132].

Some Church Fathers repeated this story, further embellishing it with supernatural interventions. Many Christians believed - even in recent times - that the Greek translation of the Old Testament had truly been inspired by God, going so far in some cases as to claim that there is no need to consult the original Hebrew text, as the 'inspired[^133]'! Greek translation would suffice. The Church has always held the Septuagint in high regard, but this does not mean that it can replace the Hebrew text. We shall discuss the relationship between the text and inspiration later on (pp. 11-12).

#### 12.2.2. The transmission of the Greek Bible. The four major codices

We must now mention the principal manuscripts containing the books of the Old Testament in Greek. Although the manuscripts of the Greek Bible belong, for the most part, Although the Septuagint dates from the Christian era, some ancient fragments of non-Christian origin have been found near the Dead Sea (Qumran and Nahal Hever). Among these, the scroll containing the Twelve Prophets in Greek (8HevXIIgr), found at Nahal Hever and dating from the 1st century AD, stands out.

All manuscripts containing the Greek Bible dating from after the 3rd century were copied by Christians. Indeed, it appears that in the 3rd or 4th century AD, the rabbis definitively prohibited translations of the biblical books[^134].

Among the vast number of Christian manuscripts, it is important to be aware of the oldest codices containing both the Old Testament (the Septuagint) and the New Testament, which are the following four:

- Codex Vaticanus (B), 4th century, is housed in the Vatican Library and can be viewed online: http://digi.vatlib.it.

- Codex Sinaiticus (S or א), 4th century, is now held at the British Library, London. It was discovered in 1859 by Constantin von Tischendorf at St Catherine's Monastery in Sinai, Egypt. In 1975, a further 12 pages were found at the same monastery. In 2009, a new fragment was found. Photographs and further information are available online: www.codexsinaiticus.org.

- Codex Alexandrinus (A), 5th century, is also held at the British Library: www.bl.uk/manuscripts.

- Codex Ephraemi Syri Rescriptus (C), 5th century, is in Paris, at the Bibliothèque nationale de France (Grec 9): http://gallica.bnf.fr. It is called 'Ephraem's Rescript' because it is a palimpsest: in the 12th century, text was copied over the biblical text

In the 12th century, some works by Saint Ephrem the Syrian were translated. In the image on the right, you can see the Greek biblical text at the bottom and Ephrem's Syriac text above it[^135].

All four manuscripts are on parchment. They feature the text in capital letters, which are so named not because they are larger than the lower-case letters, but because they are compressed into a bilinear layout.

'Bilinear layout' means that all the capital letters fit between two parallel lines, as in the image of below, taken from vatlib.it/it/ Greek paleography (Paleography from Antiquity to the Renaissance by T. Janz), where you can find more images and explanations. However,

the

#### 12.2.3. Critical editions

Preparing a critical edition of the Greek Old Testament is an extremely difficult task. One must consult not only the direct witnesses (the manuscripts containing the biblical books), but also the numerous indirect witnesses to the text, which come mainly from liturgical lectionaries and from quotations from the Old Testament made by the authors of the New Testament and the

lower-case

widespread from the 9th century onwards.

Greek-speaking Church Fathers. The New Testament authors wrote in Greek and therefore quoted the Old Testament in Greek. However, these quotations do not always correspond to the Septuagint as we know it, partly because they quoted from memory, and partly because other Greek translations existed.

Given these difficulties, it is hardly surprising that there are several critical editions of the LXX. The three main ones are Rahlfs's hand-edited edition, the Cambridge edition and the Göttingen edition (the latter still in the process of being published).

Alfred Rahlfs's edition is a 'manual' edition in the sense that it does not aim to present all the important variants, but rather to offer a text that is easy to consult and use. Rahlfs's edition is based primarily on B, S and A. The first edition was published in 1935. A second edition was published in 2006: A. RᴀHᴌFS (ed.), Septuaginta: Id est Vetus Testamentum Graece iuxta LXX interpretes, quam recognovit et emendavit Robert Han-hart, Deutsche Bibelgesellschaft, Stuttgart 2006. This edition is important today not because it is the best, but because it is the one used by almost all electronic editions of the Greek Old Testament.

The Cambridge edition reproduces the text of B, noting in the critical apparatus the main variants found in other manuscripts. Unfortunately, it is incomplete; it includes only the Pentateuch and the historical books, with the exception of the Maccabees. Work on the project was interrupted in 1940 due to the Second World War. The title of the work reads as follows: A. E. BRᴏᴏᴋᴇ - N. MᴄLᴇᴀN (eds.), The Old Testament in Greek: According to the Text of Codex Vaticanus, Supplemented from Other Uncial Manuscripts, with a Critical Apparatus Containing the Variants of the Chief Ancient Authorities for the Text of the Septuagint, Cambridge University Press, Cambridge 1906-1940.

Finally, we have the Göttingen edition: J. ZIᴇGᴌᴇR - J. W. WᴇᴠᴇRS - et al. (eds.), Septuaginta: Vetus Testamentum Graecum auctoritate Academiae Scientiarum Gottingensis editum, Vandenhoeck & Ruprecht, Göttingen 1974-. It draws on a large number of manuscripts and therefore features a very comprehensive critical apparatus. Above all, however, it is characterised by its attempt to reconstruct the original text of each individual book[^136]. As of today (August 2021), nine books are still missing to complete this monumental work: Joshua, Judges, 1-2 Samuel, 1-2 Kings, 1 Chronicles, Proverbs and the Song of Songs (www.adw-goe.de). The last two are also missing in the Cambridge edition and must therefore be consulted in Rahlfs.

## 13. The text of the New Testament

In principle, tracing the original texts of the 27 books of the New Testament should be a simpler task than is the case with the Old Testament, because it is a collection shorter and more recent, written in a single language and over a much shorter period of time. Nevertheless, the textual landscape of the New Testament is highly complex, mainly for two reasons:

- we have an enormous number of textual witnesses:

- the number of textual variants is also very high.

Having explained these two points, we shall now turn to critical editions.

### 13.1. Textual witnesses

To begin with, here is a schematic overview of the types of New Testament textual witnesses, which can be direct or indirect:

> a) Direct witnesses are those that directly reproduce the text of the New Testament in the original language (Greek). There are four types:

> -Papyri. These are important above all for their antiquity (…). Due to the nature of the material, they are usually fragmentary and the quality of the text varies.

> -Oncial codices. So called because of the type of script used until approximately the 9th century (consisting solely of capital letters and with no spaces between words). (…)

- -Minuscule manuscripts. These date from the 9th century right up to the invention of printing.

- -Lectionaries.

- Indirect. These are those lacking some of the characteristics of direct witnesses:

> -Biblical quotations in the works of early Christian authors. Their advantage lies in the possibility of dating the origin of the text in relation to the life of the respective author. They have limitations arising from the adaptation of the quotations to the text in which they appear, as well as the fact that these works also require textual criticism.

> -Ancient translations. Their importance stems from the fact that the translators had access to manuscripts in the original language that were older than those preserved today. Among these, the Latin versions (Vetus Latina and Vulgate), Syriac versions (Vetus Syra, Peshitta, Filoxeniana and Harklensis), Coptic versions (in various dialects) and Armenian versions^137 stand out.

We cannot provide here a complete list of the manuscripts and other textual sources (quotations, liturgical lectionaries) for the New Testament text, as this would require several pages.

There are currently more than five thousand manuscripts containing books or fragments of the Greek New Testament, a figure considerably higher than that for manuscripts containing the Old Testament. Of these manuscripts, around 100 date from before the year 800; and of these 100, only 14 date from before the year 400[^138].

The most important manuscripts are undoubtedly the four major codices mentioned above in connection with the Septuagint, namely B, S, A and C (cf. pp. 124-126).

To these four must be added the Codex Bezae (D), dating from the 5th century, which contains the four Gospels and the Acts of the Apostles. It is an important manuscript not only because of its age, but also because it contains a text that is generally very different from that of the others.

It is worth mentioning the oldest surviving Greek manuscripts of the New Testament. The oldest is known as P 52 and contains several verses from John (Jn 18:31-33); a transcription can be viewed here: itseeweb.bham.ac.uk/iohannes/transcriptions/?witness=P52&language=greek. It is usually dated to the first half of the 2nd century, between 100 and 150 AD.

Dating from around the year 200 are P 46 - which contains Rom, Heb, 1-2 Cor, Eph, Gal, Phil, Col and 1 Ts -, P 66 (Jn) and P 75 (Lk and Jn). The letter P indicates that these are papyri.

### 13.2. Variants

According to a rough estimate, there are approximately 500,000 textual variants in the Greek New Testament, not including orthographic differences[^139]. It should be noted that the vast majority of these variants are of little significance: for example, 'Christ Jesus' instead of 'Jesus Christ'.

A study of the textual witnesses of the New Testament leads to the conclusion that the majority of the variants are ancient, more specifically dating from before the year 200.

(my translation).

> There is substantial evidence to suggest that, during the early decades of transmission[^140], the texts in circulation underwent numerous alterations: words or entire lines were inadvertently omitted or copied twice; stylistic changes were made; words were substituted; clearly awkward expressions or outright errors were corrected; and so on (…). A striking feature of our textual evidence is that the oldest copies of the various books that eventually made up the New Testament differ from one another far more than the later copies, which were produced under much more controlled conditions in the Middle Ages. Even the New Testament quotations found in the writings of the early Church Fathers show a considerable degree of textual variation dating back to these very earliest stages in the history of transmission 1.

The extremely high number of variants is a surprising fact that requires an explanation.
