For some authors, this is linked to the rapid expansion of Christianity, which meant that texts were copied as best they could, without being able to rely, in the majority of cases, on professional copyists. To this we may add that, in the early decades, the books that later formed the New Testament were not yet held in high regard as sacred scriptures, and were therefore copied with a degree of freedom. Another important factor, particularly for the three Synoptic Gospels, is the tendency to harmonise differences in the accounts of the same event, as in the example of Mark 3:14

# 3,14 seen above (pp. 117-118[^141]).

### 13.3. Critical editions

The most famous and widely used critical edition of the Greek New Testament is the 'NestleAland', named after its first editor, Eberhard Nestle (1851-1913), and his successor, Kurt Aland (1915-1994), both Germans. It is abbreviated to 'NA' followed by the edition number: NA 27, NA 28. The twenty-eighth edition[^142] was published in 2012. A digitisation project is currently underway: nttranscripts.uni-muenster.de.

The Nestle-Aland belongs to that class of critical editions which are technically referred to as 'eclectic' or, more accurately, 'reconstructed', because rather than being based on a single principal manuscript and noting the main variants in the critical apparatus in relation to the tri - as the BHS does with the Leningrad Codex or the Cambridge edition of the LXX with the Vatican Codex - presents a text reconstructed word for word from the variants found in a vast number of manuscripts, following the various rules of textual criticism[^143].

It is also worth mentioning the *Novum Testamentum Graecum: Editio Critica Maior* (ECM), a critical edition of the New Testament prepared by the *Institut für Neutestamentliche Textforschung* (INTF) in Münster. It follows different methods to the Nestle-Aland edition and offers a greater amount of information. The edition is still incomplete, but has recently picked up momentum with the publication in 2021 of the volumes on Mark and the Acts of the Apostles. Prior to these, the volumes corresponding to the seven Catholic epistles had been published (egora.unimuenster.de/intf/veroef/ausgaben_en.shtml). In collaboration with the INTF, another group of scholars, The International Greek New Testament Project (IGNTP), is preparing the edition of the Gospel of John for the ECM. It is worth exploring the resources available on their website: www.igntp.org.

Another recent edition of the New Testament is that by Michael W. Holmes, *The Greek New Testament: SBL Edition*, Society of Biblical Literature, Atlanta 2010, available free online at sblgnt.com. It is not a critical edition in the strict sense; it presents a Greek text of the New Testament based on a synthesis of previous critical editions.

## 14. Inspiration in relation to the canon and the text

In the faith of the Church, the inspiration and canonicity of the books are regarded as two conceptually distinct yet, in fact, inseparable characteristics. On a theoretical level, it is interesting to consider the status of prophetic or apostolic books which, having been lost, do not belong to the canon, such as the letter that Saint Paul says he sent to the Laodiceans (cf. Col 4:16). Could it be said that such books are 'inspired, but not canonical'? If one of them were to be discovered today, should it be added to the canon? Should such a discovery come to pass, it seems logical to think that such a text is not strictly speaking inspired, because the Holy Spirit did not ensure that it was preserved within the Church. Furthermore, it does not seem necessary that everything written by a prophet or an apostle should contain revelation in an inspired manner.

In the section on the canon, when discussing the criteria for determining which books are canonical, we shall see that the recognition of certain books as sacred is identical to their recognition as inspired. Indeed, if one asks what criteria the Church used to recognise the inspiration of the books, the answer is identical to that regarding the criteria for recognising canonical status, namely the apostolic tradition, which the Church discerns by looking to her own life (cf. pp. 164-168 and pp. 201-202). In the light of tradition, the Magisterium has solemnly defined the biblical canon. Following the Council of Trent's declaration on this matter, there can be no further doubt as to which books are canonical and inspired (pp. 198-201). However, the same cannot be said regarding the precise content of these books, that is, regarding their text.

As we have seen, some books have been handed down in two or three different textual forms, such as Esther and Jeremiah. Faced with this diversity, it is natural to ask which of these forms is to be regarded as the inspired text. A similar problem arises with those books whose original has been partially lost, such as Sirach, or entirely lost, such as 1 Maccabees. Should we perhaps extend the charism of inspiration to the respective translators[^144]? 1

The Church has not taken an official position on these cases. No Pope or Council has specified exactly which texts are to be regarded as inspired. The only definition on this matter is that of Trent, which speaks of the books in their entirety 'with all their parts'. As we shall see (cf. p. 200), when speaking of the 'parts' of the books, the Council Fathers intended to confirm three passages from the Gospels, the inclusion of which in the text was disputed: Mk 16:9-20, Lk 22:43-44 and Jn 7:53-8, 11. Yet even in these cases, the Council of Trent did not specify the exact words comprising these passages. Nor did it say anything either about which languages were to be preferred, or about the problematic cases in the Old Testament just mentioned. Let us therefore recognise which books are canonical, whilst the canonicity and inspiration of individual words remain open to discussion on a case-by-case basis.

Ultimately, the theological problems linked to the transmission of the text in various forms are to be connected with the Church as the recipient of the revelation attested to and expressed in the books. According to a simplistic model, inspiration should concern only those words which the Holy Spirit suggested to the writer at the time the book was composed. However, it must be remembered that the moment of reception of the message is essential for communication. We can be certain, on the basis of historical evidence, that God did not intend for the Church to receive the books in a uniform and error-free form (this also touches on the divine and human nature of the Bible, which we discussed in the introduction). Therefore, inspiration must in some way extend to the various variants through which the biblical text has been handed down. The Holy Spirit has also made use of flawed manuscripts to ensure that the word of God resounds within the Church. It would certainly be arrogant to think that it is only in the modern era that we have succeeded to discover, thanks to textual criticism, the 'true texts' of the Bible, obscured for centuries by clumsy copyists.

It must be added, however, that this principled openness to textual diversity does not preclude the need to study the variants seriously and to select those that appear to be older and therefore closer to the original. With the resources available to us today, to neglect this work would be nothing short of laziness.

In the books mentioned above, the most reasonable response is to extend the influence of the Holy Spirit to the various forms in which they have come down to us. For example, both the Hebrew Book of Esther, on which the Vulgate is based, and the longer Greek version, read for centuries in the Byzantine Church, are to be regarded as inspired and canonical books. A representative example of this trend is the parallel presentation of two Italian translations of the Book of Esther in the CEI Bible: one taken from the Hebrew text, the other from the Greek. In the case of Jeremiah, both the short Greek version and the long Hebrew version should be regarded as inspired and canonical 1.

A similar solution can also be applied to the question of the inspiration of translations into other languages. In what sense are the Latin Vulgate, the CEI Bible, the New Jerusalem Bible and many other biblical translations-which we shall discuss in the following chapter-inspired? If it is meant that the translators received the charism of inspiration, it is clear that the answer is no. On the other hand, insofar as they present the biblical text-which is the fruit of inspiration-all translations-even nonCatholic ones-share in this inspiration to the extent that they faithfully render the meaning of the text.

## 15. Translations of the Bible

### 15.1. The main ancient versions

Ancient versions of the Scriptures are of great value to textual criticism, primarily because of their antiquity, but also because they generally tend to translate literally and thus enable the reconstruction of the text from which they derive.

#### 15.1.1. Non-Latin versions

The oldest of these versions is the translation of the Torah - and subsequently of other books into Greek, known as the Septuagint (LXX), which we discussed above (pp. 123-127). Also of nonChristian origin is the so-called Targum (plural targumim or targumin). The targumin are Aramaic translations of the Torah, the Prophets and almost all the Writings (there are no targumin of Ezra, Nehemiah or Daniel). They have a very distinctive style, being more akin to paraphrases than to proper translations[^145].

In the Christian tradition, biblical texts - particularly the Gospels - were translated into the languages of the various places where missionaries travelled, almost always using Greek as the source language. The principal ancient Christian translations of the Bible are the Syriac versions (among which the Peshitta stands out), the Armenian version - known as the 'queen of translations' for its quality and beauty -, the Coptic versions, the Gothic version, the Georgian version, the Ethiopian version and the Latin versions, the Vetus Latina and the Vulgate, which we shall discuss briefly[^146].

#### 15.1.2. The Latin versions

The term 'Vetus Latina' refers to the earliest translations of Scripture into Latin, the main characteristic of which is that they were based not on the Hebrew text but on the Greek text (which they generally sought to render slavishly[^147])!. The Vetus Latina, in its various forms, circulated mainly before the spread of St Jerome's version, which from the 5th century onwards began to gain the upper hand and eventually became established in Western Europe. The Vetus Latina was the Bible used by the early Latin Christian authors, such as Tertullian and Saint Cyprian of Carthage, right up to Saint Augustine. These were North African authors. In the Church of Rome, however, although Latin was spoken, Greek was still preferred as the literary language until the mid-4th century[^148].

To explain what the Latin version known as the Vulgate (Vg) is, we must briefly recount the life of its author, Saint Jerome. Born in Stridon (Dalmatia) around 145, Jerome went to study in Rome, where he was baptised in 166. He moved to Aquileia and then to Syria, where he lived as a hermit. There he was able to perfect his Greek and began to learn Hebrew. From 180 to 182 he was in Constantinople, where he met Gregory of Nazianzus and Gregory of Nyssa. In 182, accompanying Bishop Paulinus of Antioch, who had ordained him a priest, Jerome returned to Rome. He remained in Rome - perhaps living where the church of San Girolamo della Carità stands today - to work as secretary and adviser to Pope Saint Damasus. Damasus asked him to improve the Latin translation of the New Testament by comparing existing versions with the original Greek. However, the Pope died in 184 and Jerome - who in the meantime had made some enemies - was forced to leave Rome. In 186, he settled in Bethlehem, where he died in 419 or 420[^149].

From 190 onwards, Jerome began work on a translation of the Old Testament based on the original texts, in Hebrew and Aramaic, rather than on the Greek version (LXX), as he himself and previous translators of the Bible into Latin had done up to that point. As he liked to say, Jerome followed the 'hebraica veritas '. This translation, completed around 405, became widely circulated in the West: for this reason, in modern times it came to be known as the Vulgate[^150].

In the early Church, in fact, the Old Testament was read in Greek, not in Hebrew. After the first century, Hebrew and Aramaic had become virtually unknown languages amongst Christians, which should come as no surprise given the rapid spread of the Gospel amongst the pagans and bearing in mind, too, that, unfortunately, relations between Christians and Jews were generally not good[^151].

When Jerome's translation began to spread, many were surprised by his choice to translate from Hebrew rather than from Greek, as was customary. Among these, St Augustine stands out; in the year 194 or 195 (before 196, the year in which he became

(a bishop) wrote a letter to Jerome expressing his concerns. This led to an exchange of letters between the two on this and other topics, of great historical and doctrinal value. For our purposes, it suffices to note that Augustine eventually acknowledged, albeit with some reluctance, the validity of Jerome's decision to translate from the Hebrew, as he tells him in a letter written in 404 or 405:

> As for your translation, you have now convinced me of the purpose you set out to achieve in translating the Holy Scriptures from the Hebrew text, which was to highlight the words omitted or altered by the Jews[^152].

Alongside this highly commendable choice of the 'Hebraica veritas ', St Jerome made another that is highly questionable; indeed - as we can say in the light of subsequent history - certainly mistaken: he regarded as canonical only those books which the Jews accepted. We shall discuss this later, in the section devoted to the canon (cf. pp. 192-194).

From a textual point of view, the Vulgate is an important witness, above all, to the Hebrew and Aramaic texts of the Old Testament, because Jerome translated almost all the Old Testament books from manuscripts containing a text that was practically identical to the future Masoretic Text. The major exception is the Psalter, which Jerome translated three times. Firstly, he revised the ancient Latin version (the so-called 'Roman Psalter'). The Latin version of the Psalms found in the Vulgate (and which St Pius V introduced into the Roman Breviary) corresponds to the 'Gallican Psalter', which is not a translation of the Psalms from the Hebrew, but from the Greek of the Hexapla. Later, St Jerome produced a new translation of the Psalter, this time from the Hebrew[^153].

Except for the Psalms, therefore, the Vulgate is based on a Hebrew text with consonantal vowels very similar to the MT, translated almost always in a fairly literal manner (as in 1 Sam 13:1, cited on p. 116). For this reason, if in any instance the Vulgate coincides with the LXX and not with the MT, it is possible to conclude that it is in the current MT that the text has changed, whereas the Vulgate and the LXX independently attest to an older variant and are therefore closer to the original.

On occasion, the Vulgate reads the Hebrew consonantal text differently from how it would later be vocalised by the Masoretes. For example, the 'midwives of the Hebrews' in Ex 1:15 (LXX and Vulgate) are, according to the vocalisation of the Masoretic Text, 'Hebrew midwives'. In Jer 49:1, the Hebrew term מלכם (mlkm) is translated as Melchom in the Vulgate (Μελχομ in the LXX), as a proper name. In contrast, in the Masoretic Text, the word has been vocalised as םךַּמְלָ (malkam), meaning 'their king'.

### 15.2. Modern versions

#### 15.2.1. Introduction: Trent and the authenticity of the Vulgate

In the early centuries of the Church, the biblical text used by the vast majority of Christians was the Greek one, that is, the version of the LXX together with the New Testament. Even today, this remains the Bible of the Greek Church. In Latin-speaking churches, translations were used which, for the Old Testament, were based on the LXX (the Vetus Latina) and which, from the 5th century onwards, were gradually replaced by the Vulgate, as we have mentioned.

In the medieval West, the Vulgate was practically the only Bible that was read and circulated, although there were paraphrases or short translations into the languages spoken by the people. It is usually said that the first to translate Scripture into modern languages were the Protestants from the 16th century onwards. It is historically more accurate to say that they were the first to distribute on a massive scale the Bible translated into modern languages directly from the original languages. In previous centuries, there had been some important translations, such as the one into Old Bulgarian (also known as Old Slavonic or Paleoslavic) produced in the 9th century by Saints Cyril and Methodius, the 12th-century French translation, or, closer to Luther's time, the English translation produced by Wyclif[^154].

Luther's translation of the Bible into German (1545) is of enormous historical, religious and cultural significance. In English, the King James Version (KJV) of 1611, produced on the model of the Luther Bible, was equally significant. Both Luther's Bible and the KJV were for centuries 'the' Bible in their respective languages and contributed significantly to the literary development of German and English.

As regards translations within the Catholic Church, the Council of Trent did not prohibit translations from the original texts, but neither did it encourage them. A study of the proceedings of the council's discussions reveals that this silence was not accidental. Indeed, some council fathers wished to see all versions, except the Vulgate, prohibited. Others, however, felt it more appropriate to encourage the dissemination of the Bible amongst the faithful and therefore pressed for translations to be made into various languages. In the end, pro bono pacis, the Council chose to say nothing on the matter[^155].

At the same time, Trent recommended the Vulgate as the preferred text amongst the various Latin versions that existed at the time, as it was a doctrinally sound edition. Here are the Council's words, from the second decree on the Scriptures (8 April 1546):

> This most holy synod, considering that it will be of no small benefit to the Church of God to know clearly, amongst all the editions in circulation, which is the authentic edition of the sacred books, decrees and declares that the ancient edition of the Vulgate, approved by the Church itself through centuries of usage, is to be regarded as authentic in public lectures, in debates, in preaching and in exposition, and that no one, for any reason whatsoever, may presume to reject it (EB 61).

This recommendation, combined with the Council's silence on translations and certain highly restrictive decrees of the Holy Office, meant that when Catholics wished to translate the Bible, they took the Vulgate as their source text rather than the texts in the original languages[^156].

Surprisingly, this situation-perhaps understandable at the time of Trent for reasons of prudence-persisted for a full four centuries! Indeed, it was only with the encyclical *Divino Agente Spiritu* (1943) that the scope of the Tridentine decree on the Vulgate was clarified, and Catholics were encouraged to produce translations from the Hebrew and from the Greek. The authenticity of the Vulgate must be understood in a doctrinal sense and not in a critical sense, and therefore it cannot replace the original texts. Here are the words of Pius XII:

> That if the Council of Trent intended the Vulgate to be the Latin version 'which all should use as authentic', this concerns, first and foremost-as everyone knows-only the Latin Church and the use to be made of Scripture within it; moreover, there is no doubt that this in no way diminishes the authority and value of the original texts. In fact, the issue at that time was not the original texts of the Bible, but the Latin translations in circulation at the time; and amongst these, the Council itself rightly determined that preference should be given to the one which 'had received approval through its long-standing use over many centuries within the Church itself'. This pre-eminent authority-or, as it is commonly called, the authenticity of the Vulgate-was decreed by the Council not primarily on critical grounds, but rather on account of the legitimate use to which it had been put in the churches over the course of so many centuries; this use demonstrates that, in the sense in which the Church understood and understands it, it is entirely free from error in all matters pertaining to faith and morals. It is from this immunity, to which the Church bears witness and which it confirms, that it follows that in debates, lectures and sermons the Vulgate may be quoted with complete confidence and without any risk of error. Therefore, that authenticity must be described not primarily as critical, but rather as juridical. Consequently, the authority which the Vulgate holds in matters of doctrine in no way prevents - indeed, in our day it almost demands - that that same doctrine be tested and confirmed by means of the original texts, and that, furthermore, recourse be made to those very texts to unpack and explain the true meaning of the Divine Scriptures ever more clearly (EB 549).

Following this clarification, all Catholics began to translate directly from the texts in the original languages. The first fruit of these initiatives is the *Bible de Jérusalem*, a French translation which we shall discuss on p. 141.

Dei Verbum also endorses this idea and encourages the dissemination of translations based on the original texts. It also mentions the possibility of preparing them in collaboration with Christians who are not in full communion with the Church:

> It is essential that the faithful have wide access to Sacred Scripture. For this reason, from the very beginning the Church adopted the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament known as the Septuagint, and has always held in high esteem the other Eastern versions and the Latin versions, particularly the one known as the Vulgate. Since, however, the Word of God must be available to all at all times, the Church ensures, with maternal care, that appropriate and accurate translations are produced in the various languages, preferably from the original texts of the sacred books. If, for reasons of expediency and with the consent of the Church's authority, these are produced in collaboration with our separated brethren, they may be used by all Christians (DV22).

#### 15.2.2. The Sixtus-Clementine and the Neovulgate (NVg)

As we have just seen, in the disciplinary decree following the dogmatic one that defined the canon, the Council of Trent recommended the use of the Latin Vulgate. This recommendation also entailed the need for a reliable edition of it.

> In the final form of the decree approved at Trent concerning the Vulgate, there is no further mention of the corrected edition to be prepared on the Pope's initiative. However, in the letter written by the legates to Cardinal Farnese on 26 April 1546, explicit mention is made of this request from the Council addressed to the Pope: 'that he might deign, with all haste, to have our Latin edition corrected first, and then also the Greek and Hebrew editions'. Work on the critical edition of the Vulgate[^157] began in Rome as early as 1546. However, it was not until the pontificate of Pius IV, in 1559, that a commission was established, of which Cardinal Guglielmo Sirleto became a member in 1563. By the close of the Council of Trent in 1561, the corrected edition of the Vulgate was still not ready. In 1569, Pius V reconfirmed the commission of scholars and cardinals tasked with gathering the material and revising the Latin text on the basis of critical evidence.

> It was only with the election to the papacy of Cardinal Felice Peretti, who took the name Sixtus V in 1575, that the first Roman edition of the Vulgate 1 was produced.

Indeed, on 1 April 1590, Sixtus V published an edition, known as the 'Vulgata Sistina', which had a very short lifespan. As the edition was unsatisfactory-because the Pope had, on his own initiative, introduced certain amendments to the work carried out by the commission of cardinals that had prepared it-it was withdrawn immediately after the death of Sixtus V in August 1590. In 1592, Clement VIII published a new edition, known as the Vulgata Clementina or Sixtus-Clementina. It became 'the Bible' for Catholics for centuries to come. It was even used as the basis for certain translations, such as the Douay-Reims (1582-1609) in English or that of Felipe Scío (1791-1793) in Spanish.

Today, the Church's official text for the Latin-language liturgy is the Neovulgate (Nova Vulgata Editio). It has therefore replaced the Sixtus-Clementine Vulgate in the Mass readings and in the psalms and biblical readings of the Liturgy of the Hours. However, since, following the liturgical reform of 1970, the Latin liturgy now uses biblical texts in the languages spoken in each locality rather than in Latin, it is clear that the Nova Vulgata does not go- attain a level of dissemination and importance comparable to that of the Sixtus-Clementine edition.

Work on the Neovulgata began in 1965. Saint John Paul II promulgated it as the editio typica on 25 April 1989 with the Apostolic Constitution Scripturae Thesaurus (EB 1984-1985). In 1986, the editio typica altera was published: a second edition, containing some corrections. The Neovulgata was produced according to two criteria: to take account of advances in textual criticism and to endeavour to preserve the text and style of the ancient Vulgate as far as possible. Generally speaking, the Neovulgata has retained a text similar to the Vulgate, but in some cases there are significant differences. For some examples, see below, p. 101.

#### 15.2.3. Some modern Bibles worth knowing

Below we shall mention some important modern translations of the Bible, without claiming to offer a comprehensive overview. Every student is advised to research the versions available in their own language, particularly the one approved by their respective bishops' conference for liturgical use.

In Italian, there is a sort of textual 'monopoly' within the Catholic sphere. The CEI version is not only the official liturgical text, but is also, in practice, almost the only version of the Bible read by the faithful, which has both advantages (it is a factor of unity) and disadvantages (there is a reliance on a single version). Work on the CEI Bible began after the Second Vatican Council, in 1966. The first edition was published in 1971. A second edition, with minor revisions, followed in 1974. Work on the third edition began in 1988; it was published in 2008 and features significant changes[^158].

Among modern versions in other languages, the Bible de Jérusalem (BJer) stands out; this is a French translation produced in the 1940s and 1950s by a group of scholars coordinated by the Dominicans of Les Éditions du Cerf and the École Biblique et Archéologique Française in Jerusalem. The first edition of the BJer was completed in 1956. The second dates from 1973. There is a third edition, with significant changes, from 1998[^159]. There will be no fourth, as the Dominicans at the École are working on a pro- published under a new title: The Bible in its Traditions. For further information, see www.bibest.org.

There is a Spanish edition of the BJer, the *Biblia de Jerusalén*, which reproduces the introductions and notes from the French edition, but contains a translation based on the original languages (which generally follows the choices made by the French translators). Something similar applies to the English versions, The Jerusalem Bible and The New Jerusalem Bible. In Italian, however, the name 'Bibbia di Gerusalemme' is given to an edition containing the CEI biblical text with the introductions and footnotes from the Bible de Jérusalem.

Still in French, mention should be made of the TOB Bible (Traduction Œcuménique de la Bible), the result of collaboration between Catholics and Protestants, published in 1975-1976 by the Société biblique française and Cerf. Orthodox exegetes also contributed to the 2010 edition.

The main English translations of the Bible are the aforementioned KJV of 1611, the Revised Standard Version (RSV, 1952) and the New Revised Standard Version (NRSV, 1990):

> The RSV is the result of discussions that began in 1928, following which a Standard Bible Committee was appointed by the International Council on Religious Education. The committee decided to revise the poorly received American Standard Version within the tradition of the King James Version. Work began in 1936. The New Testament was published in 1946, and the complete Bible on 10 September 1952.

> NRSV. Work began on a revision of the RSV in 1974 and was completed in 1990. It aimed for greater accuracy, improved clarity, more intelligible or more natural English, the elimination of ambiguity[^160], the elimination of ambiguity in oral reading[^161], better euphony!, and the elimination of some masculine references[^163].

In Spanish, there are a great many translations of the Bible. In addition to the Jerusalem Bible, we might mention the Latin American Bible and the study Bible *Dios habla*

today (1st ed. 1966) by the United Bible Societies, *La Biblia del pueblo de Dios*, and the translation by Luis Alonso Schökel, Juan Mateos and others, known as the*Nueva Biblia Española* (1975), which, with some modifications, was the version used in the liturgy in Spain until recently. At the end of 2010, the Spanish Episcopal Conference presented an official version, intended for liturgical use, entitled *Sagrada Escritura. Versión oficial de la Conferencia Episcopal Española*.

The *Biblia de Navarra* - prepared by the Faculty of Theology at the University of Navarra at the request of Saint Josemaría Escrivá - has been published in several editions. It aims to offer a faithful, accessible and elegant Spanish version.

In addition, there is a recent Latin American Spanish version, produced under the guidance of CELAM, called the *Biblia de la Iglesia en América* (BIA) 1.

In German, in addition to the aforementioned Luther Bible, the Einheitsübersetzung (1980) is significant. The full title reflects the ecumenical nature of part of this version (the Psalms and the New Testament): *Die Bibel: Einheitsübersetzung der Heiligen Schrift*, published on behalf of the Bishops of Germany; for the Psalms and the New Testament, also on behalf of the Council of the Evangelical Church in Germany and the German Bible Society.

# PART III CANON

to develop within the Church with the assistance of the Holy Spirit (…)

It is this Tradition that makes known to the Church the entire canon of sacred books.

This Tradition of apostolic origin continues Dei Verbum, no. 8.

The list of books that make up Sacred Scripture is called the 'biblical canon'. We made an initial exploration of the canon at the start of the course, listing its books (cf. pp.

38-45). We must now explain when, how and why those works were recognised as canonical, whilst other similar writings were left out or were directly excluded. Indeed, studying the canon requires not only knowing which books are biblical, but also exploring the reasons that led the Church to establish the canon, that is, to distinguish between canonical and non-canonical books.

## 16. An Introduction to the Problem of the Biblical Canon

In order to present the issue of the legitimacy of a closed and definitive collection of books proposed by the Church in an adequate manner, it is necessary to explain the meaning of certain fundamental terms, such as 'canon', 'canonical' and 'apocryphal'.

### 16.1. Some terminological clarifications

Canon and canonical. The Greek word κανών originally denoted a normative standard, a rule to which one was required to conform, for example when creating a sculpture (artistic canon) or behaving properly (ethical canon). In the early centuries of Christianity, people also began to speak of a canon or rule of faith, that is, those fundamental assertions which a Christian must believe in order to be called such.

The application of this term to the list of biblical books began in the 4th century AD within the Christian context, when a distinction was sought between normative or 'canonical' books - in the sense that they correspond to the rule or canon of faith - and those that are not[^165].

It is useful to distinguish between 'canon' in the active sense (a rule to be followed) and 'canon' in the passive sense (everything that conforms to the rule and is therefore 'canonical'). We are interested in the canon in both senses, active and passive, because Sacred Scripture is not merely a list of orthodox books, but, as the Word of God, is the rule of faith for the Church. In this part of the course, we shall focus on the canon in the passive sense, as the list of books recognised by the Church as sacred and inspired. However, it is worth bearing in mind that this sense is derived from the active sense of the canon, which is more important[^166].

Apocrypha. This term can have three different meanings depending on the context, which must be understood to avoid misunderstandings.

- Apocryphal = Hidden. Originally, 'apocryphal' (ἀπόκρυφος) meant 'hidden', 'occult' (from the verb ἀποκρύπτω, 'to hide', 'to cover'). When applied to a philosophical or religious doctrine, the term refers to a valuable teaching, difficult to acquire and therefore inaccessible to most people, but which is revealed to a select few. Consequently, these 'chosen' individuals possess a teaching superior to that available to the general public.

- Apocryphal = Heretical. The Church has never accepted that there was a teaching of Jesus distinct from the public one, accessible to all. There might have been a fuller understanding, gained by following Jesus more closely, but not one that could ever contradict the teaching of Jesus openly transmitted within the Church-that is, amongst the people of God who have received Jesus Christ in faith and who openly profess (homologein) that faith. Hence, the group (hairesis) that adheres to an apocryphal-that is, hidden-teaching which contradicts the public teaching of the Church is regarded by the Church as heretical. By extension, therefore, in the Christian context, the word 'apocryphal'

> it takes on a new meaning and becomes synonymous with 'heretical', 'unorthodox' and therefore 'inauthentic[^167]' 1.

- !) Apocryphal = Non-canonical. A third meaning of the word arises from its application to books. Since the apocryphal books-in the sense of heretical-are not canonical, by extension all books that do not form part of the biblical canon came to be called 'apocryphal'. This is at least how St Jerome uses the term (as we shall see on p.

- When applied to books, 'apocryphal' has become synonymous with non-canonical or extra-canonical, without any negative connotation. That is to say, not all non-canonical books are heretical; many 'apocryphal' books do not contradict the doctrine of the Church, such as Psalm 151 or the Shepherd of Hermas[^168].

Protocanonical and deuterocanonical. These are related terms. In his *Bibliotheca Sancta* (1566), Sixtus of Siena referred to as 'deuterocanonical' those books which, in his view, were included in the canon only after some doubt, whilst those that have always and everywhere been accepted by everyone[^169] would be 'protocanonical'.

Today, many recognise that this terminology is inaccurate and ambiguous. Nevertheless, it is necessary to be familiar with it, as it is still used by some authors. Later on, after discussing Luther's position on the canon (pp. 194-197), we shall see why Sixtus distinguished between these two groups of books.

### 16.2. The discovery of certain apocryphal books and the questioning of the canon

Among the topics covered by the IGSS, the one most widely written about and debated today is the canon. Whether due to the discovery of certain ancient books or to theological or ideological motivations, some authors have proposed changing the biblical canon-that is, removing certain books, adding others, or abandoning the very notion the very concept of the canon. Other scholars - more moderate ones - believe that the distinctions between canonical and non-canonical books should be softened, as they consider that the determination of the biblical canon is subject to factors that are more or less arbitrary or contingent. The debate is particularly lively amongst Protestant authors, but it also concerns Catholics[^170].

Before studying the history of the formation of the canon, it is useful to understand why the very concept of the canon as a closed and definitive list of sacred books is being questioned today. We can identify the following three factors in the 'crisis of the canon':

- the discovery (or rediscovery) of apocryphal books in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries has prompted reflection on the legitimacy of the traditional canon. In the nineteenth century, a number of manuscripts of the Book of Jubilees and 1 Enoch were brought from Ethiopia to Europe; these were apocryphal books whose existence was known, but of which almost all traces had been lost. In 1896, several medieval Jewish manuscripts came to light in the Cairo Genizah, including the so-called 'Damascus Document' and Sirach, as mentioned above (cf. p. 47). In the mid-twentieth century, hundreds of books were found around the Dead Sea, including biblical, apocryphal and other texts (Qumran and other sites, cf. no. 1, p. 12). In recent years, a Coptic library was also discovered at Nag Hammadi, in Egypt, in which the Gospel of Thomas stands out; it is regarded by some as the 'fifth gospel' due to its value in reconstructing the authentic words of Jesus (we shall discuss this gospel on pp. 148-152[^171]) 2.

- In the case of the New Testament canon, one must take into account the strong influence of certain authors, particularly Walter Bauer and his book *Orthodoxy and Heresy in Early Christianity*, published in 19343. In summary, Bauer proposes that during the first two centuries, various currents coexisted within Christianity, one of which subsequently prevailed, claiming for itself the status of being the original and orthodox tradition, whilst the others were regarded as heretical deviations. The current

> The biblical canon is said to have resulted from the dominance of this group, tied to Rome, over the others. Bauer's thesis, which contains several valid points but also some very profound problems, gained widespread acceptance in the 1970s thanks to authors such as Robert Kraft (editor of the English translation of the book), James Robinson (a scholar of the Nag Hammadi texts), Helmut Köster and, more recently, Bart Ehrman[^172].

- Finally, within postmodern thinking, the canon is questioned as a closed and definitive list of books, because all authority is viewed with suspicion and plurality is generally preferred to uniformity. Consequently, historical reconstructions such as Bauer's have not only been successful in academic circles but have also gained popularity amongst the general public. For example, the novel *The Da Vinci Code* asserts that the biblical canon was the result of an authoritarian and arbitrary imposition by Emperor Constantine (and is therefore open to revision).

In what follows, we must trace the history of the canon's formation in order to respond to these challenges, which primarily concern the New Testament. In reality, the determination of the canon within the Church did not follow two parallel paths-one for each Testament-but was a single process. Nevertheless, for didactic reasons, we shall study the formation of the Old Testament and New Testament canons separately. And, given its greater significance, we shall first discuss the formation of the New Testament canon and then,, that of the Old Testament canon.

### 16.3. The Gospel of Thomas

In our study of the New Testament canon, we shall outline the historical process that led to the formation of the collection of 27 books, with particular emphasis on the four Gospels (pp. 151-164). We shall now examine one of the books that was not included in the canon, the Gospel of Thomas, also known as the 'Gnostic Gospel of Thomas' or the 'Coptic Gospel of Thomas', so as not to confuse it with the 'Infancy Gospel of Thomas', another apocryphal text.

Before addressing the question of why the Church did not accept the Gospel of Thomas as canonical, it is worth providing some basic information Regarding this text. The original language appears to be Greek. The date of composition is a matter of debate, but it is generally placed in the middle of the second century[^173]. The work is a collection of sayings of Jesus in dialogue with his disciples, without a narrative framework: there is no unified narrative, and no mention is made of his passion, death and resurrection.

In 1897 and 1903, some Greek fragments containing sayings of Jesus were discovered at Oxyrhynchus, in Egypt. These fragments were identified as belonging to the Gospel of Thomas half a century later. In fact, in 1945 at Nag Hammadi, Egypt, the complete text of this gospel was found in Coptic, along with many other manuscripts in the same language[^174]. Until these discoveries, we were aware of the existence of a 'Gospel according to Thomas' only through references to it by certain ancient authors, such as Origen and Cyril of Jerusalem (see the texts cited on p. 161 and p. 163).

It is undoubtedly one of the most interesting apocryphal gospels, as it contains sayings of Jesus that are similar or identical to those handed down in the canonical gospels, particularly the Synoptic Gospels. For example, in no. 10 we read: 'I have cast fire upon the world, and I will keep it until it blazes up[^175]'!, a phrase similar to that found in Luke 12:49. In no. 54 we read: 'Blessed are the poor, for yours is the kingdom of heaven', a beatitude identical to Luke 6:20. There are also some phrases that do not appear in any of the canonical Gospels, but which at first glance might be attributed to Jesus, such as the one in no. 25: 'Love your brother as your own soul. Guard him as the apple of your eye'.

We also find other expressions attributed to Jesus which strike us as deeply disconcerting, because they are so far removed from the image of Jesus conveyed by the New Testament, such as the one reported in no. 50:

> Jesus said: 'If they ask you, 'Where do you come from?', you shall answer, 'We have come from the light, from the place where the light came into being of its own accord; it remained and manifested itself in their image.' If they ask you, 'Who are you?', you shall answer, 'We are his children; we are the chosen ones of the living Father.'' If they ask you, 'What is the sign of your Father that is within you?', you shall reply, 'It is movement and rest.'

The dialogue that concludes the book also strikes us as decidedly strange:

> Simon Peter said to them: 'Mary must leave us! For women are not worthy of life.' Jesus said: 'Behold, I will guide her so as to make her a man, so that she may become a living spirit like us men. For every woman who becomes a man will enter the kingdom of heaven' (no. 114).

Some recent authors consider the Gospel of Thomas to be 'the fifth gospel', as they believe it has a historical and theological value comparable to that of the four canonical gospels. This claim seems exaggerated for various reasons which would take too long to set out here. For now, we shall focus on just one point: why, in the early centuries of the Church, this Gospel was never a serious 'contender' for inclusion in the canon.

Craig Evans recounts that, when a student asked him why the Church had accepted only the Gospels of Matthew, Mark, Luke and John, excluding the others, he simply replied: 'Read them and you will see[^176] ' 1. In the case of the Gospel of Thomas, this response is entirely valid. Indeed, if one reads this text from beginning to end, it is easy to realise that it is not compatible with the faith in Jesus handed down by the apostles. We shall try to demonstrate this briefly with a few quotations, which will also help to give an idea of the tone of this Gospel.

Let us begin with the title: 'These are the hidden sayings spoken by Jesus, the Living One, and written down by Didymus Judas Thomas'. As well as the name of the writer, the adjective applied to Jesus' words contained in the text arouses curiosity: they are 'hidden' or secret. In verse 1, we find a scene that explains why this Gospel presents itself as a collection of Jesus' hidden words transcribed by Thomas:

> Jesus said to his disciples: 'Give me a comparison; tell me whom I resemble.' Simon Peter replied: 'You are like a righteous angel.' Matthew replied: 'Teacher, you are like a philosopher.' Thomas replied: 'Teacher, my mouth is utterly unable to say whom you resemble.' 'Jesus said to him: 'I am not your teacher, for you have drunk and become intoxicated from the gurgling spring that I have measured[^177].' And he took him aside and spoke three words to him. When Thomas returned to his companions, they asked him: 'What did Jesus say to you?' - Thomas replied: 'If I were to tell you one of the words he spoke to me, you would reach for stones to stone me, and fire would come forth from the stones and burn you.'

The scene is similar to Peter's confession at Caesarea Philippi, as recounted by the three Synoptic Gospels (Mt 16:13-23; Mk 8:27-33; Lk 9:18-22). As in those accounts, here too different opinions are expressed regarding the identity of Jesus, and it is the last one that receives his approval. Unlike the Synoptic Gospels, however, it is not Peter's answer but Thomas's that is confirmed. Jesus takes Thomas aside - and not Peter by Jesus - and speaks three words to him, which he cannot share with the others because they are too profound.

The reference to the secret or hidden nature of Jesus' teachings, known only to a few, fits perfectly within the Mediterranean cultural context of the Greco-Roman era, where religious, philosophical or mystical groups presented themselves as custodians of doctrines to which only a select few individuals had access. Such esoteric and elitist legitimisation was so common that it is hardly surprising that certain groups of this kind sought to present themselves as disciples of Jesus, who was said to have revealed the deepest truths only to them. It is precisely this characteristic of the Gospel of Thomas that most clearly reveals its divergence from the teaching received by the apostles. The Church speaks of 'mysteries' as doctrines that transcend the capacity of human understanding, not as mysteries that only certain people have the right to know.

Furthermore, this passage immediately highlights a clear distinction between Thomas and the other disciples[^178]. Neither Peter nor Matthew nor the others received the same teaching as Thomas. 'Every stage of this narrative construction aims to set Thomas apart from the other disciples ' (1). Therefore, if we say that the Gospel of Thomas is not compatible with the apostolic tradition, we are not projecting our own prejudice onto the text; rather, it is the text itself that seeks to legitimise itself as a testimony to an exclusive teaching, to a doctrine of Jesus that only Thomas received. One cannot follow both Peter and Thomas; one must choose one or the other. In the canonical Gospels, on the other hand, we find a recognition of the legitimacy of other traditions, as in John 21 concerning Peter or in Mark 16:9-20 concerning the other three Gospels.

The differences between the Gospel of Thomas and the traditions accepted by the mainstream Church are not limited to esotericism, but also relate to certain doctrinal contents, such as the concept of the kingdom of God. In this gospel, the kingdom proclaimed by Jesus is associated with selfknowledge (gnosis).

> Jesus said: 'If those who lead you say to you, 'Behold, the Kingdom of God is in heaven!', then the birds of the sky will precede you. If they say to you, 'It is in the sea!', then the fish of the sea will precede you. The Kingdom, however, is within you and without you. When you know yourselves, then you will be known, and you will know that you are the children of the living Father. But if you do not know yourselves, then you will be in poverty, and you will be poverty (no. 13).

Furthermore, the kingdom proclaimed by this Jesus is not directed towards an eschatological fulfilment, but is immanent in the world.

> The disciples asked him: 'On what day will the kingdom come?' (Jesus replied:) 'It will not come whilst people are expecting it. They will not say: 'Look, it is here!' or 'Look, it is there!' Rather, the Father's kingdom is spread throughout the whole earth, and people do not see it (no. 113).

We may also note the presence of a vision of life linked to knowledge rather than to the resurrection of Jesus (which, moreover, is never mentioned in this Gospel):

> He said: - 'Whoever discovers the meaning of these words will not taste death' (no. 1).

> 'He who knows everything, but not himself, knows nothing' (no. 67).

> 'Whoever drinks from my mouth will become like me, and I myself will become like him, and the hidden things will be revealed to him' (no. 108).

Finally, another important difference from the figure of Jesus as presented in the canonical gospels is the significance of the revelation received by Israel, which is rejected by the 'Thomistic' Jesus:

> His disciples asked him: 'In Israel, twenty-four prophets spoke, and they all spoke through you [or 'about you[^179]'] 1.' He replied to them: 'You have the Living One before you, and yet you speak of those who are dead?' (no. 52).

## 17. The New Testament Canon

For us, 21st-century Catholics, all the books of Scripture are recognised as the word of God. However, when studying the history of the formation of the canon, one must avoid projecting this conception backwards in time. One must confine oneself to examining what the ancient sources say, without anachronisms.

In determining the New Testament canon, there is no scope for differences or comparisons with Jewish scriptures. For this reason, and for others too, there are no discrepancies in the New Testament canon amongst the major Christian churches or denominations: Catholics, Orthodox and Protestants all have the same 27 books.

On a theological level, however, there is much debate today regarding the New Testament canon. As we have said, some authors criticise the arbitrary nature of the decisions to include or exclude certain books, or question the Church's authority to define the canon. At the heart of the debate lie the criteria used to determine whether a book is canonical, foremost among which is its connection to the apostles, or 'apostolicity'. Before explaining these issues, it is essential to understand the most significant historical facts regarding the formation of the New Testament.

### 17.1. Key historical evidence regarding the formation of the New Testament canon

Broadly speaking, we can say that the books comprising the New Testament were written over a period of about one hundred years, roughly between the 5th and 15th centuries AD. The process of composing the New Testament therefore spanned a much shorter period than that of the Old Testament, which lasted several centuries.

On the other hand, one must bear in mind the rapid geographical expansion of the Church. The books of the New Testament were not written in a single location. The Gospel of Mark was probably written in Rome; that of Matthew perhaps in Antioch (Syria); whilst Paul was an itinerant missionary and wrote his letters from various locations. The recipients were also diverse: some writings are addressed to individuals (Luke-Acts, Philemon, the Pastoral Epistles); many letters are addressed to local churches: to the Romans, the Corinthians, etc. By contrast, there are few writings that from the outset were intended for a deliberately and explicitly broad audience, such as the Letter of James, which is addressed 'to the twelve tribes scattered throughout the world' (1:1).

At the same time, it is logical that the apostolic writings soon began to circulate amongst the communities. The first recipients were aware of the value of the texts in their possession.

In this regard, it is interesting to note the closing greeting of the Letter to the Colossians:

> Greet the brothers and sisters in Laodicea, Nympha and the church that meets in her home. And when this letter has been read among you, see to it that it is also read in the church at Laodicea, and that you too read the letter sent to the Laodiceans (Col 4:15-16[^180]) 1.

The text shows that Paul already anticipated his writings being disseminated beyond their immediate recipients. This marks the beginning of a process that would culminate in the 4th and 5th centuries with the virtually universal recognition of the 27 books that today form the New Testament.

In broad terms, we may assume that in the early decades of the Church's existence it was physically impossible for all communities to possess the same books. Each local church had some of them, either because they had been written for it, or because it had received them from other communities. In the first two centuries, one cannot speak of a 'New Te ' as we know it today.

#### 17.1.1. In the New Testament itself

In addition to the passage just quoted (Col 4:15-16), the New Testament contains two other significant testimonies concerning the process of compiling a new collection of Scriptures: one from Paul, 1 Tim 5:17-18, and one from Peter, 2 Pet 3:15-16.

In 1 Tim 5:17-18, Deut 25:4 is brought together with a saying of Jesus:

> Elders who fulfil their duties well are to be considered worthy of double honour, especially those who labour in preaching and teaching. For Scripture says: 'You shall not muzzle an ox whilst it is threshing' and: 'The labourer is worthy of his wages [ἄξιος ó έργáτц@ τοũ µισθoũ αúτοũ].'

The second phrase appears in exactly the same words only in Luke 10:7, spoken by Jesus 1. In the text of 1 Timothy, the phrase is not attributed to Jesus. But if it were simply a popular proverb, it would make no sense to place it on a par with a quotation from the Torah. We do not know whether St Paul is quoting the Gospel of Luke or whether he took the phrase from a written collection of Jesus' sayings. But it is clear that it cannot come from an oral tradition, because in that case it would not be called 'Scripture'. A text containing the words of Jesus is regarded as 'Scripture', on a par with a text from the Torah. This bears witness to the fact that a text from the New Covenant is treated in the same way as one from the Old.

In 2 Peter 3:15-16, Paul's letters are equated with the other Scriptures:

> Consider the patience of our Lord as salvation: this is what our most beloved brother Paul has also written to you, according to the wisdom given to him, as in all his letters, in which he speaks of these matters. In them there are some points that are hard to understand, which the ignorant and the unstable twist to their own destruction, just as they do with the other Scriptures.

This time we are faced with a more substantial testimony: it refers to 'all the letters' of Paul, which are explicitly regarded as part of the 'Scriptures', despite the fact that some interpret them incorrectly. We do not know whether the author of 2 Peter was familiar with the entire Pauline corpus or only a part of it. The text's significance for the history of the canon lies in its testimony to the process by which the apostolic writings came to be equated with the Scriptures of Israel.

Both accounts are in their early stages. In what follows, we must examine how, little by little, the New Testament writings[^184] spread and came to be regarded as sacred and equated with the ancient Scriptures. Unfortunately, we are unable to examine all the relevant accounts. We do not have time to study, for example, the writings of the so-called 'Apostolic Fathers' - the letters of Saint Ignatius of Antioch, the Didache, the letters of Saint Polycarp, the First Epistle of Saint Clement of Rome (1 Clem), the Epistle of Pseudo-Barnabas (Ps-Barn), the Shepherd of Hermas - which are of interest both for their use of Scripture and because some of their writings were later occasionally cited as Scripture[^182].

#### 17.1.2. Marcion

As is the case with many ancient authors, we know of Marcion's ideas indirectly, through the works of those who opposed him. In this instance, we rely primarily on Saint Irenaeus's *Adversus Haereses* and Tertullian's *Adversus Marcionem*.

Having arrived in Rome from Pontus (a region in northern Asia Minor) around AD 140, Marcion preached a doctrine amongst Christians that distinguished between the God of the Jews-the Creator, who imposes the law upon mankind-and a good and superior God, the Father of Jesus Christ, who calls for love. The first disciples-the Twelve Apostles-failed to understand Jesus' message, and so God had to reveal the Gospel anew, this time to Paul alone[^183].

Marcion rejected the entire Old Testament, whilst of the New Testament he accepted only ten of Paul's epistles - in the following order: Galatians, 1-2 Corinthians, Romans, 1-2 Thessalonians, 'Laodiceans' (probably-

(i.e. Ephesians), Colossians, Philippians and Philemon - and the Gospel of Luke. Marcion himself prepared an edition of these texts to purge them of what he considered to be interpolations added by the Judaizers. We do not know whether Marcion was familiar with other New Testament writings 1.

Marcion was successful, attracting numerous disciples not only in Rome but also in other cities. His importance for the history of the canon lies above all in the fact that his 'Bible' provoked a reaction from the Church - first through Saint Irenaeus, then through Tertullian - which was compelled to clarify its position regarding the Scriptures rejected by Marcion. However, today we have moved away from Harnack's thesis, which saw Marcion's position as the decisive factor in the formation of the canon: according to him, the Church would have defined it solely as a reaction to the Marcionite canon[^185]. In reality, neither was Marcion so decisive, nor did the Church define the New Testament canon in the second century, but later, as we shall see.

#### 17.1.3. St Justin

In the First Apology, written between 151 and 155, probably in Rome, at the very time when Marcion was preaching his doctrines (cf. First Apology 26 and 58), Justin explains the Christian faith to the pagans. Speaking of the Sunday Eucharistic celebration, he puts it this way:

> And on the day known as the Day of the Sun, we all gather in one place from the city and the countryside, forming an assembly, and the accounts of the Apostles[^186] or the writings of the prophets are read, as long as there is time; then, when the reader has finished, the presider delivers a sermon, admonishing and exhorting us to imitate these good things (First Apology, no. 67).

The text reveals the practical equivalence between the Gospels and the ancient Scriptures, both of which are read in a public and sacred context (during the Sunday Eucharist) and from which teachings for life are drawn (homily).

Another point of interest is to ascertain which Gospels Justin is referring to. He gives no names, nor does he say how many there are, but the plural - 'the memoirs of the Apostles' - seems to indicate that he was familiar with more than one. Indeed, on examining his works as a whole, it is clear that Justin read Matthew and Luke[^187], and probably Mark as well (cf. Trif. 106,3), whilst it is a matter of debate whether or not he was familiar with John[^188].

Finally, we must add two significant observations regarding Justin and the books of the New Testament:

- It is extremely surprising that Justin never makes any reference to St Paul. Does this simply mean that he was unfamiliar with his letters, or that he had no need of them? Or perhaps he did not trust Paul, as he was a point of reference for Marcion?

- In the First Apology, he speaks of the Book of Revelation as one of 'our writings' and attributes to it the authority of Christ himself!. This testimony is historically significant, because the canonicity of the Book of Revelation would later be hotly debated.

#### 17.1.4. Tatian's Diatessaron

Tatian, a disciple of St Justin, composed a work around the year 160 called the Diatessaron[^189], which means 'through the four'. It is an account of the life of Jesus that synthesises the four Gospels. The Diatessaron enjoyed considerable success, particularly in Syria, where it was used in the liturgy until the 5th century. During Mass, they did not read the canonical Gospels, but the Diatessaron! 4

As regards the history of the formation of the canon, Tatian's work testifies, on the one hand, that he knew and accepted the four Gospels, and, on the other, that he did not regard them as sacrosanct - a characteristic typically associated with books considered sacred.

To understand this last point, one might give a somewhat crude but eloquent example: if a Jewish author of Tatian's time had decided to summarise the five books of the Torah into a single work, it would be unthinkable that this work would be accepted on the same level as the Pentateuch, nor that it would be used in synagogue readings in place of the Torah.

#### 17.1.5. Saint Irenaeus and the Fourfold Gospel

Saint Irenaeus of Lyon (c. 100-200) wrote around 190 (a few years after Tatian, though Irenaeus was not familiar with the Diatessaron). Although he served as a bishop in the West, he was born and raised in the East, in Asia Minor. This fact is significant, as his testimony reflects an understanding of how the faith was lived out in different places.

Irenaeus not only affirms the existence of four Gospels, but emphasises that there are only four, in response both to those who did not accept all four and to those who added others.

In *Adv. Haer.* III,9-11, Irenaeus speaks of the testimonies concerning God found in the Gospels of Matthew, Luke, Mark and John (in that order) and adds:

> Moreover, the Gospels cannot be either more or fewer than these. For just as there are four regions of the world in which we live, and four winds blowing across the whole earth, and the Church is spread throughout the whole earth, and the Gospel and the Spirit of life are the pillar and support of the Church, so it is natural that the Church should have four pillars, which blow incorruptibility from all sides and give life to mankind. It is therefore clear that the Word, the Creator of the universe, who sits above the Cherubim and sustains all things, having revealed himself to mankind, has given us a fourfold Gospel, yet sustained by a single Spirit[^190].

Irenaeus then compares the four evangelists to the four living creatures of Ezekiel 1 and Revelation 4: one like a lion, another like a calf, the third with a human face, and the fourth like an eagle.

It is obvious that Irenaeus's arguments in favour of the number four - the winds, the cherubim, the living creatures - carry no weight as proof. The historical value of his testimony lies in demonstrating his firm conviction that not all writings concerning Jesus enjoy the same status within the Church, even though they bear the name 'Gospels', but only four of them do. Indeed, Saint Irenaeus does not merely state that the four Gospels must be accepted; he also asserts that no other authentic ones exist. It is rightly said that with Irenaeus begins the process of establishing a closed canon of New Testament writings. The supreme norm is Christ as preached by the apostles and their successors, but to this rule is added the fact that the Church possesses four and only four Gospels.

After quoting the opening words of each of the four Gospels, Irenaeus criticises the claims of certain groups who rejected any of the four or sought to introduce others into the Church:

> Those who reject the correct understanding of the Gospel, introducing more or fewer forms of the Gospel than those we have mentioned, are vain, ignorant and, moreover, reckless. Some do so to make people believe they have discovered more of the truth; others to reject God's economy. Marcion, rejecting the whole of the Gospel-or rather, breaking away from it-boasts of possessing a part of the Gospel; others, in order to reject the gift of the Spirit-poured out upon humankind in these last days according to the Father's good pleasure-do not accept the Gospel according to John, in which the Lord promised to send the Paraclete, but simultaneously reject both the Gospel and the prophetic Spirit (Adv. haer. III,11,9).

In passing, Irenaeus also refers to the authority of St Paul's writings (cf. Adv. haer. III,11,9). But neither here nor elsewhere does he mention an exact number of the Apostle's letters. Irenaeus then resumes his discussion of the Gospels other than the four:

> As for the disciples of Valentinus, having cast aside all fear, they publish their own writings and boast of possessing more Gospels than actually exist. Indeed, they have reached such a height of audacity as to call the Gospel they wrote not long ago the 'Gospel of Truth', a Gospel which does not agree at all with the Gospels of the apostles: so that, for them, not even the Gospel is free from blasphemy. Indeed, if the 'Gospel of Truth' they have published differs from those handed down to us by the apostles, anyone who wishes may see that, as is demonstrated on the basis of the Scriptures themselves, the Gospel of Truth is not the one transmitted to us by the apostles (Adv. haer. III,11,9).

The 'Gospel of Truth' cannot be apostolic, because it 'does not agree' with the others. Here we find a reference to a criterion of canonicity which we shall mention later: compatibility with other writings already accepted as authoritative (cf. pp. 164-168).

In the writings of Saint Irenaeus, there are references to and quotations from other New Testament books, as is the case with other authors of the time. We have confined ourselves to reporting his testimony on the four Gospels, as it is of particular value for understanding the gradual recognition of the books that make up the New Testament. Although there are limitations, a comparison can be drawn between the first and principal group of Hebrew writings-the five books of the Torah-and the first body of Christian writings to be finalised: the four Gospels.

#### 17.1.6. Serapion and the Gospel of Peter

Generally speaking, the Church Fathers and ecclesiastical writers who came after Irenaeus affirm the authority of the four Gospels and of them alone. Nevertheless, recourse is sometimes made to other gospels (for example, the Proto-Gospel of James to explain the meaning of the expression 'brothers of Jesus'). It should also be noted that the Diatessaron continued to be read in Syria.

The case of Serapion, Bishop of Antioch (190-211), as reported by Eusebius (HE 6.12), is significant. The faithful of the city of Rossos or Rhossus (now Arsuz, in Turkey), whose bishop was Serapion, had asked him whether they might read the so-called Gospel of Peter. Serapion consented. But later, having read the text, he wrote a letter to the people of Rossos telling them to reject it:

> 'As for us, brothers, we accept Peter and the other apostles as we do Christ, but as discerning men we reject the false writings bearing their names, in the certainty that nothing of the sort has been handed down to us. When I was amongst you, in fact, I assumed that you were all committed to the true faith and, having not read the Gospel presented by them in the name of Peter, I was able to say that if this is all that seems to frighten you, then let it be read. But, since I have now learnt, from what has been reported to me, that their minds harboured heresy, I shall return to you as soon as possible: therefore, brothers, await my return soon. (…) We did, in fact, borrow this very Gospel (…); we were able to peruse it and found in it, alongside much of the true doctrine of the Saviour, certain additions, which we have also brought to your attention[^191]. 1

This incident shows that Serapion - unlike Irenaeus - does not appear to have been aware of a fixed collection of four Gospels, which excluded any others, and therefore initially accepted the Gospel of Peter. The incident also shows that a bishop was able to determine whether a writing was apostolic or not, and whether or not it conformed to the rule of faith: he simply had to read it.

After Irenaeus, whenever there is explicit mention of which gospels are authoritative within the Church, reference is always made to the four. All lists of New Testament books mention four and only four gospels[^192].

#### 17.1.7. Debates in the third and fourth centuries: the case of the Book of Revelation

By the end of the second century, not only the four Gospels, but also the Book of Acts, the major epistles of St Paul, the First Epistle of Peter and the First Epistle of John were widely circulated and accepted as authoritative amongst Christians.

As for the other New Testament writings, the Pastoral Epistles, the Epistle of James and 2 Peter were accepted with some reservations, but without major controversy. Little is said of other books, as they are very short, such as Philemon, 2 and 1 John, and Jude. Others, however, gave rise to significant controversy: the best-known cases are those of the Epistle to the Hebrews and the Book of Revelation.

Furthermore, in the early centuries, certain books were cited as Scripture which did not subsequently enter the canon. Origen, for example, did so with the letters of Saint Ignatius of Antioch (cf. for example De Oratione 20). Such preferences are reflected not only in the citations, but also in the major manuscripts of the 4th and 5th centuries (mentioned on pp. 124-126). The Codex Sinaiticus, for example, contains the Shepherd and the Epistle of Barnabas (Ps-Barn) alongside the 20 books of the present-day New Testament. The Codex Alexandrinus contains 1 and 2 Clement. In the Syrian Church, they read the Diatessaron and also a third letter from Paul to the Corinthians (1 Cor). The extra-canonical writings that enjoyed the greatest esteem amongst Christians up until the 4th and 5th centuries were the Shepherd of Hermas and Ps-Barn.

We do not have time to trace the history of the acceptance or rejection of each of the books. By way of example, let us take the case of the Book of Revelation to illustrate both the complexity of the history and the criteria used to accept or reject a book. As mentioned above (cf. no. 1, p. 157), Saint Justin provides an early testimony to the use and appreciation of the Book of Revelation. St Irenaeus and Tertullian also accept it without hesitation. Around 200, the presbyter Gaius, in Rome, rejected both the Gospel of

but some of them were false prophets, whilst others were true prophets, and the people possessed the gift of discerning spirits, by which they judged who was the true prophet and who was the false one; so too now, in the New Covenant, 'many' have sought to write the Gospels, but 'the expert bankers' did not accept them all, but chose some of them. What is said immediately afterwards, 'they have attempted', is an accusation against those who set about writing gospels without the gift. In fact, Matthew did not 'attempt', but wrote by the Holy Spirit. Similarly, so did Mark and John, and in the same way, Luke too. On the other hand, those who wrote the so-called Gospel according to the Egyptians and the so-called Gospel of the Twelve 'have attempted'. Now even Basilides has dared to write a Gospel according to Basilides. 'Many have attempted'; indeed, the Gospel according to r Thomas and that according to Matthias and many others are also handed down. These are the works of those who have attempted. The Church of God, however, selects only the four', Origen, Homiliae in Lucam 1.1-1.4 (PG 17, 112), my translation. By 'the expert money-changers', Origen alludes to a saying attributed to Jesus: cf. G. Bazzana, ''Be Good Moneychangers': The Role of an Agraphon in a Discursive Fight for the Canon of Scripture' in J. Ulrich A.- John's authorship of the Book of Revelation, claiming that it was not the work of John but of a Gnostic author named Cerinthus. More widespread doubts regarding the canonicity of the Book of Revelation arose from the spread of millenarianism, a doctrine based on a literal interpretation of Rev 20 and which gave rise to the Chiliast sect.

Further doubts were raised by the opinion of Saint Dionysius, Bishop of Alexandria in the midthird century. Dionysius writes that, in his view, the Book of Revelation was not written by John the Apostle. He bases this on internal evidence concerning style and composition. It is worth quoting his words:

> I shall not, therefore, deny that his name is John and that this work is by John, nor do I dispute that he was a holy man inspired by God. Nevertheless, I would not readily agree that he is the apostle, the son of Zebedee, the brother of James, to whom the Gospel known as the Gospel according to John and the so-called Catholic Epistle are attributed. From the character of both writings, the style of the discourse, and the so-called composition of the book, I deduce that they do not refer to the same person (…). Judging by the ideas, the vocabulary and the style, it is likely to be a different person from the one who wrote the Gospel[^193].

As can be seen, Dionysius does not reject the inspiration or the canonicity of the book, but others will rely on him to do so, linking the apostolicity of the book to the identity of the author. Indeed, Eusebius of Caesarea himself-who is the one who reports Dionysius's opinion to us-harbours doubts about the canonicity of the Book of Revelation.

Whether due to a rejection of millenarianism or to doubts regarding the author's identity, the Book of Revelation is omitted from some important fourth-century lists of New Testament books. Specifically, neither Saint Cyril of Jerusalem (5th century) nor Saint Gregory of Nazianzus (9th century) include it in their lists. St John Chrysostom does not use it. In the Latin West, however, it was accepted without any particular difficulty[^194].

#### 17.1.8. Magisterial Decisions of the 4th and 5th Centuries

In the 4th century, lists of canonical books[^195] began to circulate. Some of these were private documents and represented only the opinion of their authors. Others, however, expressed the official position of a bishop, a provincial council or a Pope. Among these, Letter 19 of Saint Athanasius, dated 167, which lists all 27 of the New Testament and only these (cf. EB 15).

By contrast, as we have mentioned, the Book of Revelation is missing from the New Testament list compiled by Saint Cyril, Bishop of Jerusalem. Here is Cyril's list:

> Turning then to the New Testament, there are only four Gospels; the others are false and harmful. Even the Manicheans have written a Gospel, the * * according to Thomas, which, with the outward appearance and fragrance of the name 'Gospel', corrupts the souls of the most naive. You should also accept the Acts of the Twelve Apostles and, in addition to these, the seven Catholic epistles: that of James, the two of Peter, the three of John and that of Jude. To seal it all and as the final work of the disciples, the fourteen epistles of Paul. Put everything else aside, and whatever is not read in church, do not read it in private either. What has been said on these matters is sufficient (Catechesis IV, 16; EB 10).

Later, three provincial councils in North Africa would provide the complete list of both the Old and New Testaments: the Council of Hippo in 319, the Third Council of Carthage (319) and the Fourth Council of Carthage (419). St Augustine was present at the Council of Hippo as a priest of that church; he took part in the other two as a bishop.

As regards the bishops of Rome, the only document that is certainly authentic is the letter from Innocent I to Bishop Esuperius, dated 405. It contains the same canon as the North African councils (cf. EB 21).

On the other hand, the so-called 'Gelasian Decree', which contains a list of books and was long attributed to Pope Saint Gelasius I (492-496), is now unanimously regarded as inauthentic. It is a text probably composed in the 6th century by a private author (cf. EB 26-27).

The same must be said regarding the authenticity of Canon 60 of the Council of Laodicea (of the year 160), which contains a list of books. Some authors believe that indeed It may date from that period, but it is thought to be a private list inserted later into the council's proceedings. A list is provided in which the Book of Revelation is missing from the New Testament (for the Old Testament, it corresponds to Letter 67 of St Athanasius, to which we shall return later: cf. p. 190[^196]) 1. Whether or not it is the product of a council, this Canon of Laodicea is historically significant, as it was later adopted by the Trullan or Quinisestus Council of 692 in Constantinople. This council sought to clarify which books form part of the biblical canon. However, rather than issuing a decree of its own, it referred to various earlier catalogues, including that of Laodicea, the list from the Third Council of Carthage and others. If one combines all the lists mentioned by the Trullan Synod, one obtains the current canon, plus 1 Maccabees. However, this council, regarded as ecumenical by the Orthodox Church, has never been fully accepted in the West[^197].

The Orthodox Churches have not defined the biblical canon as a truth of faith, as the Catholic Church did at the Council of Trent, and there are still some doubts amongst them regarding the canonicity of certain books. But essentially, their canon is practically identical to ours. In the liturgy, the Greek Orthodox Church reads the same books as the Catholic canon, plus 1 Esdras, 1 Maccabees, the Prayer of Manasseh and Psalm 151[^198]. We shall return to some of these lists when discussing the Old Testament canon. And at the end of this section, we shall examine the definition of the biblical canon as a truth of faith established by the

Council of Trent in 1546 (pp. 198-201).

### 17.2. The criteria of canonicity and the apostolicity of the New Testament
