In summary, the first New Testament texts to be recognised as sacred and to be finalised as a collection were the four Gospels, according to the evidence we have. This is logical, given that they record the words and deeds of Jesus, the supreme authority for Christians.

Paul's epistles spread rapidly and are cited as Scripture as early as the second century. The case of Marcion demonstrates that, by the middle of that century, there was knowledge of at least ten Pauline epistles, which will never be called into question, nor will they be omitted from subsequent canonical lists.

The current controversy, in which the question is raised as to whether or not to include in the New Testament other ancient texts rediscovered in recent decades, such as the Gospel of Thomas or that of Judas, is artificial, because it fails to take history into account-at least the history handed down to us by the sources. This issue has never arisen within the Catholic Church, because the content of these gospels and others like them is clearly heterodox, that is, it does not conform to the rule of faith. A text such as the Gospel of Thomas was not lost due to chance or arbitrary decisions by the authorities, but because Christians realised its incompatibility with the tradition handed down from the apostles. Indeed, if one reads this gospel, one sees that its author does not wish to be in communion with other believers in Jesus (pp. 148-152).

The case of certain minor writings, however, remains more obscure: these are of orthodox doctrine and very ancient, some of which are included in the canon-such as Philemon, Jude or 1 John-probably because they are attributed to the apostles, whilst others are excluded, such as 1 Clement, the Didache or the Shepherd of Hermas. The question therefore remains as to what criteria the Church used to distinguish between the books, to decide which were sacred and canonical and which were not[^199].

When discussing these criteria, as Pasquale Basta observes, one must bear in mind that:

> There is no single, truly exhaustive rule of judgement; rather, a series of criteria emerges, which together serve to lend some reasonableness to the act of selecting and adopting a specific literary corpus for use in the life of the Church. The criteria in question were never laid down in advance or established as the foundation of a system in an extrinsic manner, but can be deduced on the basis of the most frequently recurring statements within those ecclesiastical documents which, to a certain extent, have addressed the biblical Canon[^200].

Among the criteria for canonicity, perhaps the inspiration of the Holy Spirit should be mentioned first and foremost: a book is accepted as canonical when it is judged to have been inspired and therefore to be the word of God. However, in practice, inspiration does not function as a criterion, because it cannot be ascertained on its own. The inspiration of a book is in itself invisible, although it can be discerned through other signs.

Indeed, the work of the Spirit leaves traces, which are precisely the criteria used by the Church to discern which books are inspired. Scholars of the formation of the canon identify the following characteristics of the books' content as criteria used in the early Church to discern canonicity:

- apostolicity,

- antiquity,

- orthodoxy,

- agreement with other books already accepted,

- comprehensibility,

- edifying nature,

- usefulness beyond the specific circumstances of a particular moment (not merely occasional).

In addition to these criteria, there are others, derived not from the books themselves but from their ecclesiastical use. It must be ascertained whether a book:

- is read in other churches, especially those of apostolic origin,

- is used in the liturgy,

- is cited as Scripture,

- has been recognised by ecclesiastical authorities.

As can be seen, these are distinct criteria. Some - particularly those in the second group could be easily verified; others, however, could not, such as antiquity or comprehensibility. Nor can all the criteria be applied to every book. For example, the possibility of agreement with other books already accepted is relevant only where such books exist.

If one wishes to summarise the whole of these criteria in a single concept, one must speak of the living tradition of the Church. Dei Verbum states simply, yet profoundly, that it is Tradition 'which makes known to the Church the entire canon of sacred books' (DV 8). The Church did not determine the canon through complex historical research, but 'discovered' it, so to speak, by looking at her own life, seeing what she believes, and thus discerning which books correspond to the tradition received from the Apostles. And in this task, we believe that the Church cannot err, because she is assisted by the Holy Spirit, as the conciliar document itself affirms (cf. DV 8; on the role of the Magisterium in the formation of the canon, see also below, pp. 201-2023). In this sense, it must be added that, amongst the criteria mentioned above, some are more important than others. In the case of the New Testament, the criterion which in some way encompasses all the others is apostolicity. All the early Christian authors, in one way or another, affirm the equivalence between believing in the apostolic origin of a book and believing in its normative value for the Church, in its inspiration and canonicity.

Indeed, it is no coincidence that almost all apocryphal writings take the name of an apostle in an attempt to gain authority: we have the Gospel of Thomas, the Apocalypse of Peter, the Acts of Andrew, etc. The Church was able to discern their non-apostolic nature, above all because their content did not correspond to the rule of faith. These cases show that being apostolic is not something limited to the author to whom a book is attributed. Indeed, the Epistle to the Hebrews presents the opposite case. The text appears anonymous, as is also the case with the four Gospels. Origen himself recognised that Hebrews has a different style from that of Paul and that no one knows who wrote it:

> As for me, if I were to express my opinion, I would say that the thoughts are those of the Apostle, whilst the style and composition are those of someone who recalled the apostolic doctrine-so to speak, an editor who transcribed what was said by the teacher. (…) As for who wrote the letter, God knows the truth[^201].

The traditional attribution of the Epistle to the Hebrews to Saint Paul must therefore be understood as a way of affirming its apostolicity and thus defending its canonicity. The case of the Epistle to the Hebrews is interesting because it shows how, in early Christian times, there was a tendency to simplify matters, equating apostolicity with the work having been written by an apostle. Today, it is advisable to distinguish between the human author-who may have been an apostle or an 'apostolic man', such as Mark, Luke or the anonymous author of Hebrews-and apostolicity as an original characteristic, as a link with the apostles, the first witnesses to revelation (on who is to be considered an apostle, cf. pp. 268-280).

By making this distinction, we avoid artificial problems: believing that Saint Paul did not write the Letter to the Hebrews in no way detracts from the apostolic nature of this writing. We have the testimony of Origen just cited, and also that of St Augustine, who, whilst always accepting the canonicity of the Epistle to the Hebrews, at a certain point in his life became convinced that it could not have been written by St Paul[^202].

We may conclude by quoting the words of an author who, using different terms, arrives at a formulation equivalent to what we have just said regarding the criterion of apostolicity:

> The apostolicity of the New Testament is a theologoumenon, that is, a theological construct with a theological conceptual core, but one which at the same time rests upon the facts of historical tradition. The theological concept in itself does not hold up without reference to a certain historical basis. However, the exact manner in which the historical validity of the apostolic origin of a particular book can be verified is not authoritatively defined. Nor is it required that the apostolic character of a book be defined in exactly the same way in every single case. The Magisterium of the Church, including the Second Vatican Council's Dei Verbum, has always considered it sufficient to affirm the apostolicity of the canonical books of the New Testament in an analogical sense of the term, without specifying in too much detail the exact ma manner in which the apostolic origin of a particular document can be verified[^203].

## 18. The Old Testament Canon

The title of this section requires clarification. By 'Old Testament' we refer to the collection of books which Christians today call by that name; but it is clear that for much of this process such a designation was not used, nor did it ever exist for the Jews.

### 18.1. The Scriptures of Israel before Jesus

To understand what follows, we need to briefly touch upon the regard in which certain books were held as sacred in Israel before Jesus. Firstly, the Torah of Moses was regarded by all Jews as a sacred and authoritative text, because it contains the words that God revealed to Moses on Mount Sinai. The oracles of the prophet Jeremiah were transmitted in writing and read aloud, as they were regarded as divine words that remain relevant to this day (cf. Dan 9). The same applies to the oracles of other prophets, such as Isaiah, Ezekiel and the Twelve. However, this was probably not the case with regard to other texts, such as Chronicles, Esther or the Song of Songs (cf. pp. 97-103 and Excursus!, pp. 281-!oo).

In what follows, we shall take a look at the main historical documents that may help us to understand how the Jews arrived at the establishment of their current Bible of 24 books.

#### 18.1.1. The testimony of Sirach (c. 190 BC)

A fascinating field of study is that of biblical intertextuality, that is, the quotations or allusions to other books found within the biblical books. Isaiah and Jeremiah include certain accounts that are also found in the Books of Kings (cf. Isa 36-19 and 2 Kings 18-20; Jer 52 and 2 Kings 24-25). Isaiah and Micah contain a virtually identical oracle (cf. Isa 2:2-5 and Mic 4:1-5); Joel alludes to the same oracle, reversing its meaning (cf. Joel 4:10). In Daniel 9, it is recounted how the prophet Daniel reads the Book of Jeremiah and reinterprets the oracle of the 'seventy years'. Many books allude to the Torah of Moses, as, for example, the Book of Wisdom does with the accounts of the Exodus (cf. Wisdom 10-19).

However, compiling a list of all these intra-biblical references would be too lengthy and laborious, and would yield little in the way of insights into reconstructing the history of the canon's formation. We shall mention just one example: the allusions to other books in Sirach.

Sirach or Ecclesiasticus offers a clear advantage for historical research, as we are able to determine its date of composition with precision. Based on various pieces of evidence, both internal and external, Jesus ben Sira must have written his book between 198 and 15 BC

BC (the place of composition is less certain, but all evidence points to Jerusalem).

From the book's content, we can glean some information about the books that were being read at the time. Indeed, in the so-called 'eulogy of the fathers' (Sir 44-50), Ben Sira gives an overview of the history of Israel, recalling its major figures. He begins with Enoch and Noah and goes as far as the high priest Simon, his contemporary, passing through the patriarchs, Moses, Aaron, Phinehas, Joshua, Caleb, the judges, Samuel, Nathan, David, Solomon, Rehoboam, Jeroboam, Elijah, Elisha, Hezekiah, Isaiah, Josiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, the twelve prophets, Zerubbabel, Joshua, and Nehemiah. The account concludes in chapter 50 with a lengthy eulogy of the high priest Simon. Shortly before the end (49:14-16), Enoch is mentioned once more, and two figures previously omitted are recalled: Joseph, son of Jacob, and Adam. It is curious that no mention is made of Ezra alongside Nehemiah, an omission that is difficult to explain. There are also no references to Daniel, Ruth or Esther.

The point of interest here is that, on reading these pages, one gets the impression that Ben Sira composed them whilst having the respective biblical books before him. In this regard, the mention of the 'twelve prophets' as a group is particularly significant, given that, as historical figures, they actually belong to different eras.

> May the bones of the twelve prophets blossom anew from their graves, for they comforted Jacob and redeemed him through their steadfast hope (Sir 49:10).

This is a clue suggesting that by this time they were all included in the same scroll[^204].

In Ben Sira's time, therefore, in addition to the Torah, there was probably also a collection of books similar to the second part of the present-day BH, the 'Prophets' (Judges, Joshua, Samuel, Kings, Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel and the Twelve). Ben Sira was also familiar with the Book of Proverbs, as the eulogy to wisdom in Sir 24 is based on Prov 8-9.

However, there are almost never any actual quotations to be found in Sirach[^205]. And, apart from the book of the Torah (cf. Sir 24:23), no 'books' as such are mentioned. Ben Sira made use of texts, but he is not yet a commentator on the Scriptures; he merely quotes and explains them as texts regarded as superior or normative. Ben Sira regards himself as belonging to the same tradition as earlier authors (cf. Sir 24:28-14;

33,16-19; 51). The very composition of the book seems to indicate that the idea of a definitively closed canon did not yet exist. This impression appears to be confirmed by the prologue, which presents the book in comparison with the other writings of Israel.

#### 18.1.2. Prologue to Sirach

The end of the prologue allows us to determine the date of its composition: it was written after 'the thirty-eighth year of King Evergetes', which corresponds to 112 BC. The anonymous grandson of Jesus Ben Sira, in presenting his own Greek translation of his grandfather's work, mentions the books of Israel three times, divided into three groups:

> Many profound teachings have been given to us in the Law, the Prophets and the other subsequent writings, and for these Israel is to be praised as a learned and wise people. For it is necessary that readers should not be content merely to become knowledgeable for their own sake, but that scholars should also be of service to the uninitiated through their words and writings; my grandfather Jesus, too, having devoted himself at length to the study of the Law, the Prophets and the other books of our forefathers, and having attained a remarkable level of expertise therein, was moved to write something concerning teaching and wisdom, so that lovers of knowledge, having assimilated this as well, might progress ever further in a way of life in accordance with the Law.

> You are therefore invited to read it with kindness and attention, and to forgive us if, despite the care taken in the translation, it should seem that we have failed to convey the full force of certain expressions. Indeed, what is said in Hebrew does not have the same- their full force when translated into another language. And not only this work, but also the Law itself, the Prophets and the rest of the books retain a considerable advantage in the original text.

This text is of great importance, for it is the oldest evidence we have of the existence of an organisation of the writings of Israel that divides them into three groups (very similar to that which still applies to Jews today).

The first group is the Torah; the second is called 'the Prophets', which should correspond more or less to what in the BH are referred to as the earlier prophets and the later prophets. The third group, on the other hand, has no specific name, which suggests that it is not a closed collection (not least because the Book of Sirach itself would have to be included in it). Furthermore, the text says nothing about the specific content of these three groups, nor about their sacred nature (God is not explicitly mentioned).

Furthermore, the text provides evidence that a Greek translation existed not only of the Torah, but also of books from the other two groups. Thanks to the prologue to Sirach, we know that in the second half of the 2nd century BC, Greek translations of not only the Law, but also the Prophets and 'the other writings' were in circulation in Egypt.

#### 18.1.3. The translation of the Torah and other books into Greek

We have already touched upon the question of when and why the five books of the Torah of Moses and subsequently other books were translated into Greek[^206] in our discussion of the Old Testament text (pp. 123-127). We may now ask what can be gleaned from the Septuagint version to reconstruct the history of the formation of the biblical canon.

Unfortunately, we do not know the exact scope of this translation, nor the time at which the various books were translated into Greek 1.

Almost all the manuscripts containing the Greek Bible that have come down to us date from the Christian era and do not include the same books. For example, the Vatican Codex (4th century) contains none of the Books of Maccabees; in the Sinaiticus Codex (4th century) we find 1 Maccabees and 4 Maccabees, but not 2 Maccabees; whilst the Alexandrinus Codex (5th century) contains all four Books of Maccabees. And there are other differences[^207].

More fundamentally still, the fact that a book was translated into Greek - and placed alongside the Torah - does not automatically imply that it was regarded as sacred or inspired. Therefore, on its own, the Septuagint does not help us to know precisely which books were considered sacred before Christ, apart from the five books of the Torah of Moses.

#### 18.1.4. The books accepted by the Sadducees

Thanks to the Gospels and Josephus, we know that the Sadducees did not believe in the resurrection of the dead (cf. Mt 22:23-33; Mk 12:18-27; Lk 20:27-40; BJ 2.165 et par.). In the Acts of the Apostles, it is stated more explicitly: 'The Sadducees maintain that there is no resurrection, nor angels, nor spirits' (Acts 23:8; cf. 4:1-2).

Some authors maintain that the only Scripture accepted by the Sadducees was the Torah. This would explain why Jesus resolved the controversy over the resurrection by quoting from Exodus. Others disagree with this assertion. We are simply interested in noting that the Sadducees could not accept the books that speak of the resurrection of the dead, such as Daniel (and also 2 Maccabees and other books).

We can therefore be certain that, in Jesus' time, if a canon of 22 or 24 books existed, it was not accepted by all political-religious groups. The same conclu tion can be reached by examining the library found at Qumran.

#### 18.1.5. The Qumran Library

Texts from various periods and of various types have been found in the caves of Qumran, as we mentioned when discussing the languages of the books (cf. pp. 46-49). We are now interested in finding out which books the members of the Qumran community possessed and whether, in any way, they regarded some of them as different from the other writings in their possession[^208].

Firstly, we can say that manuscripts of all the books of the Hebrew Bible, except Esther, were found in the Qumran caves. Habakkuk is also missing, but there is a commentary (pesher) on it. As we have seen in relation to languages, at Qumran they also possessed copies of certain books that today form part of the Catholic Bible but not the Hebrew Bible: fragments of Sirach in Hebrew; a fragment of the Letter of Jeremiah in Greek (Bar 6); a fragment of Tobit in Hebrew and four in Aramaic. There are also several copies of certain books that are not canonical today, notably 1 Enoch and the Book of Jubilees, but also others such as the Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs. There is There are also some manuscripts containing biblical psalms intermingled with non-biblical prayers.

Finally, at Qumran they also read certain books specific to the community, namely texts that deal with their rules of life (the Damascus Document, the Community Rule) or that interpret other books by applying them to their own situation (the so-called pesharim).

The key question is whether the users of this library distinguished between the books and, if so, how. For example, some books are written in different scripts, the so-called Paleo-Hebrew script, perhaps as a sign of special veneration. This is the case with some copies of the five books of the Torah and of Job. However, we cannot conclude that only these books were considered sacred. It is more interesting to see which books were the subject of commentary. Commentaries (pesharim) on Isaiah, Hosea, Micah, Habakkuk, Zephaniah and some of the Psalms have been found at Qumran. Obviously, this criterion does not allow us to establish a complete list of the books regarded as sacred, but it reflects the fact that at least some of them were considered prophecies of the future and therefore the word of God. Another criterion for distinction stems from the quotations from books found in the community's own writings. The Torah, Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Daniel, the Minor Prophets and the Psalms 1 are cited as scripture.

Therefore, as can be seen, the books definitely regarded as sacred at Qumran do not differ greatly from the future Jewish canon. However, it is not unlikely that at Qumran they regarded certain books specific to the group, such as the Temple Scroll, as sacred. Furthermore, it must be borne in mind that the library belonged to a community that had separated itself from the priesthood and the Temple in Jerusalem. Consequently, there is debate as to the extent to which Qumran can be regarded as representative of the common view of the Jews of that period.

Finally, it is unclear whether a 'canonical consciousness' existed within the community living at Qumran.

The following table outlines the situation regarding the books of the Hebrew Bible and other similar texts[^209]. I am quoting these figures as they help to illustrate the point just made:

### 18.2. Jesus and the New Testament

#### 18.2.1. Quotations from the Old Testament in the New Testament

As we have seen (pp. 90-94), the Gospels tell us that Jesus referred both to the 'Scriptures' in general and to specific passages. From Luke 24:44, it appears that Jesus was familiar with a threefold division of the Scriptures of Israel - the Law of Moses, the Prophets and the Psalms - similar to that attested in the prologue to Sirach.

If we bring together the four Gospels, we see that Jesus quotes from all five books of the Torah (particularly Deuteronomy), from the majority of the Prophets (especially Isaiah) and from some of the Writings (particularly the Psalms[^210]) 1.

In addition to the references to the Scriptures that the Gospels attribute directly to Jesus, there are those made by the authors of the New Testament. Adding up all the explicit quotations from the Old Testament in the New Testament yields the following result[^211].

- There are quotations from the five books of the Pentateuch: Gen (19 times), Ex (24), Lev (14), Num (1) and Deut (42).

- Of what we call the historical books, only Joshua (1), Samuel (5) and Kings (1) are explicitly quoted.

- Of the poetic and wisdom books, there are explicit quotations only from Job (1), Psalms (54) and Proverbs (4).

- Of the prophetic books, none of the major prophets are missing: Isa (48), Jer (9), Ezek (2) and Dan (2). The minor prophets are quoted 22 times in total; taken separately, Hos, Gl, Am, Jo, Mic, Ab, Ag, Zech and Mal are quoted. That is to say, there are no explicit quotations from Abd, Nah and Zeph, but since at that time the minor prophets had by then formed a single book - the Scroll of the Twelve - their absence is not significant.

- In the New Testament, there are no explicit quotations from Judges, Ruth, 1-2 Chronicles, Ezra-Nehemiah, Tobit, Judith, Esther, 1-2 Maccabees, Ecclesiastes, Wisdom, Sirach, Lamentations and Baruch.

From the number of quotations, one can infer the importance attributed to certain books, such as Deuteronomy, Isaiah and the Psalms. Conversely, however, it is not valid to conclude that a book not quoted was unknown to the New Testament writers or that it was not regarded as sacred. The New Testament does not allow us to reconstruct the list of books that its authors regarded as sacred, simply because they quote various scriptures only occasionally, without intending to provide a comprehensive catalogue.

Furthermore, it should be added that the New Testament contains some surprises. There are allusions to books that do not form part of our Old Testament. The clearest example is found in the Epistle of Jude. After alluding to an episode recounted in a book called The Ascension of Moses, the author of the epistle explicitly quotes a passage from another extra-canonical book, the Book of Enoch:

> Enoch, the seventh from Adam, also prophesied concerning them, saying: 'Behold, the Lord has come with thousands upon thousands of his angels to bring everyone to judgement, and to prove the guilt of all concerning every wicked deed they have committed and every insult which, as ungodly sinners, they have hurled against him' (Jude 14-15; the quotation corresponds to 1 Enoch 1:9).

What can be said in light of this? Clearly, if there was a canon of Scripture amongst the early Christians corresponding to today's, the author of Jude either was unaware of it or deviated from it. However, it seems more reasonable to assume that one should not speak of a biblical 'canon' in the first century AD, because at that time there was not yet an official list of books or a definitive collection of Scriptures. For the author of the Epistle of Jude, the Book of Enoch contains a prophecy suitable for illustrating a teaching teaching 1. Later on, the universal Church would not accept this book as inspired and canonical. Without denying that there is a certain tension, it must be recognised that these are two distinct issues[^213].

The explicit quotation from 1 Enoch in the Epistle of Jude should serve as a warning against applying to the rest of the New Testament a simple equivalence between the quotation of a book and the recognition of its canonicity.

#### 18.2.2. The biblical canon in the time of Jesus

Today, the majority of scholars maintain that the formation of the Old Testament canon took a long time and that, at the time of Jesus, there was as yet no canon of books common to all, apart from the Torah of Moses. Indeed, when we speak of 'Judaism' at the time of Jesus, we are oversimplifying matters, because at that time there were various groups. We have already mentioned the Sadducees and the members of the Qumran community. There were others: Pharisees, Essenes, Herodians, Zealots, disciples of John the Baptist, apocalyptic groups, etc. And as far as we know, it seems that not all of them used or recognised the same Scriptures.

Be that as it may, the diversity was not absolute. In fact, all Jews and even the Samaritans accepted the five books of the Torah of Moses (more or less in the form we know today). The books of the 'Prophets' - that is, Joshua, Judges, Samuel, Kings, Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel and the Twelve Prophets - were also widely accepted (the book of Daniel has a separate history; in the current Hebrew canon it is not found amongst the Prophets, but amongst the Writings). Other books such as Psalms, Job and Proverbs were also recognised by all. But accepting certain books is not the same as having a definitive (and therefore closed) list of sacred books.

To provide an overview, we may refer to these words from the PCB:

> Today it seems more likely that, at the time of the birth of Christianity, the closed collections of the books of the Law and the Prophets existed in a textual form substantially identical to that of our present-day Old Testament. The collection of the

> 'Writings', on the other hand, was not so clearly defined, either in Palestine or in the Jewish diaspora, both in terms of the number of books and the form of their text[^214].

As we have attempted to show, the absence of a fixed canon does not mean that there was total uncertainty in Israel as to which books were to be regarded as sacred Scripture. But it is equally clear that we must reject the idea that the Apostolic Church received the biblical canon from Israel, in the sense of a concrete and defined list of books. It must also be ruled out that Jesus himself handed down a list of books to the apostles.

More importantly, Jesus declared that the Torah and the Prophets extend 'up to John', as we saw in the first part of the course. If Jesus brings God's historical revelation to his people to its fulfilment, it follows logically that there is no room for other books of the Old Covenant. Ultimately, what matters is the concept of revelation underlying the canon. From a Christian perspective, it is reasonable to assume that no canon existed in Jesus' time, since God's revelation to Israel was not yet complete, as Aranda argues:

> (…) at the time of Jesus there was no Scripture constituting a canon in the strict sense, as if God had communicated his entire Word to his people. It is certain that there were sacred writings which were also transmitted in closed collections, such as the Law and the Prophets or the other, undefined group of Writings. This can be inferred from the Prologue to Ecclesiasticus, the Books of Maccabees, and the New Testament (cf. 1 Maccabees 1:56-57; 2 Maccabees 2:13-15; Luke 24:44). However, the existence of these collections does not amount to that of a biblical canon in the strict sense; or, at the very least, we have no evidence that the question had arisen in the e Judaism of that period[^215].

### 18.3. Among the Jews at the end of the first century

The destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem and the other events of the year yo d. C. marked a turning point in the history of the Jewish people. Following the defeat in the first war against Rome (66-73), certain Jewish groups disappeared from the history: the Sadducees, the Qumran community, etc. Others, such as the Zealots or certain groups of an apocalyptic nature, survived for a little while longer, but it appears they were definitively wiped out after the Second Jewish War (132-135). Of the various Jewish groups existing in the first century AD, only two would survive into the following centuries: so-called Rabbinic Judaism (or 'Rabbinism') and Christianity, which opened itself up to the Gentiles, became universal and developed into a movement separate from the synagogue.

After the Exile, the people of Israel-now deprived of the Temple, without a priesthood or worship, but still in possession of the sacred books-had to reorganise themselves. The Scriptures would form the basis for defining their religious and cultural identity. It is precisely in this situation, at the end of the first century AD, that we find the first references to a fixed number of books: Flavius Josephus speaks of 22 books, whilst 4 Esdras alludes to 94.

#### 18.3.1. The twenty-two books according to the testimony of Flavius Josephus

In a work entitled *Contra Apionem*, which is an apology for the Jewish people written at the end of the first century, Flavius Josephus seeks to defend the antiquity of his people against their detractors. The latter base their arguments on the fact that no reference to Israel appears in Greek literature or historiography. If Greek historians do not mention them, it follows that the Jewish people cannot be as ancient as their members claim. In this polemical context, Josephus discusses the credibility of the Jewish historical books, in contrast to those of the Greeks.

The text is of great interest not only for the history of the canon, but also for the concept of inspiration. This is the only instance in all of Josephus's works where the term 'inspiration' (ἐπίπνοια) appears. This divine inspiration is not a characteristic of the 22 books, but is presented solely as the source of the prophets' knowledge of certain events (those 'distant and ancient'). Above all, however, this text constitutes the first historical evidence mentioning a precise number of Scriptures in Israel.

Here are the words of Flavius Josephus:

> [!y] It is therefore natural, or rather necessary (since not everyone is permitted to write history, nor is there any divergence in our writings, for only the prophets have clearly recounted distant and ancient events, having learnt them through divine inspiration [uατà τ2ν ἐπίπνοιαν τ2ν áuò τοũ θsοũ], contemporary events as they unfolded before their very eyes), [38] it is natural, I say, that there should not be among us an infinite number of books at odds with one another and full of contradictions, but only twenty-two which contain the annals of all ages and are held in due esteem. [39] First and foremost are the five books of Moses, which encompass the laws and tradition from the creation of mankind right up to his own death. This spans a period of approximately three thousand years. [40] From the death of Moses up to Artaxerxes, successor to Xerxes on the throne of Persia, the prophets who came after Moses recounted the history of their time in thirteen books. The remaining four contain hymns to God and moral precepts for mankind. [41] From Artaxerxes to the present day, all events have been recorded, but these writings are not accorded the same credibility as the preceding ones, since the prophets no longer succeeded one another in an orderly manner.

> [42] The facts demonstrate the respect with which we treat our books. After so many centuries have passed, no one has dared to make an addition, a deletion or an alteration. It is natural for all Jews, from the moment of their birth, to believe that these books contain the divine will, to respect them, and, when the occasion arises, to die for them with joy. [43] Thus, many among them have been seen, whilst in captivity, to endure torments and every kind of death in the amphitheatres rather than utter a single word contrary to the laws and the annals that accompany them. [44] Among the Greeks, who would endure so much for their own writings? Even if it were a matter of saving all their writings, no one would risk the slightest damage. [45] For they believe that these are words composed according to the writers' own will; and they are not wrong to think this even of the most ancient writers, since I now see some who dare to write about events at which they were neither present nor do they make any effort to enquire of those who were there[^216].

The first question to ask concerns the reliability of Josephus's testimony. Is he exaggerating for rhetorical purposes? More precisely, one might ask whether he is truly recording a belief held by the Jews or whether he is merely expressing his own personal opinion, presenting it as if it were common doctrine. Indeed, some of his statements seem dictated by the needs of his argument. For example, his insistence on the perfect harmony between the 22 books seems to stem more from rhetoric than from the reality of the facts. Furthermore, to say that all Jews are prepared to die for their books is inaccurate: according to various historical sources, such an attitude is found amongst the Jews only in regard to the Torah[^217].

Despite these problems, it should be noted that, had Flavius Josephus wished to do so, it would have been much simpler for him to state that all Jewish books deserve the same trust, even the more recent ones (among which is his account of the Jewish war against Rome). Instead, he draws a distinction between the 22 books and those written 'after Artaxerxes', probably because such a distinction was real.

In the same vein, the mention of four non-historical books does not fit well with the argument and therefore seems to reflect the fact that a distinct group of twenty-two books did indeed exist.

We might ask ourselves which 22 books Josephus is referring to. 'The books of Moses, five in number' undoubtedly correspond to the Pentateuch. As for the rest, identification becomes much more uncertain. Some authors start from the premise that the 22 books must be the same as those that make up the current BH, which has 24. The discrepancy is resolved by stating that Josephus counts Judges and Ruth as a single book and does the same with Jeremiah and Lamentations (indeed, this equivalence between 22 and 24 books was known to St Jerome; see p. 192). Thus, the 22 books would be Joshua, Judges + Ruth, Samuel, Kings, Chronicles, Ezra-Nehemiah, Isaiah, Jeremiah + Lamentations, Ezekiel, the Twelve Prophets, Daniel, Esther and Job. Finally, the other four books would be Psalms, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes and the Song of Songs.

In reality, this identification is somewhat far-fetched. Or at the very least, we can say that it is an unprovable hypothesis. For example, as regards the four books that 'contain hymns to God and moral precepts for mankind', it seems logical to think of the Book of Psalms (which in Hebrew is called tehilim, 'hymns') and the Book of Proverbs, which essentially contains instructions for living. But Ecclesiastes and, above all, the Song of Songs do not fit this description.

In truth, we do not know which 22 books Flavius Josephus is referring to, although, as is logical, it is likely that they were a group very similar, if not identical, to the 24 books of the current Hebrew Bible.

Another point worthy of consideration is the justification proposed by Josephus to establish the distinction between the 22 books and those written later, that is, the end of the succession of prophets. Some later rabbinic texts speak of God's silence from the time of the Babylonian exile onwards, or of the cessation of prophecy following the deaths of the prophets Haggai, Malachi and Zechariah[^218].

Without delving into the subject - which is complex - it must be said that such assertions appear to be explanations constructed after the fact. Once a fixed collection of books has been established, an attempt must be made to provide a justification for it. Thus, since it is believed that all the books are ancient, it is inferred that there were no further books because prophecy had come to an end in the distant past. Along these lines, many centuries later, certain Jewish authors would put forward the hypothesis that the establishment of the canon was the work of Ezra at the time of the people's return from the Babylonian exile.

#### 18.3.2. The ninety-four books (24 + yo) according to 4 Esdras

The so-called Fourth Book of Ezra (4 Ezra), also known as the Apocalypse of Ezra, is a Jewish work written around the year 100 AD, probably in Hebrew.

A side note: why is it called the 'Fourth Book of Ezra' when there is only one such book in our Bibles? The answer is a little complicated. In the Greek biblical tradition, the canonical books of Ezra and Nehemiah are regarded as a single book, known as 2 Esdras or Ezra p, because they are preceded by an extra-canonical book called 1 Esdras or Ezra α (which is a collection of passages from other books: it consists of the last two chapters of 2 Chronicles and a repetition of the Book of Ezra, plus some passages from Nehemiah; the only original part is found in!,1-5,6, the story of the three young men). In contrast, in the Vulgate manuscripts, Ezra and Nehemiah are separated, becoming 1 and 2 Ezra, and Ezra α is then added, becoming! Ezra. This is why, in the Latin tradition, the apocryphal book we are now considering is called 4 Ezra, whilst in other traditions it is called 2 Ezra. Here is a summary table of these names:

|  | Book of Ezra | Book of Nehemiah | Paraphrase of 2 Chronicles $5-$6 + Ezra + Neh 7,$8- 8,12 + the story of the three young men | Revelation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| BH | Ezra | Nehemiah | not included | none |
| LXX | Part of 2 Ezra (Ezra p) | Part of Ezra (Ezra p) | 1 Ezra (Ezra α) | not available |
| Vg | 1 Esdras | 2 Esdras | ! Ezra | 4 Esdras |

The opening words of 4 Ezra provide the narrative context of the book: Ezra, speaking in the first person, says he is in Babylon thirty years after the fall of Jerusalem (cf. 4 Ezra!,1). He addresses a prayer to God, asking why He has delivered His people into the hands of the Babylonians. The Lord answers him first through the words of an angel, then through visions. Finally, Ezra engages in direct dialogue with the Lord, before being taken up into heaven. I reproduce a lengthy passage to provide the context. Ezra speaks:

> [1] On the third day, as I was sitting under an oak tree, a voice came from a thornbush in front of me, saying: 'Ezra, Ezra!' (…) [19] I replied, 'May I speak before You, O Lord! [20] Behold, I shall go as You have commanded me, and I shall rebuke the people who are here; but those who come after them, who will admonish them? For the world lies in darkness, and those who dwell in it are without light,

> [21] for the Law has been burnt, and so no one knows the works You have done, or what You are yet to do. [22] For if I have found favour in Your sight, pour into me the Holy Spirit, and I shall write down all that has been done in the world from the beginning-the things that were written in Your Law-so that men may find the way, and that those who wish to live may live in the last days'. [23] He answered me and said: 'Go, gather the people, and tell them not to seek you for forty days; [24] but you, prepare many tablets for writing, and take with you Saria, Dabria, Selemia, Ethan and Asihel-these five, for they are able to write quickly; [25] you shall come here, and I shall kindle in your heart the lamp of understanding, which shall not be extinguished until what you are to write has come to an end. (…)

> [!y] I took the five men, as I had been instructed; we set out for the countryside and stayed there. [38] The next day, behold, a voice called out to me, saying: 'Ezra, open your mouth and drink what I give you.' [39] I opened my mouth, and lo, I was offered a full cup: it looked as though it were filled with water, but its colour was like fire. [40] I took it and drank, and as I drank, my mind was filled with understanding and wisdom grew within my heart, for my spirit retained the memory; [41] my mouth opened, and it never closed again. [42] The Most High, however, granted understanding (also) to the five men, and whatever was told to them they wrote down in characters they did not know, remaining there for forty days, writing by day and eating bread by night, [43] whilst I spoke by day, but did not remain silent by night.

> [44] Ninety-four books were written during these forty days. [45] It came to pass that, when the forty days were completed, the Most High spoke to me, saying: 'Make public the twenty-four books you wrote earlier, so that both the worthy and the unworthy may read them; [46] but keep the seventy written last, to hand them over to the wise among your people, [47] for in them lies the wellspring of understanding, the fountain of wisdom, and the river of knowledge!' [48] And so I did, in the seventh year, in the sixth week, five thousand years after the creation, three months and twelve days. In those days, Ezra was taken up and led to the place where those like him are, after he had written all these things; and he was called the scribe of the knowledge of the Most High, for ever and ever[^219].

With these words, 4 Esdras comes to an end. The reader then realises that this very book is one of those which God commanded Esdras not to publish, distinct from the other 24 books already published and available to all, whether worthy or unworthy. It is also worth noting that all these books are regarded as part of God's single Torah ('Law').

The author of 4 Esdras appears to disagree with those who accept only 24 books. Or at the very least, he is seeking to establish the authority of his own book and of other similar works, which are not recognised by everyone.

We can now compare the testimony regarding the books of 4 Esdras with that of Flavius Josephus. Firstly, there appears to be a similarity regarding the date at which the final writing of the books is situated (whether one speaks of the compilation of the last books, as in Josephus's case, or describes the rewriting of all of them, as in 4 Esdras). Indeed, Josephus refers to the time of Artaxerxes, whilst the vision described in 4 Esdras takes place thirty years after the destruction of the Temple. That is to say, in one case we are talking about the 5th century BC and in the other about the 6th century BC.

Both mention a number of books: 22 in one case, 94 in the other. The number of books publicly known is almost identical: 22 and 24. These two figures share a common feature: both correspond to the number of letters in an alphabet-the Hebrew alphabet (22) or the Greek alphabet (24). This is probably not a mere coincidence. Perhaps both authors received the information that, amongst the Jews, the number of books accepted is equal to the letters of the alphabet, but each interpreted this as referring to a different alphabet[^220].

### 18.4. Rabbinic literature

It is interesting to examine the rabbis' discussions concerning the canon, as they demonstrate that the list of books they regarded as sacred was not yet entirely clear. But we must first explain what rabbinic literature is.

# UTET, 1989.

At the time of Jesus (and even earlier), there were rabbis in Israel (from 'rabbi', meaning 'teacher'), who devoted themselves to studying the Torah and teaching it to their disciples, as Gamaliel did, for example, with Saul (St Paul) in Jerusalem:

> 'I am a Jew, born in Tarsus in Cilicia, but brought up in this city, trained in the school of Gamaliel in the strict observance of the Law of our fathers, full of zeal for God, just as all of you are today' (Acts 22:3).

On several occasions, Jesus is called 'Rabbi' (Mt 26:25, 49; Mk 9:5; 11:21; 14:45; Jn 1:38, 49; 1:2; 4:11; 6:25; 9:2; 11:8). He is often asked for a legal interpretation - for example, the question about the most important commandment: cf. Mt 22:34-40; Mk 12:28-34; Luke 10:25-28 which was what was expected of a rabbi.

As we have said, after the two wars against Rome, Jewish identity would essentially survive in the form of Rabbinism, centred on the reading and interpretation of the Torah, without priests conducting worship and without political messianic hopes.

A distinct body of literature developed within the rabbinical schools, always centred on the Torah. The most important works are the Mishnah, the Tosefta and the Talmud. The PCB explains them as follows:

> The sacred texts of the Bible leave many questions open regarding the correct understanding of the faith of Israel and the conduct to be observed. This has led to (…) a long process of producing written texts, from the Mishnah ('Second Text'), compiled at the beginning of the third century by Jehuda ha-Nasi, to the Tosefta ('Supplement') and the Talmud in its two forms (the Babylonian and the Jerusalem Talmuds). (…) The Mishnah, the Tosefta and the Talmud have their place in the synagogue as texts for study, but they are not read during the liturgy. In general, the value of a tradition is measured by its conformity to the Torah[^221].

The oldest of these writings, the Mishnah, compiles sayings of rabbis, some from the time of Christ or even earlier, up to the year 200 AD, the approximate date of the publication of this compilation, written in Hebrew.

#### 18.4.1. A text from the Mishnah and the hypothesis of the canon established at Jamnia

With regard to the history of the canon, one text from the Mishnah is of particular interest. It sets out various opinions on the sacredness of two books, Ecclesiastes and the Song of Songs. The conclusion is that both 'soil the hands', that is to say, they are to be regarded as writings- sacred writings. It is worth quoting the text also to understand the distinctive style of rabbinic literature:

> All sacred writings soil the hands. The Song of Songs and Ecclesiastes soil the hands.

> Rabbi Judah says: the Song of Songs defiles the hands, but there is debate regarding Ecclesiastes.

> Rabbi Joseph says: Ecclesiastes soils the hands, but there is a dispute regarding the Song of Songs.

> Rabbi Simon says: Ecclesiastes belongs to the 'light' books according to the school of Shammai, and to the 'heavy' books according to the school of Hillel.

> Rabbi Simon ben Zakkai said: 'I have heard a tradition from the seventy-two elders, when they elected Rabbi Eleazar ben Azariah as head of the council of sages, according to which the Song of Songs and Ecclesiastes defile the hands.'

> Rabbi Akiba said: God forbid! No one in Israel has ever disputed that the Song of Songs defiles the hands, for the whole world is not worth the day on which the Song of Songs was bestowed upon Israel; for all the Ketubim are holy, but the Song of Songs is the Holy of Holies. If there was any debate, it concerned only Ecclesiastes.

> Rabbi Johanan ben Joshua said: 'It was discussed and decided as Ben Zakkai[^222] said.'

As can be seen from the text, the discussions concerning certain books lasted a long time, continuing at least until the compilation of the Mishnah. And discussions continued even after the year 200 AD. Indeed, in texts dating from after the compilation of the Mishnah, it is evident that, in addition to Ecclesiastes and the Song of Songs, the rabbis debated the sacred status of Esther and Ezekiel (accepting it) and of Sirach (rejecting it[^223]) 2.

When the text cited mentions the seventy-two elders who elected Eleazar ben Azariah as their leader, it is referring to an assembly held at Jamnia (Jamnia or Jabneh is a town on the Mediterranean coast, near present-day Tel Aviv). Johanan ben Zakkai - a rabbi who, according to legend, managed to escape from Jerusalem during the Roman siege by hiding in a coffin - obtained authorisation from the Romans to establish a school in Jamnia, which became one of the centres for the reorganisation of the Jewish people.

It is worth noting that the hypothesis - now abandoned - according to which the Jews established the canon at a 'council' or 'synod' held around the year 90 AD in Jamnia - was based fundamentally on this text from the Mishnah. However, as can be seen from the text, it is not true that a 'canon' - that is, a complete and definitive list of sacred books was established during this assembly. According to the text, the only matters discussed at Jamnia were the sacred status of Ecclesiastes and the Song of Songs. Otherwise, it is not clear why the discussions amongst the rabbis continued throughout the second century[^224].

#### 18.4.2. A baraita from the Babylonian Talmud

Once the Mishnah was published, it became the subject of commentaries. These commentaries came to be known as the 'Gemara'. Later, the Mishnah and the Gemara were compiled together, and thus the Talmud came into being (in fact, there are two: the Jerusalem Talmud and the Babylonian Talmud).

Within the Babylonian Talmud, certain texts are known as 'baraitot'. A baraita is a tradition found in the Talmud but not in the Mishnah. In other words, they are ancient texts that were excluded during the compilation of the Mishnah and were subsequently recovered.

We are interested in the baraita from the tractate Baba Batra 14b-15a, which may date from around 200 CE. The text (quoted on p. 40) lists all the books that make up the current BH, with some differences in order (it does not mention the Torah, but there was no dispute regarding these five books and their order). The fact that this ancient list was not included in the Mishnah may be an indication that it was not accepted by all rabbis at the beginning of the third century[^225].

### 18.5. The formation of the Old Testament canon in the early Church

Among the early Christian authors, we see a use of the Scriptures of Israel similar to that attested in the New Testament. They quote various books as the occasion requires, sometimes even apocryphal ones, without ever providing a list of them. For example, the same book mentioned in the Epistle of Jude - Enoch -is cited as Scripture by Tertullian and Clement of Alexandria.

#### 18.5.1. The two trends: the Hebrew canon or the 'long' canon

As it would be too lengthy to present all the testimonies of the Church Fathers and ecclesiastical writers regarding the Old Testament canon, we shall confine ourselves to providing some essential information. As a starting point, it can be said that two trends coexisted within the Church between the 2nd and 5th centuries:

- On the one hand, the influence of the Hebrew canon, which was taking shape during these years, led some to prefer a 'short' canon, that is, one excluding Tobit, Judith, 1 and 2 Maccabees, Wisdom, Sirach and Baruch.

- On the other hand, ecclesial practice, although varying according to different places and times, generally did not exclude these books.

Shortly after the mid-2nd century, in a bitterly polemical tone, Saint Justin accused the Jews of having removed certain passages from the Scriptures because they referred to Jesus (cf. Dialogue with Trypho, nos. 71 and 120).

Origen (3rd century) was aware that the Jews did not accept certain books. He states this more clearly and with greater composure than Justin, whilst sharing his fundamental approach: Origen does not attribute normative value to synagogue practice for the Church. It is worth pausing here for a moment, as some authors have sought to present Origen as a defender of the Jewish canon, which is not true.

In a text quoted by Eusebius, Origen states that the Old Testament contains only 22 books 'according to the Jews', a piece of information he appears to have taken from Flavius Josephus[^226] (1). However, nowhere in his works is there a list of the books 'according to the Christians'. Did he perhaps think that one should adhere to the Jewish canon?

In his treatise on prayer, *De Oratione*, Origen supports a teaching found in Daniel 1:24 and Tobit 1:1-2. He then points out that these texts are not accepted by the Jews and therefore adds a third, 1 Samuel 1:10-11. Here is his justification:

> But since those of the circumcision have placed an obelus in the cited text from Daniel, as if it did not exist in Hebrew, and reject the Book of Tobit as not belonging to the Testament, I shall add that of Hannah from the First Book of Kings[^227].

This passage does not mean that Origen was in favour of the Jewish canon, because in the same book he frequently uses Wisdom, Tobit and Judith, citing them as Scripture without any hesitation. He also cites 2 Maccabees on one occasion. However, if we bear in mind that *De oratione* is not a work addressed to Jews but to Christians, the passage just quoted seems to be directed at certain Christians who had doubts about the authority of those books, precisely because they were not accepted by Jewish communities[^228].

We have a more explicit text by Origen on his position regarding the extent of the Old Testament canon. In a letter to Julius Africanus (PG 11,48-85), he discusses the legitimacy of reading the story of Susanna (Dan 13), despite the fact that the Jews do not recognise it. Origen therefore defends the Church's use of certain books or, more precisely, defends the Church's independence from the synagogue in determining the canon[^229].

Alongside the line taken by Justin and Origen, there were some authors within the Church who believed that only the books recognised by the Jews should be accepted. The earliest known example is that of Melito, Bishop of Sardis at the end of the second century, who wrote a letter to another bishop, Onesimus, telling him which books constitute the Old Testament, based on his enquiries 'in the East' (Jerusalem?):

> Meliton greets his brother Onesimus. Since (…) you also wished to ascertain the exact number and order of the ancient books, I took pains to...

> to undertake such a work (…). Having travelled to the East, I went to the place where these doctrines were preached and practised, and, having learnt precisely which books constitute the Old Testament, I drew up a list and sent it to you. The titles of the books are as follows: the five books of Moses: Genesis, Exodus, Numbers, Leviticus, Deuteronomy; followed by Joshua son of Nun, Judges, Ruth, the four Books of the Kings, the two Books of Chronicles, the Psalms of David, the Proverbs of Solomon or Wisdom [zο ʎ οµĩno@ Παροιµíαι i uαì zοφíα], Ecclesiastes, the Song of Songs, Job; the prophets Isaiah, Jeremiah, the Twelve in a single book, Daniel, Ezekiel, Ezra[^230].

Curiously, Leviticus appears after the Book of Numbers. Meliton's list is not identical to the current BH, chiefly because Esther is missing. Perhaps Nehemiah is also missing, if it is not included with Ezra, and Lamentations, if they are not included with Jeremiah. Furthermore, Melito seems to confuse Wisdom and Proverbs. The total number of books is not specified: the result is 25; it should be noted that Melito counts 1-2 Samuel and 1-2 Kings as four books of Kings, which corresponds to the Greek tradition, not the Jewish one, whilst he follows the Jews in considering the Twelve Prophets as a single book. Finally, he makes no explicit mention of the Jews, perhaps because he did not wish to do so, or perhaps because he learnt this list not from them, but from Christians living in Palestine[^231].

Similar, but more explicit, accounts appear in the fourth century. At this time, as we have seen, a single manuscript could contain the entire Bible. It was also the century in which the Church enjoyed, for the first time, a long period of peace and freedom throughout the Roman Empire, with almost no persecution. Against this backdrop, the problem arose of clarifying which books belonged to the Scriptures.

Around the year 150, Saint Cyril of Jerusalem taught catechumens that there were 22 books of the Old Testament handed down by the apostles and provided a list based on the Jewish method of counting (uαρ' Épραíοι@). He presents Ruth as part of Judges and, as works associated with Jeremiah, not only Lamentations but also Baruch and the Letter of Jeremiah-that is, two writings absent from the current BH (Catechesis IV, 11-16).

A few years later, in Letter s9 of the year 367 (text in EB 14-15), Saint Athanasius, Bishop of Alexandria and Doctor of the Church, writes:

> The total number of books of the Old Testament is therefore twenty-two. I have in fact heard that this is the number of texts handed down among the Jews. Here they are in their order and by their names.

The list follows, though it does not entirely correspond to the BH: Esther is missing, whilst Baruch and the Letter of Jeremiah are included, counted as a single book together with Jeremiah, to which Lamentations is also added. At the end of the letter, Athanasius adds that Wisdom, Sirach, Esther, Judith, Tobit, the Didache and the Shepherd are not canonical, but are recommended for reading.

St Jerome should also be placed in the tradition of Cyril of Jerusalem and Athanasius; we shall discuss him later (cf. pp. 192-194).

On the other hand, St Augustine is to be placed in the tradition of Justin and Origen. The canonical Scriptures are to be determined according to the usage of the various churches, especially the most authoritative ones:

> [The reader] shall follow the authority of the majority of Catholic churches, above all those which have had the privilege of being the seats of the apostles and of receiving their letters. In this regard, he shall observe the rule of giving precedence to the canonical Scriptures accepted by all Catholic churches over those which some do not accept. Among those which are not accepted by all, he shall give precedence to those accepted by the more numerous and authoritative churches over those which have been accepted by a smaller number of churches of lesser authority. If, however, he should find that some are accepted by more churches, whilst others are accepted by more authoritative churches - though such a case ought not to arise -, I believe he must regard these Scriptures as possessing equal authority[^232].

Having set out these criteria, it is hardly surprising that the list proposed by Augustine in the following paragraph is the 'long' one, that is, the one which includes the books not accepted by the Jews[^233] (cf. *De doctrina christiana* II, 8, 13). It should be added that, as far as can be seen from his works, Augustine was never aware of the differences with the canon accepted by the Jews 2.

It was precisely during Augustine's lifetime that a number of official decisions by the Church's magisterium regarding the biblical canon appeared, as already mentioned in connection with the New Testament (pp. 161-164). The provincial councils of Hippo (year 193, cf. EB 16) and Carthage (197), the letter from Pope Innocent I dated 405 (cf. EB 21) and a third council at Carthage

(419) propose the complete list of Old Testament books, which would be adopted by the Council of Florence a thousand years later. For a universal decision with the character of a definition of faith, one must wait until the Council of Trent, which we shall discuss on pp. 198-201.

#### 18.5.2. St Jerome and the 'Hebraica veritas'

In the history of the Old Testament canon, St Jerome[^234] played a fundamental role. As we have seen in relation to the Vulgate (pp. 133-143), Jerome did not translate the Old Testament from the Greek, but from the original Hebrew, thereby going strongly against the trend of his time. We can now add that this principle of following the hebraica veritas was not limited to the original language of the books, but also applied to the canon. Jerome believed that the Church should accept only the 24 books of the Jewish Bible. The most important text in this regard is the preface to his translation of the Books of Samuel and Kings - known as the *prologus galeatus* - which serves as an introduction to the entire translation project[^235].

In this text, Jerome explains that the Jews have only 22 books, a number corresponding to the letters of the Hebrew alphabet, and adds that this number is equivalent to 24, because when counted as 22, Ruth is included within the Book of Judges and Lamentations within Jeremiah (cf. Prologus in libro Regum, Vg Weber 364-365, nos. 23-47). St Jerome then states:

> This prologue to the Scriptures may serve as a shield for all the books we have translated into Latin from the Hebrew, so that we may know that those books which lie outside it are to be regarded as apocryphal fi. Therefore, the Wisdom commonly attributed to Solomon, the Book of Jesus son of Sirach, Judith, Tobit and the Shepherd are not part of the canon. I have also found the First Book of Maccabees in Hebrew; the Second, however, is in Greek, as can also be deduced from the style itself[^236].

The mention of the Shepherd of Hermas, a second-century Christian work, is out of place, as it has nothing to do with the Old Testament canon, but rather with that of the New Testament. Furthermore, there is no reference- I refer to the Book of Baruch and the Letter of Jeremiah. The criterion, however, is clear: for Jerome, the authentic books are those of the Hebrew canon; the rest are to be considered 'apocryphal', that is, non-canonical.

A few years later, in the prologue to Proverbs, the Song of Songs and Ecclesiastes, Saint Jerome reiterates this view. After stating that Wisdom and Sirach are not found among the Jews, he explains:

> Just as the Church certainly reads the books of Judith, Tobit and the Maccabees, but does not accept them as canonical writings; so too may it read these two volumes [Wisdom and Sirach] for the edification of the people, but not to confirm the authority of ecclesiastical dogmas[^237].

It is surprising that Jerome presents as the Church's position what was in fact his own opinion, which is, moreover, less clear in other texts. Indeed, at times St Jerome seems to forget the 'hebraica veritas '. For example, when commenting on Is 1:12, the phrase 'Scriptura sacra' is followed by a quotation from Sirach[^238]. And with regard to Jer 1:7, he quotes a text from the Book of Wisdom as 'another prophet speaking[^239]'!.

One must bear in mind that, in Jerome's time, the Church had not yet defined the canon. Perhaps he was a little imprudent in presenting a personal opinion as if it were common doctrine, but he is certainly no heretic! Despite his view on the canon, Jerome is not a Lutheran before his time, because he does not question the authority of the Church's magisterium. His focus on the text and the Jewish canon goes hand in hand with a reading of the Scriptures that always seeks to remain Christian and ecclesial:

> It is incumbent upon us to interpret the Scriptures as they are read in the Church, whilst not omitting the Jewish truth[^240].

After some initial resistance, Jerome's translation was received with great esteem in the centuries that followed. However, the canonical aspect of the hebraica veritas has not enjoyed similar success, as can be seen from the inclusion in the Vulgate of books that Jerome did not accept. Paradoxically, the principal Vulgate manuscripts contain the very books that Jerome did not consider canonical! Tobit and Judith were translated by Jerome himself, at the request of two bishops and perhaps also because he appreciated these two books[^241]. He also translated the so-called 'Greek parts' of Daniel and Esther. However, for the other five books (1-2 Maccabees, Sirach, Wisdom, Baruch), earlier Latin translations were used and incorporated into the Vulgate. The desire to include those books prevailed over the personal opinion of the great translator[^242].

## 19. The modern debate surrounding the canon

Jerome's view on the canon continued to influence certain Christian intellectuals - few in number but significant - right up to the Council of Trent. Hugh of Saint Victor (†1141), John of Salisbury (†1180), Hugh of Saint Caro, O.P. (†1261), Nicholas of Lira,

O.F.M. (†1140), St Antoninus of Florence, O.P. (†1459) and, shortly before Trent, Tommaso De Vio Gaetano, O.P. (†1534) distinguished between the 22 books of the Jews and the others, which were 'useful' or 'edifying' but not strictly canonical[^243].

The Old Testament books which Luther, as we shall see, would describe as 'apocryphal' had a long history of debate and doubt regarding their canonicity, which explains why Luther's view on the matter met with some success.

### 19.1. Luther and the Canon

The Protestant principle of sola Scriptura (cf. above, pp. 86-88) necessarily requires that a doctrinal core be found within Scripture itself, one that allows the rest of biblical teachings to be judged, thereby fulfilling the role of the external authority that has been rejected.

Luther identifies this criterion in Jesus Christ, in that he saves through faith. There is a centre to Scripture: Jesus crucified and risen. He is the standard, the 'canon'.

The search for a core of Scripture that holds greater authority than the rest, and provides the key to its interpretation, has implications for the determination of the biblical canon. Luther asserts that the true substance and essence of all the biblical books is found in the Gospel of John and his First Epistle, in the Pauline epistles - especially Romans, Galatians and Ephesians - and in 1 Peter[^244].

By contrast, other New Testament books, such as Hebrews, Revelation, James and Jude, belong to a secondary category. In the preface to the Epistles of James and Jude, for his German translation, Luther expresses himself clearly:

> All true sacred books agree on this, in that they always preach and teach Christ. This is also the true touchstone for evaluating all books: to see whether they teach Christ or not, since the whole of Scripture reveals Christ (Romans 3), and St Paul wants to know of nothing but Christ; cf. 1 Corinthians 2(2). Whatever does not teach Christ is not apostolic, even if St Peter or St Paul were to say it. Conversely, whatever preaches Christ is apostolic, even if Judas, Annas, Pilate and Herod were to do so. But this James does nothing but insist on the law and works[^245].

The Epistle of James cannot be apostolic - argues Luther - because it does not teach Christ. The criterion of canonicity does not depend on the authority of the Church, nor on liturgical usage, nor on apostolic tradition, but on the content of the text interpreted according to the principle of justification in Christ through faith alone. Ultimately, authority is transferred to the individual interpreter.

This ranking of the books has consequences for the biblical canon and, more radically, for the unity of the entire Bible. When Scripture is separated from the tradition of the Church, one ends up, after a long and complex but inevitable process, destroying the unity of Scripture itself[^246].

Several centuries after Luther, the problem would arise concerning the value of the Old Testament. At the end of the eighteenth century, Johann Salomon Semler (1725-1791), a leading exponent of the Enlightenment rationalism, he rejected the 'crude Jewish prejudices' found in much of the Old Testament and proposed creating an abridged version[^247]. Semler was the first author to call the biblical canon into question: it is a list of books arrived at belatedly for contingent historical reasons, and therefore its authority must be relativised[^248].

It is hardly surprising that, later on, liberal Protestantism went so far as to demand that the Old Testament be dispensed with entirely, as a necessity for the evolution of the Christian spirit. Adolf von Harnack (1851-1930) stated this unequivocally:

> The thesis to be argued below is as follows: rejecting the Old Testament in the second century was an error which the Catholic Church rightly avoided. Retaining it in the sixteenth century was an inevitability from which the Reformer was unable to escape. But continuing to retain it even in the nineteenth century as a canonical document within Protestantism is the consequence of religious and ecclesiastical paralysis[^249].

As is the case with many of Luther's followers, the principle of sola Scriptura leads to the rejection of that of tota Scriptura, and this applies not only to the Old Testament but also to the New. Harnack himself was the first author to draw attention to the early Catholicism present in many New Testament texts. The same observation reappears in the work of Rudolph Bultmann (1884-196). Realising this led Heinrich Schleiermacher, a disciple of Bultmann, to join the Catholic Church. In contrast, another of Bultmann's disciples, Ernst Käsemann, proposed-following an idea of Schleiermacher-the need to establish a 'canon within the canon' in order to remain faithful to Lutheranism. According to Käsemann, the New Testament is not the foundation of the Church's unity; on the contrary, the multiplicity of Christian denominations arises from the New Testament itself. In his view, within the New Testament there is a form of proto-Catholicism, represented by Luke-Acts, 2 Peter and, above all, the Pastoral Epistles; a form of proto-Protestantism (Romans, Galatians); and also a form of proto-Orthodoxy (the Johannine writings).

Part of the current debate on the biblical canon revolves around this issue of the possibility and legitimacy of establishing a canon within the canon. Or, to put it another way, in current discussions concerning the canon, an important point concerns the unity of the New Testament and the possibility of distinguishing different traditions within it, which According to some, these would be incompatible with one another and would justify the current division at among Christians.

### 19.2. The Biblical Canon among Protestants

In his German translation of the New Testament, Luther placed Hebrews, James, Jude and Revelation in an appendix. Luther's low regard for these four books stems from his understanding of faith in Christ, as discussed above. On this point, however, Luther's view did not gain any traction: all Protestant Bibles present the 27 books of the New Testament in the usual order[^250].

As for the Old Testament, Luther separated certain books, placing them in an appendix under the heading 'Apocrypha'. In his view, these books are not inspired, but are useful for edification. In the list of apocryphal books, he mentions Tobit, Judith, 1 and 2 Maccabees, Wisdom, Sirach and Baruch (with the Letter of Jeremiah), plus the Greek portions of Daniel and Esther. However, he also includes the Prayer of Manasseh in the main text, which is curiously absent from the list. All Protestant denominations have followed Luther in this choice, either by placing these books in an appendix under the heading 'Apocrypha' (the most common approach), or by excluding them entirely from their Bibles.

The question to ask is why Luther and the other Reformers denied the canonicity-and therefore the inspiration-of those books and parts of the Old Testament. It is not possible to invoke the principle of justification by faith in Christ apart from the works of the law, which served to dismiss Hebrews, James, Jude and the Book of Revelation, because then all the Old Testament books would have to be excluded! Without going into the specific motivations of each reformer, those books and passages share the common feature that they are not part of the Bible. The argument thus invoked (at least implicitly) by Protestants is the same as that already found in other authors, especially St Jerome (cf. pp. 192-194), and can be summarised as follows: since the Church received the Old Testament from Israel, it must conform to the canon in force amongst the Jews.

Before explaining the problems with this argument, it is necessary to understand the Catholic Church's response to the reformers regarding the canon, as set out by the Council of Trent.

### 19.3. The Biblical Canon at the Council of Trent (1546)

At the fourth session of the Council of Trent, on 8 April 1546, the Council Fathers approved two decrees concerning the Bible. The first of these (cited more than once by Dei Verbum) deals with the canonical Scriptures and provides a list of them[^251].

#### 19.3.1. The list

In the section on the canon, the decree presents the list of books with these words:

> To avoid any doubt regarding the books recognised by this Council, it deemed it appropriate to append the list to this decree (EB 57).

This is followed by the list of books. I quote the text in the original Latin, which is easy to understand:

> Sunt vero infrascripti. Testaments of the Old Testament: the five books of Moses, namely, Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy; Joshua, Judges, Ruth, the four Books of Kings, the two Books of Chronicles, Ezra the First and the Second (also known as Nehemiah), Tobit, Judith, Esther, Job, the Psalter of David comprising one hundred and fifty psalms, the Parables, Ecclesiastes, The Song of Songs, Wisdom, Sirach, Isaiah, Jeremiah with Baruch, Ezekiel, Daniel, the twelve minor prophets, namely: Hosea, Joel, Amos, Obadiah, Jonah, Micah, Nahum, Habakkuk, Zephaniah, Haggai, Zechariah, Malachi, the two Books of Maccabees, the First and the Second.

> The New Testament: the four Gospels, according to Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John; the Acts of the Apostles, written by Luke the Evangelist; the fourteen epistles of the Apostle Paul: to the Romans, two to the Corinthians, to the Galatians, to the Ephesians, to the Philippians, to the Colossians, two to the Thessalonians, two to Timothy, to Titus, to Philemon, and to the Hebrews; two epistles of Peter the Apostle, three of John the Apostle, one of James the Apostle, one of Jude the Apostle, and the Apocalypse of John the Apostle (EB 58-59).

Before explaining the scope of this decree, two brief observations are in order:

- the Lamentations are understood to be included with Jeremiah.

- the intention of the Council Fathers was to provide a precise list of the books, not to define who their authors were. References to Moses or David are ways of identifying which books are being discussed. The same applies to the inclusion of the Epistle to the Hebrews amongst the writings of St Paul, and to the other authors' names that appear.

Firstly, it should be noted that the decree makes no distinction between the books other than the general one between the Old and New Testaments. This is a deliberate and therefore significant omission. Indeed, the Council's proceedings reveal that some Council Fathers had proposed distinguishing between different classes of books, as they considered that not all of them enjoyed the same authority. Pietro Bertano O.P., Bishop of Fano, and Girolamo Seripando, General of the Hermits of St Augustine and a theologian of substance, wished to distinguish between two types of books, following St Jerome: on the one hand, the 'authentic and canonical' books, upon which the faith depends, and, on the other, the 'merely canonical' books, useful for teaching and reading[^252].

As can be seen, there was still debate amongst Catholics regarding the precise boundaries of the canon. The Council Fathers rejected this proposal and others like it. Furthermore, they added a sentence at the beginning of the decree stating that the Council accepts and venerates all the books pari pietatis agectu ac reverentia, 'with equal piety and veneration' (EB 57).

##### 19.3.2.!.2. The anathema

After listing the books, the Tridentine decree adds a condemnation or anathema:

> If anyone, then, does not accept these books as sacred and canonical, in their entirety and with all their parts, as they are customarily read in the Catholic Church and as they are found in the ancient edition of the Latin Vulgate, and knowingly disregards the aforementioned traditions: let him be anathema (EB 60[^253]) 2.

In these terms, the biblical canon becomes the subject of a dogmatic definition. To be in communion with the Church, one must accept all the books listed as sacred and canonical by the Council of Trent.

The text speaks of 'books in their entirety, with all their parts'. It is necessary to explain what these words refer to. Thanks to the acts of the Council, we know that the Fathers debated certain 'parts' of the New Testament: the ending of Mark (Mk 16:9-20), the angel comforting Jesus and the sweat of blood (Lk 22:43-44), and the episode of the adulterous woman in John 7:53-8, 11. These verses gave rise to doubts regarding their canonicity, not because of their content, but for reasons of textual criticism: they are, in fact, absent from many ancient manuscripts. The Council Fathers did not wish to mention these passages explicitly in the decree, so as not to cause confusion or scandal amongst the laity.
