But it is clear that they refer to these and only these[^254]. Therefore, the Tridentine declaration does not apply a priori to all passages about which doubts exist, such as John 5:4 (the angel who descended to stir the waters of the pool of Bethesda), 1 John 5:y (the three witnesses in heaven) or the prologue to Sirach (it is debatable whether this forms part of the book or not, and therefore whether it is inspired). Trent seeks to defend the integrity of the Bible, but each case must then be examined individually to determine whether a particular text truly forms part of it.

To provide a criterion for the integrity in question, the text refers to the books 'as they are customarily read in the Catholic Church and as they appear in the ancient Latin Vulgate edition'. Perrella comments:

> the two phrases should not be taken in a disjunctive sense, as if they were two different criteria, but in a conjunctive sense. In fact, both express in concrete terms the same reality, namely the practice of the Church, that is, the Church's use of the book (or passage) in question[^255].

Ultimately, taking the practice of the Church (that is, the universal use of the books) as a criterion is a way of referring to the living tradition. Trent seeks to do nothing other than proclaim the faith of all ages. The only true innovation of the Council - which is no small matter - lies in the solemn and definitive authority with which it presents the biblical canon.

Indeed, if one asks on what criteria Trent bases its proposal for the list of books, the fundamental criterion is none other than the tradition of the Church, expressed in its practice (especially in the liturgy, but not exclusively) and formulated by its magisterium. In the acts of Trent, there is no discussion of a historical or theological nature concerning the canonicity of this or that book. In fact, it was not a matter of resolving a problem- technical problem, typical of specialists, but rather to clarify a fundamental aspect of the life of the Church. As is always the case with declarations of the Magisterium[^256], Trent did not wish to introduce innovation; that is to say, it did not claim to define the canon from scratch, but rather to confirm the Church's established practice, in order to avoid doubts.

For this reason, the list is deliberately identical to that proposed a century earlier by the Council of Florence (or, more precisely, the Council of Basel-Ferrara-Florence, 1438-1445), which in turn reiterates earlier declarations of the Magisterium 1.

With regard to these pre-Tridentine magisterial declarations, a quite reasonable question may arise concerning the context of the Tridentine decree on the canon. Why were there still doubts within the Church regarding the canonicity of certain books, if an ecumenical council had already defined the canon? The Council of Florence had mentioned the biblical books in the profession of faith, within the decree of union with the so-called 'Jacobites' (an Eastern Christian community), because they had more books (cf. EB 47). However, this profession of faith was made after the departure of the Greeks who had taken part in the council. Consequently, some questioned whether the text was actually part of the ecumenical council. Furthermore, the Florence definition says nothing about a possible distinction between two classes of canonical books. This is why the Council of Florence had failed to dispel all doubts amongst Catholics[^257].

### 19.4. Conclusion: who establishes the canon and why?

The diversity of opinion within the Church on a matter as important as that of the biblical canon may give rise to some perplexity. The study of this process raises various questions of a historical and theological nature.

Without claiming to exhaust the subject, a few key points may be highlighted:

- As has been stated on several occasions, Christianity is not a religion of the book, but of a person, Jesus Christ. Therefore, neither the inspiration nor the canon of Scripture, whilst important, constitutes the central object of our faith. The earliest doctrinal clarifications and the first dogmatic definitions relate to the

the person of Jesus, the Son of God, both in relation to the other persons of the Trinity (the Councils of Nicaea and Constantinople) and in the explanation of the union in Christ of the two natures, human and divine (the Councils of Ephesus and Chalcedon).

- Even the most important dogmas of the Christian faith, such as the mystery of the Holy Trinity or the union of Christ's two natures, have been the subject of arduous and complex discussions; in comparison, the differing opinions on the canonicity of certain books represent a much lesser problem.

- The Church's work in determining the Old Testament canon did not start from scratch, but from a fairly solid tradition, that of Israel. In the historical overview provided so far, we have emphasised the diversity among movements, authors and communities at the time of Jesus. Yet a common foundation shared by all is undeniable. It is true that the Church did not receive a defined and definitive canon of scriptures from Israel; nevertheless, it did receive certain collections of writings (the Torah and the Prophets) that were relatively well-established. The doubts and discussions always relate to specific books, not to all of them.

- The supreme authority in the Church is Jesus Christ, in whom the Scriptures are fulfilled, as we saw in the first part. Therefore, a clear criterion for rejecting certain books depended on discerning their incompatibility with the person and teaching of Jesus. Indeed, some writings presented a conception of God or the Messiah that was clearly incompatible with the work and mission of Jesus (for example, the Psalms of Solomon present a nationalistic and violent form of messianism).

The position of the defenders of the Hebrew canon did not prevail, which shows that the Church determined the canon by placing greater trust in its own living tradition than in external factors. Indeed, the Church recognises the canon through its tradition. It is not the Magisterium that arbitrarily and externally declares which books are to be regarded as sacred; the Magisterium's task is to clarify to the faithful which books are accepted by tradition as inspired. The canon is not the result of an authoritarian decision, but of the very life of the Church[^258].

At the same time, it must be recognised that the Magisterium has the authority to have the 'final say' on the canon, in two senses: on the one hand, its pronouncements are more than mere opinions, because they enjoy apostolic authority and the assistance of the Holy Spirit (to a degree that varies according to their universality and solemnity), and they are therefore capable of putting an end to doubts and debates; on the other hand, it is also a 'final word' in that it is preceded by a long process in which the whole Church participates to a certain extent.

> Ultimately, what is at stake in the determination and establishment of the biblical canon as such, and what ultimately guarantees its stability, is the authority received from the apostles and present in the Magisterium of the Church. At the same time, however, the process of canon formation reveals that, alongside the element of authority inherent in the Church's Magisterium - which, with regard to the establishment of the canon, only becomes explicitly apparent at the end of the process - there are other factors such as common acceptance, liturgical use and the consensus of the Churches. These factors demonstrate the involvement of the whole Church in the determination of the canon[^259].

To summarise the process of the formation of the Christian biblical canon, I reproduce here a paragraph from the 1993 PCB document, which also ties in with what we have said above regarding the criteria for canonicity:

> Numerous factors influenced this process: the certainty that Jesus, and the apostles with him, had recognised the Old Testament as inspired Scripture and that its Paschal mystery constituted its fulfilment; the conviction that the New Testament writings authentically stem from apostolic preaching (which does not imply that they were all composed by the apostles themselves); the observation of their use in Christian liturgy and their conformity with the rule of faith; and, finally, the experience of their harmony with the ecclesial life of the communities and their capacity to nourish that life[^260].

# PART IV INTERPRETATION

Then the Spirit said to Philip, 'Go forward and draw near to that chariot.' Philip ran ahead and, hearing that he was reading the prophet Isaiah, said to

him: 'Do you understand what you are reading?' He replied, 'How can I, unless someone guides me?' And he invited Philip to come up and sit beside him. Acts 8:29-11

Having described Sacred Scripture in relation to revelation-as the inspired Word of God-and in its material form (canon and text), the most important, yet also the most difficult, task remains: that of reading it and understanding what it means. This part of the course aims to offer some principles and reflections on interpretation in general and on the interpretation of the Bible within the Church.

The interpretation of Scripture has many links to very concrete contemporary issues. One need only think of the many literalist interpretations put forward by sects or similar groups, such as the Jehovah's Witnesses, or of ideologically driven readings, such as those inspired by radical feminism or materialism.

How should the Bible be understood? What consequences does the inspired nature of the texts have for their interpretation? Is Scripture a book like any other, insofar as it is subject to interpretation? With what mindset should one approach the sacred text? Does one need faith to understand the biblical books correctly, or is it enough to be a competent and honest reader? Is it right to interpret certain passages symbolically, as allegories, or must everything be taken literally? And what does it mean to

'literally'?

Alongside these fundamental questions, other topics in biblical hermeneutics include the history of exegesis, the definition and classification of the meanings of Scripture (literal and spiritual meanings), the importance of tradition and the Magisterium for the interpretation of the Bible within the Church, and the various methods and approaches to interpretation.

This section is intended to address these topics. However, the time available does not permit a systematic and comprehensive presentation of all of them, but only of a few general principles. The author of these pages takes comfort in the thought that the interpretation of Scripture is truly understood when it is put into practice. There will be oc opportunities for further study in the biblical courses included in the Bachelor's degree in Theology.

## 20. The Interpretation of Texts in General

As with many other theological topics, a philosophical introduction is useful, or perhaps even necessary, in this case too. Therefore, before discussing the specific characteristics of biblical interpretation, we shall reflect on what it means to interpret in general.

### 20.1. What does it mean to interpret?

If we look up 'interpretare' in an Italian dictionary, we will find several meanings. Here are the four given in the Garzanti Dictionary:

> 1 to make something that is or seems obscure understandable and clear: to interpret a text, a document, an inscription; to interpret correctly or arbitrarily

> 2 to give an explanation or meaning to something: to interpret behaviour, an event | to intuit or grasp the deeper meaning of something: to interpret someone's desires | (psychoanal.) to offer an interpretation: to interpret a dream, an unconscious conflict, a symptom

> ! to play a part or role in a play or film: to perform in a play; to play Hamlet, Ophelia | (mus.) to perform: to perform a piano sonata; to perform Mozart, Bach

> 4 (law) to ascertain the exact meaning of legal provisions in order to apply them to specific cases.

Behind this diversity of meanings lies a fundamental unity. Put simply, to interpret is to understand and to communicate. Indeed, in every case, 'interpreting' involves understanding something in depth - a text, a behaviour, a dream, a musical score, a law - to the point where one can explain it to another person or apply it correctly. To interpret is to assimilate something 'strange' to the point of making it one's own. In a sense, it can be said that interpretation is present throughout human existence, in as human beings are spiritual beings, capable of knowing themselves and the world around them, and who regulate their own actions according to the idea they have of themselves and others.

However, we speak of interpretation primarily in relation to the reading and understanding of written texts. This is the meaning of particular interest here, because Scripture is a collection of texts or, more precisely, a collection of interconnected literary works. For this reason, it will be essential to clarify the object of interpretation (the concept of text) and the means by which it is carried out (general principles, exegetical methods).

As we saw in the first part of the course, within the Church, Scripture is received as the word of God. Therefore, when reading it, Christians seek to discern what God wishes to communicate to them. For this reason, the interpretation of the Bible must take into account both the principles of interpreting literary texts and those of communication. Let us recall the fundamental model of communication:

writer → message ‖ message → reader

This model shows that interpretation is the task of the recipient, who must decipher the message sent to them by the sender. When reading a text - which is when communication takes place - the reader or listener must seek to 'reconstruct', so to speak, what the author intended to say. When reading, the reader recreates the reality, idea or feeling that the author expressed through their words. In simple terms, we can say that the interpretation of a text consists of reconstructing what the author intended to communicate through the text.

The various exegetical methods aim to bridge the gap between the act of writing and the act of reading. The purpose of exegesis is to provide the conditions so that the meaning reconstructed during reading comes as close as possible to that present in the original utterance of the message. However, it must be borne in mind that, in ancient texts, the notions of 'author' and 'original utterance' are not as straightforward as they might seem at first glance. We shall return to this later, in connection with the definition of 'text' (cf. pp. 218-219).

At the same time, it is worth always bearing in mind that, despite the rigour of exegetical methods, total transparency is unattainable. Interpretation can never be a completely methodical and objective process, because behind every interpretation there is an interpreter, a human being immersed in a specific culture, who occupies a different position from that of the author of the message, who is also a human being historically conditioned. For this reason, there is no interpretation of a text that can be considered unique and definitive in the strictest sense-that is, valid for everyone and for all time in every respect-just as there can never be a total understanding between two human beings, such as that which exists between the three Persons of the Trinity. Each of us is a mystery even to ourselves, which does not mean that it is impossible to arrive at the truth. We shall return to this point in connection with philosophical hermeneutics (pp. 208-211).

Furthermore, in order to overcome the dead ends to which rationalism has led, we must reclaim the personal, volitional and affective-and therefore not merely intellectual-dimension of all human knowledge. We know from experience that it becomes easier to understand a message when the subject matter interests us existentially, or when we care for the person addressing us. 'Understanding (…) is the encounter, the closeness, the affinity, the communion with the speaker, with their intentionality, their world, their message. And the hermeneutical principle par excellence is r love[^261]' 1.

### 20.2. Some examples of difficult texts

The difficulty of a text can stem from various causes. Augustine (De doctrina christiana II,15) divided difficult texts into two groups: obscure passages (i.e. those that cannot be understood) and ambiguous passages, which can be understood in different ways. Here is an example of an ambiguous biblical passage:

> Seeing the crowds, Jesus went up the mountain; he sat down, and his disciples came to him. He began to speak and taught them, saying: 'Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven' (Mt 5:1-3).

The ambiguity concerns the intended audience. To whom is Jesus addressing the Sermon on the Mount? Only to the disciples? We might attempt to answer this with theological arguments, but there is no need, because, if we continue reading, the text itself resolves the ambiguity:

> When Jesus had finished these words, the crowds were amazed at his teaching (Mt 7:28).

It is clear, then, that Jesus had not gone up the mountain to get away from the crowds and that he had been speaking to the multitude from the very beginning. This example also illustrates one of the fundamental rules of interpreting any text: to resolve a difficulty in understanding, the first source to which one must turn is the text itself (we shall discuss the criteria for determining what constitutes a text on pp. 218-219).

Let us now look at an example of an obscure passage that is difficult to understand. At the beginning of the Epistle to the Romans, St Paul says:

> For in it [the Gospel] the righteousness of God is revealed, from faith to faith (έu uíστsm@ sí@ πίστιν), as it is written: 'The righteous shall live by faith' (Rom 1:17; the quotation comes from Hab 2:4).

The text presents no problems of textual criticism or grammar, but it does raise questions of interpretation. What does 'from faith to faith' mean? From the faith of the Jews to that of the Gentiles (cf. Rom 1:16)? From the faith of the Law to that of the Gospel (Origen's interpretation)? From the faith of the preacher to that of the listeners (St Augustine)? From the faith of the new believer to that of the mature believer (Luther)? From the faith of God to the faith of man (Karl Barth)?

An analysis of the letter's rhetorical structure shows that Rom 1:16-17 constitutes the propositio, that is, the thesis to be defended. In Paul's time, it was customary to use elliptical language when stating the propositio, with the aim of raising questions in the minds of the addressees. We can therefore say that in this case the ambiguity is deliberate and must remain so, at least at this stage of reading the letter[^262].

If we wish to derive a rule from this example, we can speak of the need to be familiar with literary conventions. Paul was familiar with the rules of classical rhetoric and, with a certain degree of freedom, followed them. The development of the argument in Romans is a clear example of this. We shall discuss these conventions and literary genres at in connection with DV12 (pp. 216-218).

### 20.3. Philosophical hermeneutics

To round off this discussion on interpretation in general, it is worth mentioning so-called 'philosophical hermeneutics', as it forms the basis of the considerations just made, even though this discussion moves away from the strictly biblical sphere[^263].

The practice of interpreting texts has always existed. Although the term 'hermeneutics' is a modern one, its principles had already been highlighted in antiquity, particularly by St Augustine in *De doctrina christiana*. Firstly, he advises the reader to approach the text with faith and humility (subjective dispositions); he then adds several practical rules: a thorough knowledge of languages and their expressions (philological competence), an understanding of the realities referred to in the text (erudition, knowledge of the so-called auxiliary sciences, such as history, archaeology, geography, etc.), understanding obscure passages by means of clearer ones, first within the same book, then throughout Scripture. In the 17th century, the term 'hermeneutics' began to be used to refer to the emerging art or technique of interpreting texts, which sought to develop certain rules for correctly understanding texts. This hermeneutics, as ars interpretandi, arose in two distinct contexts: the legal and the biblical. Indeed, both legal and biblical texts are normative; that is to say, their interpretation has immediate practical consequences. Whether a person is found guilty or innocent, or whether they go to prison or not, depends on the interpretation of a law. The validity of a particular Christian doctrine or practice, the distinction between an orthodox and a heretical thesis, or the choice of a way of life may depend on the interpretation of a biblical passage.

Underlying the emergence of this *ars interpretandi* is the Lutheran principle of *sola Scriptura*. Luther and the other Reformers had undermined confidence in tradition (see above, pp. 86-88), which until then had been regarded as the fundamental criterion of interpretation. Objective criteria were therefore needed to determine precisely the meaning of normative texts. The main purpose of the *ars interpretandi* was to help the interpreter resolve difficult cases.

So-called 'philosophical hermeneutics', as a general theory of understanding, emerged in Germany in the early nineteenth century[^264], within the context of the Romantic movement. By the end of the eighteenth century, an intellectual reaction had taken shape against the objectivism of the sciences and literary classicism, though always within the framework of the rationalism inherited from the Enlightenment. This reaction, known as Romanticism, sought to restore the value of the individual, of emotions, of mysteries, of artistic inspiration, of legends and folk traditions - in short, of everything that does not belong to the sphere of the rational-universal, reduced to physical and mathematical science. From the Romantic mindset arose a renewed interest in the human past which had been disparaged by the myth of progress - underpinning the great development of archaeology, history and philology in the nineteenth century 1.

One of the leading figures of the Romantic movement, F. Schleiermacher (1768-1834), a Protestant pastor and translator of Plato, is regarded as the father or founder of philosophical hermeneutics, in that he extended the art of hermeneutics to all texts (that is to say

(not only the difficult ones), because he sought a science of understanding in general. Schleiermacher's ideas were developed by W. Dilthey (1833-1911), but philosophical hermeneutics is associated above all with the thought of H. G. Gadamer (1900-2002). With him, the universalisation of hermeneutics envisaged by Schleiermacher reaches its zenith, in that it is no longer a prescriptive technique on how to interpret, as in Augustine or in the *ars interpretandi*, but becomes a philosophical reflection on what it means to understand, since interpreting and understanding are not two distinct operations, but one and the same.

Gadamer emphasises the necessary involvement of the subject and their point of view in every act of interpretation. This leads him to rediscover the value of tradition and to recognise the inevitable presence of prejudices. All human knowledge - which is an interpretation - involves an interpreter, who occupies a position not of their own choosing, even if they may assume it consciously. There is no 'point zero' upon which all knowledge can be founded, as Descartes maintained. Like his mentor Heidegger, Gadamer is highly critical of modernity and its ideal of scientific objectivity.

A significant consequence of Gadamer's thought is that, to better understand a text, it is advisable to study how it has been understood by others, in other eras. Gadamer introduced the concept of *Wirkungsgeschichte* or 'history of the text's effects', which, in the biblical context, has led to a re-evaluation of the history of exegesis. It is also reasonable to argue that an interpretation is never definitive, not because truth is unattainable, but because each generation returns to the texts with new questions.

On the other hand, Gadamer's proposal has the limitation of leaving little scope for methods, that is, for the possibility of interpreting texts according to objective rules[^265].

It is no coincidence, then, that less positive offshoots have emerged from his work, such as Gianni Vattimo's so-called 'weak thought' or the various forms of postmodern relativism, which renounce the possibility of finding a true interpretation. In this regard, it is worth recalling what Saint John Paul II stated in the encyclical *Fides et Ratio*:

> Faith (…) clearly presupposes that human language is capable of expressing, in a universal way - albeit in analogical terms, though no less significant for that - the divine and transcendent reality (cf. Fourth Lateran Council, De errore abbatis Ioachim, II: DS 806). If this were not the case, the Word of God-which is always the divine Word in human language-would be incapable of expressing anything about God. The interpretation of this Word cannot merely lead us from one interpretation to interpretation, without ever leading us to arrive at a statement that is simply true; otherwise there would be no revelation of God, but merely the expression of human conceptions of Him and of what He presumably thinks of us[^266].

### 20.4. The distance between author and reader

We may conclude this chapter by introducing one of the most illuminating concepts developed by philosophical hermeneutics, that of 'distance'. To explain this, the etymology of the word 'hermeneutics' offers us a good starting point. In Greek mythology, the god Hermes (Éρµί@, Latin: Mercurius) served as the messenger of the gods. His task was to descend to earth to make known to mortal men the wishes of the inhabitants of Olympus, especially the will of Zeus, his father. It appears that the proper name 'Hermes' gave rise to the noun ἑρμηνεύς (hermêneus), which was applied above all to one who worked as a translator. From ἑρμηνεύς comes the verb ἑρμηνεύω (hermêneuô), which primarily means 'to translate', but also takes on the meanings of 'to express', 'to explain' and 'to interpret'.

The verb ἑρμηνεύω therefore denotes the act of taking something that is not understood and making it comprehensible. This is particularly evident in the case of someone reading a text written in a language they do not know: the need for interpretation or translation is undeniable.

But texts in an unfamiliar language are not the only instance in which a hermeneutic process is required. In fact, it is not just difficult texts, but all messages that need to be interpreted, because they all need to be understood. It is therefore no coincidence that in Classical Greek the same verb means both 'to translate' and 'to interpret'. The need for interpretative work is most evident when faced with obscure texts, but this must not lead us to forget that the act of interpretation is present in every act of understanding.

Alongside this fundamental principle of philosophical hermeneutics, we can set out another that complements it: the greater the distance between the author and the recipient, the greater the need for interpretation. The distance between author and recipient may be spatial or temporal, but what matters above all is the cultural distance. For example, each of us should be able to understand a signal such as this:

Anyone who drives a car knows that this road sign means the maximum speed limit is 60 kilometres per hour. You cannot tell a police officer that the text is ambiguous! But it should be noted that this is not a matter of understanding without any presuppositions. We are able to understand this sign because it is part of our culture to drive on roads, to be familiar with a certain system of speed measurement (km/h), to know that speed is regulated by law, and so on and so forth. A person from another era or another culture would be unable to

interpret such a complex sign.

If today I were to read a poem written in the Akkadian language in Babylon in 1000 BC, such as the *Enûma eliš*, I would need help, even if I were reading an Italian translation. And such help is necessary because the text contains references and assumptions that would be incomprehensible to me. In a sense, the number of footnotes accompanying a text serves to measure the distance that must be bridged for the message to reach the recipient correctly. Without these aids, without adequate preparation, we are unable to read ancient texts; we are, in effect, illiterate:

> One of today's problems, not only in religion, is illiteracy: there is a shortage of hermeneutical skills that would make us credible interpreters and translators of our own cultural tradition[^267].

The need to bridge this gap becomes even greater when the text speaks to me of profound, nontrivial realities which are, in themselves, difficult to understand.

Even in the case of Sacred Scripture, there is a great gulf between us, men and women of the twenty-first century, and the mindset and culture of the biblical writers. There is therefore a need for philological, historical, geographical and other explanations, without which many biblical passages would remain obscure.

Let us try to give an example of how knowledge of the cultural context can help us to understand Scripture. In Psalm 89, the psalmist addresses these words to the God of Israel:

> You subdue the pride of the sea; you still its stormy waves. You have wounded and trampled down Rahab; with a mighty arm you have scattered your enemies. Yours are the heavens, yours is the earth,

> you have founded the world and all that is in it (Ps 89:10-12).

We may also quote these words from the second part of the Book of Isaiah, which are likewise a prayer to the Lord:

> Awake, awake, clothe yourself with strength, O arm of the Lord. Awake, as in days of old, as in generations past. Was it not you who shattered Rahab, who pierced the dragon? Was it not you who dried up the sea, the waters of the great deep, and made a way through the depths of the sea, so that the redeemed might pass through? (Isaiah 51:9-10)

To understand these two texts, one must know who or what 'Raab' is. From the psalm we can deduce that Raab is an enemy of God. From the text of Isaiah, we gather that Raab is a dragon or something similar. In both cases, there is mention of a victory of the Lord over Raab following a battle. The psalm speaks of wounding and trampling, whilst Isaiah speaks of tearing to pieces and piercing.

In Mesopotamian myths about the origin of the world, Raab or Tiamat is the name of a female sea monster (a dragon), the personification of primordial chaos. Raab represents salt water, the water of the sea. In ancient Middle Eastern culture, the waters of the sea are synonymous with darkness, chaos and death. In the poem *Enûma eliš*, it is recounted that Marduk, the god of Babylon, after a fierce battle, succeeds in defeating this monster. Marduk then uses its corpse to create the world: with one half he establishes the celestial vault, whilst with the other he constructs the surface of the earth. Thus, the earth and the sky form a space amidst the waters.

In the texts cited above (Ps 89:10-12; Is 51:9-10; and cf. also Job 26:12), familiarity with this mythical account of the origin of the world is taken for granted, and it is applied to the Lord (as is also done, incidentally, in Gen 1 and Job 18). It is the God of Israel, and not Marduk, who defeated the sea, brought order out of chaos and brought light into the darkness.

Taking this background into account, a good number of biblical texts can be better understood. For example, the universal flood (Genesis 6-9) is not merely a flood, but a process of destruction that is the exact opposite of God's creative act described in Genesis 1, which consists in opening up a habitable space between the waters above and the waters below. The episode of the parting of the sea (Ex 14-15), as well as the Gospel passage on the calming of the storm (Mk 4:15-41 and parallels), should be understood as manifestations of divine dominion over the world's most powerful forces, those most hostile to God.

References to the Leviathan (Isaiah 27:1; Psalm 74:13-14; 104:26; Job 3:8; 41:1) should also be understood within this framework. The Leviathan is a sea monster, similar-if not identical-to Rahab.

In many psalms, the waters and enemies are spoken of in parallel as synonyms (cf. Ps 18, Ps 46, Ps 65, etc.). We may also recall the fear of the waters in the story of Jonah, who, in order to flee from God, is prepared to board a ship (the Hebrews were never seafarers) and ultimately has to throw himself into the sea, where he is swallowed by a great fish. In other words, because of his disobedience to God, Jonah experiences the greatest nightmare of an Israelite. The narrative aims to show how the power of the Lord extends to the depths of the sea and to the creatures that dwell there[^268].

The list of biblical texts reflecting Mesopotamian cosmogony could go on almost indefinitely. Finally, we may recall that the theology of Christian baptism takes account of the negative connotations associated with water. Water is not only a symbol of life, but also of death. This is why St Paul says that in baptism-which means 'immersion'-we have been buried with Christ (cf. Rom 6:4; Col 2:12).

On the other hand, the very tradition that has handed down these texts to us from a distant past helps us, despite the cultural distance, to understand them and even to live in accordance with them. In the examples given (Ps 89 and Is 51), the fundamental meaning of the verses can be understood even without knowledge of Babylonian mythology, because we know who the God being spoken of is and we recognise his supremacy over creation. Here it is worth recalling that understanding a text is not a purely intellectual task, but an activity that involves the whole person. If the reader has a personal relationship with God, it is clear that this will have a significant influence on the way they understand the texts that speak of him.

## 21. The Interpretation of the Bible in the Church

The fundamental principle in approaching this chapter is, once again, the analogy between the Bible and the mystery of the Incarnate Word. Scripture is the word of God and therefore requires a theological interpretation. At the same time, the books of the Bible are literary works, subject to the limitations and cultural influences inherent in the language and culture of their time, and must be understood as such. In this regard, it is worth returning to and re-reading the words of Saint John Paul II, quoted on p. 24, concerning the analogy between Scripture and the mystery of Christ, true God and true man.

### 21.1. The Church as the original locus of biblical hermeneutics

The title of this section is taken from the Apostolic Exhortation *Verbum Domini* (2010). With this expression, 'The Church: the original locus of biblical hermeneutics', Benedict XVI opens the section devoted to biblical interpretation (nos. 29-49), in which he recalls the hermeneutical value of the Church as the context or 'locus' in which the interpretation of Scripture is brought to fulfilment.

In his homily at the Mass marking his installation as Bishop of Rome, the Pope himself had already clearly expressed the same idea:

> Where Sacred Scripture is detached from the living voice of the Church, it falls prey to the disputes of experts. Certainly, everything they have to say is important and valuable; the work of scholars is of considerable help to us in understanding the living process through which Scripture has developed, and thus in grasping its historical richness. But scholarship alone cannot provide us with a definitive and binding interpretation; it is unable to give us, in interpretation, that certainty by which we can live and for which we are even willing to die. For this reason, a greater mandate is required, one that cannot spring solely from human capabilities. For this reason, the voice of the living Church is needed-that Church entrusted to Peter and to the college of apostles until the end of time (Homily in the Basilica of St John Lateran, 5 May 2005).

In reality, emphasising how the texts are interpreted within the ecclesial faith - 'the authentic hermeneutics of the Bible can only be found in the ecclesial faith' (Verbum Domini, no. 29) - is no different from taking into account the role of apostolic tradition in the formation of the deposit of revelation, which we studied in the first part.

Since in the first part we explained the interdependence between Scripture and tradition (pp. 7688), we can now focus our attention on the principles offered by the Dei Verbum n for the interpretation of the written Word of God.

### 21.2. Principles for Interpretation (DV 2)

The interpretation of Sacred Scripture is a vast field. In what follows, we shall confine ourselves to highlighting a few fundamental principles, following the guidelines set out in DV 12, which begins with these words:

> Since God has spoken in Sacred Scripture through human beings in a human manner (cf. St Augustine, De Civ. Dei, XVII, 6, 2: PL 41, 537; CSEL 40, 2,228.), the interpreter of Sacred Scripture, in order to understand properly what God wished to communicate to us, must carefully seek out what the authors of the Scriptures truly intended to say and what God was pleased to reveal through their words.

This paragraph establishes the starting point, which is linked to the understanding of Sacred Scripture as the word of God expressed in human words and to the notion of God as the author of the Bible. We discussed this in Part I and shall return to it on pp. 242-261. Furthermore, 'by speaking of 'what God wished to communicate to us' (and thus not only to the original recipients of the sacred writings), the Council paves the way for the demands of modern hermeneutics to bring the biblical message into the present day[^269]' 1.

#### 21.2.1. Literary genres and the concept of text

The second paragraph of DV12 echoes what Pius XII had taught regarding the need for the interpreter to be familiar with the literary genres in use at the time the texts were composed:

> In order to ascertain the intention of the authors of the sacred writings, one must also take into account, amongst other things, literary genres. For the truth is presented and expressed in different ways in texts that are historical, prophetic, poetic, or even in other forms of expression. It is therefore necessary for the interpreter to seek out the meaning which the sacred writer, in specific circumstances and in accordance with the conditions of his time and culture, intended to express-and did in fact express-through the literary genres in use at that time. For in order to understand precisely what the sacred author intended to assert in his writing, due attention must be paid both to the customary and distinctive ways of thinking, expressing oneself and narrating that were prevalent in the hagiographer's time, and to those that were then in use in human relations in various places.

Some commentators have observed that this paragraph of DV12 is incomplete if one wishes to regard it as an enumeration of the exegetical resources necessary for understanding the meaning of biblical texts. Indeed, many are missing, such as textual criticism, the study of languages or synchronic methods of interpretation[^270].

This observation is essentially true, whilst recognising that Dei Verbum did not intend to offer an exhaustive description and therefore states that literary genres must be taken into account 'amongst oth ro'.

a) Literary genres and modes of expression

The Bible is not a uniform and homogeneous text, but is composed of different books, each of which must be interpreted according to its own literary genre: poetic, sapien- wisdom, narrative, legal. Even within a single book we can find different genres: in Exodus we have narrative texts, legal texts, and a song (Ex 15:2-21). A comparison with the literature of other peoples can shed light on this. The Book of Job, for example, follows a wisdom-literature model-that of the suffering righteous man-which is also found in Mesopotamia and Egypt. It does not, therefore, claim to be read as a historical account, but as a reflection in narrative form.

Rather than offering a definition or classification of literary genres, it will be useful at this point to provide some examples to illustrate their interpretative value. A typical example is the words of the Miserere psalm on sacrifices. The psalmist expresses the conviction that the sacrifice most pleasing to God is the inner one, and to reinforce this message he uses antithetical terms:

> You do not delight in sacrifice; if I offer burnt offerings, you do not accept them. A contrite spirit is a sacrifice to God; a contrite and broken heart, O God, you do not despise (Ps 51 [50], 18-19).

We are dealing with a poetic text, and poetry cannot be judged by strictly logical criteria. It would therefore be a mistake to take these words as though they were an argument intended to deny the propitiatory value of the sacrifices prescribed by the Torah.

The principle of literary genres is important in relation to the claims to historicity made by certain narratives. If we open a book that begins with the words 'Once upon a time…', we immediately think of a fairy tale or some other kind of fictional story. No competent reader would think that the Good Samaritan (cf. Lk 10:25-37) or the Prodigal Son (cf. Lk 15:11-12) are historical figures, because they read those texts as 'parables' and therefore as fictional narratives. In the same vein, some books of the Old Testament contain indications of non-historicity: this is the case with Tobit and Judith, which begin by presenting an impossible setting (cf. Tob 1; Jud 1:1).

Furthermore, it should be borne in mind that, despite their diversity, the books of the Bible especially those of the Old Testament - reflect the mindset typical of the peoples of the Middle East, who are passionate and therefore prone to extremes and exaggeration. The culture of the ancient Near East is the polar opposite of the systematic, precise and dispassionate nature of Northern European culture. When, for example, figures are given - the lifespans of the patriarchs, the number of people who left Egypt, the number of enemy soldiers, and so on - this information is often not to be taken literally. Sometimes the numbers have a symbolic value; at other times, they are merely devices to heighten the intensity of the narrative:

> The Israelite historian readily employs literary devices and editorial techniques. For example, in the compilation of genealogies: in terms of content, he sometimes summarises an entire historical period (Gen 10); in terms of form, he sometimes omits names for reasons of literary effect and symmetry (Gen 5; 11:10-12; Matt 1:1-17, etc.). He uses numbers regarded as sacred, with a conventionally approximate value (e.g. seven, forty, etc.). He often disregards chronology. He reports the words of others only in substance and not verbatim; he sometimes employs the same method when quoting from other books[^271].

We shall return to the importance of literary genres later, in connection with the ity and truth of the biblical books (pp. 212-222).

b) The concept of 'text' as applied to Scripture

As a complement to DV12,2, we may add certain aspects of the notion of 'text' as it has been developed and described by linguistics. The object of biblical interpretation is, in fact, texts. An adequate understanding of what a text is offers great assistance in overcoming the fragmentation towards which historical-critical exegesis tends[^272].

The concept of 'text' can be defined in various ways, depending on the perspective: economic, sociological, structuralist, etc. Once again, it is useful for us to return to communication theory. In this context, a 'text' is the result or product of an act of production by someone who wishes to communicate something to another in writing. More succinctly, a text is a 'written message[^273]'!.

A series of disjointed words written on a sheet of paper does not constitute a text. The identification of a text as such depends on two main factors, which reflect the pragmatic act of communication: the presence of a beginning and an end, and internal coherence. The application of this concept of 'text' does not depend on its physical length: the Divine Comedy and a road sign, for example, are both texts, although they differ greatly in nature, length and quality.

In the case of Sacred Scripture, it can be said from the outset that every book is a text. The Book of Isaiah as we know it is a text, although there are good reasons to One might think that the present book is the result of a long process of composition, because it has a beginning (the title) and an end, and because it possesses a certain internal coherence: it is written entirely in Hebrew, and certain themes-such as Jerusalem or divine holiness-recur throughout.

Having said that, it may be added that the category of 'text' allows for various combinations. For example, in the liturgy, a complete book is never read; instead, a fragment (from a Gospel, for instance) is taken and presented as a unit. Within that pragmatic context of communication-which is the liturgical celebration-that fragment becomes a text.

An interesting case from this perspective is that of the Book of Psalms. Within it, each psalm can be regarded as a text. Yet the collection of 150 psalms is not an arbitrary compilation; rather, it exhibits signs of a unified structure. Indeed, the Psalter is an example of what some call a 'macrotext', that is, an organised collection of smaller texts, such as a collection of letters or an anthology. The Pentateuch, too, constitutes a macrotext, consisting of five texts. In these cases:

> The coherence of the text must be considered within a progression in which the later phase assimilates the earlier one. That is to say, each text generally retains its own autonomy and internal cohesion, but is then encompassed within a broader autonomy and cohesion. The proportional relationship between the two types of cohesion may vary, depending on whether the structure of the macrotext is more or less rigid[^274].

The category of macrotext offers a useful analytical model not only for certain biblical books, such as the Psalter, the Pentateuch or the Book of the Twelve Prophets, but also for the entire Bible, as a structured collection of books[^275].

#### 21.2.2. Content and Unity of the Whole of Scripture

The final paragraph of DV12 completes the picture, recalling certain characteristics of the object of interpretation - Scripture received by the Church as the word of God - which must inform exegetical work.

> Therefore, since Sacred Scripture must be read and interpreted in the light of the same Spirit through whom it was written, in order to ascertain the precise meaning of the sacred texts, one must pay no less attention to the content and unity of the whole of Scripture, taking due account of the living tradition of the whole Church and the analogy of faith.

The living tradition, within which the Church receives Scripture, re-emerges, as we have said repeatedly. However, as can be seen from the syntax of the paragraph, the need to pay attention to the content and unity of Scripture is brought to the fore, whilst the tradition of the Church and the analogy of faith appear in the background, as a sort of pre-understanding[^276].

What does it mean to pay attention to the content and unity of the whole of Scripture? The Christian biblical canon is not a mere accumulation of heterogeneous material. Whilst each book is a text that conveys its own message and, as such, must be interpreted whilst respecting its uniqueness, that meaning will not yet be properly 'biblical' until it is placed in relation to the message of the other books.

The Christian Bible possesses a certain cultural and literary unity: indeed, the books have not come down to us in isolation, transmitted independently of one another. In this sense, even for those outside the tradition of the Church, it is reasonable that the interpretation of a biblical book should take the rest of the books into account:

> The unity of Sacred Scripture as a hermeneutical criterion for interpreting individual books or passages is not merely a dogmatic premise, a premise of faith. In doing so, we do not entirely abandon the realm of scientific hermeneutics, which - from Schleiermacher onwards - has operated on the basis of this premise of a 'holistic view of the literary whole[^277]' 2.

The unity of the Bible is also attained 'from above', that is, through the authority of Christ, who said that all Scripture speaks of him, and through the life of the Church, which has recognised which books form part of Scripture (the canon). The entire Old Testament, to be sure, must be understood in the light of the full revelation that took place in Christ. He is the principle that confers the Bible's deepest unity.

In *Verbum Domini*, after quoting the passage from *DV 12* which speaks of taking account of the unity of Scripture, it adds: 'this is today called canonical exegesis'. Verbum Domini says no more on this, although in no. 19 it returns to the subject of the unity of Scripture. In reality, since the Bible is a canon, any 'biblical' exegesis will necessarily be 'canonical'. But in practice, this equivalence has not always held true. As we mentioned in relation to the notion of text, historical-critical exegesis studies each book separately and tends to fragment the texts in an attempt to identify the various stages of composition. In response, some exegetes have proposed to inter- interpret the Bible, taking into account not only the unity of each book but also the unity of the various books within the canon[^278].

The canonical approach deserves a positive assessment on the whole, in that it seeks to give practical hermeneutical significance to the reality of the canon. This is also the view that emerges from the PCB document of 1993:

> The canonical approach rightly reacts against the exaggerated emphasis on what is supposed to be original and primitive, as if only this were authentic. Inspired Scripture is that which the Church has recognised as the rule of her faith. In this regard, one may emphasise either the final form in which each of the books currently exists, or the whole that they constitute as a canon. A book becomes biblical only in the light of the entire canon[^279].

However, to say that biblical texts must be interpreted taking into account the unity of the whole of Scripture is a statement that is easy to make in theory, but difficult to tra d convincingly in exegesis[^280].

#### 21.2.3. Analogy of Faith

DV12 speaks of taking into account, in interpretation, the 'analogy of faith', an expression of biblical origin (cf. Rom 12:6) which requires comment. The hermeneutical role of the 'analogy of faith ' corresponds to that played by the 'rule of faith' discussed by Saint Irenaeus, Origen, Saint Augustine and other Church Fathers. The term 'analogy' emphasises the connection between the various truths handed down by the apostles. The term 'rule', on the other hand, highlights the normative core of this tradition, namely the fundamental truths upon which all others depend.

> By 'analogy of faith' we mean the cohesion of the truths of faith amongst themselves and within the entirety of the plan of Revelation[^281].

One of the first authors to speak of the 'analogy of faith' in this sense was St John Henry Newman[^282]. Later, in the encyclical *Providentissimus Deus* (1893), Pope Leo XIII took up this notion and applied it to biblical exegesis. In interpreting biblical passages whose meaning has not been definitively established:

> (…) one must follow the analogy of faith and adhere, as to a supreme norm, to Catholic doctrine, as received from the authority of the Church. Since, in fact, God Himself is the author of both the sacred Books and the doctrine, of which the Church is the depositary, it is certainly not possible for a legitimate interpretation to yield a meaning of any scriptural passage that is in any way at odds with the Church (EB 109[^283])!.

It is not difficult to understand how the 'analogy of faith' can function as a hermeneutical criterion. If the interpretation of a passage contradicts a truth of faith, it is logical to conclude that such an interpretation is incorrect. The criterion also operates in the opposite direction. That is to say, the study of Scripture may help to clarify the formulation of certain truths of faith. For example, biblical exegesis has helped to clarify that the doctrine of limbo is not part of Christian revelation[^284].

When one reads paragraph 12 of *Dei Verbum* in its entirety, as we have done, it becomes clear that, in the Church's interpretation of the Bible, attention to the text - employing all the methods that aid in understanding it deeply and rigorously - must be held in harmony with the theological aspects: the unity of the Bible (the canon), tradition, the Magisterium and faith: both faith as content and as the disposition of the interpreter. It is important to remember that the truths of faith are not simply propositions or verbal statements, which are written down in a book and thus serve as a guide to interpretation. The truths of faith (fides quae) are inseparable from their vital assimilation by believers (fides qua), which begins with the reception of Baptism.

To appreciate the significance of the presentation in DV12, it may be helpful to speak briefly of 'secularised' hermeneutics, that is, the presuppositions that guide an approach to the Bible which excludes a priori the theological elements just mentioned.

In *Verbum Domini*, immediately after recalling the principles for interpretation set out in DV12, Benedict XVI introduces a paragraph entitled 'The danger of dualism and secularised hermeneutics', in which he summarises certain ideas he had expounded on many occasions before becoming Pope. I would like to quote from paragraph 15. The context is, as mentioned, a commentary on DV12, which considers two levels of biblical interpretation-one, so to speak, 'human' and the other of a theological nature-which must not be separated or set in opposition to one another, in order to avoid negative consequences:

> a) First of all, if exegetical activity is reduced solely to the first level, then Scripture itself becomes merely a text of the past: 'One can draw moral consequences from it, one can learn history, but the Book as such speaks only of the past, and exegesis is no longer truly theological, but becomes pure historiography, the history of literature'. [Benedict XVI, Address to the XIV General Assembly of the Synod (14 October 2008): Teachings IV, 2 (2008), 493; cf. Proposition 26]. It is clear that, in such a reduction, one cannot in any way comprehend the event of God's Revelation through his Word, which is handed down to us in the living Tradition and in Scripture.

> b) The lack of a hermeneutic of faith with regard to Scripture does not, however, manifest itself merely as an absence; in its place, another hermeneutic inevitably takes holda secularised, positivist hermeneutic, whose fundamental premise is the conviction that the Divine does not appear in human history. According to this hermeneutic, whenever a divine element appears to be present, it must be explained in another way and reduced entirely to the human element. Consequently, interpretations are put forward which deny the historicity of divine elements.

We shall return to the subject of positivism and the problems associated with the historicity of di r divine interventions in history in connection with the truth of the Bible (cf. pp. 211-219).

### 21.3. The Role of the Magisterium in Interpretation (DV 10 and 12)

As we have seen (pp. 69-88), Chapter II of Dei Verbum describes the transmission of divine revelation. We have read and commented on nos. 1-9, where Scripture is presented within tradition in the broad sense. In no. 10, another further element, inseparable from the others: the living Magisterium of the Church, that is, the pastors insofar as they exercise the function of teaching, as successors of the Apostles:

> The task of authentically interpreting the Word of God, whether written or handed down, is entrusted solely to the living Magisterium of the Church, whose authority is exercised in the name of Jesus Christ. This Magisterium, however, is not superior to the Word of God but serves it, teaching only what has been handed down, inasmuch as, by divine mandate and with the assistance of the Holy Spirit, it devoutly listens to, sacredly preserves and faithfully expounds that Word, and from this single deposit of faith draws everything it proposes for belief as revealed by God. (DV 1)

It is essential to clarify that Dei Verbum does not speak of interpreting 'authentically' as opposed to 'erroneously' or 'falsely'. In this context, 'authentic' means 'with the authority of Christ' (cf. LG 25[^285]) 1. That is to say, all members of the Church may interpret Scripture, but only some have the function and authority to do so in an authoritative and normative manner, a ministry for which they enjoy the assistance of the Holy Spirit.

The Magisterium does not interpret Scripture from the outside, like a despotic ruler or a judge who applies the law arbitrarily, but from within, like a son who must administer a treasure entrusted to him by his father.

The shepherds of the Church continue the mission entrusted by Jesus to the apostles to proclaim and safeguard divine revelation. They are not above the word of God, but at its service, for they have received it from the apostles and must safeguard it as a gift that does not belong to them. The Magisterium listens to, safeguards and expounds Scripture as part of the deposit entrusted to it.

In order to safeguard the deposit it has received, the Magisterium of the Church claims for itself the right to interpret the meaning of Scripture. The Council of Trent did so in response to the. This assertion appears even clearer in the, where the exclusivity of this function is emphasised: the authentic interpretation of the Word of God belongs solely to the Church's Magisterium. However, if one traces

Protestants, and the First Vatican Council reaffirmed this[^286] cited text of *Dei Verbum* the history of the teaching of the Popes and the ecumenical councils, one discovers that the Magisterium has almost never exercised this authority in the sense of offering an interpretation of a specific biblical passage. Far more frequent has been the indirect exercise of this authority - the use of texts in support of certain truths of the faith - or the negative exercise - the identification of errors in the interpretation of certain biblical passages, not on exegetical grounds, but because a particular interpretation contradicts the rule of faith[^287] -. Indeed, the Magisterium of the Church is not an exegete, nor does it wish to be one. Further on, in no. 12, Dei Verbum distinguishes between the work of biblical scholars and that of pastors:

> It is the task of exegetes to contribute, in accordance with these norms, to a deeper understanding and exposition of the meaning of Sacred Scripture, so that through their studies, which are in some sense preparatory, the judgement of the Church may mature. For what has been said here regarding the manner of interpreting Scripture is ultimately subject to the judgement of the Church, which fulfils the divine mandate and ministry of preserving and interpreting the Word of God (DV 12).

When the Magisterium refers to biblical interpretation, it does so within the broader context of safeguarding and proclaiming Christian doctrine, the Word of God in the broadest sense, 'written or handed down', as DV 10 states. As F. Dreyfus explains:

> When the Church carries out its mission of providing the authentic interpretation of a biblical text, its definition does not focus primarily on the content of the inspired author's message (the author's intention), but on the reality to which the text refers[^288].

The author himself provides an example:

> When the canon of the Council of Trent states that the sacrament of Extreme Unction[^289] instituted by Jesus was promulgated by James, it does not claim that the author of the epistle had a clear understanding of the difference between the sacramental order proper (the seven sacraments) and the body of sacramental activities of the Church in what would later be called the 'sacramentals'. What the Council affirms is that the reality of which James speaks is identical to that which the Church would designate under the name of the sacrament of Extreme Unction!.

In times of crisis, tensions may arise between the exegete's academic perspective and the pastoral and doctrinal perspective of the Magisterium. But if each party respects its own role, the tension should not escalate into conflict. In this regard, Cardinal Ratzinger's address on the occasion of the centenary of the PCB is significant

(see above, specific bibliography). The very clear words of *Donum veritatis* on the relationship between the Magisterium and theology can also be applied here:

> Even when collaboration takes place under the best of conditions, it cannot be ruled out that tensions may arise between the theologian and the Magisterium. The meaning attributed to these tensions and the spirit with which they are addressed are by no means insignificant: if the tensions do not arise from a feeling of hostility or opposition, they can represent a source of dynamism and a stimulus that drives the Magisterium and theologians to fulfil their respective roles through dialogue. (Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith, Instruction on the Ecclesial Vocation of the Theologian *Donum veritatis*, 24 May 1990, no. 25).

If tradition is likened to a river, then one might say that the Magisterium fulfils the function of the banks, the embankments that serve to contain the water and prevent it from overflowing. It is important to add that these banks were willed by the One who gave rise to the river, Jesus Christ. That is to say, these banks are not an artificial impos, an extrinsic limitation.

# A brief overview of the scope of the PCB's decrees from the early twentieth century

Between 1905 and 1939, the PCB issued a series of responses concerning certain exegetical issues, such as the Mosaic authenticity of the Pentateuch or the Pauline authenticity of the Epistle to the Hebrews. The tone and substance of these responses were highly restrictive.

In 1955, A. Miller and A. Kleinhans, respectively Secretary and Under-Secretary of the PCB, stated that those decrees were no longer to be regarded as binding on Catholic exegetes, who were free to study the points addressed by them. In 1990, in an official address, the Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith reiterated this point, explaining that the aforementioned decrees had a limited scope in time, because their purpose was more pastoral and preventative than doctrin[^290].

## 22. The Bible and Truth

When speaking of 'truth', it is advisable to begin by clarifying what kind of truth is being referred to, as it is a broad concept with various applications.

Truth is the opposite of falsehood, but it is not the same to discuss moral theology-where truth is contrasted with falsehood, for example-as it is to discuss it in contrast to error in the scientific sphere.

The truth of Sacred Scripture is, first and foremost, the truth proper to the Word of the Lord, through which the heavens and the earth were made (cf. Gen 1; Ps 116:6; Jdt 16:14); a Word that nourishes the people and the prophets (Deut 8:1; Jer 15:16), a word which, in contrast to human promises, remains for ever (cf. Isa 40:8) and which, when it comes down to earth, cannot fail to bear fruit (cf. Isa 55:10-11), for it is living and active (Heb 4:12). 'Is not my word like fire (…) and like a hammer that breaks the rock?' (Jer 21:29[^291]) 1.

When speaking of the truth of Scripture, one must think of the truth of divine revelation which it conveys and expresses. Therefore, first and foremost, one must turn one's gaze to the fullness of revelation, Jesus Christ, the eternal Word of God made man (cf. Jn 1:1-18). In the Upper Room, Jesus tells Thomas that he is the way, the truth and the life (cf. Jn 14:6). He then declares before Pilate that he has come to bear witness to the truth (cf. Jn 18:15).

In the case of the Bible, therefore, we are not dealing with a truth that is primarily universal and abstract, as might be the case with a science or a political, philosophical or theological doctrine, but with the manifestation of a personal God, who, through the humanity of Christ, comes to meet humankind in order to bring them into communion with himself. Insofar as they speak of Christ and insofar as Christ speaks through them, the Sacred Scriptures express and convey the deepest truth that can be found in a book- -a truth for which one can lay down one's life, the only truth that saves.

### 22.1. The truth for our salvation (DVzz)

The second paragraph of DV11 provides a description of the truth taught by Scripture, which offers us a good starting point for explaining this topic:

> Since, therefore, everything asserted by the inspired authors or hagiographers is to be regarded as asserted by the Holy Spirit, it must consequently be held that the books of Scripture teach with certainty, faithfully and without error the truth which God, for our salvation, willed to be handed down in the Sacred Scriptures (cf. St Augustine, *De Gen. ad litt.*, 2,9, 20: PL 34,270-271; CSEL 28, 1,46-47, and*Epist.* 82,3: PL33,277: CSEL

> 14,2,154. - St Thomas, De Ver., q. 12, a. 2, C. - Council of Trent, Decree on Canonical Scriptures: Dz 1501. - Leo XIII, Encyclical Providentissimus Deus: EB 121, 124, 126-127

> [Dz!291ff]. - Pius XII, Encyclical *Divino agante*: EB 539). Therefore, 'all Scripture, divinely inspired, is also useful for teaching, for convincing, for correcting, for training in righteousness, so that the man of God may be perfect, equipped for every good work' (2 Tim!,16-17).

The references in brackets to various authors (Augustine, Thomas Aquinas, Leo XIII and Pius XII) make it clear that the subject of the truth of the Bible has a long history behind it, which unfortunately we do not have time to trace in these pages[^292].

To summarise briefly, we can say that the Church has always resolutely defended the truth of Scripture. At the same time, history has shown that this property can be understood in various ways, not all of which are equally appropriate. To defend the biblical accounts against the attacks of nineteenth-century rationalist criticism, Catholics spoke of the 'absolute inerrancy' of the Bible, that is, its freedom from any kind of error. Such inerrancy was seen as a direct and necessary consequence of divine inspiration. In this sense, some believed that even the smallest detail stated in Scripture enjoyed the authority of a dogmatic definition[^293]. Today we can state unequivocally that this position is no longer tenable, not only because it depended too heavily on the polemical context in which it arose, but above all because it fails to fully respect the human dimension of the biblical books, which are not a systematic exposition of the truths of the faith. 'Indeed, not every page of the Bible is assertive in nature, such that it can be judged on the basis of a simple dichotomy between true or false[^294]'!.

As one can easily surmise, it is fundamental to understanding this argument to recognise that the truth of Scripture is analogous to the truth of divine revelation. It is no coincidence that Dei Verbum first describes revelation and then speaks of Scripture and its inspiration and truth. As we mentioned above, when commenting on the first In this chapter of *Dei Verbum*, revelation is historical (pp. 60-62) and reaches its fullness in one person, Jesus Christ (pp. 61-69). Therefore, the truth that the Bible seeks to convey is not to be understood in abstract or absolute terms, as if it were a systematic treatise on theology, consisting of independent logical propositions, each of which would express a timeless truth[^295]. Nor is it a matter of artistic or literary perfection, as if the sacred books were the most beautiful pages ever written in the history of humanity. Indeed, some biblical authors are more gifted than others; suffice it to say that errors of syntax frequently appear in the Book of Revelation.

In the text we have quoted, Dei Verbum points to a fruitful path for understanding the truth contained in the Bible. Firstly, Vatican II changed the terminology, moving from the absence of error (inerrancy) to a positive affirmation of truth. Above all, it added a description of the truth contained in the sacred pages that links it to the divine intention: it is a truth willed by God for the salvation of humankind. The quotation from 2 Timothy 3 at the end of DV 11 helps us to understand the inseparability of inspiration from the purpose for which God willed the Bible.

When we speak of the truth for our salvation, we do not mean to distinguish between different parts of the Bible, as if some were salvific and others were not, or as if some were true and others were not. Dei Verbum seeks to explain the perspective from which the message of the texts is to be regarded as true. The wording of DV11 is to be understood in a formal rather than a material sense: that is to say, the contents of the Bible must not be separated, as if only those texts relating to doctrine and morality were true. The truth that leads to our salvation belongs to the whole of the Bible, not to isolated pages.

What DV11 reminds us is that the truth proper to the Bible is inseparable from the purpose with which God inspired it - a point already emphasised by Origen and Augustine (cf. pp. 262-266); furthermore, it is worth noting that DVy referred to the Gospel as 'the source of all salutary truth'. For this reason, as Farkasfalvy explains,

> divine inspiration does not imply that every passage or phrase of the biblical text must be free from error from every possible point of view. The grammarian, the scientist, the psychologist, the philosopher, the historian and others may point to a particular passage which, when examined from a certain limited point of view (…), may be found to be flawed. But such a finding does not prove that the word of God contains an error. Rather, it means that God's message is expressed, at a specific point in the history of salvation, with the imperfections inherent in human existence. Nevertheless, insofar as it serves both the specific purpose of the human author and the salvific purpose-

> , every passage expresses the truth it is meant to express in accordance with God's salvific will[^296].

Recently, Pope Francis confirmed the validity of this point. When reading the Bible, we must not forget that God has given it to us for our salvation:

> The Bible is not a collection of history books, nor of news reports, but is entirely directed towards the integral salvation of the person. The undeniable historical roots of the books contained in the sacred text must not cause us to forget this primary purpose: our salvation. Everything is directed towards this purpose, which is inscribed in the very nature of the Bible, which is composed as a history of salvation in which God speaks and acts to reach out to all people and save them from evil and death[^297].

We can summarise the Church's teaching on the truth of Sacred Scripture in the following points:

- this truth is a property of each textual unit, that is to say, not of isolated phrases or verses, and above all of the Old and New Testaments taken together, whose unity rests on Christ, the fullness of truth. As we have seen in DV12, one of the criteria for interpreting biblical texts is to take into account the content and unity of the whole of Scripture; the truth of each individual book must be judged in relation to the whole of revelation;

- Sacred Scripture, the witness to revelation, teaches us what God is like, how He has acted, and how human beings have responded. Bearing in mind the progressive nature of revelation and God's condescension, it is not difficult to speak of the truth of the Bible in this sense, knowing that, as DV15 states, the books of the Old Testament contain 'imperfect and transitory elements';

- !) The Bible does not set out to teach physics, mathematics, chemistry, biology or astronomy. The Holy Spirit 'did not intend to instruct people on these matters, which are of no importance whatsoever for eternal salvation[^298]' 1. Scripture does not seek to tell us how the heavens work, but how to get there[^299]. The efforts made by some to find harmony between the descriptions in the first chapters of Genesis and scientific discoveries (for example, in Werner Keller's book *The Bible Was Right*, which was famous in the 1950s and 1960s) are, to say the least, a waste of time. Such attempts at harmonisation also run the risk of creating false problems for the faith, in cases where such harmonisation proves impossible.

The most complex relationship remains that between the truth of Sacred Scripture and historical truth, which we shall discuss below. Indeed, the relationship between biblical truth and the truth proper to history cannot be resolved by a simple separation of the two spheres, as is the case with the natural sciences. We are faced with a more delicate problem.

### 22.2. Some observations on the relationship between the Bible and historical truth

As we have seen in the first part (cf. pp. 60-62), Christian revelation is not mythical, but historical. The biblical message cannot be separated from history, because God revealed himself in a specific place and time, through words and deeds (verbis gestisque) which are presented in narrative form as the history of salvation.

For example, it is not possible to present the teaching of Jesus as if it were independent of his life, because 'if Christ has not been raised, then our preaching is in vain and your faith is in vain' (1 Cor 15:14). For this reason, the Church firmly affirms the historicity of the four Gospels (cf. DV 19).

However, the fact that the Christian faith has an essential link with history does not mean that all biblical texts are equally historical. Many biblical passages do not contain narrative accounts, such as the poetic and wisdom books. Furthermore, not all the events mentioned in the biblical books strictly belong to the history of salvation.

Some Catholic apologists, with the intention of defending the truth of the Bible, sought to demonstrate the accuracy of every detail mentioned in the biblical narratives, because many of the attacks by nineteenth-century rationalists against the historicity of the biblical narratives stemmed from a positivist conception of history. But this approach has proved to be a dead end.

Firstly, we must bear in mind that the historicity of the texts is linked to their literary characteristics, as we shall see. However, the problem of historicity must be resolved at a deeper level, through a philosophical reflection on the ways and limits of human knowledge of the past and through a theological reflection on the relationship between reconstructible history and the history of salvation, taking into account the essential tenets of our faith.

#### 22.2.1. At a literary level

In the presentation of the list of biblical books, we have seen that the Old Testament is subdivided into three major groups: the historical books, the poetic and wisdom books, and the prophetic books. However, as one deepens one's understanding of the texts, one discovers that the books of the Old Testament generically termed 'historical' are so to varying degrees from a literary point of view. This is a matter linked to literary genres (cf. pp. 216-218).

For reasons of this kind, it is not reasonable to defend the historicity of books such as Judith or Tobit, which are best understood as exemplary stories. Perhaps it would be more accurate to speak generically of 'narrative' books rather than historical ones. It is worth clarifying straight away that understanding a narrative as non-historical does not mean that it is 'false'. The truth of the Bible is much broader than historical truth.

Taking literary genre into account, for example, leads us to read the Book of Job as a wisdom book and the Psalms as prayers, without seeking to find direct historical information in them. The same applies to a prophetic book, such as Jonah. It is true that allusions to a situation in King David's life sometimes appear at the beginning of the Psalms. For example, in Psalm 3:1 we read: 'A psalm. Of David. When he was fleeing from his son Absalom'. Such references can help us to better appreciate the drama expressed in a particular psalm. But it would be misleading to treat these psalms as if they contained a historical account of the life of King David or other figures.

On the other hand, we can find information of historical value in books that are not intended to recount history. For example, the Book of Lamentations and certain psalms (cf. Ps 74; 9; 89) reflect the consequences of the destruction of Jerusalem. From al- From these three psalms we can glean some information about the liturgy in the Temple, the figure of the king of Israel, pilgrimages to Jerusalem, or simply the structure of prayer in Israel. Indeed, historicity cannot be reduced to a formal or literary characteristic of the texts. The most suitable form for recounting historical events is narrative. But history can also be recorded through psalms or proverbs.

A problem linked to the relationship between historicity and literary form is that of the category of 'myth' and its applicability to certain passages of the Bible, especially the early chapters of Genesis. In his catechesis of 19 September 1979, Saint John Paul II spoke of the 'primitive mythical character' of the account of the creation of man in Gen 2. In the official publication *Insegnamenti* II/2 (1979), pp. 12-24, a lengthy note was included to explain the various meanings of the term 'myth', in order to clarify that it is not synonymous with false, unreal or irrational[^300].
