#### 22.2.2. At the level of historical reconstruction

A more complex problem arises from the fact that in some books - which claim to be historical - we find inconsistencies amongst themselves or contradictions with other ancient sources or with the findings of archaeology. Let us look at some examples.

As regards the conquest of the land, there are significant differences between the account given in the Book of Joshua and that in the Book of Judges. In the account of the monarchy, 1-2 Chronicles follows 1-2 Kings, but introduces changes that are at times substantial, as is the case, for example, with the end of Manasseh (cf. 2 Kings 21:1-18 and 2 Chr

33,1-20). There are conflicting accounts of the death of Antiochus IV Epiphanes (cf. 1 Maccabees 6; 2 Maccabees 1:11-17; 9:1-29).

There are many more examples of this sort. What are we to say in the face of these issues? Firstly, we must recognise that attempts to subject ancient texts to historical criticism are of interest and value even for biblical exegesis within the Church (cf. Benedict XVI, *Verbum Domini*, no. 12).

Secondly, when studying the Bible from the perspective of historical criticism, it is advisable to distinguish the issues concerning the historicity of the New Testament from those of the Old Testament, as they are of a different nature. I present below, in a concise and highly simplified manner, the situation regarding each Testament.

New Testament. The books of the New Testament recount events that took place at a time relatively close to their composition. A comparison of the various accounts of the life of Jesus and the early disciples reveals some differences - particularly between John and the Synoptic Gospels - but also agreement on many fundamental points. Furthermore, some non-Christian authors, such as Flavius Josephus and Tacitus, allude to some of these events. Archaeological discoveries generally confirm the plausibility of many New Testament accounts, the evidence for which can therefore be considered sound in substance.

Old Testament. As regards the historicity of the major Old Testament narratives - the patriarchs, the Exodus, the conquest of the land, the reign of David - the situation is very different from that of the New Testament. The Old Testament texts were composed over a long period of time, sometimes many centuries after the events they recount. As mentioned above, there are discrepancies between some of these accounts. Furthermore, whilst archaeological studies have confirmed certain aspects of the Old Testament accounts, they have cast doubt on others. For example, 'whilst Joshua 6 describes in minute detail the capture of Jericho, with the miraculous collapse of its imposing walls, excavations have shown that, at the time of the Exodus, Jericho was in ruins and had therefore been uninhabited for several centuries[^301] '(1). In short, the great narratives of the Old Testament find no confirmation of their substance

in other sources (documents, inscriptions, archaeological and numismatic finds, and so on). Of course, this does not mean that they are not true or that they have no historical value, but simply that their historicity cannot be proven according to these criteria. A clear distinction must be made between different fields and areas of expertise, because, unfortunately, many authors today leap indiscriminately from non-verifiability to the denial of historicity.

For example, we have no material evidence of the stories of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. Some conclude that these patriarchs did not even exist. Such a deduction is, to say the least, hasty. It is wiser to state that this is a problem and to seek to explore the meaning and value of the respective narratives, as the archaeologist Jacques Briend explains:

> The key question is why the Bible records a patriarchal tradition. Questioning the existence of the patriarchs is of little interest (…). The most important thing is to discover that the patriarchs bear a promise that continues to hold true throughout the history of the people, which is not without significance. Only by reading the text can one discover this function, and here archaeology is of no help[^302].

What Briend says about the patriarchs could be applied to many biblical accounts. The Old Testament[^303] narratives offer us an explanation of the origin of Israel and of its faith in one God that must be taken seriously. The study of history cannot be reduced to verification alone, but must also seek to explain the texts and the ideas they contain. As we shall see, it is advisable to avoid the danger of equating the true with the verifiable.

#### 22.2.3. At a philosophical level: knowledge of the past

At a philosophical level, the question to be asked is what 'history' means or, more specifically, how we can know the human past. We cannot offer a complete answer in these pages, but we shall make some observations that will help to assess the issues concerning the historicity of biblical narratives, especially those of the Old Testament 2.

For the purposes of our discussion, it is useful to begin by distinguishing between 'verifiable' history and 'true' history[^304]. In the nineteenth century and the early twentieth century, there was a tendency to equate these two concepts. In other words, only those 'facts' that were verifiable were considered to be true or historical. According to this conception of history, which we might call 'positivist'-

'historical science', the study of past events was regarded as an objective and methodical science, like physics or mathematics, which were considered the paradigm of all human knowledge. The historian was therefore required to base his reconstruction of past events on empirical evidence (documents and monuments), leaving no room for subjective interpretation. Consequently, any reconstruction of the past that did not meet these rigorous standards was regarded as non-historical. Under these premises, history is reduced to the study of external and measurable events.

This mindset is still very widespread in today's culture. Yet from a philosophical perspective, it is now clear that this empiricist or positivist ideal of historical science is profoundly flawed. Attempts to write history following this paradigm led not only to poor results - 'a history strictly conforming to positivist postulates would consist mainly of blank pages[^305]' 1 - but also to results that were less robust and accurate than was intended. And this is because, by its very nature, the study of history cannot be an exact science, simply because its subject matter - the human past - is not exact. This lack of exactness is an essential part of history in general - not just that recounted in the Bible! - because what people do is never exact. A single day in the life of Jesus could be recounted in great detail: the time he got up, what he had for breakfast, how he was dressed, and so on. But such an account would be historically irrelevant, because it would tell us very little about the significance of his actions. On the other hand, a more theological account may well be much truer, because it will better reflect the identity of Jesus[^306].

Indeed, the human past is not made up solely of physical 'things', but of personal experiences that cannot be reduced to their external and measurable manifestations. It is clear that many events have left no indelible and unequivocal records, but only faint traces, and are therefore not strictly verifiable for us. But this must not lead us to deny their reality. For example, the conversion of Saint Augustine is not an external fact; yet, judging by its consequences - not only his account of it in the *Confessions*, but his entire life and work - we can say that it was one of the most significant events in the history of the early Church.

Furthermore, the lack of precision in history depends not only on its object but also on its subject. In understanding the past, one cannot ignore the position of the historian, who seeks to interpret the evidence according to his own point of view. As we have said when discussing philosophical hermeneutics (cf. pp. 208-211), human knowledge is always influenced by the prejudices or preconceptions of the individual engaged in the process of understanding. The analysis of documents depends on the questions posed of them and on the interests of the historian studying them. This fact imposes insurmountable limits on any claim to total objectivity and complete transparency. Recognising one's own position is an essential condition for arriving at a credible reconstruction of the past.

The critical spirit, necessary in the study of ancient texts, must not become systematic mistrust. The historian should not start from a prejudice that is, unfortunately, all too common: suspecting everything and everyone, looking for some form of ideological propaganda behind every account, which seeks to justify the privileges of a social or political group. Without a minimum of courtesy, or better still, sympathy towards the texts (not only biblical ones), it is impossible to understand them in depth[^307].

The historian's task cannot be limited to reconstructing 'what happened' (as if this were simple), but also consists in attempting to understand the past, in seeking the causes of events in order to offer an intelligible synthesis, which usually requires going beyond what can be strictly proven.

How, then, should we approach the question of the historical truth of the Old Testament? We can say that the Old Testament faithfully recounts the essential outlines of a history, the history of salvation. This story is not recounted in a verifiable manner, partly for fortuitous reasons (the absence of independent documents and monuments to corroborate it), but also for a deeper reason: God's actions and people's experience of them cannot, in themselves, be scientifically verified. Although it may seem obvious, it is important to remember that studying biblical history requires a minimum of religious sensitivity and openness to spiritual values. To claim otherwise in the name of science would be like trying to study the history of art without aesthetic sensitivity[^308].

To speak of 'salvation history', however, we must move from the philosophical to the theological realm. Salvation history cannot be interpreted solely according to modern criteria for historical reconstruction. We must also be open to grasping the sense of God's interventions in the history of Israel.

#### 22.2.4. At a theological level: the history of salvation and the hierarchy of truths of faith

To address the relationship between the Bible and historical truth, we must first take into account a criterion which, to a certain extent, is 'external' to the texts: the rule of faith, which we know - through the tradition of the Church - before reading and analysing the texts (cf. pp. 221-223). As we have already noted, one cannot deny the death and resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth, which took place in Jerusalem around the year 10 AD, without destroying the entire Christian message (cf. 1 Cor 15:14).

> The view that faith as such knows absolutely nothing of historical facts and must leave all this to historians is Gnosticism: such a view disembodies faith and reduces it to a mere idea. For faith based on the Bible, however, the very reality of the events is a constitutive requirement. A God who cannot intervene in history and reveal himself within it is not the God of the Bible. Therefore, the reality of Jesus' birth from the Virgin Mary, the actual institution of the Eucharist by Jesus at the Last Supper, and his bodily resurrection from the dead - this is the significance of the empty tomb - are elements of faith as such, which faith can and must defend against any mere presumption of superior historical knowledge. That Jesus - in all that is essential - was in fact the person portrayed in the Gospels is by no means a historical conjecture, but a fact of faith. Objections seeking to convince us otherwise are not an expression of genuine scientific knowledge, but an arbitrary overestimation of the method[^309].

It is interesting to note that the examples of historical events offered by Cardinal Ratzinger in this quotation correspond to the New Testament. On the other hand, regarding the Book of Jonah as an exemplary narrative (and therefore without any claim to historicity) on grounds of literary genre poses no problem for the faith. Or, if one considers that the Book of Joshua contains a highly idealised account of the conquest of Canaan, in which history serves a theological purpose - the land as a gift from the Lord and not as the fruit of one's own efforts -, no truth of the faith is being attacked. Such interpretations may be open to debate on a literary level, but for believers it is important to draw a clear distinction between what belongs to the deposit of the apostolic faith and what is open to free discussion.

At this point, the question naturally arises as to which events in the Old Testament are so closely linked to the Christian faith that we cannot do without them. It is not easy to answer. In the various creeds, for example, whilst the historicity of the mystery of the Incarnation and the Paschal Mystery is clearly stated (the famous 'suffered under Pontius Pilate' in the Apostles' Creed), references to the earlier stages of salvation history are very general. In the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed, it is stated only that the Holy Spirit 'spoke through the prophets'. The creeds are highly concise and do not intend to present all the truths of the faith. But at the same it is important to note that the Church, whilst on the one hand clearly defending the value of the Old Testament against Marcion, on the other hand was unwilling or unable to specify much more. To what extent the Christian faith requires that the individual elements comprising the history of salvation be taken as events that actually took place remains - it seems to me - an open question.

We must believe that the Lord acquired a people (Israel) to whom he revealed himself as the one God, establishing a covenant with them and announcing a future salvation through his intervention. This is - very briefly - the understanding that the Jews had of their own identity in the time of Jesus and in the centuries immediately preceding it. As such, this faith is a historical reality.

> It is (…) clear that we cannot abandon the need to find some serious historical basis that points in the same direction as the biblical accounts. Otherwise, we would end up thinking of the Bible as a mere narrative of archetypes who never actually existed. Obviously, what is historical and what is not must always be sought in the individual texts and statements, verifying them on a case-by-case basis, without, however, falling into constant mistrust and general suspicion[^310].

It is here that the cognitive value of faith comes to the fore; without it, in fact, all the supernatural aspects of revelation become problematic. Faith is not a feeling, but a gift from God that enlightens the intellect and assists the will in as s to God's revelation, which also includes historical truths.

### 22.3. The 'obscure' passages of the Bible

As a complement to the theme of truth, I include here paragraph 42 of *Verbum Domini*, which refers to problematic passages in the Old Testament-not from a historical perspective, but from a moral or doctrinal one-such as the polygamy of the patriarchs, human sacrifices (cf. Gen 22, Judg 11) and violence against enemies. Dei Verbum had briefly touched upon this issue, stating that the Old Testament books, 'though they contain imperfect and transitory elements, nevertheless demonstrate a true divine pedagogy' (DV 15[^311]) 2.

Here are the words of Benedict XVI:

> In the context of the relationship between the Old and New Testaments, the Synod also addressed the issue of those passages in the Bible which are obscure and difficult to understand due to the violence they contain and the immoralities sometimes contained therein. In this regard, it must first be borne in mind that biblical revelation is deeply rooted in history. God's plan is revealed progressively therein and is slowly brought to fruition through successive stages, despite human resistance. God chooses a people and patiently guides their development. Revelation adapts to the cultural and moral level of distant eras and therefore recounts events and customs-such as fraudulent schemes, acts of violence and the extermination of peoples-without explicitly denouncing their immorality; which is explained by the historical context, but may surprise the modern reader, especially when one forgets the many 'dark' behaviours that people have always exhibited over the centuries, even in our own day. In the Old Testament, the prophets' preaching stands firmly against every form of injustice and violence, whether collective or individual, and is thus the means by which God educates his people in preparation for the Gospel. It would therefore be a mistake not to consider those passages of Scripture that appear problematic to us. Rather, we must be aware that reading these pages requires the acquisition of adequate expertise, through a formation that interprets the texts within their historical and literary context and from a Christian perspective, whose ultimate hermeneutical key is 'the Gospel and the new commandment of Jesus Christ fulfilled in the Paschal Mystery' [Propositio 29]. I therefore urge scholars and pastors to help all the faithful to engage with these passages through a reading that enables them to discover their meaning in the light of the mystery of Christ.

As can be seen, the Pope does not resolve all the problems that may arise when reading the Old Testament, but he sets out certain principles to bear in mind in such cases. Among these principles, two are closely interlinked: namely, the progressive nature of God's revelation, which had to take into account what people were capable of understanding (divine pedagogy, combined with his condescension), and the fullness of this very revelation in Christ. Indeed, many of the Old Testament accounts strike us as problematic precisely because we are Christians-that is, because we already possess the full light that God wished to communicate to us in Christ and are therefore able to perceive the obscure aspects of the earlier stages[^312].

Furthermore, we must also take into account a common mode of expression in the Bible, whereby certain actions are attributed directly to God, without mentioning the secondary causes (human or otherwise) that brought them about:

> It is often observed that the Holy Spirit, the principal author of Sacred Scripture, attributes certain actions to God without making any mention of secondary causes. This is not 'a manner

- of 'speaking' but a profound way of recalling God's primacy and his absolute lordship over history and the world (cf. Is 10:5-15; 45:5-7; Dt 32:39; Sir 11:14), thereby fostering trust in him 1.

- Catechism, no. 104.

# APPENDIX: THE NATURE OF INSPIRATION

The Holy Scriptures contain the word of God and, because they are inspired, are truly the word of God. Dei Verbum 24.

## 23. What inspiration is not

Thanks to Dei Verbum, we now understand the most important aspect of inspiration, namely, its relationship with revelation (cf. pp. 10-111).

We must now explore the nature of this charism in greater depth. What does inspiration consist of? How does the Holy Spirit act so that the books may be called inspired? How does divine influence combine with human action?

To explain the nature of inspiration, textbooks usually begin with a historical overview. They start with the Old Testament, where inspiration is not mentioned directly, but analogous realities are discussed. They then analyse the two New Testament texts that speak explicitly of it - 2 Peter 1:20-21 and, above all, 2 Timothy 3:16 - before going on to review what the Church Fathers, medieval, modern and contemporary theologians, and finally the documents of the Magisterium (from the First Vatican Council onwards) have said on the subject.

We have already covered part of this ground, discussing how Jesus and the apostolic generation regarded the Scriptures of Israel as the word of God. Unfortunately, we do not have time to continue this historical overview and will therefore have to provide a schematic presentation of some fundamental ideas.

Often, certain truths of faith are best understood by contrast with what they do not mean. Therefore, we shall first examine some errors surrounding the Christian concept of the inspiration of the Scriptures, before attempting to offer some positive statements on the matter.

### 23.1. Inadequate models due to insufficient consideration of the human factor

#### 23.1.1. Inspiration as ecstasy

The word used in 2 Tim 3:16 to describe Scripture is θεόπνευστος ('inspired by God'), a word that is not very common in Greek, but similar to other words in Hellenistic religious language.

> Some believe that 'inspired' is a term coined by the author of 2 Timothy in order to draw attention to the divine origin of the Scriptures (…). Nevertheless, the prevailing view amongst scholars is that 'inspired' is a term borrowed from ancient Hellenistic descriptions of the ecstatic experience of prophetic seers[^313].

Whatever the origin of this term, we shall see that not everything that was thought in Hellenistic culture regarding the inspiration of prophets, poets and soothsayers should be applied indiscriminately to the Bible. Here we set a first limit to the Christian notion of inspiration.

In the classical Greek mindset, prophesying required possession by a god, which caused the soothsayer or prophet (μάντις) to lose their faculties. The 'inspired' person is outside themselves, that is, in ecstasy (ἔκστασις, 'to be outside'). The prophet is unaware of what they are doing or saying whilst under the influence of this inspiration. One might think of the oracles of the Pythia at Delphi or Plato's description of the poets' enthusiasm (cf. Ion 5.5c-5.6b[^314]) 2.

The ecstatic notion of inspiration is now part of the collective imagination, as can be seen - I apologise for the rather unacademic example - in the case of the Divination teacher in the Harry Potter novels, Professor Trelawney. This character is portrayed as an incompetent teacher who, at certain moments, however, changes her demeanour and speaks of the future in enigmatic terms, which she cannot recall once she emerges from her trance.

With the aim of emphasising the supernatural origin of biblical prophecies, some second-century Christian authors adopted this kind of language. For example, Saint Justin (†165), in order to explain to pagans what prophecy is, goes a little beyond the parallel statements in 2 Peter. Here is a quote from him:

> When you hear the words of the prophets as if from their own mouths, you must not believe that they are spoken by the prophets themselves whilst they are inspired, but by the divine Word who moves them (1 Apol. 16[^315]) 1.

A contemporary of Justin, Athenagoras of Athens-himself an apologist-in his Plea for the Christians, addressed to the Roman emperors, introduces the argument based on prophecies by explaining that the prophets spoke in a state of ecstasy. To explain this, he uses a musical metaphor, inspired by the idea of the divine breath:

> (…) I believe that even you, who are so passionate about knowledge and so wise, are not unaware of the words of Moses, nor of those of Isaiah, Jeremiah and the other prophets, who, in the rapture of their thoughts [οi uατ' έuστασιν τĩν έν αúτοĩ@ ʎ ογισµĩν], moved by the divine Spirit, proclaimed what was taking place within them, whilst the Spirit made use of them just as a flute-player blows into a flute [í@ sí uαì αú ʎ цτ2@ αú ʎ òν έµuνsúσαι] (Legatio pro christianis IX[^316]) 2.

Still drawing on the musical realm, Athenagoras also speaks of the prophet as a zither played with a plectrum, which is the Holy Spirit (cf. Cohortatio ad Graecos, 8). The comparison with the plectrum is found in several Church Fathers[^317].

Other authors, when speaking of the prophet as inspired by God, draw on the image of the pen in the scribe's hand, taken from Ps 45:2[^318].

All these expressions should be seen as limited but legitimate attempts to make the supernatural origin of biblical prophecies comprehensible within the Hellenistic culture of the time.

The situation changes with the Montanists, who conceived of prophecy in a way that explicitly denied the prophets' freedom and conscience. Around the year 1 AD, Montanus and two women, Priscilla and Maximilla, arrived in the province of Phrygia (Asia Minor) and claimed to be inspired by the Paraclete to announce the imminent arrival of the Parousia. Their doctrine enjoyed considerable success, particularly in rural communities.

At the beginning of the 3rd century, the Montanists succeeded in converting Tertullian, who was drawn to their ethical rigour.

Origen was the first author to decisively reject the Montanist conception of prophecy[^319]. Following him, in the same vein, others reaffirmed the refusal to apply the ecstatic notion of inspiration to the biblical prophets. St Jerome, for example, expresses himself with undeniable clarity:

> It is not true, as Montanus and his foolish women imagine, that the prophets spoke in a state of ecstasy, so that they did not know what they were saying[^320], and whilst instructing others, they themselves were unaware of what they were saying 2.

Indeed, one need only read Scripture to ascertain that the Lord never reveals himself as Apollo did in the temple at Delphi.

The case most similar to the mantic model of a prophet in biblical accounts is that of Balaam, a sort of hired prophet, who blesses Israel against the will of the king of Moab, who instead wanted a curse. In the account in Numbers 22-24, Balaam is portrayed in a rather positive light, in contrast to Balak, king of Moab. However, despite having conveyed the words of the Lord, Balaam is presented in the rest of the biblical tradition in very negative terms (cf. Num 11:16; Deut 2:5-6; Josh 13:22; 24:9-10; Mic 6:5; Neh 13:2; 1 Chr 6:55; 2 Pet 2:15; Jude 1:11; Rev 2:14[^321])!.

*

So far we have spoken of inspiration in a broad sense, not in the technical sense as it relates to the books. But it is clear that the rejection of the mantic model of inspiration applies equally to scriptural inspiration: whether it be the prophet uttering an oracle, the apostle preaching Christ, or the one who sets these words down in writing, all act in a 'normal' manner, that is, in possession of their own physical and mental faculties and moved by their own free will.

#### 23.1.2. Mechanical Dictation and Fundamentalism

In a cultural context very different from that of the patristic period, in the 16th century, the Spanish theologian Domingo Báñez O.P. (1528-1604) described inspiration as verbal dictation: the Bible is inspired because God suggested the words to the human author, as if dictating them to him.

Báñez's formulation is correct in that it contrasts verbal inspiration with so-called 'real' inspiration, that is, the theory according to which God would have limited himself to inspiring the content of the Bible and not the words. Indeed, it is not possible to distinguish within a text between thought and words, between substance and form. Therefore, if the Bible is inspired, it must necessarily follow that the individual words are also inspired[^322].

However, Báñez's statements run the risk of reducing the human author's role to a merely passive one. Verbal inspiration and divine dictation must be rejected if these terms are used to deny the free and conscious activity of the human author. Indeed, some Protestant groups developed a conception of verbal inspiration as mechanical dictation: that God inspires the words means, then, that He dictates the text word for word into the ear of the hagiographer, who is nothing more than a copyist.

This notion persists to this day, championed by 'fundamentalist' groups and others of a similar persuasion. The 1993 PCB document expresses very clearly their understanding of the Bible as the word of God:

> The term 'fundamentalist' is directly linked to the American Bible Congress held in Niagara, New York, in 1895. At that time, conservative Protestant exegetes defined the 'five points of fundamentalism': the verbal inerrancy of Scripture, the divinity of Christ, his virgin birth, the doctrine of vicarious atonement, and the bodily resurrection at the second coming of Christ. (…) Although fundamentalism is right to insist on the divine inspiration of the Bible, the inerrancy of the Word of God and the other biblical truths contained within the five fundamental points, its manner of presenting these truths is rooted in an ideology that is not biblical, whatever its representatives may say. (…) The fundamental problem with this fundamentalist interpretation is that, by refusing to take account of the historical nature of biblical revelation, it renders itself incapable of fully accepting the truth of the Incarnation itself. Fundamentalism avoids the close relationship between the divine and the human in one's relationship with God. It refuses to admit that the inspired Word of God was expressed in human language and was written down, under divine inspiration, by human authors whose abilities and resources were limited. For this reason, he tends to treat the biblical text as if it had been dictated word for word by the Spirit and fails to recognise that the Word of God was formulated in a language and phrasing conditioned by a particular era. It pays no attention whatsoever to the literary forms and human ways of thinking present in the biblical texts, many of which are the result of a process that spanned long per periods of time and bears the mark of very different historical situations[^323].

### 23.2. Inadequate models due to insufficient consideration of the divine factor

In the nineteenth century, in order to defend the Bible against the attacks of rationalists, certain theories of inspiration emerged which we might call 'minimalist', in the sense that they sought to minimise the supernatural elements.

Two of these explanations of inspiration were formally rejected by the solemn magisterium of the Church in the constitution Dei Filius of the First Vatican Council (1870). It is worth reading the text carefully:

> These books of the Old and New Testaments (…) the Church regards as sacred and canonical, not because, having been composed by human hands, they were subsequently approved by her authority, nor merely because they contain revelation without error, but because, having been written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, they have God as their author and as such have been handed down to the Church.

> Eos [libros] vero Ecclesia por sacris et canonicis habet, non ideo quod sola humana industria concinnati, sua deinde auctoritate sint approbati; nor merely because they contain revelation without error; but because, having been written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, they have God as their author, and as such have been handed down to the Church (EB yy[^324]) 2.

In this text, the First Vatican Council did not confine itself to condemning certain errors, but also set out some positive aspects concerning inspiration, to which we shall return later (cf. pp. 255261). For now, we are concerned with the two rejected explanations, as they help us to understand, by contrast, the Church's faith in inspiration. Both theories have

varying nuances depending on their authors[^325]. But it suffices for us to comment on the words of Dei Filius.

- Inspiration cannot be explained by saying that the books, composed by human hands, were subsequently approved by the authority of the Church (the theory of subsequent approval). Inspiration therefore implies something supernatural (more than merely human) that has belonged to the books from their very origin. It is not a quality added to a book composed in a purely human manner, nor, therefore, can it be said that inspiration arises when it is recognised. It is true that the Church's acceptance of the books constitutes a decisive moment in the formation of the Bible. But the Church has always understood that this acceptance is a recognition of something that existed beforehand, independently of its reception.

- The books are not inspired 'merely because they contain revelation without error' (the theory of the absence of error). The First Vatican Council emphasises here that inspiration is a charism distinct from infallibility. Indeed, at this very same Council, in Chapter IV of *Pastor aeternus*, the infallibility of the Pope when speaking*ex cathedra * was declared a dogma of faith. The Bible, on the other hand, is not simply infallible, like the solemn declarations of the universal Magisterium of the Church. Sacred Scripture is much more than a book containing revelation without error. A document that sets out Christian doctrine without error may be of great value and may be used for many centuries, but it is always replaceable. For example, the Roman Catechism, published after the Council of Trent, is in practice no longer used today as an exposition of Catholic doctrine. And the current Catechism of the Catholic Church will probably become obsolete in a few centuries' time. The Bible, on the other hand, is always relevant because, as Vatican II would state, it is the word of God.

In the context of the First Vatican Council, we may recall another inadequate explanation of inspiration, attributed to Cardinal J. H. Newman (1801-1890), although he did not formulate it systematically. This is the so-called theory of 'obiter dicta' (incidental remarks), according to which certain circumstantial phrases are not to be regarded as inspired, such as the statement that Tobias's dog wagged its tail (Tobit 11:9 in the Vulgate) or that Paul left his cloak at Troas (2 Timothy 4:13). Thus, inspiration does not apply to all the statements in the Bible, but only to the important ones, that is, those which contain teachings of faith or morals[^326].

This is not the faith of the Church. It is true that the Scriptures contain some information that may seem superfluous or trivial, but this fact must not lead one to think that such passages are not inspired. The distinction between what is important and what is not depends on the reader's judgement and is not without a certain degree of arbitrariness. (This line of reasoning leads to the very same problems involved in establishing a canon within the canon, which we discussed on pp. 194197.) Above all, however, one must bear in mind that a sentence should never be taken in isolation, but rather within the context of the book in which it appears, where it always fulfils a specific function, even if it does not contain doctrinal teachings. As Leo XIII teaches in *Providentissimus Deus* (cf. EB 124), limiting inspiration is not the way to defend it: the whole of the Bible is equally inspired, even if not all of its content is on the same level of theological significance.

## 24. What is inspiration?

The Scriptures, 'inspired as they are by God and once and for all set down in writing, immutably convey the very word of God and, through the words of the prophets and apostles, the voice of the Holy Spirit'. Dei Verbum, no. 21

Without seeking to deny the mysterious nature of inspiration, something positive can be said on the subject. This is the path that all theological reflection seeks to follow.

In the first half of the 20th century, studies on inspiration experienced a period of great intensity. However, following the Second Vatican Council, the theme of inspiration receded into the background, at least when compared with the much more extensive discussions on the biblical canon. This decline in the study of inspiration does not mean that the subject has been exhausted, in the sense that all problems have now been resolved. In fact, quite the opposite is true: there are several unresolved issues, and a theological synthesis that takes account of the proposals put forward in recent decades is still lacking. It is no coincidence that, in no. 19 of the Post-Synodal Apostolic Exhortation *Verbum Domini*, Benedict XVI expresses the desire for a deeper theological exploration of biblical inspiration, especially in relation to the interpretation of the texts[^327].

153-194.

However, the theology of inspiration developed in the twentieth century has left as its legacy not only problems to be resolved, but also certain established principles, accepted by all or almost all authors, which help us to understand what the charism of inspiration is.

In the first section, we shall examine one of these points: the notion of instrumental cause, which enables us to understand how both God and the hagiographer are true authors of the books. In the second section, we shall explore some developments surrounding the notion of God as the author of the Scripture and touch upon some of the unresolved issues.

### 24.1. The notion of the instrumental cause and hagiographers as true auctores

Starting with St Thomas, the philosophical notion of the 'instrumental cause' is used in theology to explain certain realities of a supernatural order, such as the efficacy of the sacraments. A couple of texts show that Thomas also applied it to the Bible, in terms of a 'principal author' (God) and an 'instrumental author' (man), although he did not develop this insight[^328].

Father M. J. Lagrange O.P. (1855-1938) and other Catholic authors of the early 20th century employed the Thomist notion of instrumental cause to explain inspiration, applying it to the hagiographer, an instrument in the hands of God, the principal author of Scripture.

Thus, each of the biblical books is an effect proper to God; it is a text caused and willed by him: it is therefore the Word of God. But at the same time it is human speech, because God employed human instruments, which are truly causes of the final effect and have, so to speak, left their mark on the effect.

With varying nuances, almost all Catholic scholars agree that these philosophical concepts help to adequately express the faith in inspiration, even if they do not explain everything.

> There is in the medieval synthesis a lasting contribution of value which we cannot overlook. It is the application of the principle of principal-instrumental causality to the order of knowledge and biblical expression. (…) The use of the The concept of instrumentality succeeded in expressing, in the most coherent way, the reality of the operative communion between the Creator and the creature within a shared totality[^329].

The application of the concept of instrumental cause allows us to combine divine action and human action. We must examine how and why[^330].

Firstly, the distinction between the principal cause and the instrumental cause must be understood within the notion of the efficient cause, to be distinguished from the other types of cause (according to the well-known Aristotelian scheme of the four causes: material, formal, efficient and final). An efficient cause is what is commonly referred to as a cause, that is, the principle from which movement proceeds, as distinct from the final cause, which is the goal towards which the agent tends[^331].

Within efficient causality, further distinctions can be made according to various criteria. We speak of a principal cause and an instrumental cause when two agents together produce the entirety of an effect. That is to say, they are not two partial causes, each of which brings about a part of the effect, but both together form a single cause. The principal agent acts through the instrument, just as an artist does when using a brush. Thomas Aquinas explains it thus: 'The principal and instrumental agents are almost a single cause, since one acts through the other' (S.Th. I-II, q.14, a.1, ad 4).

The effect is therefore attributed to both causes, not one part to one and another part to the other: 'Effectus totus attribuitur instrumento, et principali agenti etiam totus secundum alium modum' (SCG III, yo).

An instrument may act on its own, but in such cases it is not called an instrument in the strict sense. What defines instrumentality is being moved, so that the instrument is at the same time both cause and effect. Therefore, to speak properly of an instrumental cause, one must add that it acts not only by virtue of its own form or nature, but also by virtue of another which moves it[^332].

Since the instrument and the principal agent are distinct, the effect-which would not have been produced without the participation of both-is not attributed to the two in the same way, but immediately to the instrument and properly to the principal cause, to which the effect resembles:

> The efficient cause is of two kinds: principal and instrumental. The principal cause acts by virtue of its own form, imprinting its own likeness upon the effect: fire, for example, warms by means of its heat. (…) The instrumental cause, on the other hand, does not act by virtue of its form, but by virtue of the impulse with which it is set in motion by the principal agent. Therefore, the effect does not resemble the instrument, but rather the principal agent (S.Th. III, q.62, a.1; translation by T. Centi and A. Belloni[^333]) 1.

The effect cannot be explained by the operation of the instrument alone. But the latter, which is a true cause, determines to a certain extent the operative capacity of the principal cause, whose action must pass through the specific potentialities of the instrument, through its nature, which is limited. If a man cuts wood with an axe, the final result is an effect proper to the principal cause, but the nature of the cut also depends on the shape of the axe.

*

When applying the notion of instrumental cause to hagiographers, one must bear in mind that an instrument always acts in accordance with its nature, which in this case is rational and free. This clarification is necessary because when we speak of an 'instrument' we usually think of an inanimate object (an axe, a paintbrush) and not of a person. But acting mechanically is not part of the definition of an instrumental cause. St Thomas, in fact, states that there can be free instruments:

> It is not necessary to exclude freedom entirely from the instrument, because a thing can be moved by another, and yet move itself; this is the case with the human soul (De Veritate, q.24, a.5[^334]) 2.

This point is of fundamental importance, because it avoids the temptation to view the hagiographer's role in composing the text as that of a mere co-author or as the result of a state of ecstasy. The human author is an instrument moved by God, but an instrument that thinks and makes free decisions, for which he is fully responsible.

Furthermore, thanks to the concept of instrumental cause, a difficulty that is constantly present in theological reflection is resolved: which part corresponds to God and which to the hagiographer. For example, following Lessius's line of thought, Cardinal J. B. Franzelin, S.I. (†1886) stated that God is responsible for the formal element of the books (their content), whilst the material element-the expressions that convey it-belongs to the human author. By contrast, thanks to the notion of instrumentality, it is possible to state clearly that the books are entirely the work of both God and the human author, each at their own level[^335].

As one might imagine, it is difficult to explore this interaction between God's action and human freedom in greater depth, as is the case in theology whenever the interaction between the supernatural and the natural, or between grace and meritorious works, is discussed, or when speaking of the saints as instruments of God.

*

The idea of the hagiographer as an instrument was mentioned in passing in Leo XIII's encyclical *Providentissimus Deus* (1893), in a famous description of inspiration (EB 125, text quoted below on page 257).

Fifty years later, in the encyclical *Divino Agente Spiritu* (1943), Pius XII explicitly embraced the Thomist notion of instrumental cause and its application to Sacred Scripture:

> Our age, whilst presenting new questions and difficulties, nevertheless, thank God, also offers exegesis new means and tools. Among these, special mention must be made of the fact that Catholic theologians, following the teaching of the Holy Fathers and, above all, of the Angelic and Universal Doctor, have examined and expounded the nature of biblical inspiration and its effects with greater precision and subtlety than was customary in past centuries. Taking as their starting point in their discussions the principle that the hagiographer, in writing the sacred book, is an organ -that is, an instrument-of the Holy Spirit, but a living instrument endowed with reason, they rightly observe that, under divine guidance, he makes such use of his own faculties and powers that from the book composed through his work everyone can easily discern 'his own character and how his per- personal traits and his character' (cf. Benedict XV, Encyclical Spiritus Paraclitus, EB461). (EB 556).

With these words, the application of the Thomist notion of 'instrument', together with the explicit mention of the hagiographer's free will, makes its entry, so to speak, into the Church's magisterium. It is an example of how theological reflections contribute to the maturing of the Church's judgement (cf. DV 12).

Twenty years later, this passage from *Divino Agente Spiritu* is quoted in the section of *Dei Verbum* devoted to inspiration:

> In composing the sacred books, God chose and made use of men in full possession of their faculties and abilities, so that, whilst He worked in them and through them, they might write, as true authors, all and only those things which He wished to be written (DV 11[^336]) 1.

Whilst drafting this sentence, the Bishop of Barbastro (Spain) suggested adding the words 'living instruments'. However, the commission rejected the proposal, stating that it wished to avoid technical terminology and that the concept of instrumentality was already present in the text[^337].

Indeed, although the word is absent, the idea that the sacred writers are instruments appears in the text when, using biblical terminology, it is stated that 'God acts in them and through them '. Furthermore, the reference to the passage from Pius XII just cited confirms the presence of the notion of instrumentality.

Thus, DV11 draws on concepts already used to describe inspiration. But there is a significant innovation: for the first time in a magisterial document, the sacred writers are referred to as authors (true auctores).

This had not been stated before, because the traditional teaching held that the author of the Bible is God, as reaffirmed by the First Vatican Council, and it was not possible to explain how a text could have two authors, one divine and one human. Indeed, during the drafting of *Dei Verbum*, three Council Fathers requested that the expression*ut veri auctores * be removed, precisely because the sacred writers are nothing more than instruments[^338]!

In reality, the correct line of reasoning is precisely the opposite: both God and man can be considered the true authors of the biblical books, because they act at different levels-that of the principal cause and that of the instrumental cause. Indeed, the application of the Thomist notion of the instrumental cause was fundamental in order to arrive at the conclusion that the hagiographers are truly the authors of their books. A biblical text is produced by two causes, but these are not two partial causes, as if each were responsible for a part, but rather two causes subordinate to one another. The Bible is therefore entirely the work of God and entirely the work of man. Each book is immediately attributed to its human author and, properly speaking, to the Holy Spirit.

As mentioned, the idea of instrumentality helps us to understand the cooperation between God and man in the composition of Scripture, each at their own level, but it does not resolve all the problems associated with the idea of God as the author or cause of the texts. The explanation of instrumental causation focuses too much on the author and fails to consider the importance of the reception of the texts as a whole. To understand in what sense God is the author not only of each book but of the whole of Scripture, one must relate the divine origin of the texts to the purpose for which he chose to inspire the authors- -as we shall now see.

### 24.2. Author - text - reader. God as author and the importance

of reception

In the sacred books, in fact, the Father who is in heaven comes with great love to meet his children and enters into conversation with them Dei Verbum no. 21

To speak of Scripture as the word of God, we have used the analogy of the Incarnation of the Word. We shall now use a different, but equally useful, framework to understand other aspects concerning biblical inspiration. This is the framework of communication, which we presented in the first part:

sender → message → recipient

For communication to take place, it is essential that the message reaches the recipient; otherwise, one cannot speak of communication. We have also seen that in the case of written communication, the model must change:

author → message ‖ message → reader Communication in this case requires a duplication of acts; and this occurred properly only in the act of reading.

Now, we can attempt to incorporate inspiration into the framework of written communication, since the Bible is a written message intended for the Church. We may ask ourselves who is inspired: the author, the text or the reader? At this point, it is clear that both the author and the text must be regarded as inspired. But so far we have said nothing about the reader.

This is the moment to return to the text of *Dei Filius* that we quoted above. If we set aside the condemnation of errors, the text states:

> These books of the Old and New Testaments (…) the Church regards as sacred and canonical (…) because, having been written under the inspiration[^340] of the Holy Spirit, they have God as their author and as such have been handed down to the Church (EB yy).

The description from the First Vatican Council brings together three elements:

- the books were written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, that is, there is divine action in the origin (in the composition) of the books;

- the books have God as their author (Deum habent auctorem): this statement follows on from the previous one. The result or effect of inspiration is that the books have God as their author: the verb is in the present tense, so this is a permanent characteristic.

- !) ut tales ipsi Ecclesiae traditi sunt, they were handed down to the Church as sacred and canonical books.

The First Vatican Council left it to theologians to explore the concept of inspiration in greater depth on the basis of these three elements. However, two further observations should be made regarding the paragraph in *Dei Filius*:

- In Latin, the term auctor is generic; it means 'cause' or 'origin', and not necessarily 'author' in the literary sense, that is, a writer. The word can therefore be understood in various ways, which the First Vatican Council did not wish to specify.

- Some have interpreted the text of Dei Filius as a definition referring, on the one hand, to inspiration (the influence of the Spirit, whereby God is the author) and, on the other, to the canon (acceptance within the Church). But this was not the intention of the First Vatican Council, which sought only to clarify the doctrine concerning inspiration. It is therefore incorrect to rely on this text to limit the inspired character of the books to their composition. We shall return to this point[^339].

In the encyclical *Providentissimus Deus* (1893), Leo XIII sought to continue the discussion on biblical inspiration begun by the First Vatican Council, by describing God's action upon the author of each book. To defend the inerrancy (the absence of errors) of the Bible, the Pope appeals to the fact that God is its author:

> Therefore, it is of no avail here to say that the Holy Spirit used men as instruments to write, as if some error might have escaped not, of course, the principal author, but the inspired writers. Indeed, He Himself so stirred and moved them to write by His supernatural power, and so assisted them whilst they were writing, that they might rightly conceive in their minds all those things-and those things alone-which He willed, and might have the will to write them faithfully and express them in a fitting manner with infallible truth: otherwise he himself would not be the author of the whole of Sacred Scripture (EB 125).

The encyclical focuses on the composition of the books and makes no mention of their delivery to the Church, that is, the moment of the recipient's reception of the message.

Following *Providentissimus Deus*, all subsequent studies on the subject continued to centre on the human author who composed the book. The most fruitful outcome of this endeavour was the notion of instrumentality, which we have just examined.

Another conclusion of this model, reached through the study of the Thomist notion of prophecy, is as follows: the fact that the human author is aware of inspiration is not part of the charism of inspiration. Perrella explains it as follows:

> As a general principle, it is not necessary for the hagiographer to be aware of inspiration; or, to put it another way, awareness of inspiration does not form part of the very notion of inspiration itself, since grace-whether sanctifying or charismatic-adapts itself to nature in such a way that man cannot distinguish whether he is acting by his own natural powers alone or by virtue of a supernatural action. St Thomas states this explicitly with regard to 'imperfect prophecy' (or general revelation), that is, inspiration. Upon examining the available evidence, the language used by the author of 2 Maccabees and St Luke does not seem at all to support the hypothesis of awareness of inspiration.

> On the other hand, God can undoubtedly make the hagiographer aware of the inspiration, but this could only occur through a revelation (in the strict sense), given separately, that is, independently of the inspiration 1.

Further developments along these lines have reached a dead end. Attempts were made, in fact, to specify how God acted upon the author's will before and during the writing of the book, or in what way He enlightened his intellect, and generally how He influenced his various faculties[^341]. But in doing so, it was forgotten that what matters is the text we have today, which may well have had dozens of different authors. Furthermore, this focus on the author's inspiration has also come under strain due to developments in linguistics and hermeneutics, which have emphasised the importance of reception in the communication process.

It has always been clear that not only the authors, but also the biblical texts themselves are inspired. Indeed, according to 2 Timothy 3:16, inspiration pertains to the text. We may ask ourselves what it means for a text to be inspired, apart from the fact that its author composed it under a special influence of the Holy Spirit.

As we have said on several occasions, a text only comes to life when it is read; otherwise, it remains silent. Consequently, the current focus has shifted to the relationship between inspiration and the reader. Should we perhaps say that every reader of Scripture receives inspiration from the Holy Spirit?

Such a claim would not be new. Indeed, Calvin (1509-1564), one of the Protestant reformers, said that the Bible is not only inspired by God, but that it is also 'God-inspiring'. In other words, those who read the Scriptures are moved and edified by the Holy Spirit. Calvin even spoke of Scripture's power to inspire as a criterion for recognising which books are inspired.

The Church has never accepted such an idea, which, moreover, can be easily refuted by experience: one can read a passage from the Bible and feel no effect attributable to the Holy Spirit.

However, even without sharing the Calvinist position, it is reasonable to think that the Holy Spirit did not intervene merely in the composition of the books, but that He also acts at the moment of their reading[^342]. Perhaps it would be desirable to find a term other than 'inspiration', but it is clear that the charism of inspiration would be useless if no one were able to perceive its effects-that is, to hear the voice of God whilst reading.

It is, in fact, logical to think that the Holy Spirit acts during reading, though not in every instance of reading-that is to say, not with every possible reader of the biblical pages.

Here we may attempt to reclaim the role of the Church as the recipient of the sacred books, as suggested by the First Vatican Council. It is the Church that receives the sacred books and thus recognises that God is their author. It is the Church, especially in the liturgy, that hears the divine voice in the sacred pages. 'God, who spoke in the past, does not cease to speak to the bride of his beloved Son' (DV 8[^343]) 1.

Dei Verbum never states that the Bible is the word of God solely within the Church. But in fact, it always speaks of Scripture within the life of the Church. In addition to the texts already cited, one need only read Chapter VI of the constitution, entitled 'Sacred Scripture in the Life of the Church'. For example, DV 21 states:

> (…) It is therefore necessary that ecclesiastical preaching, like the Christian religion itself, be nourished and guided by Sacred Scripture. In the sacred books, indeed, the Father who is in heaven comes with great love to meet his children and enters into conversation with them; and the word of God possesses such efficacy and power that it is the support and strength of the Church, and for the children of the Church the strength of their faith, the nourishment of the soul, and the pure and everlasting source of spiritual life. Therefore, what has been said must be applied par excellence to Sacred Scripture: 'The word of God is living and effective' (Heb 4:12), 'which has the power to build up and grant an inheritance among all those who are sanctified' (Acts 20:32; cf. 1 Thess 2:13).

To speak of the Church is to speak of tradition. If one reads the Bible outside of tradition, inspiration loses at least part of its effectiveness. To put it another way, one cannot consider the charism of inspiration in isolation from the divine plan for the preservation of the Word of God. This is in keeping with the realisation that the Holy Spirit acts in the proclamation and preservation of the Word of God, according to the various charisms that structure the Church.

> If one asks what this action of the Spirit consists of, one must be careful not to reduce it to the inner illumination of believers' hearts; in reality, it accompanies, animates and supports, through charisms, the very exercise of the ministries that give Tradition its defined structure. Such is the set of concrete means by which the Spirit ensures the Church's infallibility in the faith[^344].

This action of the Spirit is not identical to inspiration, but it is essential for inspiration to be effective.

To understand why and how the Holy Spirit acts in the reading of the Bible within the Church, St Thomas offers us a clue when, after explaining prophecy, he speaks of the charism of the word:

> Charisms, as we have explained above, are granted for the good of others. Now, the knowledge received from God could not be directed towards the good of others except through the word. And since the Holy Spirit ensures that nothing is lacking that is beneficial to the good of the Church, he has provided for its members in this regard as well: not only by enabling them to speak in such a way as to be understood by different peoples, through the gift of tongues; but also by enabling them to speak effectively, through the charism 'of the word' (II-II, q.177, a.1, in c[^345].) 1.

If we replace 'word' with 'Scripture' in the text, we can apply this reasoning to the charism of inspiration. Knowledge becomes useful to others through Scripture. The Holy Spirit must act not only in the composition of the text, but also in its reading, so that it may be properly understood. The ultimate recipient of this 'knowledge', which is divine revelation, is none other than the Church. As we have said, the inspired author may be unaware of his own inspiration; the Church, on the other hand, is not unaware of the inspiration of the texts she reads 2.

- The following quotation would require some clarification, as we have not yet discussed the concept of prophecy in St Thomas, but it may be helpful: 'From the hagiographer's point of view, the charism of inspiration is prophetic only in an imperfect sense, because it normally lacks visions and, above all, because the recipient is not always aware of God's action upon them, nor do they perceive the full scope of their words. (…) On the other hand, when considering the reception of Scripture within the Church, one can speak of true and proper prophecy, since the elements that led to speaking of imperfection in some cases of prophecy disappear. Firstly, the Church is certain of the divine origin of the books and is therefore aware of God's revelatory action present in the texts-she knows that God is their author, which is why she regards them as inspired and canonical. Secondly, the Church does not read without understanding, as Caiaphas prophesied, but comprehends the content of the revelation, albeit not entirely. Finally, the Church receives apostolic revelation, which, according to the degrees described by St Thomas regarding the progress of revelation (II-II, q.1 and 4, a.6), corresponds to the highest degree of prophecy', J. C. Ossandón Widow,

# Excursus 1: History of the: a general introduction to Sacred Scripture

Theology has developed considerably over the centuries and, as a result, has been divided into 'treatises' or specialised branches. Some of these branches have existed for centuries, such as the treatise on the Triune God or that on the Incarnate Word (now known as 'Christology'), whilst others are more recent. Ecclesiology, for example, only emerged as such in the 20th century, although it is clear that earlier theologians had spoken of the Church. St Thomas Aquinas discusses it, but in the *Summa Theologiae* there is no treatise *De Ecclesia* (the topic appears, amongst other places, in the treatise *De Verbo Incarnato*, when he examines the principal grace of Christ: III q.8).

A similar phenomenon occurs with the IGSS. Continuing with the example of Thomas's *Summa*, although he constantly quotes the Bible and devotes several articles to it (especially in the first question of the first*pars*), there is no*quaestio* entitled *De sacra Scriptura*.

In what follows, we shall attempt to offer a historical overview of the IGSS. However, rather than a reconstruction of the entire history, we shall take a brief survey, focusing on certain significant authors who have contributed to the current understanding of the Bible[^346].

## 25. Origen and Augustine

Before the Modern Age, among the works that most closely resemble a manual on IGSS, Book IV of Origen's *De principiis* stands out; in it, an attempt is made to demonstrate the inspired nature of Scripture and certain criteria for its interpretation are proposed, and and then St Augustine's *De doctrina christiana*, famous for its distinction between*res* and *ver bum*, and for the hermeneutical principle of charity.

### 25.1. Scripture in Origen's *De principiis*

In a certain sense, Origen of Alexandria (c. 185-254) may be regarded as the first great theologian in the history of the Church.

In one of his works, entitled *De principiis* or *Peri Archôn*, published around the year 220, he sets out to provide a systematic presentation of the truths of the faith. Among these, Sacred Scripture occupies a central place, to which he devotes the entirety of Book IV.

Indeed, the two major themes of Origen's presentation of the Bible in Book IV-divine inspiration and spiritual interpretation-form part of the teachings received from the apostolic tradition. In the preface to *De principiis*, Origen mentions them as doctrines taught by the apostles:

> It is also handed down that the Scriptures were composed by the Spirit of God and contain not only the meaning that is manifest, but also another that eludes most people[^347].

At the beginning of Book IV, Origen sets out to demonstrate, from a rational perspective, the divinely inspired nature of the Scriptures (IV,1). He puts forward two arguments: the fulfilment in Christ of the ancient prophecies and the spread of Christianity, as foretold by Jesus. The first serves to demonstrate the inspiration of the Old Testament, whilst the second helps to understand the supernatural origin of Jesus' teachings, contained in the New Testament.

In keeping with the logic of his argument, Origen draws a conclusion that is worth quoting:

> It must, however, be recognised that the divine character of the prophetic writings and the spiritual meaning of the Law of Moses were revealed with the coming of Christ: indeed, prior to this, it was not possible to put forward clear arguments regarding the inspiration of the Old Testament. Instead, the coming of Jesus prompted those who might have doubted the divine nature of the Law and the Prophets to recognise them clearly as writings of heavenly grace (IV,1,6).

It is not that Origen denies the inspiration of the Holy Spirit at the time the books were composed. Rather, he postpones the possibility of recognising this divine action until time of the Church. We shall have to return to this point in the section devoted to inspiration. For now, it is simply worth noting that, when speaking of the Bible, Origen begins with inspiration, because everything else depends on it. Origen then devotes the following two chapters to biblical interpretation (IV,2-!).

He sets out the famous doctrine of the three senses of the text - somatic, psychic and spiritual which gave rise to the patristic and medieval distinction between the literal sense and the spiritual sense. At present, however, we are interested in other statements by the Alexandrian scholar, which help to define the Bible.

In IV,2,y, Origen explains that in order to interpret Scripture correctly, one must bear in mind 'the purpose which the Spirit had in mind' in enlightening the prophets and apostles, namely: to reveal 'first and foremost the ineffable mysteries of the human condition', so that souls might attain perfection. Since such perfection cannot be attained without a profound and precise knowledge of God, it was ordained that God and his Only-Begotten Son should reveal themselves to humankind. Therefore, the purpose of the revelation contained in Scripture is to make God known to human beings, so that they may become perfect.

In inspiring the Bible, however, the Spirit - as Origen tells us again in IV,2,8 - took care not to reveal the truths about God to those unworthy of receiving them, in accordance with Jesus' command: 'Do not give what is holy to the dogs, and do not throw your pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet and then turn and tear you to pieces' (Mt 7:6). Therefore, in the sacred pages, the mysteries are described symbolically, through accounts of wars or legal prescriptions.

For Origen, not all readers of the Bible are capable of penetrating the spiritual meaning hidden behind the letter. There is a correlation between the degree of perfection of each individual Christian and the depth of meaning in Scripture. The meanings are therefore not defined on the basis of what the author of Scripture-who is God-intended, for that is an infinite meaning, but on the basis of what the reader understands, according to their capacity. There are therefore as many meanings as there are types of readers. The Bible, then, is not the same for everyone!

Today, Origen's terminology strikes us as strange (the 'mysteries' revealed to the 'perfect', etc.). Undoubtedly, his understanding of the Bible presents some debatable aspects: if the meanings hidden within the text are infinite, it becomes impossible to say that one interpretation is better than another. But the framework within which Origen understands Scripture is one we can agree with: its origin is divine and its purpose is to make the 'mysteries' known to humankind so that they may attain spiritual fulfilment.

### 25.2. St Augustine's *De doctrina christiana*

The work entitled *De doctrina christiana* - which St Augustine began in 395 but did not complete until 426 - served as a key reference text throughout the Middle Ages. It is divided into four books. The first three deal with Scripture; the fourth, however, addresses a subject that lies outside the scope of our interest and may therefore be set aside.

The fundamental distinction proposed by Augustine is that between sign (signum) and reality (res). Both are of interest to the interpreter, because Scripture teaches 'things' through 'signs', which are the words. The structure of the first three books of *De doctrina christiana* is shaped by this distinction.

In Book I, Augustine presents the 'realities', that is, the content of Scripture - the summa res being the Trinity -, beginning with the distinction between uti and frui, between things intended to be used as means and those which, on the other hand, exist to be enjoyed, not utilised. The Holy Scriptures fall within this classification, in that they are a certain 'thing' and have a purpose intended by God. For Augustine, the Bible is a means, an instrument for coming to enjoy (frui) God, for reaching heaven and finding delight in the Trinity. To this end, the Scriptures impart to us the twofold precept of charity, towards God and towards one's neighbour.

If Scripture is a means to lead to love, those who have attained it can do without it. Indeed, the Bible is not an end in itself; those who possess charity 'have no need of the Scriptures except to instruct others' (non indiget scripturis nisi ad alios instruendos, I, 19, 43). Herein lies the entire content of the Bible. Charity is the end and the fulfilment of the Scriptures. (Hence, St Augustine will say, in a famous passage, that any interpretation of a biblical text which contributes to the growth of charity is valid).

In Books II and III, Augustine discusses the signs of Scripture. At the beginning of Book II, he discusses the nature of signs in general (II,1,1-II,4,5). In the remainder of Book II and in Book III, he addresses the question of how to resolve biblical passages that are difficult to interpret. The difficulty for the interpreter may stem either from the ambiguity of the signs or from their obscurity. The Bible presents a whole range of difficulties. However, Augustine explains, there is no truth expressed in an obscure manner that is not clearly set out elsewhere in Scripture itself.

> In a magnificent and wholesome way, the Holy Spirit has proportioned the content of the Holy Scriptures, satisfying hunger with passages of the clearest meaning and dispelling dislike through the more obscure ones. Indeed, almost nothing is gleaned from these obscure passages that is not expressed elsewhere in the clearest possible way[^348].

This principle rests on a theological premise that should be noted: God, the author of Scripture, intended it as a means to save us, not to confuse us.

If the words of the Bible are signs, then they point to something other than themselves. What, asks Augustine, should one seek when reading the Scriptures?

> To understand the thoughts and intentions of its authors and, through them, the will of God, in accordance with which we believe those men spoke (II,5,6).

The ultimate aim of reading is to discover the divine will. But to arrive at this, one must necessarily pass through human authors. This point was not included in Origen's presentation, which does not take hagiographers into account. This is a fundamental principle of biblical interpretation, as we shall see in Part IV.

Although he starts from a different framework and uses less intellectual language, St Augustine agrees with Origen in situating Scripture within God's plan of salvation. But his consideration of the nature of words as signs opens up scope for reflecting on the human language through which God speaks to us in the Bible.

## 26. From Sixtus of Siena to the present day

Strictly speaking, the first treatise on the IGSS was written by the Dominican Sixtus of Siena (†1569). The book was entitled *Bibliotheca Sancta* and was published in Venice in 1566, that is, twenty years after the first session of the Council of Trent, at which the list of sacred books was defined as a dogma of faith (as discussed above). It is therefore not surprising that Sixtus begins by discussing the biblical canon. He then examines the theme of God as the author of the Bible and subsequently biblical interpretation. The book concludes with a history of exegesis from St Augustine up to the sixteenth century. The entire book is imbued with an apologetic tone, directed against the Protestant reformers. Sixtus's work had a major influence on the structure of the IGSS treatises right up to the beginning of the

. From Sixtus onwards, the study of the Bible began with the canon, which was a departure from the approach of Origen and Augustine, who began with inspiration.

This approach would change again in the nineteenth century, a century characterised by philosophical rationalism and the development of philology, history and archaeology.

A new, desacralised and often aggressive approach to studying the Bible thus emerged, one that cast doubt on the historicity of many accounts, denied the reality of miracles, denounced the immorality of certain precepts, highlighted the limitations of biblical cosmology, and so on. In short, philosophy and the human sciences, combined with positivist assumptions, launched fierce attacks against the Scriptures. The attitude of Catholics became predominantly apologetic. In 1870, the First Vatican Council identified certain philosophical errors linked to rationalism and reaffirmed the Catholic faith in the inspiration of the Scriptures (for the Council's words, see p. 247).

It was during this period that an IGSS textbook was published which, for a long time, was the most famous and influential. In 1885, the Jesuit Rudolph Cornely, together with other Catholic exegetes, began publishing the *Cursus Scripturae Sacrae*. The first volume is entitled *Historia et critica introductio in Utriusque Testamenti libros sacros*. Vol. I: Introductio generalis sive de U.T. Canonis, Textus, Interpretationis Historia. The title highlights that the book's content broadly corresponds to Sixtus of Siena's manual: Cornely begins with the canon, continues with a discussion of the original text and the authority of the versions, and concludes with interpretation. Curiously, despite developments in the doctrine of inspiration, Cornely makes no mention of it. The reason he gives is that this is a subject that falls more within the scope of dogmatic theology. However, in 1893, Cornely published his*Compendium introductionis historicae et criticae in libros sacros Utriusque Testamenti *, in which he included an appendix on inspiration.

In 1893, Leo XIII published the encyclical *Providentissimus Deus*, which constituted the Magisterium's first response to the so-called 'Biblical question', that is, to the problems raised by rationalist criticism. Among other things, the Pope speaks of the truth of the Bible and the inspiration of the biblical authors. It is worth noting that, from *Providentissimus* onwards, inspiration - closely linked to the defence of biblical inerrancy (that is, that the Bible is free from all error) - became a central feature of all IGSS textbooks right up to the Second Vatican Council. For example, one of the finest textbooks of this period, that by Gaetano Perrella-which remains useful to this day for its erudition-is structured in four parts: inspiration, the canon, the text and hermeneutics.

After Vatican II, the Bible began to be presented from a less apologetic perspective. For example, rather than defending inerrancy at all costs, the preference was to speak of the truth that the Bible conveys for our salvation. And the textbooks drew particularly on the main contribution of *Dei Verbum*: situating Scripture within the framework of divine revelation. This is what we have also attempted to do in the first part of the programme.

# Excursus 2: 'Apostle' in the New Testament

Who are the apostles? We usually think of the Twelve and St Paul. But according to the liturgical calendar, 11 June is the feast day of 'St Barnabas the Apostle'. In addition to the question of who should be identified as apostles, there is also the question of the definition of the term. What does 'apostle' mean? What characteristics distinguish an apostle?

It is worth dwelling on this topic, because ever since the origins of Christianity, 'apostolicity'-of revelation, of the Scriptures, of the Church-has been invoked as a synonym for authenticity: authentic faith is the faith of the apostles; the true Church is that in which apostolic succession is preserved, and so on. St Thomas Aquinas summarised this principle clearly: 'Our faith is founded on the revelation made to the prophets and apostles' (Innititur enim fides nostra revelationi apostolis et prophetis factae, S.Th., I, q. 1, a. 8, ad 2). The importance of the apostles stems from the fact that they, like the prophets before Christ, were the first recipients of revelation.

Generally, in the documents of the Church's Magisterium, the term 'apostle' and similar terms are frequently used, but without specifying their meaning. Dei Verbum says nothing on this matter, either because it assumes this to be common knowledge or perhaps because it prefers not to offer an overly precise definition. Nor is there a definition of 'apostle' to be found in the decree of Trent cited in Dei Verbum[^349].

A comparison between the use of the word in the Church's tradition and its usage in the books of the New Testament reveals certain differences. More precisely, the Magisterium uses- It interprets the term in a narrower sense compared with the meanings of 'apostle' (, áuóστο ʎ ο@) in the New Testament.

## 27. The meanings of 'apostle' in the New Testament

In what follows, we shall list all the passages in which the term 'apostle' appears, or, categorised according to the meaning it conveys in each instance[^350].

### 27.1. Etymology of the word and its use in John and Hebrews
