In Greek, ἀπόστολος derives from the verb ἀποστέλλω, which means 'to send'. Etymologically, 'apostle' means 'one who is sent'. However, prior to Christianity, it was not a term in common usage. Outside the New Testament, it occurs very rarely, almost always in a military context[^351]. By contrast, the word appears throughout almost the entire New Testament - it is absent only from 2 Thessalonians, James and the Epistles of John - which is a sign of its importance as well as its widespread use amongst the early Christians.

It is highly likely that the use of the term 'apostle' in the New Testament has a Semitic origin, as it corresponds to the Hebrew word shalûah (חוּלַשַׁ), which means precisely 'one who is sent', 'ambassador', as does the corresponding Aramaic term shelîah (ִליַחשְׁ).

> According to the Semitic substrate, the term means a delegate or ambassador who carries out a mission as a plenipotentiary on behalf of someone who holds authority and who is, in some way, made present through the delegate. The apostle represents the one who gave him the mandate[^352].

Sometimes, the word is used in the New Testament simply in this sense, which we might call 'etymological-Semitic'. This is the case with the mention of certain 'apostles' in 2 Cor 8:23, which the CEI Bible correctly translates as 'delegates': 'As for our brothers, they are delegates [ἀπόστολοι] of the churches and the glory of Christ'. The same can be said of Phil 2:25:

> I felt it necessary to send Epaphroditus to you, my brother, my fellow worker and fellow fighter, and your envoy [úµĩn &έ áuóστο ʎ ον], to help me with my needs.

We can draw a parallel with this meaning in the use of the term in the Gospel of John, where ἀπόστολος appears only once, on the lips of Jesus as he addresses his disciples:

> 'Truly, truly, I say to you: a servant is not greater than his master, nor is a messenger [ἀπόστολος] greater than the one who sent him' (John 13:16).

Here the meaning corresponds perfectly to the Semitic one, but it must be added that in John the vocabulary relating to 'sending' takes on enormous theological depth. Indeed, the verb ἀποστέλλω is extremely frequent in John (28 occurrences), as is the verb πέμπω (12 occurrences), which also means 'to send'. John the Baptist is a man sent by God (1:6; 1:28); Jesus is the one sent by the Father (1:1; 4:14; 5:16, etc.), who in turn sends out his disciples (4:18; 1:20; 1:18; 2:21[^353]) 1.

Jesus' statement in John 20:21 neatly summarises the use of these verbs in the Fourth Gospel (and, incidentally, demonstrates the equivalence between ἀποστέλλω and πέμπω):

> Jesus said to them again: 'Peace be with you! As the Father has sent me [ἀπέσταλκέν µs], so I send you [πέμπω úµã@]'.

On the other hand, although the Fourth Gospel frequently refers to Jesus' disciples and to the more specific group of the 'Twelve', chosen by him (6:6, 67, yo, 71; 20:24), it never calls them 'apostles'.

In summary, in John, 'apostle' is used in the etymological-Semitic sense of 'one sent', with a profound theological significance, within the theology of mission characteristic of the Fourth Gospel. It is clear that this meaning does not correspond exactly to the use of the term in Dei Verbum, which, when it speaks of 'apostles', does not refer to 'those sent' in general, but always to the apostles of Jesus Christ. In John, this concept is present, but without a specific term.

The same can be said of the Letter to the Hebrews, where the word is used only once (Heb 1:1), which is also the only instance in the New Testament where it is applied to Jesus:

> Therefore, holy brothers and sisters, you who share in a heavenly calling, fix your eyes on Jesus, the apostle and high priest of the faith we profess.

### 27.2. The Group of the Twelve

#### 27.2.1. The Eleven sent by Jesus (Mt 28:16-20;

Mk 16:14-15)

When *Dei Verbum* speaks of 'apostles', it is not referring to envoys or delegates in general, but to those sent by Jesus Christ - men who have received a specific commission from him, namely, to preach the Gospel to all people. Indeed, the first mention of the apostles in Dei Verbum is accompanied by a reference to two passages from the Gospel (Mt 28:19-20 and Mk 16:15), which can guide our inquiry (cf. DVy, text cited on p. yy). Here are the respective texts from Matthew and Mark:

> Meanwhile, the eleven disciples went to Galilee, to the mountain which Jesus had indicated to them. When they saw him, they fell down before him. Yet they doubted. Jesus came up to them and said, 'All authority in heaven and on earth has been given to me. Go, therefore, and make disciples of all nations, baptising them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, teaching them to observe all that I have commanded you. And behold, I am with you always, to the end of the age.' (Mt 28:16-20).

> Finally, he also appeared to the Eleven whilst they were at table, and he rebuked them for their unbelief and hardness of heart, because they had not believed those who had seen him after his resurrection. And he said to them, 'Go into all the world and proclaim the Gospel to every creature.' (Mark 16:14-15).

Curiously, however, in these two passages, Jesus' commission is addressed to the 'Eleven disciples' (Mt 28:16) or simply to the 'Eleven' (Mk 16:14). The word 'apostle' does not appear!

If we were to stop here, the apostles to whom DVy refers would be these Eleven, that is, the Twelve minus Judas Iscariot. But this identification is not sufficient; indeed, in DV8 there is a reference to 2 Thessalonians 2:15 which presupposes that Saint Paul is regarded as an apostle. Before analysing the case of Paul, however, we need to complete the information on the group of the Eleven or the Twelve.

#### 27.2.2. The group of the Twelve in Luke's writings (Lk-Acts)

When we hear the expression 'the apostles', we immediately think of the group of 'the Twelve Apostles', mentioned in the Synoptic Gospels and in the Book of Acts. In reality, it is above all in Luke's writings (Lk and Acts) that the correspondence between 'the apostles' and 'the Twelve' is found[^354].

Furthermore, according to the Gospel of Luke, the title 'apostle' comes from Jesus, who bestows it upon the Twelve after having personally chosen them:

> When day came, he called his disciples to him and chose twelve of them, whom he also named apostles [οὓς καὶ ἀποστόλους ὠνόμασεν]: Simon, whom he also named Peter; Andrew, his brother; James, John, Philip, Bartholomew, Matthew, Thomas; James, son of Alphaeus; Simon, known as the Zealot; Judas, son of James; and Judas Iscariot, who became the betrayer (Lk 6:116; cf. 9:10; 11:5; 22:14; 24:10[^355]) 2.

During his public ministry, Jesus chose 'through the Holy Spirit' (Acts 1:2) twelve men from among his disciples 'to be with him and to send them out to preach, with the power to drive out demons' (Mark 1:14-15). It is noteworthy that the three Synoptic Gospels take care to list their names, always beginning with Peter and ending with Judas Iscariot. This serves to emphasise the uniqueness of this group and its importance for the early Christians.

Why twelve? The only explanation for this number mentioned in the New Testament has an eschatological dimension: sitting on twelve thrones to judge the twelve tribes (cf. Mt 19:28; Lk 22:10). The figure does not correspond to the seventy members of the Sanhedrin - the religious authority at that time - but refers to the twelve tribes of Israel. The selection of twelve men is intended to signify the eschatological renewal of Israel envisaged by Jesus.

The role of the Twelve does not end when Jesus ascends into heaven, even though they can no longer 'be with him'. On the contrary, from that moment onwards the group takes on central importance. Indeed, the Acts of the Apostles is one of the books that speaks most extensively of the Twelve. When the term 'the apostles' is used, it almost always refers to the Twelve - or to the Eleven, as in Acts 1:2 -: cf. Acts 1:2, 26; 2:37.42-43; 4:33.35-37;

5:2, 12, 18, 29, 40; 6:6; 8:1, 14, 18; 9:27; 11:1; 14:4, 14; 15:2, 4, 6, 22-23; 16:4.

In Acts 1:13, the names of the Eleven are given. A little further on, one of the first accounts is that of the reconstitution of the group of the Twelve. Addressing the approximately 120 brothers listening to him (cf. 1:15), Saint Peter explains the need to replace the traitor (1:16-20) and then sets out clearly the requirements for the 'candidates':

> It is therefore necessary that, from among those who have been with us throughout the time that the Lord Jesus lived amongst us-beginning with John's baptism right up to the day he was taken up into heaven from our midst-one should become a witness, together with us, of his resurrection [μάρτυρα τῆς ἀναστάσεως αὐτοῦ σὺν ἡμῖν γενέσθαι] (Acts 1:21-22).

The choice, made by lot to show that it was not the result of a human decision, fell on Matthias, 'who was numbered among the eleven apostles' (1:26). He therefore holds the title of apostle, even though he was not chosen directly by Jesus nor was he present when the Eleven were sent out to preach to the whole world. But Saint Matthias will be present on the day of Pentecost and will receive the Holy Spirit together with the others (cf. Acts 2:1ff.).

From the words of Saint Peter just quoted, it follows that there may have been several men who met the various requirements (apart from Matthias, there was at least 'Joseph, called Barsabbas, nicknamed Justus', Acts 1:23). Therefore, the difference between the group of the Twelve and the wider group of disciples who accompanied Jesus during his earthly life and saw him after his resurrection does not lie in the fact that the former knew Jesus and the latter did not. Nor can it be said that only the Twelve saw the risen Jesus. In fact, the role of the Twelve seems to be that of publicly bearing witness to the resurrection: this explains why Peter speaks of becoming a witness to the resurrection (1:22). Many accompanied Jesus and saw him risen, but they were not entrusted with the public role of bearing witness to it in the same way as the Twelve Apostles. Here we see a link between being an apostle and being a witness to the resurrection of Jesus (cf. also Acts 4:33; 5:32), which we shall also find in the Pauline epistles.

It is interesting to note that, after Herod Agrippa had James, son of Zebedee, put to death (Acts 12:2), the apostles once again numbered eleven, yet they did not replace James. If Judas had left his place vacant, it was because he had not managed to fulfil his mission of bearing witness to the resurrection of Jesus.

As we have said, in the Acts of the Apostles there is almost always equivalence between 'the apostles' and 'the Twelve'. The word 'almost' is necessary because in Acts 14:4, 14 Paul and Barnabas are called 'apostles', even though they are not part of the Twelve. In his letters, Saint Paul unambiguously claims the title of 'apostle' for himself. We must examine what means by this.

### 27.3. 'Apostle' in Paul's letters

In the salutation of almost all his letters, Paul introduces himself with the title 'apostle of Christ Jesus' (Rom 1:1; 1 Cor 1:1; 2 Cor 1:1; Gal 1:1; Eph 1:1; Col 1:1; 1 Tim 1:1; 2 Tim 1:1; Tit 1:1). When another sender of the letter is mentioned, it remains clear that the title belongs solely to Paul, as in the First Epistle to the Corinthians:

> Paul, called to be an apostle of Christ Jesus by the will of God, and Sosthenes our brother, to the Church of God in Corinth (1 Cor 1:1-2).

According to the First Epistle to the Corinthians itself, being an apostle is linked to the status of eyewitness to the Risen One:

> Am I not free? Am I not an apostle? Have I not seen Jesus, our Lord? And are you not my work in the Lord? Even if I am not an apostle to others, at least to you I am; you are, in the Lord, the seal of my apostleship. My defence against those who accuse me is this: do we not have the right to eat and drink? Do we not have the right to take a believing wife with us, as do the other apostles and the Lord's brothers and Cephas? Or is it only I and Barnabas who have no right not to work? (1 Cor 9:1-6).

From this text, it is not entirely clear whether having seen Jesus is sufficient to be an apostle. Nor can one say with certainty whether Paul regarded Barnabas as an apostle like himself, although it would seem so. It is also interesting to note that some did not recognise Paul as an apostle.

Further on in the same letter, we find further information about who the apostles were. This is the list of witnesses to the Resurrection in chapter 15, where 'all the apostles' refers to a wider group than the 'Twelve':

> For I passed on to you, first of all, what I myself received: that Christ died for our sins in accordance with the Scriptures, that he was buried, and that he was raised on the third day in accordance with the Scriptures, and that he appeared to Cephas and then to the Twelve[^356].

> After that he appeared to more than five hundred brothers at the same time: most of them are still alive, whilst some have died. He also appeared to James, and then to all the apostles. Last of all, he appeared to me too, as to one born out of due time. For I am the least of the apostles and am not worthy to be called an apostle, because I persecuted the Church of God (1 Cor 15:3-9).

It is clear from this passage that Saint Paul does not equate 'the apostles' with 'the Twelve'. As Benedict XVI explains:

> We normally, following the Gospels, identify the Twelve with the title of apostles, thereby referring to those who were companions in life and listeners to Jesus' teaching. But Paul, too, considers himself a true apostle, and it is therefore clear that the Pauline concept of the apostolate is not restricted to the group of the Twelve. (…) He had a concept of the apostolate that went beyond that linked solely to the group of the Twelve and handed down above all by Saint Luke in the Acts of the Apostles (cf. Acts 1:2, 26; 6:2). Indeed, in his First Letter to the Corinthians, Paul makes a clear distinction between 'the Twelve' and 'all the apostles', mentioned as two distinct groups who benefited from the appearances of the Risen Lord (cf. 15:5, y). (Benedict XVI, General Audience, 1 September 2008).

If we consider that 'apostle' means a witness to an apparition of the Risen Jesus, then even the more than five hundred brothers would be apostles (cf. 1 Cor 15:6). But the text is not unequivocal on this point. In any case, it is clear that there are more than twelve apostles. Perhaps it is in this sense that the term is applied to Andronicus and Junia in Rom 16:7:

> Greet Andronicus and Junia, my kinsmen and fellow prisoners: they are distinguished among the apostles [ἐπίσημοι ἐν τοῖς ἀποστόλοις] and were in Christ even before me.

Since Junia is undoubtedly a woman's name, it is clear that, at least in a broad sense, 'apostle' was not a title reserved for men. It should be noted that Andronicus and Junia are described as 'apostles', but not as 'apostles of Jesus Christ', and perhaps the title should be understood in a broad sense, as 'missionaries'.

Furthermore, some have called Mary Magdalene 'apostle of the apostles' (apo-stolorum apostola): indeed, Mary saw the Lord, who sent her to announce the resurrection to the apostles (cf. Jn 20:1118[^357]) 1. The expression has been added recently in the Roman liturgy, as the title of the new preface prepared for the saint's feast day on 22 July. However, some invoke this tradition to demand the sa ordination of women to the priesthood, a proposal which has not been accepted by the Church[^358].

### 27.4. Towards a synthesis

The passage from 1 Corinthians 15 quoted above establishes, on the one hand, that for Paul 'the Twelve' cannot be identified with 'the apostles', but, on the other hand, it does not say that having seen the risen Jesus is sufficient to be an apostle. Benedict XVI explains the Pauline concept of the apostolate by stating that, to be apostles, three requirements must be fulfilled:

> What, then, according to St Paul's conception, is it that makes him and others apostles? In his Letters, three main characteristics emerge which define the apostle. The first is to have 'seen the Lord' (cf. 1 Cor 9:1), that is, to have had an encounter with him that was decisive for one's life. Similarly, in the Letter to the Galatians (cf. 1:15-16), he states that he was called-almost as if chosen-by the grace of God through the revelation of his Son, with a view to bringing the Good News to the Gentiles. Ultimately, it is the Lord who establishes one in the apostolate, not one's own presumption. (…) The second characteristic is that of 'having been sent'. The Greek term apóstolos itself means precisely 'sent, commissioned', that is, an ambassador and bearer of a message; he must therefore act as the agent and representative of the one who sent him. This is why Paul describes himself as 'an apostle of Jesus Christ' (1 Cor 1:1; 2 Cor 1:1), that is, his delegate, wholly at his service, so much so that he also calls himself 'a servant of Jesus Christ' (Rom 1:1). (…) The third requirement is the 'proclamation of the Gospel', leading to the establishment of Churches (Benedict XVI, General Audience, 1 September 2008).

Following the path indicated by the Pontiff, we can say that it was not only those who had seen the Lord - such as the five hundred brothers - who were called apostles, but also those who had received a 'mission' or 'commission' from Jesus (ἀποστολή, cf. Acts 1:25; Rom 1:5; 1 Cor 9:2; Gal 2:8), a mission consisting in the proclamation of the Gospel. For the Eleven, this sending took place at the time of the Ascension; for Paul, on the road to Damascus. In the Acts of the Apostles, preaching appears to be the principal task of the Twelve: this is why seven deacons were appointed for other services (Acts 6:1[^359]).

To complete the picture, it must be said that, when the Church speaks of the faith of the apostles, it seems to refer to a hierarchical group exercising authority. In the Acts of the Apostles and the rest of the New Testament, various ministries are mentioned: teachers, prophets, doctors, etc. It is often unclear what precise functions they had, but it is undeniable from the New Testament writings that a hierarchy existed within the early Christian community, as evidenced, for example, by the role of Peter, the Twelve themselves and Paul; and as suggested by the mention of presbyters or bishops in Christian communities outside Jerusalem. Within this hierarchy, Peter and the Twelve occupy the foremost position: this is particularly evident in the Acts of the Apostles, where it is recounted how various practical or doctrinal issues were brought before them. Their decisions were not merely advisory, but binding.

Paul also uses the title of 'apostle' to denote an office implying the authority to govern (cf. Gal, 2 Cor 11-12). And in the Pauline epistles, the apostolic office is mentioned as a ministry to which the foremost position corresponds. In 1 Cor 12:28 (and cf. Eph 4:11) it is stated:

> Therefore God has appointed some in the Church, first of all as apostles, secondly as prophets, thirdly as teachers; then there are those who work miracles, then the gift of healing, of helping, of governing, and of speaking in various tongues.

In addition to authority, we may point to another feature, implicit in the texts, which is fundamental for distinguishing true apostles from false ones. This concerns their communion with one another and, more specifically, with the Twelve and with Peter. In this regard, the most significant passage is found at the beginning of the Letter to the Galatians, where Paul, having defended the divine origin of his own calling to the apostolate (cf. Gal 1:1, 11-19), takes care to clarify that his gospel is in accordance with that of those who were apostles before him (cf. Gal 2:1-10).

All of them received a truly apostolic mission. This applies primarily to the Twelve, through whom the early Church appears as a well-structured organisation (Acts 1:1, 21-26; 2:14; 5:9). By contrast, having been called directly by the risen Christ without having known him during his earthly life, Paul is an apostle by virtue of this calling (Gal 1:2, 12; Rom 1:4ff.) and a minister of the Gospel on a par with the Twelve'. My translation; original text in French: '(…) men called by Jesus to the apostolate and appointed by him as witnesses to his resurrection (Acts 1:8, 21-22). Immediate contact with the risen Christ is therefore essential for him. But even amongst those who benefited from this (quite a number, according to 1 Cor 15:6), and who could therefore recount Jesus' deeds and words (…), not all received a strictly apostolic mission. This is essentially the preserve of the Twelve, through whom the early Church appears as a solidly structured body (Acts 1:13,21-26; 2:14; 5:9). On the other hand, having been called directly by the risen Christ without having known him during his earthly life, Paul is in this respect an apostle (Gal 1:2, 12; Rom 1:4ff.) and a minister of the Gospel on a par with the Twelve', Ibid., The Bible, Word of God, 15.

### 27.5. Other characteristics of the apostles

So far we have seen the characteristics that define an apostle: sent by Jesus as witnesses of the Risen One to proclaim the Gospel, endowed with authority and in communion with one another. We can now add other characteristics that are less essential, in that they derive from the previous ones, but which are more relevant for understanding the role of the apostles in the transmission of the Word of God. The apostles appear as irreplaceable, guarantors of authenticity, recipients of revelation, and the foundation of the Church.

One very clear point is that the apostles cannot have successors in the strict sense. The successors of the apostles are never called apostles. Speaking of the apostles or of the apostolic generation implies a temporal limitation: see what was said in the first part regarding DV4 and the closure of revelation.

Being an apostle, as we have just seen, implies having received from Jesus the authority to preach the Gospel and govern the community. This gives rise to the use of the adjective 'apostolic' in the sense of 'legitimate', or rather 'authentic'. When some accuse Paul, attempting to strip him of his authority, he defends himself - against the 'pseudo-apostles' or 'super-apostles' (cf. 2 Cor 11) - by stating that he is truly an apostle and that therefore his gospel is authentic, that is, it proceeds from Jesus Christ.

In the Epistle of Jude, there is a reference to the apostles which clearly reflects the fact that authenticity and normative authority were very soon associated with apostolicity:

> But you, dearest friends, remember the things foretold by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ. They told you: 'In the last days there will be false teachers, who will act according to their own ungodly desires' (Jude 17-18).

Jude appeals to the words of the Lord's apostles as an authority recognised by all (cf. also 2 Peter 3:2). The more time passes and the apostolic generation begins to fade away, the more frequent these appeals to recall their teachings will become. A prime example is that of Saint Irenaeus of Lyon, through whom the idea of apostolic succession as a guarantee of fidelity to Christ takes shape.

Why was such great and unique authority attributed to the apostles? Thanks to their contact with Christ, they were the direct recipients of his revelation, during his earthly life and especially in the period from his death to his ascension into heaven. Indeed, the mystery of Christ:

> was not made known to people of previous generations as it has now been revealed to his holy apostles and prophets through the Spirit (Eph 3:5) 1.

As regards revelation and its transmission, there is no access to Jesus Christ except through the apostles. For this reason, Saint Paul affirms that the Church is founded upon them, who in turn are founded upon Jesus:

> So then, you are no longer strangers or guests, but fellow citizens with the saints and members of God's household, built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets, with Christ Jesus himself as the cornerstone. In him the whole structure is joined together and grows into a holy temple in the Lord; in him you too are being built together to become a dwelling place for God through the Spirit (Eph 2:19-22).

This architectural metaphor is found in the Book of Revelation (and, with reference to Peter, also in Mt 16:18). In his description of Jerusalem coming down from heaven, John says:

> 'The walls of the city rest on twelve foundations, and on them are the twelve names of the twelve apostles of the Lamb' (Rev 21:14).

## 28. The apostolicity of the books of the New Testament

In Dei Verbum - and in magisterial documents in general - the term 'apostle' is used to refer to certain men who were witnesses to the life of Jesus, saw the risen Christ and received from him the commission or mission to preach the Gospel, thereby assuming the highest hierarchical office in the early Christian community. According to this description, the apostles par excellence are the Twelve chosen by Jesus himself, excluding Judas. Paul and Matthias hold the title of apostle in a somewhat 'exceptional' manner, since the former did not know Jesus during his earthly life, whilst the latter was not chosen directly by him[^361]. Perhaps Barnabas also falls into this category of exceptions, but we do not have sufficient evidence to decide: indeed, in the case of Acts 14:4, 14, the term may be used in its etymological-Semitic sense.

On the basis of the review of New Testament texts just given, it is clear that some were indeed called apostles in a broader sense, which is difficult to define precisely. They probably did not enjoy the same authority as the Twelve or as Paul, an authority which, amongst other things, included the power to lay hands on others to appoint successors.

For the purposes of our discussion, the most important thing is to understand in what sense it is said that Scripture is apostolic. Well, 'apostolicity' indicates a relationship with the apostles, but we must specify what kind of relationship. The Bible is apostolic, fundamentally, because it faithfully contains - directly, in the case of the New Testament; indirectly, in that of the Old Testament - the apostolic preaching, and as such it has been received by the Church.

The books of the New Testament are not, therefore, called 'apostolic' because it is thought that they were written directly and in their entirety by the apostles, in the sense of the Twelve and Paul. If no distinction is made between these two meanings of the adjective 'apostolic', one might think that if the writer is not an apostle, the book is not apostolic and therefore not canonical. Fortunately, from the very earliest times, the cases of Mark and Luke have made it impossible for such a simplification to become a theological criterion. Indeed, as we have seen, the DVy refers to 'apostolic men' to avoid such an identification.

Furthermore, as is well known, there are many books from the early days of the Church which present themselves as having been written by an apostle: the Gospel of Thomas, the Gospel of James, the Acts of Andrew, the Apocalypse of Peter, etc. The Church did not include these books in the canon, because it was able to discern that their content did not faithfully correspond to the deposit of apostolic preaching, which it safeguards. We have dealt with this subject in the section on the biblical canon, because one of the decisive criteria for accepting or rejecting a book is precisely apostolicity, correctly understood (cf. pp. 164-168).

In any case, the almost systematic use of the apostles' names in extra-canonical literature is a clear indication of how apostolicity was regarded as synonymous with authenticity: those who wished to spread a doctrine did so by hiding behind the name of an apostle.

# Exc ursus!: From the composition of the books to their regard as the word of God

The economy of salvation foretold, narrated and explained by the sacred authors is found as the true word of God in the books of the Old Testament.

Dei Verbum, no. 14

In what follows, we shall ask whether the Bible, or any of the books that make it up, presents itself directly as the word of God or rather as a testimony to an earlier divine word; whether there appears to be a gap between historical revelation and its recording in writing; and what value the books held for the people of Israel.

It would be naive to attempt to reconstruct every stage of the revelation and the recording of the biblical books. The available information is too scant. However, certain clues can be found in the Bible itself, which should be taken into account to avoid an anachronistic view. The Bible, in fact, did not exist from the very beginning as a collection of writings containing the word of God, even though revelation had already taken place in history. Rather than seeking historical data, the aim of the following pages is to highlight the ideas concerning revelation and its recording-whether implicit or explicit-in the texts we possess.

Along the way, we shall need to gather information about the process of composing the texts who wrote them, when, whether by God's command or on their own initiative, whether there was any divine intervention in their drafting, and so on. But we shall be even more interested in information concerning the reception of the texts, that is, the authority attributed to them, by whom and why. To put it another way: we shall conduct an investigation seeking clues regarding the creation and reception of the biblical books, in order to establish their relationship with revelation or the word of God. The aim is to see how certain writings, whilst being inspired from the outset, came to be regarded as sacred books by ancient Israel (and subsequently as the word of God by the Church).

Finally, let it be clear that in what follows we shall not analyse all the biblical data on the Word of God in general, but only those that relate to its written form. The Bible almost never speaks of 'revelation' in the sense that this word is used in fundamental theology; however, it does speak-and at length-of the 'Word of God', of how God reveals himself to humankind and establishes contact with them.

In the Old Testament, only two types of divine messages that are written down are mentioned: the word of the Torah and the prophetic word. In the New Testament, however, the concept of the word of God () is applied to all the Scriptures in a comprehensive sense (as we have seen on pp. 9-103).

## 29. The Word of the Torah

All the people heard the thunder and the lightning, the sound of the trumpet and saw the mountain shrouded in smoke. The people saw this, were seized with fear and kept their distance. Then they said to Moses: 'Speak to us yourself, and we will listen; but let not God speak to us, lest we die!' (Ex 20:18-19).

For modern Jews, the Torah lies at the heart of their Scriptures. We Christians place the Pentateuch not at the centre, but at the beginning, because it recounts the early stages of the history of salvation. For both groups, it is a sacred text; we must now ask ourselves in what sense, and whether it has always been regarded as such.

We cannot go into detail here regarding the composition of the Pentateuch, that is, the process by which oral traditions and written documents were brought together to form these five books as we know them today. As a starting point, it suffices to say that it is now thought that the written Torah attained its arrangement into five books and, broadly speaking, its current textual form during the post-exilic era, in the Persian period (5th-4th centuries BC[^362]) 1.

In what follows, we shall confine ourselves to gathering the clues provided by the text itself regarding its composition and reception.

### 29.1. References to the written Torah in the Pentateuch

In the Bible, the first figure said to have written down the words God communicated to him is Moses. Neither Genesis nor the rest of the Bible mentions any writing activity during the time of the patriarchs. Neither Abraham, nor Isaac, nor Jacob, nor his twelve sons wrote anything that is mentioned in the canonical Scriptures[^363].

#### 29.1.1. The Account of the Exodus

The first references to a divine message being put into writing appear in connection with the theophany at Sinai and the bestowal of the Torah, within the context of the covenant. This is a moment of transcendental importance in the history of salvation. The people of Israel become God's own possession, a kingdom of priests and a holy nation through a covenant which, through the mediation of Moses, the Lord proposes to them after having freed them from slavery (cf. Ex 19:1-8).

The second part of the Book of Exodus (chapters 19 to 40) describes how the covenant is established. The story will be somewhat troubled, because the people's 'original sin'-the worship of the golden calf-threatens to bring the whole thing to ruin. But we are concerned only with recalling the references to the writing down of God's words.

The first time Moses ascends Mount Sinai, God communicates various laws to him, including the Ten Commandments and a long series of precepts, the so-called 'code of the covenant' (Ex 20-21). Moses descends from the mountain and conveys these words to the people. In Exodus 24, the transition from the spoken divine word to the written word appears for the first time. The Torah is written down so that it may endure, and is immediately read out in public:

> Moses went to tell the people all the words of the Lord and all the statutes. All the people answered with one voice, saying, 'All the commandments that the Lord has given, we will carry out!' Moses wrote down all the words of the Lord. He rose early the next morning and erected an altar at the foot of the mountain, with twelve pillars for the twelve tribes of Israel. He appointed some young men from among the Israelites to offer burnt offerings and to sacrifice young bulls as peace offerings to the Lord. Moses took half the blood and placed it in several basins, and poured the other half onto the altar. Then he took the Book of the Covenant and read it out in the presence of the people. They said, 'As the Lord has said-

> 'Lord, we will do it and we will listen to you.' Moses took the blood and sprinkled it on the people, saying, 'This is the blood of the covenant that the Lord has made with you on the basis of all these words!' (Ex 24:3-8).

It is worth noting that Moses first recounts to the people, orally, what the Lord has said, and then writes it down and reads it out. The response is the same in both cases.

This is followed by a similar account, with Moses' second ascent of Mount Sinai, accompanied by Aaron, Nadab, Abihu and seventy elders of Israel. The Lord gives further laws (Ex 25-11), but this time there is an important new development: after the oral communication of the laws, mention is made of the handing over of a text written by God himself!

> When the Lord had finished speaking to Moses on Mount Sinai, he gave him the two tablets of the Testimony, tablets of stone, inscribed by the finger of God (Ex 31:18; cf. 24:12).

So far we have two texts: the Book of the Covenant, written by Moses, and the stone tablets, inscribed by God. If we were to continue reading the account of the Exodus, we would see that the distinction between the two is not very clear. The Book of the Covenant is not mentioned again. The stone tablets will have a very short life: Moses destroys them when he comes down and discovers that the people have sinned with the golden calf (cf. Ex 32:15-19). This is followed by forgiveness and a new theophany - God shows Moses not his face, but his back (cf. Ex 33:23) - with the handing over of new stone tablets bearing the words of God. Who writes them? The Lord promises that he will do so himself (Ex 34:1), but it is then stated that Moses did so (cf. Ex 34:28, where the subject is omitted, but from the context it seems to refer to Moses).

The reader may be somewhat confused by the repetitions and apparent inconsistencies in the narrative. We cannot undertake an analysis of the Book of Exodus here, as it brings together various traditions. However, the underlying coherence behind the variations should be noted: in all cases, the content of the texts consists of the words that God, through Moses, addresses to his people as the requirements of the covenant. The origin of the words is always oral and divine. That is to say, God first utters them orally before Moses and they are then set down in writing. Therefore, it does not matter whether it is God or Moses who utters or writes the commandments: in every case, they are the word of God.

In this sense, it is illuminating to compare two parallel passages from the Gospels, in which Jesus quotes a precept from the Decalogue (cf. Ex 20:12 and Deut 5:16). The commandment is regarded as the word of God, but in one instance it is attributed to Moses, and in the other to God:

> Moses said: 'Honour your father and your mother ', and: 'Whoever curses their father or mother shall be put to death.' But you say: 'If anyone tells their father or mother: 'Whatever I might otherwise have given you is korbàn, that is, an offering to God', you do not allow them to do anything more for their father or mother.

> In this way you nullify the word of God by the tradition you have handed down. And you do many things like this (Mark 7:10-13).

> God said: 'Honour your father and your mother'; and again: 'Whoever curses their father or mother shall be put to death.' But you say: 'Whoever tells their father or to his mother, 'Whatever I might otherwise have used to help you is an offering to God', is no longer obliged to honour his father.'

In this way you have nullified the word of God by your tradition (Matthew 15:4-6).

The identity of the writer is irrelevant: whether it be Moses or God, the texts always contain God's words which are normative for the people by virtue of the covenant. The covenant presupposes a prior historical revelation, involving deeds and words. The Torah of Moses does not present itself as a book that fell from heaven, as a word of God written independently of history, a community and a tradition.

#### 29.1.2. References to the written Torah in Deuteronomy

In Deuteronomy, the final book of the Pentateuch, there are many references to the Torah being set down in writing. Unlike what we saw in Exodus, Deuteronomy draws a clearer distinction between what God wrote - the Ten Commandments on the two tablets (cf. Deut 4:13; 5:22; 9:10; 10:2-4) and what Moses wrote, namely the rest of the Torah.

At the end of Deuteronomy, it is stated with particular solemnity that Moses wrote 'this law', which seems to allude to the Book of Deuteronomy itself:

> When Moses had finished writing down all the words of this law in a book, he commanded the Levites who carried the Ark of the Covenant of the Lord: 'Take this Book of the Law and place it beside the Ark of the Covenant of the Lord your God. Let it remain there as a witness against you' (Deut. 31:24-26; cf. also 11:9-19).

The identification of the Law with 'this book' appears frequently in Deuteronomy: cf. Deut 28:58, 61; 29:19-21; 31:10. It should be noted that the book is to be placed beside, and not inside, the ark: 'the book is therefore, for the history yet to come, and regardless of the future tribulations of the ark (which will in fact disappear), the public aspect, in the form of a witness, of divine revelation[^364] ' (1).

It is not only Moses, however, who has the privilege of being able to write the Book of the Torah. In Deut. 27,2-3, he commands that all the words of the law be inscribed on stone when the people enter the Promised Land, a task which will be carried out by Joshua (cf. p. 288). In Deut. 1:18-20, Moses stipulates that the king must have a personal copy of the Law. And finally, in the famous text of the Shema, every Israelite is commanded to write the Lord's precepts 'on the doorposts of your house and on your gates' (cf. Deut. 6:9).

The important point is that these ways of speaking, which are more or less metaphorical - such as the allusion to the finger of God - convey a clear and distinct idea: all the laws of the covenant are the words of the Lord, regardless of who put them in writing.

There is also a second idea implicit in the first: the Torah does not lose its original authority when it is put into writing. Disobeying the written law (cf. Josh 10:1-26) is equivalent to disobeying a word of the Lord (cf. 1 Sam 15:1-10).

Furthermore, once written down, the Torah becomes enduring, so that it can be at d by every generation, just like the covenant (cf. Deut 5:2-11).

#### 29.1.3. The Book of the Torah and the Pentateuch

So far, we have considered the main references in the Pentateuch to the recording in writing of certain words of God, specifically His precepts. However, there is no mention of any identification between the written Law and the five books which are today called the Torah or the Pentateuch. The only book that seems to speak of itself is Deuteronomy. And what of the other four-are they the word of God? What, then, of the narrative texts, which contain no rules or precepts, such as the entire Book of Genesis and many passages in Exodus, Leviticus and Numbers?

Neither in Leviticus nor in the Book of Numbers are there any references to the written recording of the Lord's words. But in Numbers 20:2 there appears a divine command to write which is worth quoting:

> Moses wrote down their points of departure, stage by stage, by the Lord's command; these are their stages in the order of their points of departure. They set out from Rameses in the first month (…)

The mention of Rameses as the point of departure recalls the beginning of the Book of Exodus. The rest of the chapter describes the people's journey through the desert. In this instance, God commands that not laws be written down, but an account of the journey; that is to say, he wishes the story to be remembered in which he manifested his mercy and established the covenant. The idea of remembrance as a motive for writing appears explicitly in a similar passage. After the victory over Amalek, 'the Lord said to Moses: 'Write this down in the book as a memorial'' (Ex 15:14).

God desires not only that the legal norms be written down, but also the account of historical events. In the Pentateuch we find laws set within a narrative framework, which is necessary to understand what they are and why they must be observed (cf. for example the famous creed in Deut 26:1-11). Indeed, the entire book of Genesis and the first half of Exodus can be understood as the historical prologue to the laws of the covenant.

However, the fact that the accounts were written down at God's command is not sufficient to regard them as the word of God. The Pentateuch does not claim to be the word of God. One must look beyond it to see that, in fact, for the Jews, many centuries before Christ, the Book of the Torah of Moses was regarded in its entirety as the word of God.

### 29.2. Evidence outside the Pentateuch

Outside the Pentateuch, we find various testimonies to the sacred value attributed to the written Torah.

#### 29.2.1. In the Book of Joshua

As the Lord had foretold, Moses died without crossing the Jordan, that is, without entering the Promised Land. But his book remains: whilst no one knows where Moses' tomb is (cf. Deut. 14:6), his legacy, on the other hand, enters the land in the form of a book: 'Moses does not cross the Jordan, but he does not die without crossing another threshold, that which leads from oral to written communication[^365]' 1.

At the beginning of the Book of Joshua - that is, immediately after Moses' death - the Lord encourages Joshua, son of Nun, to fulfil his mission as successor. He does not set out a military strategy for the conquest, but prescribes constant meditation on the Torah:

> You, therefore, be strong and very courageous, so that you may observe and put into practice all the law which Moses, my servant, has prescribed for you. Do not turn from it to the right or to the left, and so you will succeed in all your undertakings. Let not this Book of the Law depart from your mouth, but meditate on it day and night, so that you may observe and put into practice all that is written in it; thus you will make your way prosperous and succeed (Joshua 1:7-8) 1.

'Meditating'-or more precisely, 'muttering' or 'whispering'-the Torah day and night forms part of the description of the godly man with which the Book of Psalms begins (cf. Ps 1:2). In this way, both the Prophets and the Writings, according to the tripartite division of the BH, begin with a reference to the Torah. At the end of his life, Joshua will give the people the same advice: 'Be strong to observe and to put into practice what is written in the Book of the Law of Moses, without turning from it to the right or to the left' (Joshua 23:6).

After crossing the Jordan and destroying Jericho, the Israelites captured a town called Ai. At this point, Joshua put into practice what Moses had commanded in Deuteronomy 27:

> Joshua built an altar to the Lord, the God of Israel, on Mount Ebal (…). There Joshua inscribed on the ston s and a copy of the Law of Moses, which Moses had written in the presence of the Israelites. (…) Joshua then read out all the words of the Law, the blessings and the curses, as they are written in the Book of the Law. Of all that Moses had commanded, there was not a single word that Joshua did not read out before the whole assembly of Israel (Joshua 8:30-35).

This account alludes to the Book of Deuteronomy, in which Moses' commandment, as well as the blessings and curses, are found. At the same time, however, the fact that 'a copy of Moses' Torah' could be inscribed on stones suggests that it must have been a rather short text.

Furthermore, in the Book of Joshua we find valuable evidence that the Book of the Torah was continually enriched with new laws. In the account of the solemn ceremony of the renewal of the covenant (Joshua 24), it is clearly stated:

> On that day Joshua made a covenant with the people and gave them statutes and laws at Shechem. He wrote these words in the Book of the Law of God (Joshua 24:25-26).

Thus, the Bible itself bears witness that, after Moses' death, material continued to be added to the Torah in order to adapt its content to new historical circumstances (cf. also 1 Sam 10:25). It should be noted that in doing so, Joshua appears to be transgressing Moses' explicit command not to add anything to the Torah (cf. Deut 4:2 and 11:1), if taken literally. In reality, Moses himself first writes down the law (Deut

11:9) and then finishes writing it (Deut. 31:24), thus adding Deut. 31:14-23 1. If one accepts the existence of a progressive revelation to Israel (cf. above, pp. 61-69), it is not problematic to admit that the texts containing it also grow.

If, after reading Joshua, we were to continue with the subsequent books, we would have to gather all the references to the Book of the Torah (cf. for example 1 Kings 2:3; 2 Kings 14:6; 2 Chronicles 23:18;!o:16). Such an undertaking would require too much space. We shall therefore confine ourselves to pointing out that in these books, events and characters are always judged according to their fidelity to the covenant and to the Torah of Moses.

However, it is worth dwelling on two historically significant moments in which the role of the written Torah is explicitly evident. The first is the reform of King Josiah, which took place towards the end of the 7th century BC, in 622, some thirty years before the destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem (58 BC). The second moment corresponds to the work of re-establishment and reorganisation undertaken by Ezra and Nehe mia following the return from the Babylonian exile.

#### 29.2.2. The reform of Josiah (2 Kings 22-23)

Following the long reign of the wicked Manasseh (687-642 BC) and the much shorter reign of his son Amon (642-640 BC), one of the few successors of David whom the Bible unreservedly approves ascended the throne of Judah: Josiah:

> Before him there had been no king who, like him, had turned to the Lord with all his heart, with all his soul and with all his strength, in accordance with the whole Law of Moses; after him, no one like him arose[^368].

In the eighteenth year of his reign (that is, 622 BC), some restoration work was carried out in the Temple in Jerusalem. At that time, the high priest Hilkiah found the 'Book of the Torah' in the Temple. He handed it over to Shaphan, the king's secretary, who read it and then took it to Josiah and read it before him. 'When the king heard the words of the Book of the Law, he tore his clothes' (2 Kings 22:11), a sign of mourning and regret, 'because our fathers have not listened to the words of this book, nor put into practice what is written for us' (22:13).

After consulting the Lord through the prophetess Huldah, who foretold the punishment of Jerusalem because of its unfaithfulness (cf. vv. 14-20), the king summoned all the people to the temple and read the book to them. Then they renewed the covenant:

> The king, standing by the pillar, made a covenant before the Lord to follow the Lord and to keep his commands, decrees and laws with all his heart and with all his soul, and to carry out the words of the covenant written in that book. All the people joined in the covenant (2 Kings 23:3).

Immediately afterwards - the biblical account continues - the king undertook a wide-ranging religious reform. Josiah took measures against idolatry, destroyed all places of worship except the Temple in Jerusalem (centralisation of worship) and ordered the celebration of the Passover:

> The king commanded all the people: 'Celebrate the Passover in honour of the Lord your God, as it is written in the Book of this Covenant.' Indeed, a Passover like this had never been celebrated since the time of the judges who ruled over Israel, that is, throughout the entire period of the kings of Israel and the kings of Judah. It was not until the eighteenth year of King Josiah's reign that this Passover was celebrated in honour of the Lord in Jerusalem (2 Kings 23:21-23).

Josiah drew inspiration from the precepts of the newly rediscovered book, which, judging by the details emerging from the account in 2 Kings, corresponds in content to Deuteronomy, probably in a shorter version than the one we have today. Be that as it may, the authority attributed to the book's content is striking. It is implicit in the account of Josiah's reform that the Book of the Torah is regarded as an expression of God's will, as his word, binding upon Israel by virtue of the covenant.

#### 29.2.3. Ezra and the Proclamation of the Torah

Following the difficult years of exile in Babylon, the identity of the people needed to be reestablished. They had returned to the Promised Land and once again possessed a temple, a priesthood and a system of worship, but they no longer had a Davidic king nor did they enjoy political independence.

During the Persian period (perhaps around the mid-5th century BC), two figures helped to shape the new form of Judaism: the scribe Ezra and the governor Nehemiah. To understand their efforts to apply the Torah and rebuild the walls of Jerusalem, one must read the Books of Ezra and Nehemiah. One passage in particular is significant here: Ezra's public reading of the Torah in Jerusalem, as described in Nehemiah 8.

Having gathered the whole people together in assembly, Ezra read a portion of the Book of the Torah of Moses, from daybreak until midday (cf. Neh 8:1-18). He continued to read for the following seven days. As in the days of Josiah, the reading had immediate effects: on the second day they realised that at that time of year they were due to celebrate the Feast of Tabernacles, and they immediately set to work to observe, down to the last detail, everything laid down in the Torah (cf. Neh 8:14-17) 1.

An interesting insight comes from the time taken for the reading: the book rediscovered in Josiah's time was read several times on the same day; with Ezra, however, it took several days to complete the reading. At least in terms of length, the book of the Torah read by Ezra may have been very similar to the present-day Pentateuch[^369].

So far, we have briefly seen how the Book of the Torah of Moses was regarded up until the 5th4th centuries BC. We can say that the written law was accorded the same value as the word of God originally revealed to Moses, although over time the text underwent a continuous process of enrichment and updating[^370].

However, we must ask whether, of all the words contained in this book, only the legal texts were regarded as the word of God, or whether this regard extended to

- Other passages in the books of Ezra and Nehemiah contain similar accounts, which show how the reading of the Torah has practical consequences for the life of the community (cf. Ezra 1:2-4; 6:18; Neh 9:1; 10:14-16; 11:1). On the importance of the public reading of the law for understanding the rhetorical structure of the Pentateuch, see J. W. Watts, 'Public Readings and Pentateuchal Law', Vetus Testamentum 4 (1995) 540-551.

reference to the entire book, including the editorial passages and narrative texts that frame the word of the law. From what we have seen, there is insufficient evidence to answer in the affirmative. Certainly, for Christians, the entire Pentateuch is pa ola of God, but we must consider whether this was also the case before Christ.

#### 29.2.4. The significance of the Book of the Torah in the Second Temple period

Many texts attest that the term 'Torah' was applied not only to the laws of the covenant, but also to the book (or books) containing them (cf. 1 Maccabees 1:48; Sirach Prologue 2:8, 24; Matthew 6:1ff; 12; etc.). In general, it may be noted that the five books of the Torah were regarded as authoritative not only because they contain divine laws, but also as a whole-including the narrative passageswhich show how God acts and how man ought to respond.

The book as such becomes a point of reference for conduct. In 1 Maccabees 1:48 (cf. also 2 Maccabees 8:23), it is recounted that, before the battle, Judas and his soldiers:

> fasted and put on sackcloth, sprinkled ashes on their heads and tore their garments. They opened the Book of the Law to discover therein what the pagans sought to learn from the idols of their gods.

Judas Maccabeus believed that in the book of the Torah he would find divine guidance to steer him through the impending battle: a religious attitude perhaps bordering on superstition, but one which reveals the sacred regard in which he held the book.

Far more profound is the assessment of the Pentateuch found in Sirach (or Ecclesiasticus), a wisdom book written around 180 BC. In chapter 24, divine wisdom praises itself, inspired by the speech of personified wisdom in Proverbs 8. In Sir 24, Wisdom says that she came forth from the mouth of the Most High and that she travels throughout the earth, seeking a place to settle, until God commands her to pitch her tent in Israel. Then Wisdom takes up residence in the holy land, worships in Jerusalem, spreads throughout Israel and bears much fruit. At the end of Wisdom's speech, the author of the book takes the floor, explaining:

> All this is the book of the covenant of the Most High God, the law which Moses prescribed for us, an inheritance for the assemblies of Jacob (Sir 24:2; cf. Bar 4:1).

The eternal wisdom of God, through which the heavens were created, has taken root within the boundaries of the chosen people and has been enshrined in a book, which is none other than the Pentateuch. This is not an absolute identification of divine wisdom with the Mosaic Law. Rather, Sir 24:23 seeks to highlight how God's wisdom is manifested in a special way in the book of the Law

. The written Torah, understood as historical revelation and not merely as a body of legal precepts, is regarded as a participation in divine wisdom[^371].

*

In Jesus' time, the central importance of the Torah was a conviction shared by the various movements of the period (Pharisees, Sadducees, Essenes, apocalyptic groups), even though there were significant differences between them in other areas:

> For all the currents of Judaism (…), the Law occupied a central place. For it is in the Law that we find the essential institutions revealed by God himself, intended to govern the religious, moral, legal and political life of the Jewish nation after the exile[^372].

## 30. The word 'prophet'

When we hear the term 'prophet', we usually think of someone who foretells the future. In the Bible, the word has a broader meaning, which we can summarise by saying that to prophesy means to speak in the name of God, to convey a divine oracle to the people. In the Old Testament, the prophetic word is explicitly presented as the word of God, usually preceded by the so-called messenger's formula: 'Thus says the Lord'.

The prophetic word, therefore, is not always a prophecy in the modern sense of the term, equivalent to a prediction. The content of the words of the biblical prophets concerns both the pastspecifically the Covenant and the Torah-and the future, the 'Day of the Lord'. It is precisely by looking in these two directions that the Book of Malachi, the last of the prophetic books, concludes (cf. Mal 1:22-24).

Although the divine nature of its origin and content is made very clear, the word of God spoken by the prophet differs significantly from that of the Torah: by its very nature, it is not intended to endure in written form. Normally, the prophet conveys a divine word orally in order to change people's behaviour in specific circumstances of place and time. Indeed, we may assume that a large number of prophetic oracles were never put down in writing. Not all prophets wrote books[^373].

Some prophetic oracles foretold punishments or promised salvation and thus referred to the future. There was a felt need to set them down in writing, so that the word might remain in anticipation of its fulfilment. It is logical, in such cases, to wish to record it; indeed, sometimes it is God himself who commands the prophet to write down the oracle. As an emblematic example, we may recall what is recounted in the Book of Jeremiah[^374].

Towards the end of his prophetic ministry in Jerusalem, shortly before the destruction and the exile, God asked Jeremiah to write down everything He had told him:

> 'Take a scroll and write on it all the words I have spoken to you concerning Jerusalem, Judah and all the nations, from the time of Josiah until today. Perhaps the people of the house of Judah, on hearing all the evil I intend to bring upon them, will turn from their wicked ways; and then I will forgive their iniquities and their sins' (Jer 36:1-3-!).

The oracles that Jeremiah had proclaimed orally must now be set down in writing. The circumstance giving rise to the book is that Jeremiah is prevented from going to the Temple (cf. Jer 36:5) and is therefore unable to preach orally. But there is a deeper, salvific reason: God continues to hope that his words will produce the desired effect-the conversion of the people and thus forgiveness. Since the situation has not changed, the oracles of previous years have not lost their relevance[^375].

Jeremiah dictates the words of the Lord to Baruch, who writes them on a scroll and reads it out before the people in the Temple (Jer 36:4-8; cf. 10:1-2; 51:59-64). Baruch is then taken to the royal palace, where he reads the scroll again, this time before the leaders, who are terrified. The story continues as follows:

> Then they asked Baruch, 'Tell us how you came to write all these words.' Baruch replied, 'Jeremiah dictated all these words to me personally, and I wrote them down in the scroll with ink.' The leaders said to Baruch, 'Go and hide with Jeremiah; let no one know where you are.' They then went to the king in the inner chamber, having first placed the scroll in the room of Elishama the scribe, and reported all these words to the king.

> Then the king sent Jehudi to fetch the scroll. Jehudi took it from the room of Elishama the scribe and read it before the king and all the officials who were with the king. The king was sitting in the winter palace - it was the ninth month - with a brazier burning before him. Now, whenever Judah had read three or four columns, the king would tear them up with his scribe's penknife and throw them into the fire on the brazier, until the entire scroll had been destroyed in the fire of the brazier. Neither the king nor any of his officials trembled or tore their clothes on hearing all those words (Jer 16:1-24).

In contrast to his father Josiah, Jehoiakim did not react well to the reading. He did not recognise in it a normative expression of the divine will for the people. On the contrary, he sought to nullify the prophetic word by destroying the text that contained it. The story continues:

> After the king had burnt the scroll containing the words that Baruch had written under Jeremiah's dictation, the word of the Lord came to Jeremiah: 'Take another scroll and write on it all the words that were in the first scroll burnt by Jehoiakim, king of Judah. Say to Jehoiakim, king of Judah: Thus says the Lord: You have burnt that scroll, saying, 'Why have you written: 'The king of Babylon will come, lay waste to this land and cause both people and animals to perish?'' Therefore, thus says the Lord against Jehoiakim, king of Judah: He shall have no heir to the throne of David; his corpse shall be exposed to the heat of the day and the cold of the night. I will punish him, his descendants and his officials for their iniquities, and I will bring upon them, upon the inhabitants of Jerusalem and upon the men of Judah, all the evil I have threatened, because they have not listened to me.'

> Jeremiah took another scroll and gave it to Baruch, son of Neriah, the scribe, who wrote down, as Jeremiah dictated, all the words of the scroll that Jehoiakim, king of Judah, had burnt in the fire; moreover, many words similar to those were added to it (Jer 16:27-12).

God's harsh judgement on Jehoiakim shows that rejecting his written word is tantamount to rejecting the Lord directly. By tearing his clothes, Josiah had earned God's forgiveness, who promises him a burial in peace (cf. 2 Kings 22:19-20). The contrast with the oracle concerning Jehoiakim's corpse is striking[^376].

It is interesting to note that God still wishes the oracles to be set down in writing. The fulfilment of this command provides valuable insight into the origins of the present Book of Jeremiah (cf. Jer 25:13). But what concerns us is that the Bible shows how certain prophetic oracles can retain their significance beyond the moment they were spoken, and for this reason God himself wishes them to be written down.

Although we have explicit evidence of this divine will only in the case of the collection of Jeremiah's oracles, the very existence of prophetic books testifies that something similar must have happened with other prophets[^377].

The written oracle seems to enjoy the same divine authority that it had when it was spoken orally. For example, during the Babylonian exile, the prophet Daniel reads a proclamation by Jeremiah (cf. Jer 25:11-12; 29:10) and seeks to interpret it (cf. Dan 9). In Tobit 2:6, there is an explicit quotation from Amos (Amos 8:10), and Tobit 14 refers to the prophecies of Nahum and all the prophets.

In summary, the prophetic books demonstrate the conviction that the written oracle does not lose its value compared to the same oracle at the moment of its oral proclamation. The preservation of prophetic oracles in written form occurs primarily in anticipation of their fulfilment, but also so that they may serve as a point of reference for the conduct of the people. In any case, the original prophetic word is always oral; writing appears only in certain instances as a means of ensuring its permanence, and not at the moment of its origin, unlike the Torah.

Broadly speaking, the situation regarding the prophetic books appears similar to that of the Torah or Pentateuch. However, in their case, there are no grounds for regarding the books in their entirety-that is, including the narrative passages-as the word of God. These passages merely provide the context for the oracles.

## 31. The value of the other books of the Old Testament

So far we have discussed the Pentateuch and the prophetic books. What can be said about the historical books and the wisdom and poetic books? None of them presents itself, even in part, as the word of God, except of course for the passages where words are attributed to God- directly from the mouth of the Lord. The way in which the body of Israel's scriptures is assessed in the prologue to Sirach, written by Ben Sira's grandson, is significant:

> Many and important teachings are given to us by the Law, the Prophets and the other subsequent writings, for which Israel is to be praised for its doctrine and wisdom.

No expression similar to the title 'the word of God' is used. The Law, the Prophets and the other writings constitute Israel's paideia, its education, upon which its culture and therefore its identity depend. The prologue itself then states that the purpose of these writings is to help people live in accordance with the Mosaic Law; therefore, they are not on the same level, but regard the Torah as their centre:

> My grandfather Jesus, having devoted himself for a long time to reading the Law, the Prophets and the other books of our forefathers, and having attained considerable expertise in them, was himself moved to write something on matters of doctrine and wisdom, so that lovers of knowledge, having assimilated this too, might progress ever further in living in accordance with the Law.

Let us take another example, this time from the Book of Tobit. In 12:20, the angel Raphael asks Tobit and his son Tobiah to write down everything that has happened as a result of his intervention, which was the fruit of prayer. Yet the book in no way presents itself as the word of God. Instead, a significant part of its message consists in demonstrating the fruitfulness of obedience to the precepts of the Book of the Torah of Moses, particularly with regard to good works: giving alms and burying the dead. Incidentally, the book also speaks of the efficacy of the prophetic word.

We may conclude that in the Old Testament, only the words of the Torah and the prophetic oracles are regarded as the written word of God:

> The Old Testament explicitly grants the status of the word of God only to the prophetic oracle, at the moment of its utterance, and to the law, at the moment of its promulgation and in its noetic content as a revelation of the divine will. It does not attribute this divine status either to wisdom or to Scripture as such[^378].

A special case is that of the Book of Psalms. The psalms are always prayers; that is to say, by their very nature, they appear as human words addressed to God, and not as the word of God to humankind. However, in this case we have various testimonies indicating that, in Judaism, the Davidic psalms had been equated with the prophetic word. At Qumran, writings (the Pesharim) were found which comment on certain prophetic books- prophetic books, but also the Psalms, as prophetic writings. For his part, Philo of Alexandria (c. 2nd century BC-5th century AD) quotes the Psalms using phrases such as 'the prophet says' or similar expressions.

## 32. From sacred content to the sacredness of the book

So far, we have seen that the books of the Old Testament are presented more as written testimonies of the word of God than as revelation. Whilst in some cases they contain the word of God, this is always a word that was first spoken and then written down.

But we cannot conclude this discussion without alluding to a particularly strong trend in the centuries immediately preceding and following Jesus, in which the books present themselves directly as written divine revelation.

In a sense, this idea of written revelation stems from prophetic literature. The Book of Nahum presents itself as a written oracle. In Ezekiel, albeit metaphorically, there is mention of a book that is the word of the Lord (cf. Ezek 2:8-1:4). In the Book of Baruch, the written text possesses the same power as the divine word spoken orally: revelation takes place in writing, in that the book presents itself as a text originally written as such (cf. Bar 1:1-14). The distinction between the word coming from the Lord, spoken once, and the written text that reproduces it disappears.

This trend finds abundant examples in the so-called 'apocalyptic genre'. This is not the place to explain in detail the characteristics of this literary genre. Suffice it to say that apocalyptic works are books which present accounts of heavenly visions received by illustrious figures of the past, such as Adam, Enoch, Moses or Ezra. In the Old Testament, there is no book that belongs entirely to this genre, although some chapters of Daniel come very close to it. However, outside the canon there exists a vast body of Jewish literature of the apocalyptic genre.

We are interested in the idea of the sacredness of the book that is characteristic of this tradition. In the apocalyptic mindset, revelation takes written form, with no oral precedents. God speaks through a text, either because it was dictated by him - or by one of his angels - or because it is nothing other than an exact copy of the heavenly tablets, on which the destinies of mankind are inscribed. Although these texts are not regarded as inspired works in their entirety, nor are they said to have God as their author, the idea emerges that God communicates his plans through written texts[^379]

.

To find a well-known example of this idea, one must turn to the New Testament, which contains a canonical book of the apocalyptic genre, namely the Book of Revelation. The book presents itself as a written revelation - hence its name: Ἀποκάλυψις means 'revelation' -. The command to write appears more than once, addressed to the seer (cf. Rev

1:19; 14:13). From the outset, the author addresses the reader (who proclaims the text aloud) and those listening to the reading:

> The Revelation of Jesus Christ, which God gave him to show his servants the things that must shortly take place. And he made it known by sending it through his angel to his servant John, who bears witness to the word of God and to the testimony of Jesus Christ, and who records what he has seen. Blessed is the one who reads, and blessed are those who hear the words of this prophecy and keep what is written in it: for the time is near (Rev 1:1-3).

Finally, the book speaks of itself once more, with a warning regarding the need to respect the integrity of the text:

> To everyone who hears the words of the prophecy in this book, I declare: if anyone adds anything to them, God will inflict on them the plagues described in this book; and if anyone takes anything away from the words of this prophetic book, God will deprive them of the tree of life and the holy city, described in this book (Rev 22:18-19).

These prohibitions - neither to add nor to take away - were a standard formula applied to what was considered sacred. In Deuteronomy, it refers to the commandments (Deut 4:2; 11:1); in Ecclesiastes, to creation as God's work (Eccl 3:14); whilst in Revelation, it is applied to the book itself[^380]

.

Having reached this point, we may ask ourselves whether it is in this sense that we Christians regard the Bible as a sacred book. Is the Bible the word of God because it is revelation set down directly in writing? The answer to this question can be unequivocal: this is not the case. The Church's faith in Scripture as the word of God stems from Jesus' teaching on the matter. And, as we have seen, Jesus interprets the Scriptures of Israel primarily according to the prophetic model and not according to the apocalyptic one. Therefore, the books are regarded as the word of God because they proclaim Jesus Christ; in him the Scriptures have been fulfilled as law, as history and, above all, as promise (cf. pp. 88-103).

Even the Book of Revelation, whilst employing the apocalyptic literary model, presents its message within the framework common to the rest of the New Testament, namely: the authority of the visions is founded on Jesus Christ, glorious yet bearing the marks of his passion and death; the revelation is conveyed through an apostle; and it is addressed to the whole Church[^381].

Excursus s: From the writing of the books to their regard as the Word of God!oo

2003, 201-216, 216.
