[^5]: In the Greek version of the Old Testament, the singular 'Scripture' is sometimes used, but it does not refer to a collection of books: cf. 1 Chr 15:15; 2 Chr 10:5; Ezra 6:18.

[^6]: In the New Testament, the adjective 'holy' (ἅγιαι) is applied to the writings only once: St Paul speaks of the 'holy Scriptures' (γραφαì ắγιαι, Rom 1:2).

[^7]: Curiously, Josephus never uses 'holy' (ἅγιος) in reference to the books, but always 'sacred' (ἱερός), perhaps in reference to the temple (τὸ ἱερόν) in Jerusalem, where a copy of the scriptures was kept. Cf. C. Gerber, 'Die Heiligen Schriften des Judentums nach Flavius Josephus' in M. Hengel and H. Löhr (eds.), Schriftauslegung im antiken Judentum und im Urchristentum, Mohr Siebeck, 1994, 91-111.

[^8]: Cf. J. C. Ossandón Widow, *The Origins of the Canon of the Hebrew Bible: An Analysis of Josephus and 4 Ezra*, Brill, 2018, 19-22.

[^9]: A medieval theologian, Hugh of Saint Victor (1096-1141), expressed this conviction with remarkable clarity: 'The whole of Divine Scripture constitutes a single book, and this single book is Christ; it speaks of Christ and finds its fulfilment in Christ' (De arca Noe, 2, 8: PL 176, 642 C-D). We shall endeavour to explain later (cf. pp. 9-103) how the unity of the Scriptures is arrived at on the basis of Christ, without eliminating the differences between the books.

[^10]: Cf. J. H. Crehan, 'The Analogy between Verbum Dei incarnatum and Verbum Dei scriptum in the Fathers', Journal of Theological Studies 6 (1955) 8-9; P. Beauchamp, Parler d'Écritures Saintes, Seuil, 198, 21-24.

[^11]: This analogy first appeared in a magisterial text in 1943 in Pius XII's encyclical *Divino Agente Spiritu* (cf. EB 559); after the Second Vatican Council, it was taken up again by John Paul II: cf. St John Paul II, Address on the centenary of the encyclical 'Providentissimus Deus' and the fiftieth anniversary of the encyclical 'Divino Affiante Spiritu' (2 April 1993), nos. 8-9 (EB 1247-1248).

[^12]: See L. Bouyer, 'Où en est le mouvement biblique?', *Bible et vie chrétienne* 1 (1956) 1-21, 18; L. Alonso Schökel, *La palabra inspirada: la Biblia a la luz de la ciencia del lenguaje *, *Cristiandad*,! 1986, 49-53.

[^13]: John Paul II, Address on the centenary of the encyclical 'Providentissimus Deus' and the fiftieth anniversary of the encyclical 'Divino Agente Spiritu' (2 April 1993), nos. 8-9 (EB 1247-1248).

[^14]: During the Hasmonean and Roman periods, the term 'Judea' (Ιουδαία, Iudea) was applied not only to the territory of the tribe of Judah, but to a wider area. Herod the Great was 'King of Judea', a title encompassing Samaria, Galilee, Perea and Idumea. Following the Bar Kokhba revolt (115 AD), Emperor Hadrian changed the name to Syria Palaestina. From the 4th century AD onwards, the territory came to be known simply as Palestine.

[^15]: Originally, the system consisted of around a thousand 'pictographic-ideographic' signs, that is, signs that suggested or represented things. After one or two centuries, signs began to be used to represent not only things, but also the sounds of the names used in the spoken language. Half a millennium after its invention, writing became a developed system, capable of producing written documents. Cf. J. Bottéro, *La religión más antigua: Mesopotamia *, Trotta, 2001, p. 12.

[^16]: See A. Demsky, 'Writing in Ancient Israel and Early Judaism: The Biblical Period' in M. J. Mulder - H. Sysling (eds.), *Mikra: Text, Translation, Reading and Interpretation of the Hebrew Bible in Ancient Judaism and Early Christianity*, Van Gorcum, 1988, 2-20, 2-9.

[^17]: For the following, see H. Y. Gamble, Books and Readers in the Early Church: A History of Early Christian Texts, Paideia, 2006, pp. 1-100, especially pp. 1-8. Original English edition: Ibid., Books and Readers in the Early Church: A History of Early Christian Texts, Yale University Press, 1995 (Chapter 2). For this section, see also J. Trebolle Barrera, *The Jewish Bible and the Christian Bible: An Introduction to the Study of the Bible *, Trotta,! 1998, 94-111.

[^18]: Gamble, Books and Readers, p. 2.

[^19]: In Jer 36:2, the Greek version translates 'the scroll' (הלִּגָמַּהְ) as 'the papyrus' (ó χάρτης). Qumran is the name of a site near the Dead Sea, on its north-western shore, which became famous thanks to the discoveries made from 1947 onwards. Several hundred scrolls were found in eleven caves, all dating from before the site was abandoned during the First Jewish-Roman War. Indeed, after destroying Jerusalem, the Roman Tenth Legion marched towards the Dead Sea to put down the last pockets of resistance, as far as the fortress of Masada, which fell in AD 12 AD. The most widely accepted hypothesis regarding the identity of the group that lived at Qumran is that they were a sect or community associated with the Essenes. The group is thought to have left Jerusalem around 150 BC in protest against the corruption of the high priests. Indeed, some of the writings reveal a rejection of the worship practised in the Temple in Jerusalem.

[^20]: Cf. M. Bar-Ilan, 'Writing in Ancient Israel and Early Judaism: Scribes and Books in the Late Second Commonwealth and Rabbinic Period' in M. J. Mulder - H. Sysling (eds.), *Mikra: Text, Translation, Reading and Interpretation of the Hebrew Bible in Ancient Judaism and Early Christianity*, Van Gorcum, 1988, 21-26.

[^21]: 'The term 'parchment' is often used to refer to leather and vellum as well as to parchment proper, but although they are all made from animal skin, there are marked differences between these materials. As it is not tanned, parchment is thinner, softer and lighter in colour than leather, and was treated in such a way as to allow writing on both the hair side and the flesh side. Vellum is a finer type of parchment, made from calf or kidskin. The production of parchment appears to have begun around the 3rd century BC. According to the traditional, though false, account, its invention is said to date back to the city of Pergamon, in western Asia Minor. Perhaps due to having perfected the manufacturing process or to the city's role as the leading producer, Pergamon gave one of the names attributed to this material in antiquity, as the Greeks called it pergamênê and the Romans pergamena. These names, however, are of rather late origin; before the 4th century AD, the Greeks called parchment 'diphthera', and the Romans 'membrana', meaning 'skin'', Gamble, Books and Readers, 74.

[^22]: Ibid., Books and Readers, y!-74.

[^23]: Cf. Trebolle Barrera, The Jewish Bible and the Christian Bible, 97.

[^24]: 'To assemble the scroll, the sheets were arranged with the front side facing upwards and were joined by overlapping and gluing a narrow edge (1 or 2 cm) on the right-hand margin of each sheet to the left-hand edge of the next in the series. (…) The joins were smoothed out and made virtually imperceptible, so that, when writing on the scroll, the pen would encounter no obstacles as it passed over them. A papyrus scroll produced in this way could, in theory, be of any length, but it seems that the standard length was twenty sheets, amounting to approximately 1.5 m. The strip of glued sheets was then rolled up with the recto-that is, the side intended for writing-on the inside, so that it would be protected. In this form, as a scroll known as chartês (in Latin charta or volumen), the papyrus was transported by sea, stored and sold on the retail market. It was then possible to obtain scrolls of any length by cutting or joining these units', Gamble, Books and Readers, 72-73.

[^25]: Translation taken from Plato, Phaedrus: edited by Giovanni Reale, Arnoldo Mondadori, 2001.

[^26]: Cf. Trebolle Barrera, The Jewish Bible and the Christian Bible, 106-110.

[^27]: 'This editorial procedure made it possible, above all, to add material at the beginning and end of the books. The chapters added to the end of the Book of Judges (chapters 1-18 and 19-21) and 2 Samuel (chapters 22-24) may have been introduced using a similar editorial technique, applied to scroll writing', Ibid., The Jewish Bible and the Christian Bible, 106.

[^28]: Gamble, Books and Readers, pp. 5-6. Cf. Trebolle Barrera, The Jewish Bible and the Christian Bible, p. 106.

[^30]: The Jews refer to these five books as the Měgillôt: the Song of Songs, Ruth, Ecclesiastes, Lamentations and Esther. They form a certain unity within the Hebrew Bible because they are read on specific festivals: the Song of Songs at Passover, Ruth at the Feast of Weeks - our Pentecost -, Ecclesiastes at the Feast of Tabernacles or Sukkot, Lamentations on the day commemorating the destruction of the First Temple, and Esther at the festival of Purim.

[^68]: Here is the full text of St John in Spanish: 'Because in giving us, as he did, his Son - who is his Word, and he has no other - he spoke everything to us together and at once in this one Word, and he has nothing more to say. And this is the meaning of that authority with which Saint Paul seeks to persuade the Hebrews to turn away from those earlier ways and dealings with God under the Law of Moses, and to set their eyes on Christ alone, saying: 'What God formerly spoke to our fathers through the prophets in many ways and forms, He has now, at last, in these days, spoken to us in the Son, all at once'. By this the Apostle implies that God has now become, as it were, silent, and has nothing more to say, because what he previously spoke in parts to the prophets he has now spoken in full through him, giving us the whole, which is his Son. Therefore, anyone who now wishes to ask God a question or seek a vision or revelation would not only be acting foolishly, but would also be offending God, by failing to fix their eyes entirely on Christ and desiring nothing else or anything new. For God might reply to them in this way: 'Since I have already spoken all things to you in my Word, who is my Son, and I have nothing else to reveal or answer to you that is greater than that, set your eyes solely on him; for in him I have placed, spoken and revealed everything to you, and you will find in him even more than what you ask for and desire' (Ascent of Mount Carmel 2.22).

[^69]: For example, Benedict XVI used it in his message to the PCB on 18 April 2012: 'For, whilst the act of Revelation was concluded with the death of the last Apostle, the revealed Word has continued to be proclaimed and interpreted by the living Tradition of the Church'.

[^70]: 'It is not expressly stated that revelation came to an end with the death of the apostles. For the matter is already considered settled when it is said that Christ completed revelation; yet the formulation is not without difficulties, and indeed for various reasons', F. Gil Hellín, Synopsis of the Second Vatican Council, organising the draft documents with the reports, as well as the speeches and comments of the Fathers: Dogmatic Constitution on Divine Revelation Dei Verbum, Libreria Editrice Vaticana, 1993, 34. Cardinal Ruffini's contribution can be found on pp. 558-560.

[^71]: One author comments: 'As the culmination of the history of revelation, Jesus Christ gives the events that precede him a dual dimension: on the one hand, they have revelatory value in their own right; on the other, their value is relative, in that they are events whose meaning is found in Jesus Christ. (…) In other words, the actions through which God reveals himself to the people are revelation, they are the word of God, but they are also part of the complete discourse that is found only in Christ, the sole discourse of God in revelation. Only in Christ is the discourse complete', Balaguer, 'La economía', 432.

Izquierdo, 'The Role of Texts in Tradition' in V. Balaguer - J. L. Caballero (eds.), Word of God, Holy Scripture, Church, Eunsa, 2008, pp. 5-82, pp. 5-6.

[^78]: 'It seems that Christ ought to have handed down his teaching in writing. For Scripture was devised for the purpose of ensuring that the teaching is preserved in memory for the future. But Christ's teaching was to endure for ever'.

[^79]: 'Firstly, indeed, because of his own dignity. For a more excellent teacher deserves a more excellent mode of teaching. And therefore, as the most excellent of teachers, this mode was fitting for Christ, so that he might impress his teaching upon the hearts of his listeners. Hence it is said in Matthew 7 that he was teaching them as one who had authority. Hence, even among the Gentiles, Pythagoras and Socrates, who were most excellent teachers, did not wish to write anything. For writings are intended to impress the teaching upon the hearts of the listeners as their ultimate purpose.'

[^80]: In this case, the translation is my own. 'Secondly, because of the excellence of Christ's teaching, which cannot be comprehended in writing, as stated in the last chapter of John, there are also many other things that Jesus did; if these were to be written down one by one, I do not think the world itself could contain the books that would be written. As Augustine says, it is not to be believed that the world cannot contain them because of a lack of space, but that they cannot be comprehended by the capacity of the readers. If, however, Christ had entrusted his teaching to writing, people would hold nothing of his teaching in higher esteem than what the Scriptures contain.'

[^81]: 'In relation to the Word of God, the supreme rule of faith, apostolic tradition and ecclesiastical tradition find themselves in two very different situations: the former is the means by which this Word reaches humankind and takes the form of human speech; the latter is the living environment that receives it, preserves it and brings it to fruition. The transition from one to the other does not take place at a specific point in time-for example, during the apostles' own lifetimes-simply because they entrusted the care of the Gospel and the responsibility for the churches to representatives who, unlike themselves, were no longer direct witnesses of Christ', Grelot, *La Bible, Parole de Dieu*, 22.

[^82]: On tradition in the Old Testament, cf. Ibid., *La Bible, Parole de Dieu*, 1-12; Mannucci - Mazzinghi, *Bibbia*, Chapter 4, Section 2 and Section 4 (comparison between the two traditions).

[^83]: 'When we speak of the apostolic proclamation, we are speaking of a message, of the Gospel: here the actions of the apostles are at the service of the message. When we speak of revelation, we understand that we are dealing with the language of God which we humans can comprehend, although it is clear that we do not know this language of God beforehand; it is revealed only through the actions of his discourse. When we speak of the apostolic proclamation, we are dealing with human language, constructed and known to humankind, but which does not express a human message but rather a message from God. However, whilst it is important to emphasise the differences, it is above all important to subsequently highlight the fundamental convergence: the apostolic proclamation is also part of historical revelation. To put it another way, God's revelation in history - the word of God in God's language contains within itself an articulation in human language, which expresses the word of God', Balaguer, 'La economía', 436.

[^84]: Cf. L. Buch, 'Spiritus est veritas (1 Jn 5:6): Revelation according to the Holy Spirit in the writings of St John', Annales Theologici 27 (2013) 51-83.

[^86]: 'This fear seems unfounded: for everyone knows that we are dealing with Mark, Luke, etc.' Cf. Gil Hellín, Synopsis of the Second Vatican Council: Dei Verbum, 56-57.

[^87]: My own translation. 'If it can be said that in every message the form is part of its content, in the process of Christian revelation it can be said that the human recipient also forms part of the message. It has sometimes been pointed out that faith is a revelation to which a response has been given; if this logic is applied to the form of revelation, we must conclude that the recipients of revelation form part of it in some way. This is even more pronounced in the case of the apostles: it is they-as witnesses of the risen Christ-who receive the full discourse of God's revelation and express it in human language', Balaguer, 'The Economy', 437.

Etenim Sacra Scriptura est locutio Dei quatenus divino affiante Spiritu scripto consignatur; whilst Sacred Tradition transmits the Word of God-entrusted by Christ the Lord and the Holy Spirit to the Apostles-intact to their successors, so that, guided by the Spirit of truth, they may faithfully preserve, expound and spread it through their preaching; thus the Church does not derive her certainty regarding all things revealed from Sacred Scripture alone. Holy Scripture is a sign of an object-the reference-which is the apostolic proclamation. It is evident that, just as a sign does not exhaust the reference but does point to its meaning, so Holy Scripture does not exhaust the Word of God in the apostolic proclamation, but does correctly point to its meaning. Secondly, the Word of God in the apostolic proclamation is a sign of a greater reality, namely God's revelation in history. Here too, the apostolic proclamation does not exhaust the referent, nor does it even exhaust its meaning, but it does correctly point to its meaning', Balaguer, 'La economía', 4-9.

' (Mk 12:10-11; cf. Mt 21:42; Lk 20:17). The quotation comes from Ps 118:22-23. Moreover, it is clear from the context that the rejected stone is Jesus himself, who is foretelling his own death. In Hebrew, there is a play on words. Jesus speaks of the son (in Hebrew ןבֵּ, bēn), killed by the vine-growers, and then of the stone (ֶאֶבן, 'eben) rejected by the builders.

[^203]: My translation. 'The apostolicity of the New Testament canon is a 'theologoumenon', that is, a theological construct with a conceptual core that is theological, but which is, at the same time, superimposed upon the rudimentary facts of historical tradition. The theological concept in itself is not justified without reference to some factual historical foundation. Yet the exact way in which the historical basis of an apostolic origin may be verified for one book or another is not defined with authority. Nor is it required that in each case the apostolic character of a book be defined in a univocally identical way. The Magisterium of the Church, up to and including *Dei Verbum* at the Second Vatican Council, has always considered it sufficient to affirm the apostolicity of the canonical books of the New Testament in some analogous sense of the word and without specifying in detail the exact manner in which the apostolic origin of a particular document is actually verified', Farkasfalvy, *Inspiration & Interpretation*, 48 (italics in the original).

[^296]: My translation. 'Therefore, divine inspiration does not imply that every passage and sentence of the biblical text must be found free of error from every conceivable point of view. The grammarian, the scientist, the psychologist, the philosopher, the historian, and others may point to a particular passage which, when examined from some limited point of view by some specialised branch of human learning, may be found to be flawed. But such a realisation does not prove that God's word contains error. Rather, it merely means that God's message is expressed, at one point or another in the history of salvation, with the imperfections characteristic of human existence. Nevertheless, in the way it serves both the human author's specifically defined purpose and its divine author's salvific purpose, every passage expresses the truth which it is intended to express in accordance with God's salvific will', Ibid., Inspiration & Interpretation, 2:12.

[^300]: I reproduce part of this note to illustrate the complexity of the term: 'Whilst in the language of nineteenth-century rationalism the term 'myth' denoted that which was not contained within reality-the product of the imagination [Wundt], or that which is irrational [Lévy-Bruhl]-the twentieth century has altered the conception of myth. L. Walk sees myth as a primitive, non-religious form of natural philosophy; R. Otto regards it as an instrument of religious knowledge; for C. G. Jung, on the other hand, myth is a manifestation of archetypes and an expression of the 'collective unconscious', a symbol of inner processes. M. Eliade discovers in myth the structure of reality that is inaccessible to rational and empirical inquiry: for myth transforms the event into a category and enables us to perceive transcendent reality; it is not merely a symbol of inner processes [as Jung asserts], but an autonomous and creative act of the human spirit, through which revelation is brought about [cf. Traité d'histoire des religions, Paris 1949, p. 16; Images et symboles, Paris 1952, pp. 199-215]'. The note continues with references to P. Tillich, H. Schlier and P. Ricoeur. [^332]: Thomas Aquinas explains: 'An instrument is something that is moved by a principal agent and yet can perform an operation of its own dependent on its form, as has been said of fire. Therefore, the action of the instrument insofar as it is an instrument is not distinct from the action of the principal agent, but the instrument can perform a distinct operation insofar as it is a reality in its own right (S.Th. III, q.19, a.1, ad 2, translation by T. Centi and A. Belloni, available online. Original text 'A thing is called an instrument because it is moved by the principal agent: yet, apart from this, it can have its own operation according to its form, as has been said of fire. Thus, the action of the instrument, insofar as it is an instrument, is no different from the action of the principal agent; yet it may have another operation insofar as it is a thing in its own right'.

[^333]: 'There are two kinds of efficient cause: the principal and the instrumental. The principal, indeed, acts by virtue of its form, to which the effect is assimilated: just as fire warms by its heat (...) The instrumental cause, however, does not act by virtue of its form, but solely by the motion with which it is moved by the principal agent. Hence the effect is not assimilated to the instrument, but to the principal agent'.

[^334]: My translation. 'The concept of freedom need not be entirely excluded from the instrument, for something may be moved by another whilst nevertheless moving itself; and such is the case with the human mind'.

[^343]: We might ask whether, outside the Church, God speaks through the Bible. In reality, the Church has never addressed questions of this sort. It is not easy to do so, on the one hand because we cannot establish with precision where the boundaries of the Church lie. On the other hand, because it seems logical that, if He so wishes, God can communicate with any person who reads the Bible in order to help them convert.

[^344]: My translation. 'If one asks what this action of the Spirit consists of, one must be careful not to reduce it to the inner illumination of believers' hearts; in reality, it accompanies, animates and assists, through charisms, the very exercise of the ministries that give Tradition its defined structure. Such is the totality of the concrete means by which the Spirit ensures the Church's indefectibility 'dans la foi', Grelot, *La Bible, Parole de Dieu *, 25.

[^345]: Translation by T. Centi and A. Belloni. 'Gratiae gratis datae dantur ad utilitatem aliorum, ut supra dictum est. Cognitio autem quam aliquis a Deo accipit, in utilitatem alterius converti non posset nisi mediante locutione. And since the Holy Spirit does not fall short in anything pertaining to the benefit of the Church, He also provides the members of the Church with the gift of speech, not only so that someone may speak in such a way as to be understood by different people-which pertains to the gift of tongues-but also so that they may speak effectively, which pertains to the grace of speech'. The following quotation would require some clarification, as we have not yet discussed the concept of prophecy in St Thomas, but it may be helpful: 'From the hagiographer's point of view, the charism of inspiration is prophetic only in an imperfect sense, because it normally lacks visions and, above all, because the recipient is not always aware of God's action upon them, nor do they perceive the full scope of their words. (…) On the other hand, when considering the reception of Scripture within the Church, one can speak of true and proper prophecy, since the elements that led to speaking of imperfection in some cases of prophecy disappear. Firstly, the Church is certain of the divine origin of the books and is therefore aware of God's revelatory action present in the texts-she knows that God is their author, which is why she regards them as inspired and canonical. Secondly, the Church does not read without understanding, as Caiaphas prophesied, but comprehends the content of the revelation, albeit not entirely. Finally, the Church receives apostolic revelation, which, according to the degrees described by St Thomas regarding the progress of revelation (II-II, q.1 and 4, a.6), corresponds to the highest degree of prophecy', J. C. Ossandón Widow, 'The Meanings of Scripture: An Approach to a Theological Definition of the Literal Sense', Excerpta et Dissertationibus in Sacra Theologia 49 (2006) 9-10, 6-68.

[^346]: For a comprehensive historical overview, see Tábet, *Le trattazioni teologiche *.

[^347]: De principiis, Praef., n.8, translation taken from Origen, The Principles; edited by Manlio Simonetti, UTET, 1968.

[^348]: De doctrina christiana, II,6,8 and cf. II,9,14. Translation: Augustine, The Christian Doctrine.

[^349]: Even in the Catechism of the Catholic Church, the definition seems to be taken for granted. The CTI has a document entitled 'The Apostolicity of the Church and Apostolic Succession' (1973), but it does not explain what it means to be an apostle. Benedict XVI devoted several general audiences to the apostles, but confined himself to explaining the role of the Twelve according to the four Gospels (cf. General Audience, 22 March 2006, 'The Apostles, Witnesses and Envoys of Christ') and to commenting on DV 10 and 8 (cf. General Audience, 1 May 2006, 'Apostolic Tradition').

[^352]: Latourelle, 'Apostle', 81.

[^353]: In John's letters, the statement that God sent his Son (cf. 1 John 4:9, 10, 14, always using the verb ἀποστέλλω) appears again, but, as mentioned, the word 'apostle' does not appear.

[^354]: In Matthew, the word 'apostle' appears only once, applied precisely to the Twelve (Mt 10:2). In Mark, too, there is only one occurrence (Mk 6:10), which likewise applies to the Twelve. But in both cases, the term is used in a sense very close to, if not identical with, the etymological-Semitic meaning of 'delegate'.

[^355]: In Mk 1:11-14, the CEI version reads: 'He then went up the mountain, called to him those he wished, and they came to him. He appointed Twelve - whom he called apostles -'. However, the words 'whom he called apostles' [οὓς καὶ ἀποστόλους ὠνόμασεν] are missing from some manuscripts and appear to be a harmonisation with Luke 6:13.

[^356]: Why does it say 'the Twelve' if there were only eleven at the time of the appearances of the risen Jesus? The most logical answer is that the name 'the Twelve' had by then become a standard expression. It is also worth noting, here and in the passage quoted from 1 Corinthians 9, the separate mention of Cephas (Peter), even though he was one of the Twelve.

[^357]: The expression is usually attributed to Hippolytus of Rome (Comm. in Canticum, ed. G. Nathanael Bonwetsch, n. 67). Cf. also St Jerome (Comm. in Sophoniam, prologue), St Bernard (Sermones super Cantica Canticorum, sermo 15, 8, in Bernardi opera, ed. J. Leclercq, C.H. Talbot and H.M. Rochais, 1957-1958, vol. 2, p. 251), and St Thomas Aquinas (In Ioannem Evangelistam Expositio, ch. 20, l. 1, 6). Cf. Burnet, 'La notion d'apostolicité', 188, n. 13.

[^358]: On the grounds, within the apostolic tradition, for rejecting the ordination of women, cf. P. Grelot, *La tradition apostolique: règle de foi et de vie pour l'Église*, Cerf, 1995, 19-161 ('Will there be 'women priests' in the Church?').

[^360]: The 'prophets' mentioned here and in Ephesians 2:20 are not those of the Old Testament, but a category of witnesses to Christ within the first generation of Christians, who occupied second place after the apostles, as is also evident in Ephesians 4:11 and 1 Corinthians 12:28. Cf. also Luke 11:49.

[^361]: 'The expression 'the twelve apostles' is a summary rather than a rigorous enumeration: the term 'the Twelve' was used without thereby excluding Paul and Barnabas from the apostolate, and without concern that the Twelve might number fourteen', Batiffol, L'Église naissante, 65.

[^362]: For a summary of the various hypotheses regarding the composition of the Pentateuch, see M. Tábet, Introduction to the Pentateuch and the Historical Books of the Old Testament, Apollinare Studi, 2001, 19-67; F. Giuntoli, 'The Pentateuch' in P. Merlo (ed.), The Old Testament: A Historical-Literary Introduction, Carocci, 2008, 99-127.

[^363]: Outside the boundaries of the canon, the situation changes. In the Book of Enoch (z Enoch), a collection of written revelations is attributed to Enoch, one of the pre-Flood patriarchs, who 'disappeared because God had taken him' (Gen 5:24; cf. Sir 44:16; 49:14). There is also a work called the Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs, that is, the last words of each of Jacob's twelve sons. Later works include the Apocalypse of Abraham, the Apocalypse of Adam and various writings attributed to Seth, son of Adam.

[^364]: 'The book is therefore, for the history to come, and regardless of the future tribulations of the Ark (which will, in fact, disappear), the public aspect-in the form of a witness to the charge-of divine revelation', J.-P. Sonnet, ''When Moses had finished writing' (Deut. 1:24). A 'narrative theory' of writing in the Pentateuch', Recherches de science religieuse 90 (2002) 509-524, 520.

[^365]: 'Leaving behind the founding era (which began with creation and the patriarchs, and culminated in the theophany at Horeb), the Moses of Deuteronomy leads the people of the sons of Israel into 'modern' existence, that is to say, into 'earthly' existence - in the Promised Land, beyond the Jordan and the 'theological' desert. Moses does not cross the Jordan, but he does not die without crossing another threshold, that which marks the transition from oral to written communication', Ibid., 'Deuteronomy and the Modernity of the Book', *La nouvelle revue théologique* 118 (1996) 481-496, 482.

[^367]: 'Moses,' the text tells us, 'was the first to do what the biblical scribes have always done: add to the text. The text of the Law is therefore characterised by a dual dynamic: that of canonisation (…) and that of 'supplementation'.' (…) Here, too, therefore, it is scribal practices and techniques that are projected onto the narrative stage, staged within the founding event, in the form of a dialectic that brings into play both the growth of a revealed corpus and its closure', Sonnet, 'Lorsque Moïse', 521-522.

[^368]: 2 Kings 23:25; cf. 22:2. Another king praised by the hagiographer, again on the basis of his fidelity to the Mosaic Torah, is Manasseh's father, Hezekiah: cf. 2 Kings 18:3-6.

[^369]: In 2 Chr 14:18, a parallel text to 2 Kings 22:10, it is stated that, after receiving the book found in the temple, 'Shaphan read some of it before the king', whereas in 2 Kings it simply states that he read it. The author of Chronicles adapts the ancient account to the book of the Torah with which he is familiar, which would have been impossible to read in a short space of time.

[^370]: A text that reflects the depth of veneration for the Torah characteristic of the religion of Israel, as a manifestation of God's will, is Psalm 119, the longest in the Psalter. According to Mannucci-Mazzinghi, *Bibbia*, chapter 8, 1.1c, the psalm is a praise of the written Torah, because it is alphabetical; that is to say, the beginning of each stanza is constructed according to the Hebrew alphabet (as in Lamentations 1-4 or Proverbs 11:10-11). However, if one reads the psalm carefully, it becomes clear that it never mentions the Book of the Law, nor does it speak of the reading of the precepts, rules and commandments. The alphabetical structure is therefore not sufficient to conclude that everything the psalm says about the Torah should be attributed to the book that contains it.

[^371]: 'It does not therefore seem to me that Ben Sira makes a strict identification between Wisdom and the Law, but rather that he recognises in biblical revelation the finest expression of divine Wisdom', Gilbert, 'Sirach', 1427. [^372]: Pontifical Biblical Commission, The Jewish People, no. 11.

[^373]: 'In the law, the normative content is fundamental and, by its very nature, requires permanence- 'the permanence and stability it normally acquires through transcription. Prophecy is the word of God in its own right at the very moment of its utterance', Artola - Sánchez Caro, The Bible and the Word of God, 14.

For a commentary on some of these texts and others like them, see H. Najman, 'The Symbolic Significance of Writing in Ancient Judaism' in H. Najman and J. H. Newman (eds.), The Idea of Biblical Interpretation: Essays in Honour of James L. Kugel, Brill, 2004, 119-121.
